# Jean-Luc Marion, *Sur le prisme métaphysique de Descartes* — Commentary

References are to the pagination of the PUF/Épiméthée edition (Paris, 1986); Descartes is cited by Adam-Tannery volume, page and line, following Marion's own convention (AT; DM for the *Discours*; the volume number omitted for the *Meditationes* and *Responsiones*).

Four confusions beset this book's reception and are worth naming in advance. First, Marion's "metaphysics" is a measured concept, drawn from Aristotle, the Jesuit commentators and the German school manuals, and then from Heidegger's onto-theo-logical model; the question is never whether Descartes "has a metaphysics" in the loose sense historians use, but whether his thought satisfies criteria fixed independently of it. Second, onto-theo-logy does not mean "a metaphysics that includes a God." It requires that one and the same foundational logic ground being twice over — in the most common being and in the supreme being — each grounding the other in a determinate order; readers who take the term as a synonym for rational theology will find chapter II unintelligible, and will miss that Descartes turns out to sustain not one such constitution but two. Third, the prism is an instrument, not a result: onto-theo-logy is achromatic, and the "grey ontology" and "white theology" of the earlier volumes are the colours it separates out, not rival names for it. Fourth, *clôture* is not termination. To close Descartes' metaphysical constitution is to fix its limits, and fixing a limit is what makes an overcoming thinkable — which is the business of chapter V, not a concession appended to it. To these should be added a caution about the word "transgression," which throughout carries the schoolmen's sense of *post physicam seu ultra physicam*: metaphysics oversteps a science by founding it, and therefore confirms what it demotes.

## The Closure of a Question

Marion opens (p. 1) by refusing to treat as settled the question of whether Cartesian thought belongs to metaphysics at all. The first index is philological and admittedly slight: Descartes wrote *Meditationes de prima Philosophia*, and the adjective in *Méditations métaphysiques* is the translator's addition. Marion declines to load the fact with more than it bears, but the asymmetry it exposes is real — common usage assigns metaphysics an eminent function in a Cartesian title that does without the word. Behind the asymmetry lies a genuine difficulty. Expounding the doctrine of the *Meditationes* and establishing that this doctrine accomplishes what the tradition before Descartes meant by metaphysics are two different tasks, and only the first has been performed, magisterially and repeatedly. The same holds for the claim to *philosophia prima*. Marion's own undertaking is accordingly not exposition but measurement: to determine whether and how far the doctrine constitutes a metaphysics, by the criteria that defined metaphysics before and during Descartes' lifetime. The footnote sharpens the charge into a survey. Alquié fixes "metaphysical" only by an undetermined *dépassement*; Gouhier states outright that his book presupposes Cartesian metaphysics as known — which means, Marion observes, that the notion of metaphysics itself is presupposed as known; Vuillemin, Röd, Beyssade and Beck are named to the same effect. The quality of these studies is what makes the omission conspicuous.

Two motives, Marion insists (p. 2), make the enterprise close to unavoidable, and neither is a search for manufactured originality.

The historical motive (pp. 2–4) turns on a displacement Marion locates precisely. By the time Descartes appears, the tradition has secured a reasonably firm concept of metaphysics through work reaching from the earliest Aristotle commentators across the whole medieval period. Aquinas, who uses the term sparingly, has the Philosopher determine in the *Metaphysics* both being in general and the first being separate from matter; Suarez, for whom the term is fundamental, defines the science that contemplates being as being, or as it abstracts from matter. On either definition metaphysics bears on *ens*, whether common and taken as such or first and separate. What Kant finds when he takes up the last scholastic tradition is something else entirely: Baumgarten's metaphysics as the science containing the first principles of human cognition, transposed exactly in the precritical Kant, where the first philosophy containing the principles of the use of the pure understanding *is* metaphysics. Metaphysics now bears not on being in its states but on cognition, viewed from the human understanding — which is why a "metaphysics of metaphysics" can unfold as a critique of pure reason: metaphysics already coincides with the principles of the pure understanding, and the critique merely redoubles it as knowledge of the principles of knowledge. Marion's question is how metaphysics could migrate so completely from one domain to the other, and his answer names Descartes rather than Spinoza, Malebranche or Leibniz, who are decisive only through what remains Cartesian in them. The evidence is Descartes' own commentary on the title he devised: he will not treat God and the soul in particular but "en général de toutes les premières choses qu'on peut connaître en philosophant" (AT III, 235, 13–18). Primacy passes from the first being to be known to knowing itself, which may subsequently be fixed in a being; the being as such, even as first, drops out. In the title's silences and its avowed intentions the reversal is already accomplished that separates the earlier scholasticism from the later, Aquinas and Suarez from Kant and the *Schulmetaphysik*. Two questions follow, and the second is the sharper: did Descartes redefine the received concept deliberately and in full knowledge, and if he did, does the modification not forbid retaining the word? Having renounced the concept, should he not have renounced the name *Metaphysica* as well? These are referred forward to chapter I, and they carry the argument out of the historical register into the matter itself.

The conceptual motive (pp. 4–6) asks whether the thought of the *Meditationes* can still claim the title *Metaphysica* after rejecting its predecessors' concept — a question on which also hangs the metaphysical authenticity of the post-Cartesian philosophies, which up to Kant is more problematic than is usually admitted. Hegel's remark about the concept of Cartesian metaphysics carries authority but settles nothing; what is needed is a concept of metaphysics as such against which Cartesian thought can be measured. Marion passes over the school articulation of metaphysics into *ontologia* and a special metaphysics divided into rational theology, psychology and cosmology, and adopts instead Heidegger's onto-theo-logical model, noting in passing that Schopenhauer had already spoken of an ontotheological proposition of Descartes. Two reasons are given, and Marion ranks them. The stronger is that the onto-theo-logical model is at present the most fruitful available and nearly the only one; the point is not to impose it on Descartes but to use it to test how Descartes constitutes himself according to a figure of onto-theo-logy — and, reciprocally, to subject the model itself to a fresh trial of its validity, from which it may emerge modified. The reciprocity is not a courtesy. Heidegger never attempted an explicit onto-theo-logical hermeneutic of Descartes, though he risked one for Plato, Aristotle, Leibniz, Kant, Hegel and Nietzsche, and Marion presses the omission both ways: it may index a problematic metaphysical status in Descartes, or it may indicate that Descartes' exceptional situation threatens a model of *simple* onto-theo-logy. The weaker reason is autobiographical. *Sur l'ontologie grise* had extracted, beneath the anti-Aristotelian epistemology of the *Regulae*, the counter-ontology that alone permits the mind to disqualify the *ousia* of beings and reduce them to objects; *Sur la théologie blanche* had located the site of the first principle and the ambiguity of the primordial being, reading the 1630 doctrine of created eternal truths as at once a resumption of the question of the *analogia entis* and a transition toward the problematic of foundation, hence toward the principle of reason. Joining the two now requires more than addition. It requires that a single logic harmonize the two beings that ontology and theology respectively concern, and — the load-bearing claim — that a new term appear, absolutely real though not ontic: metaphysics itself. Grey ontology can be articulated onto white theology only in the crucible of a metaphysics older than either, which governed both before it ever surfaced as a concept. That non-ontic reality is what the two earlier volumes could not supply and what this one is written to establish.

Such a constitution, if the texts sustain it, casts a new light on the whole of Cartesian thought (pp. 6–8) — or more exactly, it operates on that thought and on its own evidence as a prism operates on the light it filters, making the metaphysical spectrum appear by decomposing it. What Marion had earlier risked under the titles of grey ontology and white theology were already products of that decomposition: seen through the prism of onto-theo-logical constitution, even the ostensibly non-metaphysical texts of the corpus release, like primitive colours, the constituted and constitutive elements of a metaphysics. The third title carries no colour because it names not a band of the spectrum but the prism that produces the bands and therefore precedes them; onto-theo-logy stays achromatic exactly because it provokes elementary metaphysical colours. The spectral analysis is then directed at two privileged beings, the ego (chapter III) and God (chapter IV), and Marion states the double charge with care. In each case one must verify whether these signal beings assume the characters of onto-theo-logy as the metaphysicians themselves define them; and in each case the concept of onto-theo-logy is itself put under load, since it must interpret both the primacy of a finite being and the polysemy of an infinite one. The substantiality of the *res cogitans* and the plurality of the divine names are thrown at the onto-theo-logical definition of metaphysics as two challenges. The ambition is therefore symmetrical: to determine whether Descartes attains the dignity of a metaphysician by constituting himself according to onto-theo-logy, and to submit Heidegger's thesis of an essentially onto-theo-logical constitution of metaphysics to comprehension, verification and, at the risk of simplifying, falsification — applied to the philosopher Marion knows least badly.

The closing paragraph (p. 8) answers both questions in advance, and the qualification it attaches is the book's whole trajectory. Yes on both counts — up to a point only. For if the onto-theo-logical constitution strictly delimits the Cartesian constitution of metaphysics, then it fixes that constitution's limits; and in closing it, it opens the possibility of its overcoming. The title of the introduction should be read from here: *clôture* is at once the completion of a question and the drawing of a boundary, and the boundary is what makes chapter V's *Dépassement* something other than a change of subject.

## I. Metaphysics

### § 1. An Indeterminate Question

Marion begins (pp. 9–11) with the philological facts, and insists on fixing them before any speculation about a Cartesian concept of metaphysics. The word arrives late and stays scarce. Its first occurrence is not before the letter to Mersenne of 15 April 1630, and there it is an adjective, not yet a substantive: one can demonstrate "les vérités Métaphysiques" (AT I, 144, 15). It remains *rare* — Marion allows himself the pun on Descartes' "corps rare" (AT XI, 23, 5) — with two occurrences in the *Discours*, again adjectival, and in the *Meditationes* a single adjectival hapax that is also disparaging: the "valde tenuis et, ut ita dicam, Metaphysica dubitandi ratio" (AT VII, 36, 24–25), rendered in French as an opinion that is slight and, so to speak, metaphysical. Does metaphysics then amount to the frivolity of a paradoxical, overstretched, sophistical procedure, foreign to the sound solidity of real knowledge — is Descartes conceding to the cheap mockery that disqualifies the enterprise as such? Before drawing that conclusion Marion sets a second fact beside the first. Descartes wrote not *Meditationes metaphysicae* but *Meditationes de prima Philosophia*: the discretion of *métaphysique* is owed to the pre-eminence of first philosophy, which doubles it and can replace it. The 1647 preface to the French *Principes* is significant here as the first place in a signed work where Descartes defines metaphysics in his own right — the first part of true philosophy, containing the principles of knowledge, among them the explanation of God's principal attributes, of the soul's immateriality and of all the clear and simple notions in us; then the tree, whose roots are metaphysics, trunk physics, branches the remaining sciences reducible to medicine, mechanics and morals (AT IX-2, 14, 7–28). Marion calls the formula too famous, and identifies what its schoolroom clarity conceals. It is not self-evident that metaphysics is the root of physics or of anything else, if it is simultaneously granted to crown the other sciences as the slightest, most abstract and most hyperbolic of them; and it is not self-evident that it should justify itself by utility, mediated through the derived sciences it makes possible, since the indirect utility conceded would have sense only if metaphysics had to serve for something at all. The note tracks the criterion of utility back through the *utilitas methodi* of the *Regulae* and the *Discours*' conversion of a speculative philosophy into a practical one, where utility slides toward synonymy with truth — so the tree is not an innocent pedagogical image but the terminus of a long-standing decision. Above all, the apparently unreserved recognition dissimulates that metaphysics does not exercise plenary authority, for two reasons. It is inscribed within the wider field of philosophy, understood as the study of wisdom and hence as complete knowledge of everything a man can know, for the conduct of life as much as for health and the invention of the arts. And within the philosophical field the name is exchangeable with others — with *prima philosophia* above all, as in the dedicatory letter to the *Principia*, where those versed in metaphysics recoil from geometry and the geometers cannot grasp what he wrote on first philosophy: the opposition holds between geometry on one side and, indifferently, metaphysics or first philosophy on the other. The *Principia* confirm the ambivalence structurally, since their first part, serving as the metaphysics, is devoted to the *principia cognitionis humanae* without naming metaphysics at all. The difficulty can no longer be evaded. If Descartes grants the term only the role of substitute and qualifier for first philosophy, can he be said to have thought metaphysics in its most original essence and to have constituted a particular figure of it? Or did he approach it obliquely, by accident and tactics rather than by any necessity of thought, as so many others have? Does terminological embarrassment license the conclusion that Descartes conceptually evaded any constitution of metaphysics?

By no means, Marion answers (pp. 11–12), and the first of three motives is the most important methodologically. The absence of homogeneous and recurrent occurrences of the lemma forbids nothing about a meditation on the essence of metaphysics; otherwise Aristotle, wholly ignorant of the neologism the tradition imposed on a collection of his treatises, would have been the first to desert the question, and Aquinas, who uses the term sparingly, would be next. Even total omission would prove nothing, since such a silence would be one more avatar of the historial destiny of metaphysics — a destiny, Marion adds, composed more of disappearances than of resumptions. The reversal that follows is the load-bearing move: in Descartes' hesitations and uncertainties a figure of metaphysics may be at play precisely as half-drawn and half-visible, and by keeping almost entirely silent about metaphysics Descartes could be taking a decision about its essence. Absence of the word thus becomes evidence rather than an obstacle to it — which is what licenses a whole book on a term that occurs a handful of times.

The second motive (pp. 12–14) is documentary and shows that the silence was not available to Descartes as an option. When he sends Mersenne the manuscript of the *Meditationes* he admits his design of writing an entire course of his philosophy in the form of theses and printing in the same volume an ordinary course of philosophy with his notes at the end of each question, the whole closing with a comparison of the two philosophies (AT III, 233, 4–15). The note records what the plan concedes: such a comparison would not merely juxtapose two incompatible philosophies but would follow the School's order. To execute it Descartes had to consult his scholastic contemporaries, and therefore to read them — he wants to reread their philosophy, which he has not done for twenty years, and asks Mersenne for the names of the most followed authors, remembering only Coimbra, Toletus and Rubius and hoping for a compendium. Among the candidates he settles on Eustache de Saint-Paul, whose *Summa Philosophiae quadripartita de rebus Dialecticis, Moralibus, Physicis et Metaphysicis* had appeared in Paris in 1609, and it is very probably on this common title-form that he provisionally names the future *Principia* a *Summa Philosophiae*, so that it may pass more easily into the conversation of the schoolmen. But the title brings the quadripartition with it, and all the more strictly because Descartes means to mark his distance by strict parallel. He had gone through that fourfold course at La Flèche, as he reminds Voetius in claiming to have learned school philosophy — logic, metaphysics, physics — at least tolerably; and the missing fourth term surfaces in the standing opposition between what holds *in Ethicis* and what holds *in Metaphysicis*. Respected also by Abra de Raconis and Scipion Dupleix, the quadripartition made it conceptually as well as tactically obligatory that Descartes treat metaphysics as such: without it the didactic ambition he keeps confessing to his correspondents would have collapsed.

The third motive (p. 14) converts the residual obscurity into positive evidence. If, under these constraints, the Cartesian determination of metaphysics still remains shadowed by withdrawals and obscurities, the ambiguities cannot be put down to ignorance or negligence; they attest, if only negatively, the difficulty of the reinterpretation Descartes is attempting, and they measure *a contrario* a mutation in the essence — not merely the lexicon — of metaphysics within its historial destiny. The programme for chapter I follows: examine systematically the rare occurrences of *métaphysique* and *metaphysica* in the corpus, and measure the innovations there by "comparison" with the entire body of philosophy that had put the question of metaphysics to Descartes in the first place. That requires reconstituting, from Aquinas, Pererius, Fonseca and Suarez, and equally from Goclenius, Eustache de Saint-Paul, Scipion Dupleix and Abra de Raconis, the path of thought by which revisions and displacements carried Descartes to a radically new concept of metaphysics.

### § 2. Metaphysics as Transgression

The first determination (pp. 14–16) is drawn from the 1630 letter, whose occasion Marion concedes is lost: Mersenne had put a question Descartes reclassifies as metaphysical rather than theological, on the ground that it does not depend on revelation and must be examined by human reason. What the question was cannot be recovered, and matters less than the two determinations of the concept the reply makes possible. The first is that Descartes claims to have found how to demonstrate metaphysical truths more evidently than the demonstrations of geometry (AT I, 144, 14–17), and that among the metaphysical questions is the thesis that the mathematical truths Mersenne calls eternal were established by God and depend entirely on him, like the rest of the creatures (145, 6–10). Metaphysical truths therefore overstep mathematical ones and the geometrical demonstrations alike: metaphysics surpasses mathematics in certainty and in dignity together, because it alone manages to conceive their dependent and created status. Marion defers the scope and nature of the transgression, but registers immediately what accompanies it — the extreme difficulty, and perhaps the impossibility, of each science understanding the other. Descartes appends at once that he says this by his own judgement and does not know whether he can persuade others, and the remark divides into two claims. Mathematicians, holding strictly to the rationality of their science, cannot think that God is a cause whose power exceeds the bounds of the human understanding; and metaphysics and mathematics for the most part exclude one another, there being no more men fit for metaphysical study than for geometrical, and those versed in the one recoiling from the other. The exclusion is not an accidental shortfall of minds unequal to two sciences but follows from a contradiction between conditions of possibility: the part of the mind that most assists mathematics, namely imagination, hinders metaphysical speculation more than it helps. Marion's gloss identifies why. Imagination serves as auxiliary to the pure understanding, allowing it to grasp geometrical extension as a real object, even though extension in fact appears only by abstraction from particular physical objects — so the faculty that secures mathematics is the faculty metaphysics must suspend. Hence the first characteristic: metaphysics transgresses mathematics, and oversteps them precisely in positing them, apprehending them as created, that is, as at once dependent on a prior institution and instituted by an instance that remains unintelligible to them because it founds them.

The second determination (pp. 16–19) is transgression of physics, and it works by foundation rather than by creation. Descartes moves to metaphysics only insofar as he seeks a foundation for physics: he could not have found the foundations of physics had he not sought them by this route (AT I, 144, 9–11). Physics needs nothing but itself except when its foundations are in question; then one must touch on several metaphysical questions within physics (145, 5–6). Sensible experience taken alone remains without foundation (AT I, 97, 21), and physics would find its rationality only by transgressing itself toward metaphysics. Metaphysics accordingly stands in direct equivalence with the principle of physics, both in its achievement — the little metaphysics sent to Mersenne contains all the principles of his physics, the six meditations all its foundations — and in its intention, since he hopes one day to have first demonstrated the principles of physics by metaphysics. The example Marion selects is Descartes' formulation of what will become the principle of inertia, presented as one of two physical principles required to answer Mersenne. The first is negative, refusing real substantial qualities; the second is positive, that whatever is or exists always remains in the state it is in unless some external cause changes it. This second principle in turn demands a strictly non-physical, literally metaphysical foundation: Descartes proves it by metaphysics, on the ground that God, author of all things and wholly perfect and immutable, cannot be supposed to have given any simple existing thing the principle of its own destruction (AT III, 649, 12–25). It is the same God who, in 1630, was author of the essence as of the existence of creatures and introduced metaphysics from within physics. Marion insists on the lucidity of the procedure: Descartes does not found a physical thesis directly on a metaphysical principle but secures on a more fundamental principle what physics *already* treats as a principle. The obvious objection — why demand a principle of the principle, a foundation of the foundation, in a requirement that transgresses indefinitely without arriving — receives a precise answer. The physical principle redoubles metaphysically exactly so as not to dissolve into infinite regress, securing itself in the ultimate instance, the creator of the eternal truths, and borrowing from it a kind of epistemological eternity that lifts it out of the indefinitely regressive causal chain. What has to be secured, Marion adds, is a paradox. Inertia implies that any body, even and especially a moving one, may be considered as remaining of itself always in the same state; physically and empirically it is untenable to treat a motion as a stable state, however theoretically fertile. The second law of nature repeats the paradox by supposing every motion rectilinear, when observed motions are almost never rectilinear or indefinitely prolonged. Founding for physical theory though they are — or rather because they are — such paradoxes cannot be established within the field they make possible, and must be established beyond it, in the transgression that leads to metaphysics: physical principles are discovered, like the mathematical truths and by way of them, to be eternal truths and therefore created. God impresses on creatures, in the creative act, the characters of his own divinity, so that motions are exercised as immutable states of rectilinear transport except where collision intervenes. The upshot is Marion's guard against a standard misreading of this whole procedure. The metaphysical foundation of physical principles entails neither confusion of the two sciences nor dogmatism: the paradoxes of theory, insoluble because principial, are founded not by regression to protocol statements or to a system of axioms but by recourse to a heterogeneous model — here a theological one — which secures their possibility without merging with them. Divine immutability renders intelligible, as a metaphysical model, the physical paradox of a motion counted as a state. Metaphysics is thus characterized in 1630 by the transgression of physics as well.

The Aristotelian topic (pp. 19–21) is where the two determinations become a structure. Succinct as the 1630 evidence is, the concept of metaphysics has received two precise determinations: the metaphysical moment of philosophy transgresses physics by foundation and mathematics by creation. Metaphysics attests its pertinence only by overstepping — and so disqualifying as much as confirming — two other sciences, which are themselves bound together by the relation of codification established in *Sur la théologie blanche*, mechanics serving as the operator by which nature acts according to the exact laws God imposed. This double transgression schematizes the Cartesian relation of the three principal sciences, and in doing so reproduces the topic of the same three sciences in Aristotle. Marion verifies this against the hierarchy of the theoretical sciences in *Metaphysics* E. Physical science considers things in motion, hence affected by matter and so not eternal, and separate, that is, distinct and individualized in act. Mathematical science considers things immobile, since abstracted from their matter, but not separate, since only abstraction distinguishes them from the physical matter they come from. Theological science considers things at once separate and immobile, beings free of all matter not by abstraction but by accomplishment: if there is something eternal, immobile and separate, its knowledge demands a theoretical science which will coincide neither with physics nor with mathematics but is prior to both, *protera amphoin* (1026a11–13). This science considers the immobile, eternal and separate things that serve as causes for what is visible among divine things, so that if the divine subsists anywhere it subsists in such a nature; and it is therefore to be called theological, and given the rank of first science or first philosophy. Marion's long note here does real argumentative work rather than bibliography, and it registers a capital difference beneath the parallel. For Descartes mathematics governs physics, by way of mechanics; for Aristotle, if theological science were per impossibile lacking, physics and not mathematics would be first (1026a29). Descartes therefore substitutes a continuous hierarchy running physics–mathematics–metaphysics for Aristotle's double and parallel transgression of physics and mathematics by a single theology — though in 1647 he returns to the Aristotelian arrangement, making physics the second part of true philosophy after metaphysics. The note also refuses the merely bibliographical reading of *ta meta ta physika*, citing Reiner's case that the title reaches back behind Andronicus and coheres with *prōtē philosophia*; and it adds Marion's own reservation, that the entire debate proceeds within a strictly ontic determination of *physis*, whereas another and ontological interpretation of *physis* would open a radically different resumption of the essence of metaphysics — the reference is to Heidegger's 1929–30 course. The parallel between the two topics, in other words, holds at the level of the ordering of sciences and not at the level of what the ordered sciences are about.

Three precise features confirm the rapprochement, and the first (p. 21) concerns what mathematics means on each side. Aristotle does not subordinate to theological science merely the particular mathematical disciplines, geometry and arithmetic and the rest; he envisages a mathematics common universally to all, *katholou pasōn koinē*, a universal science of quantity and measure which, without coinciding with those disciplines, precedes and makes them possible. Such a meta-mathematical science anticipates the *mathesis universalis* of Rule IV, at least as regards the parameter of measure — order remaining the properly Cartesian addition. In both cases, then, the transgression bears on mathematics taken at their highest dignity, in their meta-mathematical figure as a science of measure in general. What metaphysics oversteps is nothing less than the ultimate accomplishment of mathematics in their totality, and not merely their particular branches.

The second feature (pp. 21–23) concerns theology, and Marion stresses that it needs stressing: Descartes introduces theology no more than Aristotle did, and the intervention of a theological instance is anything but comfortable under his pen. He normally refuses to meddle with theology because he defines it strictly as revealed, not natural — what depends on revelation is what he properly calls theology (AT I, 144, 2–3) — and the *Discours* is blunter still, assigning to theology the teaching of the way to heaven and requiring for success some extraordinary assistance from heaven and that one be more than a man. He therefore mentions theology mostly in order to decline it. The 1630 exception is thus doubly marked: Descartes claims for metaphysics a question Mersenne had counted as theological, and the declaration is unambiguous — the question does not touch what depends on revelation, is rather metaphysical, must be examined by human reason, and all who have received the use of that reason are obliged to employ it principally to know God and themselves; and by this route alone could he have found the foundations of physics (AT I, 143, 25–144, 11). This is the first Cartesian occurrence of *métaphysique*, and it appears in a rare and remarkable synonymy with *théologie*. That the theology in question is natural does not weaken the parallel with Aristotle's theological science but reinforces it: metaphysics transgresses mathematics by creation and physics by foundation precisely so as to discharge an explicitly theological function, since it is as theologically occupied with the greatness of God (AT I, 145, 21–22) that it transcends the other two — just as it was as aroused by a divine *physis* that the science later called *metaphysikē* overstepped the theoretical sciences *mathēmatikē* and *physikē*. The nomenclature of the three sciences is therefore unchanged between Aristotle and Descartes, with Descartes recovering the Aristotelian use of "theology" alongside *métaphysique* despite his own revealed acceptation of the term.

The third feature (pp. 22–23) sets the thesis against the alternatives actually available in 1630, and it is here that the Aristotelian affiliation becomes visible as a choice rather than an inheritance. Kepler, Mersenne and Galileo hold the mathematical truths uncreated, attributing them to the divine understanding; on that arrangement natural theology transgresses physics only by divinizing mathematics in return, since God created the physical world by following a univocal and unique mathematical rationality. Mathematics thereby acquires an immediately theological status and shares with God, through uncreated eternity, transcendence over the physical universe — and the corollary Marion draws is that no metaphysics remains possible or even required, the univocal rationality of a divine mathematics having taken its place. When Descartes establishes, against the reigning opinion, the creation of the mathematical truths, he is certainly asserting his own explicitly Christian apprehension of divine transcendence, refusing the pagan subjection of God, as of a Jupiter or a Saturn, to Styx and the fates. But he is at the same time doing justice to the Aristotelian topic of the theoretical sciences, in which — with less paganism than in many moderns — mathematical science in general is deposed from the divine rank held by theological science alone, to the point of ceding even second place to physics. Holding strictly to the hierarchy of sciences and setting aside the metamorphoses their objects undergo (from *physis* to world, from the mathematics common to all to *mathesis universalis*, from *to theion* to the creator God), the creation of the eternal truths restores and respects the Aristotelian arrangement. In the letter of 15 April 1630 Descartes recovers the definition of *prōtē philosophia* won in *Metaphysics* E: a first philosophy borne jointly by theology, natural or at any rate unrevealed, and by a metaphysics still literally unknown to Aristotle, and established by transgressing mathematics and physics alike.

A second similarity (pp. 23–27) confirms the result: in 1630 Descartes also reproduces the school definition of the notion, by now consecrated even where it is not thought. In Cartesian terms the metaphysical questions and truths become accessible only to a mind capable of conceiving an inconceivable — God as a cause whose power surpasses the bounds of the human understanding (AT I, 150, 18–19). Metaphysics opens only by surpassing the common conditions of knowledge, and does so because the formal object of this new knowledge oversteps the reach of common knowledge. Metaphysics "surpasses" the other sciences for Descartes as, for Aristotle, first philosophy ranks higher than the other theoretical sciences — Marion's "s'élève" translating *hairetōterai*, more choiceworthy, at 1026a22–23. In this Descartes takes over the etymology of *métaphysique* as transgression of the physics after which it would come, an etymology as questionable as it is widespread. Fonseca supplies the commonplace: the science treated in these books is usually called by the moderns simply Metaphysics, as the science of post- or supranatural things, whereas Aristotle often calls it first philosophy. Suarez marks the definition more sharply as a transgression that surpasses physics — the name came less from Aristotle than from his interpreters, taken from the title *tōn meta ta physika*, of things that follow the natural sciences and things, whence metaphysics as instituted after physics or beyond physics. The school manuals Descartes did not hesitate to consult use the etymology of convenience to justify how *prōtē philosophia* could, on no Aristotelian authority whatever, dissolve into a *metaphysica* that englobes or replaces it. Eustache de Saint-Paul gives it as the fourth sense of philosophy, because metaphysics follows physics in the order of teaching, or rather comes after it; Abra de Raconis as the fifth and last, a post-natural science or discipline concerning the things that follow natural things; Scipion Dupleix makes it the first and most common acceptation, on two grounds, that the science concerns supernatural things such as God or the angels and that the Philosopher so entitled his books. Metaphysics defines and constitutes itself by the very act of transgressing every other science. That is what Descartes had done in 1630 by transgressing both mathematics and physics; but in the context of the demands of method and code the word still functioned only as a qualifier, not yet as the name of an accomplished science. By 1647, drafting the Preface to the French *Principia*, he has won complete metaphysical positions by his own routes and can therefore resume the topic and lexicon of school philosophy pedagogically, without risk of confusion. Metaphysics now appears in its own right and is defined clearly by its transgression of physics, the first part of true philosophy followed by physics as the second — and the Aristotelian hierarchy even seems formally preserved, since what is transgressed is physics and not, as in 1630, mathematics directly. The project of writing an entire course of philosophy likewise presupposed the four parts that scholastically organize it, logic or dialectic, morals, physics and metaphysics, as in Abra de Raconis and Eustache; and Descartes takes up that organization in part, drawing on it more than on Porphyry for the tree, whose branches reduce to medicine, mechanics and morals. Three of the usual sections of a complete course are recognizable in the tree — metaphysics, physics, morals — and what is missing is logic or dialectic, refused since the *Regulae* in a constant rejection of the formalism issuing from the categories of being. The absence of mathematics raises no difficulty, since only the parts of true philosophy are at issue and these by right do not include mathematics; Descartes reproduces the school division of the courses without exceeding it, a division itself Stoic in origin. The appearance of mathematics in the 1630 topic is explained instead by the strict confrontation with an Aristotelian topic. The real difficulty, Marion notes, would be to determine why and how Descartes can use two disparate classifications, harmonize them and exploit the gap between them — a question left standing here. In 1647 mathematics remains implicit and presupposed, in the physics whose codification it secures and in the mechanics where it passes into effectivity, and the mention of medicine transcribes into Cartesian terms the subset common to morals and mechanics in dependence on physics. The 1647 divisions may therefore be treated as an adjustment of the school quadripartition, and so as one more confirmation of the common definition of metaphysics.

The two similarities together (pp. 27–29) delimit a Cartesian acceptation of the word, and Marion now extracts from them the essential character the common doctrine attaches to metaphysics: it abstracts its formal object from all matter. Confirmations are plentiful, above all among the Jesuits. Pererius isolates the most accomplished of the abstractions, that of the thing from all matter both really and rationally, the abstraction discerned in God and in the intelligences — which oversteps in particular simple mathematical abstraction, proceeding only rationally and not really. Suarez states unequivocally how abstraction effects the metaphysical transgression: this science abstracts from sensible or material things, which are called physical because natural philosophy is occupied with them, and so contemplates divine things separated from matter along with the common notions of being that can exist apart from matter; whence it was called metaphysics, as instituted after or beyond physics. Abra de Raconis carries the Suarezian thesis to its term by making being abstracted both from God and from creatures the adequate object of metaphysics. The convergence weighs the more heavily because Descartes' preferred schoolman takes it up: Eustache concludes a Suarezian development on the object of metaphysics with the definition of a theoretical science of real, per se and complete being, abstracted from all matter at least according to indifference. Metaphysical abstraction leads so necessarily to the consideration of *ens* as such, Marion observes, that the neologism "ontology" — still written in Greek — appeared for the first time in the history of philosophy in Goclenius' 1613 definition of abstraction: separation and abstraction from matter occurs sometimes from singular, sometimes from universal matter, and even according to reason alone, as from a simple and a mixed body; of this last kind is mathematical abstraction and hence the *ontologikē*, that part of philosophy which treats of being and the transcendentals. Abstraction here transgresses matter, but it also transgresses particular essence, reaching to *on hē on*, the being in its beingness. By which the whole of metaphysics depends on the power of abstraction — a formulation to hold onto, since it is what will make the *abductio mentis a sensibus* structurally rather than psychologically significant.

That common thesis (pp. 29–33) then illuminates four series of Cartesian texts which would otherwise stay obscure or trivial. First, metaphysics begins for Descartes too when matter disappears and the immaterial and metaphysical things appear, since it always opens through the idea of God, the idea of the soul and the ideas of insensible things. Second — and here Descartes both confirms and exceeds the common doctrine — metaphysical abstraction requires not merely that the object be void of matter but above all that the mind first abstract *itself* from sensible considerations. The repeated injunction to an *abductio mentis a sensibus* redoubles for the mind what the concept of metaphysics already implies: many had said before him that the mind must be drawn away from the senses in order to understand metaphysical things, but no one had shown by what procedure this might be done (AT VII, 131, 6–10). Descartes takes himself to have accomplished the radical abstraction effectively, because in the act of the *cogito* the *mens* perceives itself for the first time before and without the body; and more, to have accomplished it already in the doubt, which is called metaphysical for exactly that reason — the utility of so great a doubt lies in freeing us from all prejudices and laying the easiest path for drawing the mind away from the senses (AT VII, 12, 1–8). The note refuses the psychological reading squarely: the formula does not name a propaedeutic to meditation in general, such as setting aside worldly affairs or suspending the passions, but the very act by which the formal object of metaphysics is released. Marion's consequence is the important one. The *abductio* marks something like the birth certificate of the *mens*, but what the *mens* thereby transgresses is above all physics, as sensation and sensible, and mathematics, as imagination — that is, the sensible in both its states, universal and particular. Doubt does not merely make the ego possible as a cogitating being; it first makes possible the opening of the entire horizon of metaphysics, since through it the ego thinks insensible beings in general purely. The conquest of the *ego cogito* should not conceal that through it the doubt directed at the sensible is what permits the transgression of physics, as the transgression of mathematics in return imposes doubt about the imaginable. Doubt alone reaches the insensible as such, whatever it may be, and so inaugurates metaphysics in its excellence. Third, the reserves with which Descartes seems to qualify his own project must be read with care. The first meditations of 1637 are so metaphysical and so uncommon that they may not suit everyone; but "uncommon" indicates no extravagance, only metaphysical abstraction and the passage to the limit it imposes on the mind. The metaphysical certainty of 1637 is balanced in 1641 by a slight and, so to speak, metaphysical reason for doubting; but by definition every metaphysical reason must remain slight, since it is defined by abstraction. And if the hyperbolic doubts are afterwards to be exploded as laughable, it is not that they are laughable *as* metaphysical but that their hyperbole finally proves false; the uncommon tenuity was never laughable, since by strict consequence of metaphysical abstraction, doubt — and the certainty that alone lifts it — can only be stated as an extreme limit, beyond all sensible and physical perception as beyond all mathematical imagination. To exercise metaphysics and to be exercised as metaphysics, doubt must abstract so far that it can only be slight and hyperbolic; hyperbole does not disqualify it but qualifies it as metaphysical. Metaphysical transgression therefore marks the itinerary and the conclusions of the *Meditationes* definitively, even and especially where metaphysical certainty ends by filling in the hyperbolic doubt. Fourth, Descartes always admitted and even proclaimed that the transgression defining metaphysics remains in principle barely accessible to the thoughts it transgresses, and first of all to mathematical thought. From the letter of 15 April 1630 metaphysics conjugates certainty with incomprehensibility for the other sciences: the demonstrations are more evident than geometry's, but he does not know whether he can persuade anyone. Elsewhere he grants that almost no one understands metaphysics and that few are capable of understanding it — insinuating no reserve about the validity of the enterprise, only noting that its definition, by abstraction from all matter and independence of the faculties that apprehend matter, bars access to most minds, and indeed to most speculative minds, since mathematicians can study extension only by apprehending it through imagination. Metaphysics remains incomprehensible to mathematicians not through any defect of rigour or formalization but through excess of abstraction. The transition from the inferior sciences occurs only by discontinuity, since passing from one science to another means passing from one dominant faculty to another that contradicts the first's conditions of exercise: metaphysical thoughts exercise the pure understanding and make the notion of the soul familiar, while the study of mathematics exercises the imagination on figures and motions and accustoms us to form distinct notions of body. Either the mind, as pure understanding, thinks by perfect abstraction and reaches the insensible and so metaphysical things; or it binds itself to imagination in order to handle extension and reaches bodies alone. The exclusive alternative has an unanswerable consequence, that those versed in metaphysics recoil from geometry and the geometers do not grasp what he wrote on first philosophy — a consequence Descartes was willing to illustrate by naming Fermat, whom he judged to know mathematics and to reason badly in philosophy. Abstraction, precisely because it accomplishes metaphysics and effectively exhibits it, renders metaphysics inaccessible and literally incomprehensible to mathematical imagination. Constituting itself by transgression of the sensible, metaphysics establishes itself only by withdrawing from what it surpasses, and so confirms itself in its very incomprehensibility.

The section closes (p. 33) by consolidating the result and handing over the real question. Rare and scattered as they are, the Cartesian occurrences of "metaphysics" recover a genuine coherence once referred to the Aristotelian and school determinations his contemporaries accepted. From 1630 Descartes takes up Aristotle's ambition in *Metaphysics* E of constituting a science that oversteps physics and mathematics; he takes up abstraction as the privileged act by which the two second sciences are transgressed; and he enlarges its power through the *abductio mentis a sensibus*. On that secured ground the decisive question can at last be put: since "metaphysics" does occur in the Cartesian corpus, how is the *concept* of metaphysics properly understood there? The shift from lexicon to concept is what § 3 will take up, and Marion has spent two sections earning the right to ask it.

### § 3. Two Decisions for a First Philosophy

The scattered occurrences do form a system, or at least organize themselves with cohesion in their relation to contemporary philosophical usage and lexicon (p. 34). What remains is to measure, by means of these similarities, the dissimilarities the properly Cartesian decisions provoke — which comes to asking after the identity of the metaphysics instituted in 1641 by the *Meditationes*.

The displacement is accomplished most indisputably at the level of the title (pp. 34–36), since Descartes expressly denies the *Meditationes* the qualification *metaphysicae*. Their originality lies in their not being *metaphysicae*, and the point needs stressing because the French translation obscures it: in speaking of *Méditations métaphysiques* it contradicts the warnings Descartes repeated to Mersenne during the final revision of the manuscript. He does often call the work "ma Métaphysique," and Marion's note disposes of the objection that might be built on this. The phrase recurs constantly in the correspondence, but it is not an alternative title — as Alquié's systematic italics suggest, against AT's practice — but a contraction of fuller designations: the writing on metaphysics, his meditations of metaphysics, his reveries of metaphysics. The parallel with "ma physique et ma métaphysique" settles it, since "physics" never named even approximately a Cartesian work: both are conventional designations by subject matter. Twice, then, Descartes states the reason that forbids calling the *Meditationes metaphysicae*. He has affixed no title, and the fittest would be *Renati Descartes Meditationes de prima Philosophia*, because he treats not of God and the soul in particular but in general of all the first things knowable in philosophizing; and the same day, sending the writing untitled so as to make Mersenne its godfather and leave him the power of baptizing it, he repeats the formula with "par ordre" added (AT III, 235, 10–18 and 239, 2–7). Both Latin editions preserve *de prima Philosophia* unchanged, while *animae immortalitas* yields to *animae humanae a corpore distinctio*; even the French translation, illegitimately adding "métaphysiques," retains an echo in "touchant la première philosophie." The note presses the philology further, and profitably: the adjective matters so little that in those same editions it drops out where the title is repeated just before the first meditation, which is the first appearance of an exact rendering of the Latin. Marion regrets that the Beyssade edition neither notes the correction nor compares the titles, and adds a forward glance worth registering — Husserl, retracing Descartes' path, will himself arrive at metaphysics as first philosophy. The decision is therefore clear and constant, told against *métaphysique* and in favour of *philosophie première* in the strict sense of *philosophia prōtē*, following Aristotle's nomenclature. Two questions follow from the lexical choice, and without answers the decision would remain insignificant however sharp it is: what domain does each of the two opposed terms open to investigation, and what privilege allows first philosophy to prevail over metaphysics (§ 3); and what displacements do the Cartesian options impose on the topic of metaphysics, taken both in its historical state and in its historial evolution (§ 4).

The declared intention looks clear enough at first (pp. 36–38): Descartes prefers *prima Philosophia* to *metaphysicae* because he treats not only of God and the soul but in general of all the first things. The formulation surprises in itself, and more so because the developed title seems to contradict it at once, mentioning explicitly the existence of God and the real distinction of soul and body. Marion's note declines to make much of the well-worn variants — the shift from *in qua* to *in quibus* alters nothing in the choice of *prima philosophia* over *metaphysica* — while showing that the substantive change from immortality to distinction from the body, though printed only in 1642, was already made in 1640: Descartes tells Mersenne he could not demonstrate that God cannot annihilate the soul, only that it is of a nature entirely distinct from the body's and so not naturally subject to dying with it, which is all that establishing religion requires. The difficulty is real nonetheless, since the two points touching God and the soul are what characterize metaphysics in its strict acceptation, and Descartes himself describes his writing on metaphysics in exactly those terms — the route by which the nature of the human soul is known and God's existence demonstrated, the principles of metaphysics that give us knowledge of God and of our soul. In this optic metaphysics can even partially coincide with theology, since it covers two of theology's own regions, God and the soul as immortal or at least immaterial: he had always thought the two questions of God and of the soul to be chief among those to be demonstrated by philosophy rather than by theology (AT VII, 1, 7–9). Two theological questions turn out to be directly metaphysical, and metaphysics becomes equivalent to rational theology — an equivalence Descartes occasionally concedes outright in the phrase *metaphysica sive theologia* (AT III, 505, 10–11). Surprising as it is, that equivalence is what makes the opposition intelligible. Against a metaphysics thus narrowly confined to rational theology stands a "first philosophy" that passes beyond it toward all the first things in general. Put in Aristotelian terms, *prōtē philosophia* here concerns *every* really separate and immaterial nature, and so considers not only the idea of God and the idea of the soul but the ideas of insensible things in general — admitting insensible natures other than God and the soul. Since God and the soul exhaust the field of metaphysics for Descartes, enlarging that field immediately requires a new discipline: first philosophy alone bears on *all* first things, whatever they are and whatever their primacy. The Preface to the reader marks the transition sharply, where Descartes takes up again the same questions of God and the human mind and *at the same time* treats the beginnings of the whole of first philosophy. An equivalence survives, licensing "la première Philosophie ou bien la Métaphysique" almost indifferently, and two regions do remain common to the two disciplines. But first philosophy oversteps metaphysics by extending to *every* primacy, and is universal precisely in being first: it suffices that things attest themselves as first for first philosophy to confirm its universality in them. Marion draws the consequence that governs the rest of the chapter. Such an extension demands a determination of primacy strict and powerful enough to operate in a field far more general than the one in which metaphysics confines itself by simple abstraction; first philosophy must exercise more primacy than metaphysics exercises abstraction. A first decision about the essence of metaphysics has therefore been taken: what is more essential to the question of metaphysics than the metaphysical discipline itself is first philosophy, which covers the domain of the latter while overstepping it. Before the historial weight of that decision can be measured against earlier and contemporary definitions, one further question must be put — how must primacy be understood if it is to extend "in general" and overflow God and the soul? Under what relation can primacy reach the universal instead of withdrawing into a particular region par excellence? By what parameter, in short, does primacy manage to transgress the very transgression that metaphysics accomplishes and that defines it?

That conflict of transgressions leads to the second decision (pp. 39–41), which is accomplished in the same texts, whose final clause Marion had held back and now takes up: not in particular of God and the soul but in general of all the first things knowable in philosophizing, and in the second version, in philosophizing *by order*. Primacy — which alone secures first philosophy its priority over metaphysics — belongs to the order of knowledge as that order determines the Cartesian manner of philosophizing. On the order of knowledge, knowing has absolute priority, so the first being that knowing will know is the one that exercises it, the ego, which therefore necessarily precedes the knowledge of God's existence: the cognition *ego cogito, ergo sum* is the first and most certain to occur to anyone philosophizing in order (*Principia* I, § 7), which confirms in advance the privilege the 1647 Preface grants the same ego in taking the being or existence of this thought as the first principle from which the rest, that there is a God, are very clearly deduced. Marion then blocks the misreading the passage invites. Primacy does not qualify the ego as such — not that Descartes puts its primacy over God in question, since he affirms it explicitly — but the *knowledge* of the ego; what precedes the knowledge of God is not the ego directly and by itself. Primacy therefore does not come to the ego from its own resources but only from thought, which the ego does exercise par excellence. So primacy is not wrested from God by the ego, nor from God and the soul by some third being: it relativizes all beings by attaching itself to the one thought that thinks them all. More exactly, it qualifies all thoughts whatever, provided they follow the order of thought at work — conducting one's thoughts in order (DM, 18, 27). The new "first things" show themselves only within the order established for bringing them to evidence by the series of things to be known, and one thing precedes another, on the new definition of primacy, when and only when it makes knowledge of the other possible. The classification of the sciences must accordingly break with every hierarchy of beings, since the method requires that all things be disposable in series, not insofar as they are referred to some genus of being, as the philosophers divided them into their categories, but insofar as some can be known from others, so that whenever a difficulty arises we can see at once whether it helps to survey certain others first, and which, and in what order (AT X, 381, 9–16). The classification of the sciences must be regulated by the order in which knowledges engender one another. Applied to the relation of philosophy to the other sciences, this implies a revolution. Philosophy's primacy can no longer be exercised according to any order of subject matters, nor rest on a hierarchy of beings culminating in a theology and respecting the parameter of the being in its being — that is, on an ontology — since the categories and genera of being have been rendered obsolete by the method. If philosophy is to restore its primacy it can do so only by accomplishing it according to the order of thoughts, establishing itself as the first thought, or first series of thoughts, from which the others can be engendered, with no regard to the comparative dignity of the beings involved in each case. Philosophy will therefore no longer borrow its primacy from certain regions of being supposed intrinsically first — God, the soul, the separate intelligences, the domain of rational theology — nor from the being considered as such, since being no longer enters into play and is disqualified the moment the *ordo cognoscendi* comes on stage. It will re-establish its primacy over the other sciences by producing that primacy itself according to the order, instituting itself as the unique instance of all the first thoughts generative of other thoughts, whatever those thoughts and whatever the corresponding beings. Marion's own *Ontologie grise* is cited for the ontological status of the order of knowledge, which is where the claim was first secured. The radicality of this mutation in the essence of philosophy — not merely in its place within a classification of the sciences — makes the upheaval and the occasional imprecision of Descartes' titulature more explicable, since all the earlier denominations fall obsolete once the order disposed by the method appears. A first decision refused metaphysics its primacy, as title and as science; first philosophy received the primacy in its stead, without that Aristotelian syntagm's being able, in a context inaugurated by the *Regulae*, to retain the least legitimacy or rigour. How can one still speak of first philosophy once no reference can or may be made to a first being? A second decision must therefore undo the syntagm from its old acceptation in order to forge, under the same title, a new concept.

The 1647 Preface supplies that new concept (pp. 41–43), defining true philosophy whose first part is metaphysics, containing the principles of knowledge, among which is the explanation of God's principal attributes, of the immateriality of our souls, and of all the clear and simple notions in us. Three remarks follow. First, "metaphysics" here signals no reversal that would reopen the decision for first philosophy; the two terms are rather indifferently disqualified, or overwritten, by the new primacy — whence the equivalence Descartes permits himself between what may be called first philosophy or else metaphysics. Taken in themselves the two terms turn out indifferent, even interchangeable, in the light of a wholly other concept of primacy. Second, the fundamental determination of primacy was in fact given in the immediately preceding sequence, where metaphysics is the part that *contains the principles of knowledge*, and where the book's first part likewise contains the principles of knowledge. Primacy accrues to philosophy neither from the old title *prōtē philosophia* nor from the corresponding ontic region, but from its original function as the science of the principles, which the other sciences borrow from it. All the others draw their principles from this one primacy — physics, mechanics, medicine and morals, but also, and this is the load-bearing point, metaphysics in the narrow and old sense. For there are also foundations *of* metaphysics; and although metaphysics constitutes the roots, it coincides neither with the whole philosophical tree nor, above all, with the originary primacy. Third, the principles, precisely as principles *of knowledge*, are defined first by an epistemic overdetermination of the beings commonly called metaphysical: neither God nor the soul deserves, *as a being*, the title of principle of knowledge; only their "explanation" does. Next, the principles of knowledge are defined as the first causes and the true principles, as the cause or the principle: primacy defines the principle solely by the fact that the principle inaugurates knowledge through the cause, which alone is certain knowledge — properly to philosophize is to begin by seeking these first causes, that is, the principles. Whence, finally, the principles can be defined beyond God and the soul, now reduced to their explanation, as all the clear and simple notions in us. Anything counts as a first principle, and so secures philosophy its function as first philosophy, if it is an evidence irreducible to any more originary evidence, with no consideration whatever of the ontic status of its eventual object, provided only that it inaugurate a *series rerum quaerendarum* (AT X, 392, 10) — that it make further bringings-to-evidence possible. Between what is called Descartes' method and what is called his metaphysics the relations are accordingly complex enough to forbid both confusing them and separating them absolutely. Only evident knowledge attains the dignity of first philosophy; but in assuming that dignity, evident knowledge itself enters into contention with *metaphysica* as previously defined.

### § 4. Primacy and Universality — Order and Being

Descartes thinks metaphysics as first philosophy because he modifies the essence of primacy from top to bottom, and primacy in its new sense is what defines first philosophy (p. 43). The double decision would remain empty if its conceptual reach were not measured, and it can be measured only by rehearsing the displacements the Cartesian decisions impose on the earlier definitions of metaphysics and of first philosophy alike.

Marion begins from Suarez (pp. 43–45), an authority recognized in his own time and known to Descartes. Philosophy for Suarez bears at once on the noblest beings and on the most universal notions of being, since the same and single science that treats these special objects considers at the same time all the predicates common to them and to other things, and this defines the whole of metaphysical doctrine. The note collects the apparatus that matters — Suarez's own distinction of two parts of the doctrine, one on being as being with its principles and properties, the other on certain particular kinds of being and especially the immaterial; Zimmermann on the medieval prehistory of the distinction; Courtine and Siegfried on the Suarezian project; and Heidegger's § 14, which reads Suarez's concept of metaphysics as fixing the fundamental character of modern metaphysics. Whatever the variations, the two domains of the single philosophy recover in Suarez the duality Aristotle inaugurated without resolving: on one side a *prōtē philosophia* as doctrine of the most divine beings, on the other a certain science considering *to on hē on*. Aristotle unites them under no common title; Suarez unites them by the neologism *metaphysica* — and Marion's judgement on this is the section's hinge. The appearance of the term, far from showing that Suarez thought the unity of philosophy more radically than Aristotle had, marks the contrary: a purely verbal bond masks, in claiming to suppress it, the internal tension between the two dimensions of the question about the being of beings. The composition nonetheless deserves close attention, since Suarez's decision is determining for all modern philosophy by way of Descartes. Metaphysics is purely and simply one science of specific unity, and Aristotle is read as attributing to it, as to one and the same science, names and attributes that belong to it partly insofar as it deals with God and the intelligences — whence theology, divine science and first philosophy — and partly insofar as it deals with being as being and its first attributes and principles, by reason of which it is called universal science and metaphysics; it is called wisdom insofar as it embraces all of this and contemplates the first principles and first causes of things. Two features connect the schema to Descartes' choices. It terminates in *sapientia* as the science of causes and principles, just as Descartes opened his own schema with wisdom, philosophy being the study of wisdom and philosophizing properly so called beginning with the search for these first causes, that is, the principles; the fifth degree toward wisdom is precisely to seek the first causes and true principles. The intention of reaching wisdom, philosophy's ultimate accomplishment, through knowledge of the principles is common to both. And for both the task is to distribute correctly the various titles of philosophy, *metaphysica* and *philosophia prima* above all. It is therefore here that the Cartesian innovation will show itself most clearly.

The innovation announces itself as a straightforward inversion (pp. 45–50). Descartes' preference for *philosophia prima* over *metaphysica* rests on the decision to treat not in particular of God and the soul but in general of all the first things, so that *prima philosophia* covers a more general and less particular domain than metaphysics. In Suarez the situation is exactly reversed: *prima philosophia* concerns only God and the intelligences, *circa Deum et intelligentias*, and far from surpassing them toward a wider horizon. On Suarez's topic the two objects Descartes names in the developed title of the *Meditationes* would define *prima philosophia* in its most particular acceptation — precisely contradicting what Descartes tells Mersenne. Consequently, where for Descartes it is metaphysics that can occasionally merge with rational theology, for Suarez it is *prima philosophia* that reduces to theology; and where for Descartes only *prima philosophia* studies in general, Suarez grants universality to metaphysics alone. Descartes takes over the Suarezian pair, then, only to invert its characteristics: the first term becomes universal and non-theological, the second particular and theological. The inversion is the more striking because it also runs against the common usage of Descartes' contemporaries, for whom the competing titles are distinguished by the more or less exclusive consideration of God and the separate intelligences. Abra de Raconis, running through the names of metaphysics, gives *Philosophia simpliciter*, then first philosophy as speaking of the noblest things, God and the angels, then *theologia rationalis*, and only last *Metaphysica*. Scipion Dupleix, in a similar review, having registered the recent introduction of the term "metaphysics," names second wisdom, third theology or science of God, and fifth first philosophy or first science, on the ground that it considers the first being among beings. Eustache de Saint-Paul holds the same position, objecting against the narrow definition of metaphysics that its partisans assign it too small an object, since far more probable is the common proposition that the adequate and per se object of metaphysics is real, per se, complete being, common to God and to creatures. Is the Cartesian decision then absolutely without precedent? Marion sets the condition any precedent would have to meet — an authority recognized by the contemporaries and possibly known or at least accessible to Descartes — and finds it in Pererius, the Spanish Jesuit who taught at the Collegium Romanum until his death in 1610 and published in 1576 the *De communibus omnium rerum naturalium principiis et affectionibus*, itself a reworking of a commentary on the *Physics* of 1562 and often reprinted in France and Germany as well as Italy into the next century. Descartes' dependence on Pererius is documented at one other point, since he borrows the syntagm *mathesis universalis* from him in the *Regulae*, and the note adds that Pererius' influence has been traced, notably on Goclenius, the "inventor" of *ontologia*. He could as easily have known other theses, and one of them might well have struck him: against and before the Suarezian division, Pererius — Jesuit against Jesuit — accorded first philosophy a status that anticipates Descartes. He sometimes runs the competing titles together, as if to underline the difficulty, granting the common view that metaphysics excels the other disciplines in dignity because of the supreme nobility of the things it treats, dealing as it does with God and the intelligences, and so called first philosophy, metaphysics, wisdom, theology or science of God, either because God alone properly possesses such a science or because it alone contains the science of divine things. But when he turns to determining parts, and so formal objects, within philosophy, he arrives at a clean tripartition that contradicts Suarez's bipartition in advance. The first part is the principal one and the end of the others, whence the science is called metaphysics, theology and the noblest of all, and it treats things separated from matter both really and rationally, such as the intelligences and God. The second is where the transcendentals are set out — being, one, true, good, act and potency — whence this part is called most universal and holds power and right over all the other sciences. The third comprises the ten categories. Metaphysics divides, then, categories aside, into a metaphysics restricted, as for Descartes, to theology, and a *pars universalissima* made up of the transcendentals. The identity of that universality, still anonymous, is soon specified: there must be two distinct sciences, one treating the transcendentals and the most universal things, the other treating only the intelligences; the first is called *prima philosophia* and *scientia universalis*, the second properly metaphysics, theology, wisdom, divine science. First philosophy thus exchanges its characteristics with metaphysics. The region of the divine passes from first philosophy to metaphysics, which implies that metaphysics, now *de intelligentiis*, reduces to theology, and that *prima philosophia* detaches itself from the *prōtē philosophia* bound to the divine alone in order to exercise a radically new kind of primacy — one that in fact amounts to universality. Pererius therefore inverts Suarez's topic some thirty years in advance and anticipates the one Descartes adopts in 1640: *prima philosophia* is *scientia universalis*, even *universalissima*, because in fact and by right it does not treat in particular of God and the soul but in general of all the first things. Against the dominant Suarezian terminology, Descartes reproduces the original and paradoxical interpretation of *prima philosophia* as universal rather than as restricted to the divine region of being. The filiation cannot be confirmed by an avowed reading or influence, but thematically it cannot be denied in the texts themselves. And the historical identification, while securing the historical interpretation, at once raises a fresh conceptual difficulty: how can Descartes conjoin primacy with universality? The question bears on the definition at stake in the *Meditationes*, but it arose already in the *Regulae*, which by 1627–28 had reached a *universalissima sapientia* and a *mathesis universalis*, the latter under Pererius' influence. Would the conjunction of universality and primacy in 1640 involve the same universal science as in 1627–28 — will the Cartesian *prima philosophia*, exercised in general in conformity with a *scientia universalis* that becomes Pererius' first philosophy, go so far as to absorb the *mathesis universalis* of the *Regulae*?

Before the difficulty can be taken up, a connected question must be put (pp. 50–53): how does Pererius himself explain that primacy can be identified with universality? Against his answer the antagonistic answers of Suarez and Descartes become legible. Pererius' three parts of philosophy exercise very precise functions. The third puts the categories to work and belongs rather to the dialectical or logical part of the quadripartite school expositions, and so falls under the Cartesian critique of both disciplines; in any case, already for Aristotle the categories, even as *katēgoriai tou ontos*, do not belong to what will become metaphysics. The first part, qualified here as metaphysics, theology, wisdom and divine science, is confined to the intelligences separated from all matter in reason or in reality, and is therefore exactly the *metaphysica sive theologia* Descartes mentions at least once, as limited to demonstrating God's existence and the real distinction of soul and body. There remains the second part, the *universalissima*, *prima philosophia et scientia universalis* — and the decisive question is whether it already concerns what Descartes will attribute properly to first philosophy, all the first things in general knowable in philosophizing by order. Marion's answer is flat: absolutely not and in no way. That part concerns the transcendental determinations of being, and so, besides being itself, the true, the one and the good, which convert with it, together with the act–potency pair; the universality of the science follows from the universality of the determinations of being, which precede the particular rationalities as their principles. First philosophy for Pererius evidently treats of *ens in quantum ens*, and so anticipates what, after Goclenius and with Clauberg, will take the name *ontologia* and claim to answer Aristotle's science of *on hē on*. From the standpoint of being as such, the particular genera and the sciences they arouse are second and derived, partial and particular; all the other sciences, theological philosophy included, therefore yield to being as such, universal and consequently first — and to it alone. Pererius does announce Descartes in refusing to confine first philosophy to the divine genus, holding that this universal science should not treat of the intelligences per se and as a species of being, but only perhaps in relation to its subject, insofar as they are the general principles and universal causes of all beings. But when the same distinction recurs in Descartes' terms, the reason may be quite different, since metaphysics can yield in universality to *prima philosophia* on two opposed grounds: by consideration of being as such, which is Pererius' ground, or by consideration of the order of knowledge, better, of knowledge as the disposition and principle of the order, which is Descartes' choice. The universality of first philosophy thus attests either the primacy of the ontological instance or the primacy of method as the disposition of the order. In the first case first philosophy takes, against Suarez, a further step toward classical ontology; in the second it finishes disqualifying the interrogation of *on hē on*, and deserves only a grey ontology. Between Pererius and Descartes the agreement concerns the titulature of philosophy and nothing of its stakes or its conceptual definition. Both subordinate theology, paradoxically identified with metaphysics, to a *prima philosophia* that alone holds universality; but for one it is the universality of a question that interrogates every being as being, and for the other the *mathesis universalis*, which interrogates every being as knowable according to order and measure. Descartes took up Pererius' highly original titulature precisely in order to eliminate what Pererius meant to consecrate in it, the question of being as such. Terminological agreement covers a radical conceptual disagreement.

The converse must be stressed as well (pp. 53–58): Pererius' *scientia universalis*, though its title *prima philosophia* contradicts Suarez's terminology, covers Suarez's concept perfectly. Between Suarez and his elder in the Society, disagreement over the name does not prevent complete agreement on the thing. Pererius distinguishes as first part a metaphysics or theology treating only of God and the intelligences; Suarez likewise determines a theology or divine science bound to the same domain; and Descartes on this point endorses his predecessors by admitting a *metaphysica sive theologia* — as do all his contemporaries, Fonseca, Dupleix and Goclenius among them.
