It is over the second part of philosophy that the situation changes completely. Descartes, and he alone, defines *scientia universalis* by the criterion of the order of knowledge; every other author tends toward a single definition drawn from *ens in quantum ens*. The conceptual agreement between Pererius and Suarez impresses the more because their nomenclatures diverge, the one assigning the universal science the transcendentals — being, the one, the true, act and potency — the other being as being with its first attributes and principles, by virtue of which it is called universal science and metaphysics. But Suarez is not simply repeating an Aristotelian thesis, and Marion shows in three steps how determinate his sense of *ens in quantum ens* is. First, the thesis about the object: being as real being is the adequate object of this science, since the adequate object must comprehend God and the other immaterial substances but not those alone, and must comprehend not only substances but real accidents, excluding beings of reason and the merely per accidens — and no object other than being as such answers to this. Metaphysics therefore considers the genera and degrees of being indifferently, because it begins by abstracting from the gaps between separate and non-separate substances and between substances and real accidents; whence the paradox of an object that extends indifferently, if not univocally, from God to the real accident, without hierarchy or variation. Second, the definition announces its own condition of possibility, the objective concept of being: to constitute an adequate object comprehending God under it, nothing or no notion of being need be by nature prior to God — it suffices that it be prior according to the abstraction or consideration of the understanding, which involves no contradiction. And the objective concept is defined as the object itself as known and apprehended through the formal concept, or as what is immediately and adequately set over against that formal concept. The objective concept of being therefore designates being made abstract from every determination other than being conceived, common to whatever can be represented and indifferent to every difference except the possibility of being represented or not. Metaphysics' universality comes to it less from being than from the objectivation of that being, which itself follows from the primacy accorded without discussion to the representation of the being over the being itself — the formulation to hold onto, since it is what will let Marion align Suarez with the modern figure of metaphysics rather than with Aristotle. Third, and consequently, God becomes an object subject to metaphysics, among other beings reduced from the outset to what the formal concept apprehends of them: being as being comprehends God, who is without principle and without cause, and yet being as being is the most imperfect object because the most common, included even in the lowest beings, and would be far more perfect as substance, or spiritual substance, or God. God accordingly holds no more than a particular, if eminent, role within the universality of a science that embraces everything real, in the measure in which confusion and abstraction open universality. Suarez, like Descartes, enlarges the reach of philosophy beyond immaterial substances; but, like Pererius, the enlargement carries him to being itself interpreted as real, and in no way to Descartes' epistemic protology — not to all the first things in general knowable in philosophizing by order, but to what will shortly take the name *ontologia*. Nor is Descartes opposed here only to Pererius and Suarez, but to everyone who announces or takes up the Suarezian definition. Among those who announce it is Fonseca, who in defining metaphysics as a *generalis scientia* refutes four identifications of its object — God alone with Al-Farabi, separate substance with Duns Scotus, substance in general with Buridan, and predicamental being — before passing to the wider definition: being common to God and creatures must be established as the subject of this science, so that the subject of metaphysics is neither God alone nor separate substance nor substance in general, but being common to substances and accidents. Among those who take the definition over from Suarez are most of the *minores*, with serene unanimity. Scipion Dupleix gives universal science as the eighth of metaphysics' various names, both because it treats universally of all being and because its principles serve universally for all the sciences, confirming the choice by specifying that metaphysics as science of being as being excludes only being of reason, in the sense in which Suarez excluded non-real accidents, and the domain of revealed theology. More significant still are the two authors Descartes had thought of annotating. Abra de Raconis runs through several theses on metaphysics' object — God alone is not the adequate object though he is the principal one; the angels belong to the object but do not with God make it adequate; beings of reason and beings per accidens fall outside — so that a conclusion imposes itself: being abstracted from God and from creatures is to be established as metaphysics' adequate object. Metaphysics oversteps the separate substances, where first philosophy confines itself, only in favour of a being so abstract that it excludes nothing but the unreal. Eustache confirms the supremacy of the Suarezian definition in similar terms, beginning by granting the limited pertinence of the rival theses: metaphysics does bear on God if one considers its principal object, on first and immaterial substance if on its object taken purely, on substance if on the parameter under which it operates in general, on categorial being if on its means of demonstration, and on being taken in the widest sense if on the parameter that englobes without exception everything metaphysics treats. But each thesis owes its partial pertinence to a more essential definition, since none of these can be called the per se and adequate object except the one fixed — whence metaphysics is the theoretical science of real and complete being per se, abstracted from all matter, though by indifference. Goclenius, the note adds, reaches the same result more briefly. Descartes' whole environment, in short, develops the Suarezian definition at length.

The section closes by isolating what is Cartesian in all this (pp. 58–59). From Pererius to Fonseca, from Abra de Raconis to Eustache, from Suarez to Dupleix, the constitutive duality of metaphysics always issues in the primacy, over *prima philosophia* itself as rational theology, of a universal science. But that universality never derives, except in Descartes, from the primacy of knowledge by order, by bringing-to-evidence, by disposition. It marks instead the primacy — to the point of confiscating the title *prima philosophia* in Pererius — of the science of common being, abstract and universal within the limits of the real, and so announces the imminent domination of *ontologia*, which with Goclenius already shows itself in Greek. The originality of the Cartesian *prima philosophia* lies less in rejecting a purely theological definition of metaphysics, which all his contemporaries reject too, than in the instance it invokes for that overcoming. They overcome first philosophy as rational theology by the primacy of the universal consideration of *ens in quantum ens*, where the objective concept of *ens* has already radically modified Aristotle's *on hē on*; and that enterprise leads swiftly and inevitably to instituting an ontology, which in turn will compose with rational theology and its annexes the school concept of metaphysics. Descartes breaks with this current, majoritarian before him and after, since for him the universal instance that surpasses metaphysics reduced to rational theology is no longer the science of being as such, the future *ontologia*, but the ordered disposition of knowledge, knowledge according to the order of bringing-to-evidence. A universal science does overstep rational theology for Descartes too; but it is the *mathesis universalis*, or at the least the *universalis sapientia* with which the *Regulae* open and which constitutes their single design. Marion makes the timing count. At the precise moment when metaphysics is constituting itself as a science articulated, scholastically but also fundamentally, into a protology — theology, with rational pneumatology and cosmology annexed — surmounted by a universal ontology, Descartes proceeds in the opposite direction: he passes beyond the theological object of metaphysics by rejecting its ontological enterprise as well, resorting in both cases to the sole primacy of ordering according to the demands of certain knowledge. A *prima philosophia* does intervene, in which *prima* indicates neither rational theology nor the science of the objective concept of *ens*, but the science of all things as disposed in order by knowledge — first insofar as known. Metaphysics constitutes itself as a universal protology of bringing-to-evidence. The question that closes the section is therefore sharper than the one that opened it: can such a protology, which rejects both the past primacy of rational theology and the future universality of ontology, legitimately lay claim to the title of metaphysics, or even to that of *prima philosophia*? Does Descartes' double decision still authorize him to take a place within metaphysics at all?

### § 5. The Other First

The question of fact has been answered (p. 59): Descartes does construct a concept of metaphysics, since in deciding without ambiguity for *prima Philosophia* he refuses to reduce metaphysics to rational theology and equally refuses to universalize it through the objective concept of *ens*. He declines both of the principal routes open to him, theology and *ontologia*. What remains is the question of right — whether a universal protology of the disposition of order according to bringing-to-evidence still deserves, in one way or another, the name of metaphysics.

A formal answer is available at least (pp. 60–63), and Marion finds it in Aquinas, from whom Descartes could have claimed a metaphysical status for his epistemic protology even without the title *metaphysica*. Aquinas does admit the bipolarization of what we more often than he call metaphysics and what he confines himself to designating anonymously as *haec scientia*: the Philosopher determines in the *Metaphysics* at once, *simul*, common being and first being separated from all matter. The same primacy of *prima philosophia* composes with the universality of metaphysics into a certain universal and first science that scrutinizes what the particular sciences do not consider — both the common determinations that follow on common being and, equally, the separate substances, which exceed the consideration of all particular sciences. Within this binary topic, which conforms as much to the Aristotelian tension as to the Suarezian conciliation, an epistemic protology of the Cartesian kind has no place and no legitimacy whatever. But Aquinas sometimes sets out a topic of three terms, and it is there that a precedent for the Cartesian protology must be looked for. The proem to the commentary on the *Metaphysics* assigns the science three names. It is called divine science or theology insofar as it considers the substances most separated from matter, not only in reason as mathematical idealities are but also in being, as God and the intelligences are. It is called metaphysics insofar as it considers being and what follows on being, these transphysical things being discovered by way of analysis, as the more common after the less common. And it is called first philosophy insofar as it considers the first causes of things. Beside rational theology and a *metaphysica* devoted to *ens ut ens*, and so outside the couple that will exclusively dominate the later authors, a third term emerges — one that Aquinas elsewhere often dissimulates, tending to include it within theology. Marion's long note is worth following, since it does not merely gather references. Heidegger's remark is quoted, that in the Middle Ages what remained in Aristotle a problem not yet formulated is set out as a firm truth, so that the absence of a problem is raised to the rank of a principle; and Heidegger is reported as finding an incoherence in the Thomist passage, which makes the Thomist solution unworkable. Marion grants the reproach and turns it: the justified criticism must not mask what emerges here, in a new acceptation of *prima philosophia*, of the Cartesian concept of metaphysics ordered to the primacy of knowledge. The third term earns its autonomy from a structural requirement. Every particular science must receive its principles, and by the very definition of a principle none can accede on its own to what makes it possible; an autonomous science is therefore required that can found them and so precede them, at once by transgression, as first philosophy transgresses from the divine, and by universality, as the science of *ens ut ens* deploys itself. Its originality can be deployed without risk of dissolution now under the status of first philosophy — all the other sciences depend on it, receiving from it their principles and their direction against those who deny them — and now under the status of metaphysics: the metaphysician also considers the first principles, and an error bearing on the totality of beings and of the sciences is to be corrected not by the natural philosopher but by the metaphysician; among the philosophical sciences the inferior ones neither prove their principles nor dispute against those who deny them, but leave that to a higher science, and the highest of them, metaphysics, does dispute with the denier of their principles, at least if the adversary concedes something, and otherwise can only dissolve his reasons. Knowledge of the causes thus takes on the status now of metaphysics, now of first philosophy, at the risk of collapsing into one of them. What matters for Marion's purpose, though, is that the primacy of knowledge should be recognized, as such and in general, a dignity at least equal to that of the two other branches of philosophy — and that the recognition should occur under the name *prima philosophia*, which Descartes will take up exactly to designate his epistemic protology. From the three Thomist acceptations Marion draws a table that clarifies the initially confused debate Descartes conducts toward a new definition, or at least a new titulature, of philosophy. Divine science or natural theology is affirmed by all four authors, Aquinas, Pererius, Suarez and Descartes. Metaphysics as the science of *ens in quantum ens* is affirmed by the first three and denied by Descartes, in whom it is reduced to the following term. First philosophy taken as bearing on all the first things knowable in philosophizing by order is affirmed by Aquinas and by Descartes, while in Pererius it is reduced to the second heading and in Suarez to the first.

The table calls for four specifications (pp. 63–64). Divine science or rational theology appears in every author, on the double authority of Aristotle's *prōtē philosophia* and the requirements of Christian faith resting above all on Exodus 3:14 and Romans 1:20; but the permanence does not imply equal importance, since what commands the whole edifice for Aquinas is on the contrary commanded, in Pererius and Suarez, by the consideration of *ens in quantum ens*. Second, Pererius' exceptional denomination of that consideration as *prima philosophia* changes nothing in the commonly admitted duality, since what goes under the title is still the science of being in general; Descartes could have found in this fugitive innovation a lexical incitement and nothing more, in no way a theoretical acquisition. Third, the true antecedent, supposing Descartes sought one, would come rather from the autonomy Aquinas occasionally grants the science of first principles, and so from a protology irreducibly originary and in that sense metaphysical. Fourth, Descartes' originality lies fundamentally in a double operation: the elimination of all consideration of *ens in quantum ens*, already from the *Regulae*, and then the substitution, in place of the universal science of *ens*, of a new metaphysical science of the first things in general, in which primacy and universality must coincide — the primacy of *prima philosophia* and its first things, the universality of the *universalissima sapientia* and its *all* the first things. Descartes will call this *mathesis universalis*. Marion draws the consequence for the *principia metaphysica*, which are therefore not equivalent to mere *principia omnis philosophiae* and must be understood more rigorously: the primacy of the principles depends not on an already constituted metaphysics but on a metaphysics the principles inaugurate by positing themselves as first known by the mind that philosophizes according to order. Metaphysics is not paid out in principles; the principles produce metaphysics, by producing a new primacy — the only one that henceforth counts, primacy according to and through the order of bringing-to-evidence.

The Cartesian *prima philosophia* accordingly claims metaphysical dignity not by virtue of privileged beings but, beyond them, by virtue of ordering as the sole correct determination of prior and posterior, principle and derived (pp. 64–65). It claims that ordering by and for bringing-to-evidence suffices to constitute a metaphysics without recourse to *ens in quantum ens*, which comes in Cartesian terms to a *mathesis universalis* in which the being as known mimics the being as being without ever amounting to it. Taken together, Descartes' two principal initiatives aim to reproduce, and by reproducing to destroy, according to the order of knowledge what Aristotle had stated in the paradox of *prōtē philosophia*: that it is universal insofar as it is first, *katholou houtōs hoti prōtē*. The *mathesis universalis* of 1627–28 establishes the *katholou* empire of order, and of the measure that depends on order, while in 1640 *prima philosophia* aims at the absolutely *prōtē* primacy of all the first things knowable in philosophizing by order. Connected to one another, the two attempts repeat the single Aristotelian paradox and trace, by reference to it, an authentically metaphysical figure — declined according to the criterion of order and no longer according to *on hē on*.

The *mathesis universalis* comes first (pp. 65–69), and in its title only universality is named, primacy being absent. The science — or *mathesis*, since the Greek word here precisely does not designate the mathematics that is to be surpassed but a *generalis quaedam scientia* — engenders itself by virtue of a certain universality. It is universal because it operates on whatever there may be, independently of what that is: singular things are to be regarded otherwise in the order to our knowledge than if we spoke of them as they really exist (AT X, 418, 1–3). Beings are to be interpreted as knowable and no longer as *onta*, and the new interpretation works by a new criterion, no longer *to on* but *aliquis ordo* — all those things only in which some order or measure is examined are referred to *mathesis* (377, 23–378, 2). Marion pauses on the formula *illa omnia tantum*, all things and these only, which would be flatly contradictory if it purported to delimit a region of being. It is not contradictory, because it announces an interpretation bearing on every being whatever, *illa omnia*, insofar as considered under a single criterion, *tantum*; all and each submit to an interpretation that is no longer *hē onta*, no longer *prout revera existunt*, but *prout ab intellectu attinguntur*, ordered first to the understanding and then among themselves. The interpretation deploys itself the more universally as it puts the criterion of order to work the more abstractly. Order, which contains measure but precedes it since it makes measure possible, permits any object whatever to be sought, by an abstraction from matter as deep as the one the interpretation of *on hē on* produced; for what is retained of the being stripped of its beingness is only what it offers of order and measure, *id omne quod circa ordinem et mensuram, nulli speciali materiae addictam*, abstraction made from all matter and hence from every form informing it, so that the interpretation according to order proves as indifferent to the nature at issue as the interpretation *hē on* had been — it makes no difference whether the object be numbers, figures, stars, sounds or anything else. Universality comes to the *mathesis* from the unconditioned efficacy of the criterion of order, which can lay claim to every object because it depends on none of them but only on a relation, by definition always available, between the object and the knowing mind. Order orders objects among themselves only because it first orders them to the mind, *in ordine ad cognitionem nostram*, *respectu nostri intellectus*. No object can fail this order, since the order accrues to the object from outside precisely in order to refer it to what it is *not* — the very mind that interprets it as known. Since the mind never fails, and since human wisdom always remains one and the same however different the subjects to which it is applied, and so deserves the title *universalis sapientia*, the order it deploys and according to which it deploys itself secures its universality. The universal here no longer characterizes an *on hē on* envisageable from *prōtē philosophia*; it characterizes the design of embracing in thought all the things contained in this universe so as to recognize how each is subject to our mind's examination — the universality produced by the submission of things to the thought that contains them in ordering them to itself. The *universitas cogitatione* completes the *katholou* as the *prout ab intellectu attinguntur* accomplishes the *on hē on*, and in both cases by destroying it. From so constituted a universality follows a primacy equally without parallel, marked by two traits. If the first term of *katholou houtōs hoti prōtē* is declined by Descartes according to the requirements of *universalitas cogitatione*, the second can be expected to undergo a similar displacement — and it does, since primacy is first only in relation to the mind: he calls first the simplest and easiest, so that we may use it in resolving questions. Any term whatever counts as first provided it attains the maximum possible simplicity, that is, of facility, so that starting from it other questions can be resolved. Marion's note here is a public correction of his own earlier translation of this passage in the *Règles utiles et claires*, which he calls an indisputable mistranslation: on the parallel of the immediately preceding definition of the absolute, the sense is that Descartes likewise names first the term that is very simple and very easy. Primacy accordingly equals simplicity — the simplest and first operations — precisely because it is determined, as simplicity is, by its relation to the understanding alone: dealing with things only insofar as they are perceived by the intellect, we call simple only those whose cognition is so perspicuous and distinct that the mind cannot divide them into others more distinctly known. Facility attests simplicity, and both determine primacy by submitting it to the understanding. Primacy does not fix the order; it depends on it. Principles function as principles because they are the first known, so that these are not principles governing knowledge, as they remain for Aristotle in the *hothen gnōston to pragma prōton*, but principles that knowledge institutes and the order disposes. If the *prima principia* can be known only by *intuitus*, that must be understood more radically than as a repetition of Aristotle's *nous tōn archōn*: as a submission of the first principles to the primacy of the disposition of order, alone originary because alone operative. The principles too are first only insofar as known, *a primis et per se notis principiis*. Primacy therefore results, exactly as universality does, from the interpretation of the being as known. The second trait concerns the asymmetry of the title. The *mathesis universalis* decides unambiguously about universality, since it attributes universality to itself, but primacy is missing from both its title and its definition — or rather primacy is introduced only afterwards, and by universality itself: the primacy of the first criteria of knowledge in Rule II, of the first faculties in Rules III and XII, of the first operations of the mind in Rules IX to XI. Primacy multiplies precisely because it remains a *second* primacy, and the paradox is unavoidable since every term owes its first rank to the disposition in order of bringing-to-evidence — which is the *artis secretum* that sums up the entire *Regulae*. By right these second primacies refer, by their very multiplicity, to an originary primacy, the source of all the others, namely the *mathesis universalis* itself, which surpasses all the other sciences in facility of knowing. Put otherwise, Descartes lays down as *regula prima omnium* that one must first seek a *finis generalis*, namely the *universalissima sapientia*. The universality of the science erects itself directly into originary primacy, and from beginning to end of the *Regulae* it alone precedes and dominates, constructing primacy in Rules I to IV and then exercising it in Rules V to XXI — without that primacy ever becoming other than a science's. It never becomes a being's: neither God, nor the ego, nor even the *intellectus* appears here as existing, still less as a being that might claim an ontically secured primacy. Only the general Method or the *mathesis universalis* exercise a primacy, and it remains strictly epistemic. Primacy merely folds the universality of a science back on itself.

The *prima philosophia* undertakes exactly what the *Regulae* refused (pp. 69–72): to pass from an epistemic to an ontic primacy. The intention shows in the definition of its new object, all the first things — and Descartes locates the gap very lucidly in the ambivalence of the concept of principle. The word can be taken in various senses, and it is one thing to seek a common notion so clear and general that it can serve to prove the existence of all the beings, the *entia*, one will know afterwards, and another to seek a being whose existence is more known to us than any other's, so that it can serve as a principle for knowing them (AT IV, 444, 4–12). Either the principle designates a common notion, one of the simple natures, which makes possible the disposition according to order and so puts the *mathesis universalis* to work — and we are in the figure of the *Regulae*; or it is a matter of finding, as principle and still according to the order of knowledge, *entia*, a being and an existence — and we meet the figure of the *Meditationes*, which seek the first things. Universality is not lacking to *prima philosophia*, which seeks in general all the first things and does not restrict itself in advance to the two beings the developed title names, God as existing and the soul as incorporeal. The whole difficulty is to identify the type of universality at work: is it ontic, as the primacy sought here is? Marion frames the possibility symmetrically. Did Descartes take up the Aristotelian couple twice — first as *mathesis universalis* aiming at a strictly epistemic primacy, then as *prima philosophia* presupposing an ontic universality in its very aim at an existing being — deducing in the first case the missing primacy from universality and inferring in the second the deficient universality from primacy, so as to produce two homogeneous and separate couples, one exclusively epistemic in both dimensions and the other exclusively ontic in both? Certainly not. Far from each redoubling on itself, the two moments of Cartesian thought refer to one another and combine their very dissimilarities: the ontic primacy that *prima philosophia* exercises depends throughout on the exclusively epistemic universality that the *mathesis universalis* exercises. This direct relating engenders a distortion essential to Cartesian metaphysics — essential because it constitutes it. Three arguments establish the distortion provisionally. Descartes stresses without ambiguity the continuity between the method, indeed the *Discours*, and the *prima philosophia*: the general Method already permits in 1637 to overstep the various positive sciences and reach what is commonly called metaphysics, since he inserted something of metaphysics, physics and medicine into the first discourse precisely to show that the method extends to all sorts of matters. Metaphysics falls here under the method, on the regime common to all the sciences, and the later elaboration of the *Meditationes* does not disturb the continuity, since on Burman's testimony Descartes still held in 1649 that the *Method* contains an epitome of these Meditations, which the Meditations are to expound. Marion's note corrects the direction of the relation with some emphasis: in the relevant letters it is a question of clarifying the *Discours* by the *Meditationes*, and not, as many readers let themselves suppose, the reverse. This first continuity is no doubt a continuity in the order of subject matters, the most external to the Cartesian project, a pedagogical suggestion rather than a conceptual dependence. The permanence of the order of reasons is harder to contest. From the introduction of *prima philosophia* the universality of ordering determines the things sought, and if those first things remain indeterminate and are not identified straight off with God and the soul, it is because their primacy rests on no ontic excellence but on the disposition of order according to bringing-to-evidence; on that order, primacy — even the primacy of effectively existing beings — does not coincide with the customary objects of metaphysics, so that other primacies get organized and several disappear. The *rationum series et nexus* confirm here what the *mathesis universalis* had established as the *rerum quaerendarum series* and as the *nexum naturalemque ordinem*. *Prima philosophia* advances toward beings only by observing at each step what *ordo videtur exigere*, constituting series of terms organized from the simplest — existing or not, of known essence or not, relative in itself or not — so as to arrive finally at primacies that are indeed ontic but epistemically derived. It would be possible, Marion suggests, to reread the whole of the *Meditationes* as a particular case of methodical ordering, in which the order of reasons amounts to applying the *rerum quaerendarum series* to the metaphysical domain, and equally possible to establish a parallel between the principal moments of the order followed in the *Regulae* and the stages of the demonstration accomplished in the *Meditationes* — in both cases the discontinuity of the two enterprises would be sharpened rather than filled in or masked. The third argument names a difficulty still to come: the function of first principle, on a primacy conforming to the order of bringing-to-evidence, falls not to God, however infinite and perfect, but to the *mens humana* under the figure of the *ego cogito*. The violence and strangeness of the upheaval must be weighed — he took the being or existence of this thought as the first principle, from which he very clearly deduced the following ones, namely that there is a God who is author of all that is in the world. In the order of the *mathesis universalis* an existence can be established, but always by remaining first the existence of a thought, which as thought is thought or thinking; whence its precedence over another primacy no less indisputable in its own order but second according to the order of bringing-to-evidence — the ontic primacy held by God as author and creator. Nothing precedes him except the thought that recognizes him as first.

Descartes understands metaphysics in the sense of *prima philosophia*, then, in a debate with his contemporaries that is complex but in the end clear (p. 72). He wins its domain, alongside natural and soon rational theology, from *metaphysica* as science of *ens in quantum ens*, soon *ontologia*. The urgency is now to assign the exact nature of the primacy so privileged, and the formula that it concerns in general all the first things knowable in philosophizing by order defines less its essence than its contradiction. Can universality coincide with primacy? And if, to reconcile them, universality is assigned to the *mathesis universalis* and primacy to the *prima philosophia*, as Marion has just done, is the contradiction not merely displaced, since the requirement of epistemic order brackets every ontic foundation?

Fixing the acceptation of *metaphysica* in the Cartesian lexicon therefore does not close the question of the Cartesian constitution of metaphysics but at last opens it onto the concept (p. 72).

## II. Onto-theo-logy

### § 6. A Nothing of Ontology

The result reached is paradoxical (pp. 73–78). Seeking to determine the essence of Cartesian metaphysics by identifying the acceptations of the term "metaphysics" and its semantic constellation, Marion has arrived at something undecidable: Descartes does maintain a nearly univocal definition of the word, but analysis reveals in it so radical an upheaval of the earlier definitions that one may doubt whether he refers to metaphysics or constitutes one even where he uses the term. No purely lexical and philological examination could in fact succeed so long as the term sought has not been sufficiently identified, and Marion has been inquiring into the presence and sense of *métaphysique* in Descartes without yet having specified the concept of metaphysics he is looking for. The insufficiency announces itself in the doubt: faced with textually indisputable occurrences, one still doubts whether a figure of metaphysics is accomplished in them, so metaphysics must obey a strictly conceptual definition. Which one, and by what guiding thread? If Descartes has broken essentially and definitively with the earlier concepts, and if his own statements do not suffice, at least at first, to specify the original concept he produces, the interpreter can only turn to the concepts of metaphysics that indisputable metaphysicians have attributed to Descartes, if only as a provisional aid. And this is already a first confirmation, since the later tradition never hesitated to recognize a Cartesian figure of metaphysics, however novel — or rather precisely because it appears in rupture with the earlier figure. Hegel more than anyone fixed that figure, or better fixed Descartes' place within the domain of metaphysics. Marion has established from the letter of the Cartesian texts that metaphysics becomes first philosophy insofar as every being is considered not first as being but as known or knowable, so that primacy passes from the supreme being, whatever it is, to the instance of knowledge, whatever that is; and this still imprecise and fragile result finds firm confirmation in Hegel's verdict. One must indeed speak of a metaphysics of Descartes, and one must note that the unity of being and thought constitutes here the first term; Descartes is the first, and so makes modernity possible, because he is the first to have established thought, *cogitatio*, as the first principle of philosophy — the true initiator of modern philosophy insofar as he makes thought the principle, the one who knew how to go for the first time from thought to something firm and to conquer a pure beginning. What is proper to Descartes, and establishes him as the metaphysician par excellence of modernity, is that the question of the beginning and of the first term, of the primacy at work in *philosophia prima*, passes from being to thought; which is why Hegel sets down as the second characteristic trait of Descartes' metaphysics the identity in it of thought with being, evidently understood from thought and to thought's benefit. Arbitrary as this may look, the determination can be confirmed, and Marion suggests Descartes conforms to it in advance in a text where he underlines the discovery in which his innovation and hence his priority consists: he is the first, *primus enim sum*, to have considered thought as the principal attribute of incorporeal substance and extension as that of corporeal substance. He is indeed the first to have so defined them; better, the first to have dared to define a substance from *cogitatio*, and even to determine *cogitatio* itself as a substance. But the priority, indisputable as it is, would not itself have been possible had *cogitatio* not from the start taken the figure of an ego. *Cogitatio* becomes attribute of a *res cogitans* because an ego puts it to work in saying and performing the *cogito*; *cogitatio* is born of a *cogitare* that can only be performed in the first person. The priority therefore falls finally to the ego, without which *cogitatio* could reach neither its own existence — *ego sum, ego existo* — nor any substance at all whose principal attribute it might constitute. Marion's note fixes the point by contrast: Spinoza's *homo cogitat* is in no way equivalent to *ego sum, ego cogito*, since it is an ontic and anthropological description whose speaker remains indeterminate and unsecured, and not the effective performance of a first principle. Descartes would therefore not be the first to have established *cogitatio* as the principal attribute of incorporeal substance had he not leaned on the absolute priority of the ego; and that priority implies in turn a priority of the ego over *esse*, or what comes to the same, an anteriority of the *sum* over all the other forms of *esse*. Marion then makes Descartes' *primus sum* say more than it says. In declaring himself first, Descartes says, before and beyond the thesis he did in fact posit first, with what primacy first philosophy as he inaugurates it affects the question of being. *Primus sum*: the verb "to be" intervenes firstly — in two senses. The verb, and with it the question of being in the metaphysical sense, intervenes first and at the outset; and the verb appears in the first person singular, so that the question of being lets itself be determined from the start by the primacy of a first term, the ego. *Esse* is said firstly under the figure of the ego, and the formula *primus sum* comes to the developed formula *primum est: ego sum*. Marion concedes he is forcing the text, and states what the forcing is for: to let it say what Descartes doubtless never stopped trying to think, that being, including the being of beings that are not the ego, is said firstly under the figure of the *sum*. The being of beings is declined, before any other determination, in the first person singular; *esse* is declined according to its priority inclination toward the ego, and so as a *sum*. The starting point lies in the assimilation of *esse* to *cogitatio*, itself dominated by the ego. Other texts confirm it. When *Principia* I, § 7 states the first and most certain cognition, that *ego cogito, ergo sum* is the first and most certain to occur to anyone philosophizing in order, the equivalence of *cogitatio* with *esse* can be demonstrated only through the mediation of the ego, which exercises the one to the point of deserving the other; and the ego is in its turn accessible as first and absolutely certain truth only to whoever philosophizes according to order, the order of knowledge itself. The ego reveals itself first and certain, but exclusively on the order defined by the primacy of knowledge, where what is first is what is most certain. *Esse* comes back to *cogitatio* because both come back to the ego, and they come back to the ego only by virtue of a radically new primacy, the one the order of knowledge institutes and which provokes the turn of metaphysics into *prima philosophia*. In both texts the question of the *esse* of beings returns directly to the figure of the ego, by way of the *sum* and following the rules of an order determined exclusively by the priority of knowledge. Descartes philosophizes in deciding of being that it comes back to *sum* — and in this he repeats Aristotle without imitating him, since Aristotle, at the precise moment of interrogating the being as such, decided in advance and in the very statement of the question that *to on* comes back to *ousia*: *ti to on, touto esti tis hē ousia*. Being comes back either to *ousia* or to the *sum* and hence to the ego, producing two radically heterogeneous figures of metaphysics — neither of which, precisely, properly bears the title. That *esse* comes back to *sum* means that the question of *esse* has neither the time nor the freedom, in Descartes, to detach itself from the instance of the ego. As soon as the ego appears, *esse* disappears as a question, engulfed in the evidence of the *sum*; *esse* comes back to the ego, which appropriates it, because it comes back to the *sum*, in which it is swallowed; and it comes back to the *sum* because it is cut short as a question, short-circuited by the certainty of *ego sum, ego existo*. Marion allows that the reduction is perfectly justified from the Cartesian standpoint: for the order of knowledge a single certainty suffices to extinguish an interrogation and authorizes moving on to the next question, and the question of *esse* finds its solution as soon as a first being is certainly known such that it inaugurates a series of other knowable beings. That *esse* might admit senses other than *sum* and what follows from the knowing ego does not matter to Descartes, because the question no longer bears on *esse* but on knowledge by order. The question of the being of beings does not have to be heard as such; it has to be resolved — undone, finished off, reduced. To the question about the being of beings Descartes answers *primus sum*, or that *ego sum* is the first knowledge and therefore the most certain; the manner of being of beings is fixed as *sum*, being in conformity with the ego.

Two objections arise at once, and Marion treats both as confirmations rather than refutations (pp. 78–80). The first is that he has exaggeratedly overvalued partial and minor texts torn from their contexts, and with the more bad faith for having respected their letter — an objection the note attributes to Beyssade, responding to an earlier sketch of this analysis published in 1982. Marion answers that an interpreter, once the letter is respected, must never underestimate a text and should on principle overestimate it, and that in any case what has been recovered here are theses very widely established elsewhere by Descartes and admitted even by his commentators: that the question of *on hē on* disappears in favour of disposition according to the order of knowledge; that *esse* accordingly has sense and validity only according to what the ego knows of it, under the figure the ego imposes, namely the *sum*; and that the priority of the ego, reducing *esse* as question to *sum* as answer, follows immediately from the primacy of the order of knowledge over the ontic order. What the seemingly paradoxical exegeses recover is the earlier books' result, that Cartesian *prima philosophia* refuses the object of *metaphysica*, the consideration of the concept of being that will shortly give rise to *ontologia*. The second objection is incomparably stronger. That the ego intervenes at the head of the order according to knowledge, even in the case of *esse*, the *Meditationes* establish explicitly and the *Regulae* probably already do; but if *esse* becomes questionable and thinkable for Descartes only under the certain figure of the *sum* as the ego declines it, why does he never thematically broach the mode of being of the *sum*, while deploying at length the properties of the ego as *res cogitans*, *substantia*, *mens*, subject to passions? Why is the richness of the ontic determinations of the ego doubled by a massive ontological silence about the manner of being of the *sum* — about the manner of being of which the *sum* claims to be the sole paradigm? The objection goes back to Heidegger, who observed in 1927 that with the *cogito sum* Descartes claimed to establish a new and secure ground for philosophy, but left undetermined in this radical beginning the manner of being of the *res cogitans*, more precisely the sense of being of the *sum*. That the ego is, and is first, determines nothing of the sense of being of this very particular figure of *esse* that appears with *sum*. Marion holds it constant that Descartes did not answer the question because he did not hear it, from himself or from any contemporary, and doubtless could not conceive it — and he is candid about the difficulty on his own side, asking whether we can even understand the question after *Sein und Zeit*, and whether it can be pursued in Descartes' theses and texts, and answering that nothing is less assured. But a historical fact forces the question back onto the table at a lower level. Descartes' silence about what *sum* says of *esse* contrasts with the emergence, in his own lifetime, of the concept of *ontologia*. The term enters the history of metaphysics first in 1613, where Goclenius still writes it in Greek, and then above all with Clauberg in 1647 in the first edition of the *Elementa Philosophiae sive Ontosophia*, more clearly still in the editions of 1660, 1664 and 1691. The concept and the name appear, that is, during Descartes' lifetime and at the hands of one of his most declared disciples, and their fortune will run to Kant. How is Descartes' abstention to be explained in such a context? Marion sharpens abstention into denegation: Descartes never broaches the definition of *ens* or of *entia* except to refuse it intelligibility or philosophical utility. Formally, Cartesian philosophy deploys itself as an explicit and avowed non-ontology.

The non-ontology is carried out by three constant operations, an elimination, a reduction and a postulation, and the elimination comes first (pp. 80–82). It bears on what the *Regulae* stigmatize as *entia philosophica* or *entia abstracta* and what, at the far end of the literary career, the *Recherche de la Vérité* will still call *entia scholastica*. Marion's note registers that the two reproaches Descartes levels do not agree — he excludes the philosophers' *entia* because they do not fall under imagination, the understanding's auxiliary in the *Regulae*, and because their whole reality resides in the philosophers' imagination — and specifies that *ens* for Descartes does not always or first designate the abstract concept of *ens commune*, which he precisely does not thematize, but the genus, the species, the specific difference and the definitions that follow, whence his constant criticism of the definition of *homo* as *animal rationale*. Why do such beings merit so exemplarily philosophical a designation? Rule VI answers: because each belongs to some *genus entis*, and those genera are themselves constituted, as in the Porphyrian tree mentioned elsewhere, according to the philosophers' categories, hence according to the *katēgoriai tou ontos*. The initial and constant decision of the *Regulae*, and so of all the thought that follows, is not to view things as susceptible of becoming objects of *intuitus* according to the categorial figures of *ens*; and Descartes solemnly warns that this decision contains the chief secret of the art and that nothing in the whole treatise is more useful — that all things can be disposed in certain series, not insofar as they are referred to some genus of being, as the philosophers divided them into their categories, but insofar as some can be known from others. The operation recurs often in the *Regulae*, not always identifying so clearly the categorial *ens* eliminated, but rather stressing the instance toward which the *res* are diverted — diverted, Marion notes, in the precise sense of the diversion of a watercourse, or better, of an embezzlement of funds. So in Rule XII, where singular things are to be regarded otherwise in the order to our knowledge than if we spoke of them as they really exist, and where, dealing with things only insofar as they are perceived by the intellect, we call simple only those whose cognition is so perspicuous and distinct that the mind cannot divide them into others more distinctly known. What results are simple natures, which are simple, and indeed which simply *are*, only insofar as they are diverted from what they are *revera*, and which in a word are only *respectu intellectus nostri*. This diversion away from the *categoriae* that determine the *genera entium* refers the *res* to the *mathesis universalis*, *nempe ad ordinem vel ad mensuram*, without regard to the differences introduced by the least *specialis materia*. Knowledge begins when matter disappears as a determining instance, and with it the form that in each case specializes matter, specifies it, gives it form and hence *essentia*, *eidos* and hence *ousia*. Knowledge begins when the *res* loses all proper essence, and so when the order imposed by *ens* and its several senses is effaced. Each time the *Regulae* evoke the *genus entis* they eliminate it as radically as they can, and Marion explains why: a genus of being implies a new term, a *novum genus entis*, and hence a *novum ens* that inscribes and installs itself there. That new being demands recognition by virtue of an *essentia* irreducibly imbricated in the categories, and hence demands first an avowal of ignorance from the mind that does not yet accede to this new categorial region; every *novum ens* displays its novelty as a genus of being unknown to me, and ten years after the *Regulae* Descartes will still speak of a philosophical being that is unknown to him. The point is to be taken exactly: the being remains unknown just so long as one insists on viewing it as the philosophers want, according to the *categoriae entis*, instead of taking it as order requires, *respectu intellectus nostri*. Descartes is asking, in plain terms, that we quit philosophy insofar as philosophy considers beings according to their genera and hence according to the categories of *ens* — that we renounce philosophy as ontology.

Elimination remains tangential and always leaves a residue, which is why reduction follows (pp. 82–85). Two occurrences of *ens* in the *Regulae*, the only ones no disqualification touches, allow the residue's status to be fixed. In the first, from Rule XIV, an extended *subjectum* presents itself in the very particular essence of being appropriate to what geometry requires: as the mind aims in it at a figure it will consider only the character of the *figuratum*; if at a body, the three dimensions; if at a surface, two; if at a line, one; and if at a point only, it will consider it with everything else omitted except that it be a being, *praeterquam quod sit ens*. *Ens* intervenes here as finally irreducible, connoting pure and simple position without any measurable extension — an unextended position and also, to speak a Husserlian language, an irreal one, since no *res* endowed with individuality, existence or even essence appears. What merits the title of *ens*, and why does it undergo no disqualification just here? The double question has a single answer: *ens* indicates purely and simply the minimal object offered to the mind's imaginative regard, and it is ordered to the mind all the more perfectly in that it results from it. *Ens* retains nothing here of an essence, a genus, the categories, and it appears only once the initial *subjectum* has disappeared and with it every unknown philosophical being. The second occurrence, from the same Rule, speaks of a mixture of known beings or natures: the knowledge of any question, here the magnet's, comes down to the combination of already known beings. Why speak positively of beings here and call them known, when before and after they will be called unknown and disqualified for that very reason? The answer lies in the apposition — known beings *or natures* — since the privilege follows from the equivalence of these *entia* with the simple natures, which consist entirely in the knowledge the constitutive mind takes of them. *Ens* therefore remains admitted, at the end of elimination, only in the exact measure in which it reduces to just what elimination was working to release: a pure, simple, empty, uniform objectivity — uniform because absolutely stripped of all form and of the least *essentia*. What is left is an *ens* with none of the four possible acceptations of *on* in Aristotle, who distinguished it according to the categories, according to *dynamis* and *energeia*, according to *ousia* and *symbebēkos*, and finally according to the true and the false. Descartes rejects the categorial acceptation from the start, in the Rule VI text already cited. He then rejects the validity of any distinction between *actus* and *potentia*, since being in potency, properly speaking, is nothing at all — and Marion's note observes that refusing this sense of the being of beings both provokes and implies the criticism of the Aristotelian definition of motion in Rule XII. As for the pair *substantia* and *accidens*, it lapses on the epistemic standpoint definitively adopted, either because accidentality and contingency must vanish in deduction, which always aims at necessity, or because substance can be known only through its principal attribute, and the better as more attributes are besides known. There remains a fourth sense of *esse*, which Descartes indisputably privileges and seems to take over from Aristotle: that truth and falsity properly can be only in the intellect alone. The rapprochement is confirmed when Descartes explicitly introduces being, writing that truth consists in being and falsity in non-being only, so that the idea of the infinite, comprehending all being, comprehends everything true in things. *Ens* thus reduces to a single one of its Aristotelian acceptations, being in the understanding — and Marion's note is careful to add that the Aristotelian text invoked is itself already in retreat from *Metaphysics* Θ 10, which alone reaches the true as *the* sense, and the first sense, of *on*, and that the rapprochement with the *Regulae* is Heidegger's own. But the very rapprochement between *on dianoia* and *in solo intellectu* opens an abyss between the two theses about the being of beings. For Aristotle, *dianoia* contributes entirely to revealing the *eidos* and hence the *ousia* of the thing; for Descartes, the *intellectus* can accomplish the representation of an object only by abstracting from its essence, its actuality and its categorial definition, so that to be in the mode of the true is reduced to being in the mode of represented objectivity. Truth is not merely situated in the understanding among other senses of *esse*; it results from the understanding as the sole instance determining the being of beings. Being as true hypertrophies into the unique sense of *esse*, and above all radicalizes into being as representation — *ens non in quantum ens, sed in quantum repraesentatum, ut objectum*, as Marion sums it up.

The postulation completes the sequence (pp. 85–88). Descartes postulates the evidence of the question of the being of beings as going so far without saying that the answer keeps disqualifying, stifling and annihilating the question, from which it no longer proceeds and of which it has no need. The question succumbs in advance to the superabundant certainty of the answer: to be, as an object certainly representable, comes to existing, and existence calls for neither definition nor justification — so that Descartes does not so much leave the question of the being of beings fallow as substitute for it an answer always postulated and nowhere thought as such. Existence goes without saying: the distinction of essence from existence is known to all, he retorts superbly to Hobbes; no one was ever so stupid as to need to be taught what existence is; and asked where he treated the distinction between essence and existence, he does not recall having spoken of it anywhere. The evidence that characterizes them also confounds them, since in a triangle existing outside thought it seems to him manifest that essence and existence are in no way distinguished, and the same for all universals; in short, existence is nothing other than existing essence. Essence reduces to existence because being in general is led back to existence, for the ego in *ego sum, ego existo* as for God, who as perfect being is or exists. An objection presents itself: does the thesis not contradict itself in attributing to Descartes a double presupposition, being as represented and then being as existence, the latter posited by definition outside thought? Not at all, since Descartes fixes the primacy of existence precisely in that existence can be represented. From the *Regulae* onward existence counts among the simple natures, which by definition can be completely and perfectly known — anyone can intuit in his mind that he exists. And existence can be known not despite but by virtue of its exteriority to the mind, because that exteriority better accomplishes the objectity of an *objectum* disposed facing and therefore for the ego. Existence accordingly allows every thing to be conceived as an object, in consequence of its own conceivability: in the idea or concept of every thing existence is contained, since we can conceive nothing except under the aspect of the existent — possible or contingent existence in the concept of a limited thing, necessary and perfect existence in the concept of a supremely perfect being. This is one of the axioms or common notions of the geometrically ordered appendix to the Second Replies, and Descartes is postulating in it that every thing, hence every being, reduces from the outset to existence, because existence is contained in the concept in every case, whether the being be finite or infinite, contingent or necessary. The whole lexicon of the being of beings is led back to representation by the very movement that brings it back to existence: who does not know that by *res* is understood a real being, that *ens* is said from being or existing, and that the natures of things themselves are called essences by the philosophers because we can conceive them only as being or existing? The polemical impulse against Voetius frees Descartes from his usual prudence, and the postulation declares itself with corresponding sharpness: the being must be conceived, and to that end must be reduced to existence as the sole acceptation of the being of beings. But — and this is where Marion turns the analysis — as this existence works to produce evidence as an axiom of experience, and as it is itself produced in evidence as the object par excellence, the acceptation of the being of beings as existence succumbs to its own evidence and disappears in it. Existence accomplishes and produces evidence so completely that it dissolves in it, and of the being of beings as existence evident for representation nothing remains that can be said. Marion thinks Descartes may have drawn the consequence explicitly, or at least that an enigmatic line of the *Conversation with Burman* lets it be guessed: *in Metaphysica nihil intelligitur per ens*. One may understand, with a recent translator, that in metaphysics nothingness is understood only through being. But it seems equally licit to understand literally that nothing is understood, and nothing there to understand, by *being* — that in saying "being," in the sense of existence, one says nothing. Not that existence abolishes itself in a nothingness, nor that indeterminate being merges with indeterminate nothingness as in Hegel, but that in the supreme evidence of existence nothing further can be said of being. The note gathers a striking parallel in Pascal, who observes that one cannot undertake to define a word without beginning with *c'est*, so that to define being one would have to say *c'est*, employing the defined word in the definition; and that no one even knows what it is to be, since it is impossible to define. Marion also acknowledges the phrase's ancestry in Alquié's "negative ontology," despite the difference of intention. Descartes thus appears as the first among metaphysicians — *nemo ante me* — to have believed he could dispense with thinking the being of beings as such, except to disqualify it as a *novum ens* uselessly to be admitted. The question of the being of beings does not arise, because the answer precedes it: the being evidently is according to existence, of which there is nothing to hear and nothing to expect. Even the absence of a thought of nothingness confirms that there is no longer any occasion here to question the being of beings, and that what is established is a nothing of ontology.

### § 7. Principle and *causa sui*

Following his concept of *prima philosophia*, then, Descartes arrives at a nothing of ontology (pp. 88–89). But Heidegger posits that all metaphysical thought is onto-logy or else is absolutely nothing. Should one not admit once for all that the Cartesian enterprise decidedly does not belong to metaphysics? Marion refuses to treat the conclusion as absurd. Provocative and paradoxical as it looks, it would not be untenable; better, it would do justice to the claim to absolute originality Descartes never ceased to press, and it would rejoin the views of famous commentators intent on stressing in Descartes the end of all realism and the beginning of a pure reign of consciousness, or on deploring in him a degradation of ancient philosophy. The note supplies the positions and shows how symmetrical they are. Liard holds that what characterizes Descartes' physics and makes it wholly new and unprecedented is the absence of any metaphysical idea, and that in the whole of Descartes' work method and the sciences form a distinct and independent work from which what is inserted of metaphysics could be detached without injury. Maritain plays the same analysis in reverse, calling Descartes a metaphysician unfaithful to metaphysics who turns deliberately toward the plains watered by the river Mathematics, and who therefore appears as having properly degraded metaphysics. Brunschvicg formulates the motive of the supposed desertion as indifference or even rejection of being, since Descartes refuses the universals of the dialecticians and abandons to its illusory destiny the entity of the concept of being — the illusory preoccupation with the principles of being. Gilson held the same position at least until 1925. Short of contenting oneself with a vague and imprecise concept of metaphysics, would it not be more expedient and clearer to abandon the word where Descartes is concerned? Both the inquiry into terminology and the inquiry into the role of the question of the being of beings seem to point definitively that way.

Marion gives three motives for not yielding to so simple a solution, and the first concerns *ontologia* itself (pp. 89–92). The nothing of ontology was concluded from the primacy of the question of representation over that of the being as being: Descartes evacuates ontology as the science of *ens in quantum ens* because he undertakes first to fix the conditions of the representation of the being, *ens in quantum cognitum*. That result will not be called into question and will find further confirmation. But it perhaps does not lead to a nothing of *ontology* — or it would lead there only if *ontologia*, as it historically unfolds between Clauberg and Wolff, contradicted the primacy of knowledge over the interrogation of *ens* as such; only, in short, if ontology as a historically dated discipline, contemporary with Descartes, did not itself accede to *ens in quantum ens* under the presupposition of its representability, *ut cognitum*. And this, Marion argues, is exactly the case. Clauberg is the privileged example, the Cartesian who first, from 1647, institutes *ontologia* as a discipline in its own right at the rank of fundamental science, and who states his definition of *ens* with admirable clarity and equal honesty at the opening of the *Ontosophia*, still entitled metaphysics but more aptly ontology or catholic science or universal philosophy. Three senses of *ens* are distinguished. The third defines it as substance, to which accidents are opposed. The second defines it as *aliquid*, really being even without being thought, *nemine etiam cogitante*, and opposed only to pure *nihil*. The first defines it, on the contrary, from *cogitatio*, and consequently with no contrary at all: it denotes everything that can be thought, which for the sake of distinction is called intelligible. Clauberg recognizes that the third acceptation, as substance, remains the most powerful; and yet, for a better understanding of the question, he privileges the first two and in fact above all the first, since he begins universal philosophy from the thinkable being, just as first philosophy, beginning from the singular, considers nothing prior to the cogitating mind itself. *Ontologia*, that is, does not consider the being from itself — in which case it would have to envisage it, as Aristotle does, as substance, *ousia*, where it deploys itself most powerfully — but first from *cogitatio*, hence as *ens cogitabile*, being insofar as thinkable, conceived and represented, and not insofar as being. Whence the canonical definition: a being is whatever in any manner is, can be thought and said — to the point that *nihil* itself becomes a being provided I think it; of the being, ontology says first and above all that it is thinkable and thought, since a being is thought when it is perceived by the mind. Marion's note traces the filiation and its sequel: Clauberg naturally recovers Suarez's objective concept of being, which objectivates the being from the formal concept as an act of the understanding; the determination of *ens* from the essence of representation then deploys itself explicitly in Leibniz, who situates what he too calls ontology, the science of something and nothing, within the science of the thinkable in general as such; and Wolff maintains the decision by defining being from possibility and possibility from representation, in explicit agreement with Clauberg and above all with Descartes' *Principia* I, § 45. Does Clauberg not then reproduce, at the very moment of fixing *ontologia* and precisely in order to institute it, the nothing of ontology stigmatized in Descartes — namely that the science of the being is first a science of the science of the being, a science of the being envisaged from the standpoint of its representation? The debate has an explicit answer in a note Clauberg himself adds to the text that deduces the primacy of *ens cogitabile* from the primacy of the *mens cogitans*: first philosophy is so called not on account of the universality of the object it treats but because whoever means to philosophize seriously must begin with it, namely from the knowledge of his own mind and of God; and this first philosophy is contained in Descartes' six *Meditationes*. The reference admits no restriction. To justify preferring, as the first sense of the being, precisely the sense in which the being does not intervene in itself but as thinkable, and so to justify a paradoxical acceptation of primacy that opposes the traditional priority of *ousia*, Clauberg refers to the Cartesian determination of *prima philosophia*, where *prima* applies to the order of knowledge and not to the order of the being in its being. If *ontologia* establishes its empire by transposing the being from *ousia* to *ens cogitabile*, it presupposes that the being can first submit a priori to the yoke of *cogitatio*, which is to say that it leans on the Cartesian decisions about first philosophy — as Clauberg expressly acknowledges. The conclusion is that *ontologia* as a discipline depends so radically on Descartes' nothing of ontology that it could in fact appear historically only after the Cartesian institution and thanks to it: Clauberg could mount a *Defensio cartesiana* only insofar as he was himself, more essentially, defended by the Cartesian inauguration. Marion does not develop the arguments for this filiation here, but he does revise the status of the nothing of ontology in consequence. If it makes the appearance of *ontologia* possible, then on one hand *ontologia* perhaps accomplishes a new and decisive stage in the *mis*recognition, not the recognition, of the question of the being as such; and on the other hand the nothing of ontology itself doubtless harbours a radical decision about the being of beings, precisely in demoting it to the rank of represented being. In that light the nothing of ontology becomes less the index of a desertion by Descartes of the question of the being of beings than the paradoxical sign that he is deeply invested in it, voluntarily or not. All metaphysical thought is onto-logy or else it is absolutely nothing: it may be that Cartesian thought is an ontology — the one that lets ontology constitute itself as thought of the being as thinkable — and so belongs to metaphysics, and is not nothing.

The second motive comes from Heidegger again, and turns on the hyphen (pp. 92–95). Heidegger does not only say that all metaphysics is ontology in the sense of *ontologia* as a historically determined discipline; he speaks of an onto-logy, where the hyphen marks the articulation between logic and the being, or more exactly between the *logos*, of which logic offers only one of the avatars, and the *on*, of which *ens* doubtless does not retain everything. As metaphysics develops historially, the *logos* assumes the ever heavier and more powerful function of a foundation, and that foundation manifests itself finally in a strict figure whose outline Heidegger discerns under the title of the onto-theo-logical constitution of metaphysics. On that constitution, the logical foundation states the being in an onto-logy only by redoubling its own anteriority over the being with a theo-logy, in which it likewise precedes the divine, still as foundation. The *logos*, which says and founds the being in its being, says and founds it also, in a deep and confused unity, as supreme being, *to theion*. If metaphysics must be identified as essentially onto-logy, it is for that very reason that metaphysics, thought more exactly and more clearly, is onto-theo-logical. Onto-theo-logy offers the fundamental trait of all metaphysics because it marks not only the tension between two dimensions of the being — the being as such and the being in its excellence — but also the foundation that unites them reciprocally and governs them throughout: being founds beings, and beings as the most being found and support Being. The being par excellence finds its foundation insofar as it accomplishes the being in its being and exemplifies the manner of being of beings; reciprocally, the being in its being lets itself be founded on the mode of production by the being that excellently accomplishes the being of all beings. The redoubling of the foundation thus secures the specificity of both the onto-logy and the theo-logy, under the single condition of the condition — the logical one: *logos*, *ratio*, *causa*, sufficient reason, concept. Admitting this definition as a working hypothesis, Marion asks whether Descartes can satisfy it, and states two arguments for a provisionally positive answer without prejudging the analyses still to come. The first is a fact: Descartes seems to have sensed that metaphysics constitutes itself according to the tension between two foundations, onto-logical and theo-logical. The evidence is the ambiguity he himself recognizes in the concept of principle, the very ambiguity that made the mutation of primacy possible — that it is one thing to seek a common notion clear and general enough to serve as a principle for proving the existence of all the beings one will know afterwards, and another to seek a being whose existence is more known to us than any other's, so that it can serve as a principle for knowing them. The duality of principles leads on one side to a principle for reaching the existence of beings, since in the first sense it may be said that it is impossible for the same thing at once to be and not to be, and on the other side to a principle that makes a being directly known, since in the other sense the first principle is that our soul exists, because there is nothing whose existence is more notorious to us. Either the principle bears directly on a being that is first and par excellence, exemplifying the manner of being of all beings — and this is already the *ego cogito*, the being insofar as it thinks first; or it bears on all possible beings, excluding the impossible and defining them as such, by stating their manner of being beings. Marion registers the aporia attaching to each side. The first acceptation refers to theo-logy, with the aporia that the being par excellence is identified with the ego and not with God. The second refers to onto-logy, with the aporia that what is stated is not, explicitly at least, a principle Descartes used, but a formulation of the principle of non-contradiction such as defines *on hē on* in Aristotle. At least it appears clearly that the duality of the concept of principle opens the possibility of seeking in Descartes an eventual duality of an onto-logy in tension with a theo-logy. The second argument bears on the third element of the compound: metaphysics constitutes itself as an onto-theo-*logy*, since the two approaches to the being depend on the *logos*, first because both say it, and then and above all because in saying it they found this being and reciprocally found their own discourses. The *logos* precedes the *on* and the *theion* in many ways. And has it not been stressed at will that for Descartes *cogitatio* precedes *ens in quantum ens*, precisely in order to found it according to the criteria of certainty? As a priori condition and as certain foundation alike, does *cogitatio* not fill the office of the *logos* of onto-theo-logy? The hypothesis cannot be excluded from the start, on condition of showing that *cogitatio* also determines and founds the thought of the being par excellence — that it determines God. The question becomes whether Cartesian thought can accede to an authentically metaphysical status on the hypothesis that metaphysics is conceptually defined as onto-theo-logy.

A third argument emerges from the same constitution, and it is the one that most directly implicates Descartes (pp. 95–97). Heidegger notes, as a sequel to the judgement that metaphysics thought more truly and clearly is an onto-theo-logy, that being can there be accomplished only as ground; and the ground requires not only a *causa prima* but that this deploy itself into an *ultima ratio*, until the two coincide in a single being par excellence, *causa sive ratio* of all the others. When this internal requirement of metaphysics comes to be accomplished in broad daylight, the being par excellence receives a name and a definition, *causa sui*: the being of beings, in the sense of ground, can fundamentally be represented only as *causa sui*, and this is the name of the metaphysical concept of God. Otherwise put, the conciliation shows and dispenses being as the ground that brings and presents, which ground itself requires a foundation to its own measure from what is founded by it, namely a causation by the most originary of things; that thing is the cause as *causa sui*; so is formulated the appropriate name for the God of philosophy — a God to whom man can neither pray nor offer sacrifice. Marion sets aside both the theological reach of the *causa sui* and its importance in the history of philosophy, and puts the unavoidable question: can Descartes fail to belong fully to metaphysics thus defined, when it was he who first forged and imposed the syntagm *causa sui*? The textual evidence needs stressing, because the attribution usually goes to Spinoza, who is neither the initiator nor the thinker of the notion. Not the initiator, as he indicates himself in first using the phrase — if a thing be in itself, or, as is commonly said, *causa sui*, then it will have to be understood through its essence alone. The reserve, *ut vulgo dicitur*, presupposes at least one precedent, and it is hard to see whom Spinoza would be thinking of first if not Descartes, whose theses he keeps taking up and discussing in the same treatise. Nor does Spinoza think the *causa sui* originally, since he confines himself either to identifying it with the ontological argument — by *causa sui* he understands that whose essence envelops existence, or whose nature cannot be conceived except as existing — or to extending it to the production of finite modes, since it is in the same sense in which God is said to be *causa sui* that he must also be said to be the cause of all other things. The remarkable absence of any real discussion of the coherence and range of a *causa sui* can be explained, short of charging Spinoza with theoretical carelessness, only if he supposes the notion already acquired and justified by others — in fact by Descartes in the First and Fourth Replies. The burden of introducing this apparently contradictory concept fell to Descartes, who himself acknowledged that it was too harsh, and the note records Arnauld's reaction to an efficient cause with respect to itself: it seems to him plainly hard, and false. If *causa sui* characterizes the accomplished concept of metaphysics understood as onto-theo-logy, how could the man who historically first introduced the syntagm into the lexicon of metaphysics fail to fall under it — or rather, fail to fix an essential figure of it?

Three motives therefore stand (p. 97): the nothing of ontology as the presupposition of *ontologia*, the onto-theo-logical constitution sketched by the duality of the concept of principle and by the priority of *cogitatio*, and the introduction of the *causa sui*. Marion cannot on their strength give up releasing the strictly metaphysical status of Cartesian thought, despite the denials and paradoxes Descartes' lexicon inflicts on earlier usage. But the attempt can no longer succeed by confining itself to a lexical inquiry; the metaphysical authenticity of Cartesian thought can be established only by a conceptual hermeneutic. Onto-theo-logical constitution is chosen as theoretical model and working hypothesis, and the question becomes whether Cartesian thought satisfies onto-theo-logy and, if it does, what figure of it it produces.

### § 8. The First Word on the Being of Beings: *cogitatio*

The search for a Cartesian ontology had broken down over the disqualification of every *novum ens* and in fact of every *ens* as such, in favour of whatever offers itself to knowledge under knowledge's laws and requirements; Marion resumes from exactly that point (pp. 97–100), and with one positive indication. Descartes once names the residue that is fully knowable an *ens notum*, and so anticipates what Clauberg will call *ens cogitabile* and what makes an *ontologia* possible.
