The Cartesian *ens notum* therefore does not exclude but rather permits a determination of *ens* as such in its universality — a *represented* universality. It is to be heard in the obvious sense of a known being, but above all in the exact sense that the known, as purely and solely known, remains a *being*, and that to remain as known amounts to remaining as being. For the epistemic reduction of every occurrence — *ea omnia quae occurrunt*, in the first title of the *Regulae* — to what can in the end be known of it repeats, while displacing it, the reduction that Aristotle's certain science operates when it leads the *on* back to itself insofar as it is, and nothing further. This is the point where everything turns. The Cartesian reduction of the world, brought to its reduced and conditional status of object, does not entirely desert the leading-back of the world to its status as being; it repeats that leading-back with a displacement — or rather the displacement, in its very violence, attempts to take up originarily the Aristotelian gesture from which every subsequent metaphysical ontology derives. When things can serve as objects for true thoughts, when the thing exhausts itself in the operation that makes it a *res repraesentata*, when the world must bow before the *objectum purae Matheseos* — which the note insists is the essential acquisition passing from the *Regulae* into the *Meditationes*, since the true and immutable natures the *Meditationes* recover are the simple natures the doubt had revoked — what is still and finally at stake is *ens in quantum ens*. To be known delivers a manner, precisely, of being; and the manner of being that leads the being back to its status as pure being is stated in the Cartesian inauguration as being in the mode of the *objectum*. Descartes declares it explicitly: however imperfect that mode of being by which a thing is objectively in the intellect through an idea, it is certainly not plainly nothing, and therefore cannot come from nothing (AT VII, 41, 26–29) — a passage the French translation for once develops excellently, and which recurs in the reply that this mode of being is far more imperfect than the one by which things exist outside the intellect, but is not for all that plainly nothing. Objective reality is by full right an *esse objectivum*, without which it would call for no cause at all; and Marion's note is careful to distinguish this phrase from the much commoner *realitas objectiva*, which precisely omits the intervention of *esse*. Hence, replying to Burman, Descartes can define the whole and universal object of *mathesis* and legitimately assimilate it to a *verum et reale ens*, in the very sense in which he grants physics an object that is a true and real being. Physics does not reach *esse* any more than *mathesis* does — for Descartes at least, and contrary to an Aristotelian conception — because for him *esse* is not defined in its relation to *physis* but uniquely and sufficiently according to objectivity. The consequence Marion draws is the sharp one: the *objectum purae Matheseos* remains a true *ens* not *despite* its distance from the object of physics, in the manner of a Scotist *ens diminutum*, but *because of* that distance. The more perfect purity of its objectivity qualifies it as a being in the first rank; all other objects, however less imperfect they may look to a reader, remain by right and in fact more imperfectly beings because they are less certainly objects. The proof is the route: to reach them one must first pass through the *objectum purae Matheseos*, since the mind knows itself better and before the body, and the mathematical essences of material things — their theoretical models in the code — before those things themselves. The object is, precisely as object.

In acceding to objectivity, then, every thing is led back to what it is insofar as it is; but this leading-back, which stands in for an ontology and may therefore be called a grey ontology, would be impossible without the intervention of another instance (pp. 100–103) — other than the thing, though not foreign to the manner of being the thing accedes to as object. For the thing does not simply become object but object of an understanding: the intellect can never be deceived by any experience if it intuits precisely and only the thing objected to it. *Res objecta* because *objecta intellectui*: objectivity implies the objectity that objects the thing to the understanding. In another lexicon, I always apprehend some thing as the subject of my thought — and the thing accedes to the objectivity that alone can establish it as a being in the strict measure in which it submits to the requirements of *cogitatio*. The *Regulae* remain perfectly at work in the *Meditationes*, and the grey ontology does not disappear into the white theology, which on the contrary presupposes it. But the second formulation marks a step forward, since the thing submits not merely to *cogitatio* in general, the *intellectus* of the *Regulae*, but to *meae cogitationis*. Marion refuses the easy reading of that possessive. It betrays no unfortunate precritical naiveté confusing an empirical subject with pure constitutive thought; the possessive defines the very essence of pure *cogitatio*, which as such implies it exactly as, as such, it implies its reflexive. *Cogitatio* means *cogitatio sui*, and if Descartes does not use that formula to the letter he does speak of the perception of myself, the idea of myself, the idea of oneself. On this Heidegger risked, as a developed formulation of *ego cogito*, the astonishing syntagm *cogito me cogitare*; and the essential, Marion says, is neither to reject it as inauthentic, which Heidegger knew first, nor to defend it as quasi-authentic on the strength of Descartes' *cum cogitem me videre* and *concipiam me esse rem cogitantem*. The essential lies in the most proper character of *cogitatio*: it returns to itself. It belongs to it to return to itself, to the point of cogitating itself first and of constituting itself into a being, because more originarily it turns back upon itself. Unlike thought as such, *cogitatio* does not reproduce what it cogitates nor represent it purely and simply; or if it does represent it, it does so by reflecting it as a converging mirror reflects, focusing its rays on a single point so as at once to render its object perfectly visible and to appropriate it, on the model of classical perspective. And Descartes does sometimes stress that *cogitatio* converts toward itself what it sees, calling the operation of the imagining mind, or of the mind turning itself toward those species, a *cogitatio*. The single point that *cogitatio* at once aims at and produces is called the object — in the sense first of the objective it aims at in turning toward it and finally turning it back toward itself. The concentration of the gaze that assures its object the high luminosity of rationality, by exposing it in full light, depends on the mirror's curvature; and if the objectivity of knowledge depends on the object, the object depends on the objectity of its bringing-to-evidence, which depends in turn on the curvature of cogitative thought. As a curvature of thought, *cogitatio* implies a reflecting appropriation whose ultimate implication has a name: ego. The ego is not added to *cogitatio* as an adjacent and possibly superfluous specification, too mixed with psychology or subjectivity to matter; the ego delivers the proper name of *cogitatio* by manifesting its reflecting and appropriating essence. No more than an *objectum* is without a *cogitatio* to secure its objectivity could a *cogitatio* secure the certainty of its object without a curvature named ego, since a *cogitatio* without the ego would fall back into simple thought, flat and powerless to produce an object. What is commonly called the Cartesian cogito — what Descartes rather calls the first principle, that our soul exists, or the first and most certain cognition, *ego cogito, ergo sum* — appears by right directly in the exercise of every *cogitatio*, and better still in the leading-back of each thing to the status of an *objectum*. The principle requires no new operation of thought, since it alone makes thought possible as a *cogitatio* operating the *objectum*. Grant the connection and the consequence follows. The being is insofar as it is object; the object becomes *ens* only as *cogitatum*, and with the *cogitatum* a manner of being is at stake; the *cogitatum* in turn implies the *ego cogitans*. The ego therefore intervenes within the sense of being that lets the *cogitatum* be as a being in its being. The thesis that every object as such, hence as *cogitatum*, *is*, implies in turn that the ego, before and more essentially than any other being and insofar as it cogitates, exists. The ego exists before and more certainly than any other being because, and only because, every being is only as *objectum* and so as *cogitatur*; and inversely it exists par excellence and in priority because, and only because, all other beings are only as objects of a *cogitatio*, only as *cogitata* — nothing can be known prior to the intellect, since the knowledge of all the rest depends on it and not conversely. Here Marion closes the figure. A statement about the manner of being of beings, an onto-logy, maintains with a proposition about the singular existence of a being par excellence, a theo-logy, a reciprocal relation of foundation: the existence of the ego supports the manner of being of the *cogitata*, and the manner of being manifested in the *cogitata*, revealing them as beings, founds the ego in its privileged existence. This double and crossed foundation satisfies to the letter the characters Heidegger discerned under the name of the onto-theo-logical constitution of metaphysics, and the nature of the logical moment can even be specified without difficulty: the *logos* is put to work here as *cogitatio*, curvature of thought; on the strength of the logical decision *cogitatio* accomplishes, the onto-logy envisages beings as such as *cogitata* and the theo-logy releases the being par excellence in the *cogitans*, the ego. Cartesian thought therefore belongs fully to metaphysics, at least if metaphysics admits an onto-theo-logical constitution.

Two objections stand against that conclusion on strictly Cartesian ground, and the first is a question of order (pp. 103–106). The analysis of the implication between object and ego seems to deduce the ego's existence from the very nature of objects, contradicting the fundamental order of the whole Cartesian procedure, in which only the minimal something certain and unshakeable permits recovering other beings afterwards; more, it reaches the ego's existence without passing through doubt and without expressly admitting that any new operation of thought, any particular reasoning, is required — whereas the existence of the ego demands a reasoning like no other, discursive without belonging to the syllogism. The second objection is that so reconstituted an onto-theo-logy identifies the being par excellence with the ego, hence with a finite being, and not with God; besides reducing to nothing a whole part of Descartes' explicitly theistic intention, it meets a massive textual contradiction, since the syntagm *summum ens* occurs often in the *Meditationes* and always designates God, never the ego. Marion answers the first by way of the piece of wax. The procedure that leads directly from objects back to the ego should not surprise, since what is deployed is beings as *cogitata* up to the *cogitans* whose existence alone renders them thought and so renders their being possible. In the wax analysis, apparently the thing is known more distinctly than the *cogitans*, which remains an undecided *nescio quid* of me that does not fall under imagination; in reality, and at the end of the analysis, it is the wax that is left indeterminate, something extended, flexible and changeable. The reversal comes from the reduction, as if by degrees, of the wax and so of every thing, first to the status of *objectum* and then indissolubly to the status of *cogitatum* — that which I now think — which results, beyond but also by way of sensible perception and imagination, from *solius mentis inspectio*. *Inspectio* stands here for *cogitatio* as *mens* stands for the *ego cogitans*. The analysis, like its parallels in the *Regulae* and the *Principia*, does indeed release the ego directly from the *cogitatum*, or rather from the interpretation of the *objectum* as a *cogitatum* — here of the wax as nothing but a something. The cogito is released from the analysis of the *objectum* as a piece of *cogitatio*, as *cogitatum quid*, as objective for an aim, that which I comprehend by the sole faculty of judging that is in my mind. And this strict implication of the cogito in every *cogitatum* in no way contradicts the specific moment that, just before the wax analysis, presents itself as a demonstration of the ego's existence — because the two texts run strictly parallel. Just as the wax analysis in fact analyses the wax as *cogitatum*, and then the *cogitatum* as more essentially presupposing a cogito to cogitate it, so the demonstration of the ego's existence remains indifferent to the occasional identity of the *cogitatum* apropos of which the *cogitans* discovers itself as ego. The demonstration does mobilize a statement, *ego sum, ego existo*, but that statement owes its privilege more to anticipating the result — what exists is named ego — than to any particular pertinence of its own. The proof is that every operation of *cogitatio*, doubting, understanding, affirming, denying, willing, not willing, imagining and sensing, and even every operation of the still doubtful body such as walking, gives access to the ego's existence, on a single condition: that the operation or action be led back, as *objectum*, to the status of *cogitatum*, so as to make the cogitating ego appear in it. If I walk, I am — but only insofar as the consciousness of walking is a thought. The sense of the cogitated statement therefore matters little; what matters is the analysis of that arbitrary statement as *cogitatum* and the reduction of the *cogitatum* to the *ego cogito*. The canonical texts of Meditation II accordingly do not make the ego's existence spring from the statement *ego sum, ego existo* but from the actually cogitative thinking of that statement; and to stress that the conquest of the ego depends on *cogitatio* at work and not on any particular *cogitatum*, they go so far as to introduce the cogitative performance itself, since the pronouncement is necessarily true each time it is uttered by me or conceived in my mind. The sense of the *cogitatum* decides nothing even here; the reduction of an indifferent and arbitrary *cogitatum* to *cogitatio* in living operation decides everything. Since the two *cogitata*, the piece of wax and *ego sum, ego existo*, differ only in sense, and since the essential is played out in the *cogitatio* exercised and manifested in them, the two analyses come to the same thing: both lead a *cogitatum* back to the single ego, the being par excellence whose primacy founds them as real and true objects.

The second difficulty is not answered but sharpened (pp. 106–110). In the onto-theo-logical constitution whose Cartesian figure is being released, the being par excellence is identified not as God but as the ego, under the reconstituted title of *cogitatio sui*; and the distortion with Descartes' personal conviction, with the constant use of *summum ens*, and with the role of the proofs of God's existence, might seem to disqualify the whole enterprise. Marion answers first that a distortion's being surprising does not suffice to disqualify it, and then declines to reduce or mask it, since it seems to him to fix one at least of the most decisive Cartesian initiatives; he will only specify it so that the real difficulty does not dissolve into misunderstandings. Three specifications follow. The first is that the theo-logy of metaphysics remains essentially a *theio*-logy, concerning the being par excellence, the divine, without prejudging the ontic region in which it would appear — mortals, demons and angels, gods, God. Ancient thought had illustrated the uncertainty sufficiently, and Descartes remembered it when he evokes ontic instances that would dominate or substitute for the Christians' God: on one side Styx and the fates, from which the creation of the eternal truths delivers God in 1630; on the other *fatum*, *casus* and the *continuata rerum series* which, before the evil genius, replace the hypothesis of an all-powerful God in the hyperbolic doubt of the first Meditation. In that limited sense a distortion between God and the supreme being would not be without precedent. The second specification proposes a substantive reason. If Descartes defines the being par excellence of *cogitatio* as the ego, perhaps it is on a fundamental ground concerning the divine essence: God is defined neither by the understanding nor by *cogitatio* but by incomprehensible power in 1630 and by *immensa et incomprehensibilis potentia* in 1641. God is not defined by *cogitatio*, or rather his possible dignity as being par excellence cannot be reached by an instance such as *cogitatio*, too little elevated, so to speak, to be able to state the infinite. God can of course be cogitated: the *idea Dei*, though incomprehensible, remains intelligible, and even offers the most true, most clear and most distinct idea. But precisely — God remains there a *cogitatum*, and like any object must already submit to the ego that cogitatively aims at him. The more one stresses that God falls to *cogitatio* as the *cogitatum* par excellence, which itself remains to be established, the more one confirms that he undergoes and does not exercise *cogitatio*, still less *cogitatio sui*. The objection returns in a stronger form: does Descartes not sometimes recognize in God a *cogitatio* that God exercises rather than undergoes? The principal texts are cited and weighed. *Principia* I, § 54 admits two clear and distinct notions, of created thinking substance and of corporeal substance, and infers, on the strict distinction that separates them, that we can also have a clear and distinct idea of uncreated and independent thinking substance, that is, of God. Despite appearances the inference does not make *cogitatio* essentially characteristic of God, since Descartes immediately adds a restriction — the idea holds provided we do not suppose that it exhibits adequately everything in God. Determining God as *substantia cogitans* is legitimate on condition of not being held adequate; and there is nothing surprising in this, since like the concept of substance the concept of *cogitatio* falls under the general warning of § 51, that the name of substance does not apply univocally to God and to creatures. Just as substance in God must be reinterpreted from the infinity that renders it at least non-univocal with finite substance, so *cogitatio* applied to God becomes inadequate to finite *cogitatio*. Marion's note works this through by the section titles, noting that univocity is confined to creatures, so that an attribute added to God's infinite substance necessarily undergoes equivocity with created substances — and he thanks Beyssade for drawing his attention to § 54, which gave him the occasion to strengthen his own analysis by discussing Beyssade's. One might therefore risk saying that in Cartesian terms *cogitatio*, precisely because it reflects itself in a curved mirror, could only be finite. Other texts do state a *cogitatio* common to God and the ego, to the infinite and the finite: in 1637, that one acquires a very clear and, if one dare say, intuitive knowledge of intellectual nature in general, whose idea considered without limitation is what represents God to us and, limited, is the idea of an angel or a human soul; and in 1641, that it is evident that the most perfect power of thinking which we understand to be in God is represented by the less perfect one in us. But even these texts work to exclude God from *cogitatio*. First because the continuity, of intellectual nature in general as of the *vis cogitandi*, is always obtained in a relation of finite to infinite, which therefore affects with equivocity everything it touches. Second — and this is the finer point — because both are attributed to God not so much that he may exercise them as to allow the ego to know him as a *cogitatum*: an idea that *represents God to us*, a power of thinking that is *represented*. *Cogitatio* is recognized in God only so as to let us make him our *cogitatum*, and not at all so that this *cogitatio* might impose itself on the ego, rob it of *cogitatio sui*, or define it as God's *objectum*. Finally, and above all, God is never here or elsewhere defined in the strict sense by *cogitatio*. The point is not to object that God too has *cogitationes*, which goes without saying, but to understand why God is never radically defined as *cogitans* to the point of assuming *cogitatio sui*, in the sense in which Descartes elsewhere maintains that God is his own being. *Cogitatio* concerns God eminently, but it does not define him as a principal attribute makes a finite substance known, nor name him as a privileged divine name would; it remains a perfection by which we represent him to ourselves, inadequately, according to the non-univocity the infinite imposes on the representations the finite forms of it. The third specification confirms a contrario that the choice was conscious, since Descartes describes precisely the route he refused to take. Replying that it presses no harder to call the idea of God in us an *ens rationis*, he distinguishes: this is not true in the sense in which by *ens rationis* is understood what is not, but only in the sense in which every operation of the intellect is an *ens rationis*, proceeding from reason, and in which even this whole world can be called an *ens rationis divinae*, a being created by a simple act of the divine mind. Descartes is taking up Suarez's positions, on which *ens rationis* can be understood as a nothingness excluded from metaphysics' object but also as an instrument of reason admitted by consideration of its analogy with *ens reale*, so that God may be said to know *entia rationis* most perfectly. Had the position been developed it would certainly have absorbed the distortion between God and the cogitating being par excellence — but at a price unacceptable to Descartes, a real univocity between cogitating knowledge, representative of an object, and the infinite. God would have become, through an understanding attributed to him and really distinct from a will, a simple *res cogitans* raised to perfection, immediately exposed to contradicting itself between a place of possibles and a power without reason. Malebranche and even Leibniz yielded to that facility; Descartes refused it from the doctrine of the creation of eternal truths onward. It should therefore not surprise that on Cartesian ground the *cogitatio sui* of a being par excellence falls not to God but to the ego alone. God does not fall short of *cogitatio sui*; he transgresses it, as the infinite transgresses the finite. *Cogitatio sui* offers too little to be able to designate God, and a finite *res cogitans* suffices to accomplish the focused gaze of objectity.

The distortion is therefore not to be blurred nor the difficulty circumvented, since both rest on solid reasons; what must be measured is their determining bearing for conceiving the essence of Cartesian metaphysics (pp. 110–111). From *cogitatio* a result has been obtained: *ens ut cogitatum* reveals an onto-logy of *cogitatio* applying to every being as such, hence as known and no longer as being, and the reflection of the gaze that exercises *cogitatio* then designates a being par excellence, functioning as *causa sui*, which the *ego cogito* alone can and must claim. Whence a complete figure of onto-theo-logy: the being as *cogitatum* founds the being par excellence, which in return produces as *cogitatio sui* every *cogitatum*. The logical moment unifies the two terms all the more easily as it coincides with the very *cogitatio* that manifests itself in both, now as object and now as reflexive gaze. So Descartes' first word on the being of beings is stated, an onto-theo-logical constitution according to *cogitatio*. Solidly confirmed as the result is, one difficulty survives it — the distortion between the being par excellence and God, which forbids Descartes to do conceptual justice to the God he never stops, sincerely and clearly, claiming to celebrate. He would not have accepted it had powerful conceptual motives not compelled him; and that from the standpoint of an onto-theo-logy of *cogitatio* God no longer coincides with the being par excellence, abandoning it by a strange retreat to the *mens humana*, indicates first of all an intrinsic limitation of *cogitatio* itself. This fundamental trait of the being lets at least one region of the being escape — not the least region but the first, God under the name of the infinite. The infinite, otherwise named incomprehensible power, overflows the onto-theo-logy of *cogitatio*; and the overflow, provided one does not hurry to reduce it, opens a new question. Could the first fundamental trait of the being be surpassed toward a still more fundamental one, and would passing beyond *cogitatio* toward a second and more powerful instance over every being lead to identifying the Cartesian figure of metaphysics still more radically?

### § 9. The Second Word on the Being of Beings: *causa*

The being also lets itself be said, and in fact more fundamentally, as such and without exception, from causality (pp. 111–116); and Marion undertakes to set out with all possible clarity the multiform and constant declaration which, without reserve from 1641, submits every existence, and so every being insofar as it is, to causality erected into onto-logical principle and reason. Three canonical texts carry it, drawn respectively from the First, Second and Fourth Replies and overlapping almost to the letter. The light of nature dictates that no thing exists of which it is not permitted to ask why it exists, or to inquire into its efficient cause, or, if it has none, to demand why it needs none (AT VII, 108, 18–22). No thing exists of which it cannot be asked what is the cause why it exists — this can be asked even of God himself, not because he needs any cause in order to exist, but because the very immensity of his nature is the cause or reason why he needs no cause to exist (164, 28–165, 3). And the consideration of the efficient cause is the first and chief means, not to say the only one, we have for proving God's existence, which we cannot pursue accurately unless we give our mind licence to inquire into the efficient causes of all things, even of God himself, since by what right would we except God before it has been proved that he exists (238, 11–18). Three common features stand out. The first is that the *causa* here governs not only the rigour of intelligibility but above all the proof of *existentia*; it does not confine itself to its already dominant epistemological function of knowing through causes but takes charge, sufficiently and exclusively, of all existence. And *existentia*, in Cartesian terms, marks the sense of the being of beings that is at once the sense par excellence and the one that goes without saying. Through the *causa*, then, the being plays for its very being and so appears *as* being: existing insofar as caused, the being manifests itself as being in the strict measure in which it appears in the light — or rather in the shadow — of the cause of its existence. More essentially than as *ens qua cogitatum*, the *ens* declares itself in its being *qua causatum*. The advance of *ens causatum* over *ens qua cogitatum* is marked precisely in this, that the one can remain within possibility or the *ens rationis*, which for Descartes nearly merge with the *nihil*, while the other alone opens access to existence, the royal sense of being. Here disappears the necessity, which the empire of *cogitatio* imposed, of reinterpreting with care and sometimes subtlety every *cogitatum* as an *ens cogitatum*, every object of cogitation as still and always a being taken in view as such under the mode of cogitation itself — which was grey ontology. Here causality manifests *ens in quantum ens* directly, since its explicit and unique stake is named *existentia*. The second feature is a second breakthrough: the *causa* decides the existence of *every* being without exception. Such is the dignity of existence among the other manners of being, or rather of not being, that no being can or should dispense with recognizing the *causae dignitas*; no more than a being could dispense with existing can a being dispense with the *causa*, whether to exercise it or rather to undergo it and in the same gesture receive from it its right to exist. Better: if extraordinarily a being could dispense with a cause, the reason for that would still have to be given. Whence an unavoidable consequence — God himself, if he exists and if his existence can and must be demonstrated, falls under the *causa*. God makes no exception to the word on *ens in quantum causatum*, whereas he did make an exception to the first word on the being of beings according to *cogitatio*; here he appears as a being of common right rather than outside common right. Marion characterizes the situation as a formal univocity, and defends the characterization against a foreseeable objection. That the causality invoked apropos God oscillates among several determinations — between strict efficient causality and formal causality, by way of a very strange concept common to efficient and formal cause, whose *intermedium* will play no more than a brief interlude — changes nothing of that formal univocity. First because Descartes, especially at the start of the debate with Caterus where he speaks with least reserve, imposes on God not merely undifferentiated causality but efficient causality in all its brutality, having said that it is not impossible for something to be the efficient cause of itself. Marion's note here answers Beyssade squarely: the remark that any affirmation of univocity in favour of efficient cause flagrantly contradicts the Cartesian text seems to Marion itself in flagrant contradiction with certain Cartesian texts. In a late and sometimes ambiguous polemic Descartes did markedly soften his initial positions, protesting that he never wrote that God should be called efficient cause of himself not only negatively but positively; but the fact remains that he did sometimes think God as efficient cause of himself, and the correctives that introduce the *causa sui* thought from efficiency often count less as retractions than as confirmations in the mode of prudence. Before Arnauld, Descartes still takes the consideration of the efficient cause as his principle and builds his new concept of *causa sui* by analogy with the efficient cause and it alone — by analogy to the efficient, having used the analogy of the efficient cause, bearing a great analogy with the efficient and so nameable a quasi-efficient cause. The causality that underlies the *causa sui* obviously does not coincide with the efficient causality usual among finite beings, but it is constituted by reference to efficiency and never definitively detaches from it. Above all, Marion adds, this first debate is unimportant whatever its outcome, since formal univocity concerns not efficient causality but causality as such: what matters is that God exists insofar as he satisfies the requirement of a *causa*, whatever it be, even reduced to a *ratio* that merely accounts for the absence of a *causa* in God. For even and above all in that case God is clearly submitted not only to the requirement of a *causa* in order to exist but to the requirement of a *causa sive ratio*: the immensity of his nature is the cause or reason why he needs no cause to exist, and where the word cause can in no way be understood of the efficient, but only in that God's inexhaustible power is the cause or reason why he needs no cause. Even if God has no efficient cause, and even dispenses with any cause other than the immensity of his own essence — positions Descartes does not clearly distinguish — he is no less submitted to the requirement of a *causa sive ratio*. And it is hard not to recognize in that formula what Leibniz will shortly call the Great principle, the principle of sufficient reason, which alone governs existence and whose formal univocity is imposed on God himself. God has no cause other than his own essence; but far from marking any weakening of the empire of the *causa*, the particular case gives Descartes occasion to extend it further by deepening the *causa* into *ratio*. Causality, and particularly efficient causality, thenceforth serves a more essential and universal rationality that takes charge of the manners of being of every being without exception. The two advances thus divide into two compatible directions, direct access to the question of the sense of being of beings, and recovery of the divine being, until then unreachable by the interpretation of *ens ut cogitatum*. The third feature concerns the status of the principle itself. The dignity of most fundamental trait of the being is recognized in the *causa sive ratio* as an imperious evidence — the light of nature dictates, it is manifest to all — and elsewhere as an axiom. Descartes can here only practise what Marion earlier called a postulation of evidence, since setting the *causa sive ratio* at the principle remains a principle only insofar as precisely no other cause can come to give reason for it. Only this unreason makes the cause of the *ratio* a principle; that every existence must give reason for its cause becomes a principle only by imposing itself without reason or cause. Marion accordingly keeps the Latin's secondary sense-effect and hears *dictat autem lumen naturae* as a *dictate* of reason, which dictates to *ens in quantum ens* that it will be only as caused: *ens ut causatum*.

The empire of the *causa* over the being oversteps that of the *cogitatio*, and the stages of that taking of a dominant position must now be specified (pp. 116–117), since only that itinerary makes the decisive bearing of Descartes' ultimate word on the being of beings really thinkable. At the first stage, in the *Regulae*, the *causa* already enjoys among all the absolute notions an uncommon privilege. According to Rule VI the *ordo rerum cognoscendarum* upsets, among other things and principally, the order issuing from the Aristotelian categories, since it dissociates the pair until then held strictly correlative, cause and effect: among the philosophers cause and effect are correlatives, but here, if we ask what the effect is like, the cause must first be known and not the reverse. If it is a matter of knowing — and in principle nothing else is ever at stake here — the correlation comes undone and the *causa* gains an indisputable priority, since it gives reason for its effect in rendering the effect knowable. The priority is very consciously instituted, *de industria*, on the model of a single other priority, that of equality over inequality; and Marion notes that the two together suffice to fix the two parameters of the *mathesis universalis*, order and measure, that is, to set working the only two essential operators of evidence in Cartesian science, the series and the equation. The *Meditationes* take the acquisition up in asking whence the effect could derive its reality if not from the cause, and so in setting the *causa dignitas* against the *effectus indignitas*. But a difference forbids forcing the assimilation: in 1627 the anteriority of the *causa* rests on the intention to know, whereas in 1641 that anteriority bears also on *realitas* and hence in fact on existence — a gap confirming that the directly ontic pertinence of the *causa* does not follow immediately. Why the gap, one may object, since already in the *Regulae* the *causa* precedes, as absolute term, all the other notions and simple natures? Marion's answer is that it precedes only other *notions*, and limits its priority to the domain defined and apportioned by a term still more absolute than any absolute notion — the understanding itself, which refers all things to itself in the sense in which they can be useful to our purpose, and comprehends in advance how each is subject to our mind's examination. The *causa* no longer depends on the *effectus*, but it depends all the better on the *mens* that secures it its unique and conditional priority. One term at least does not fall under the absolute cause, namely the *mens* that exercises it, or the *cogitatio* which alone comprehends all things in its *universitas*. The interpretation is verified again: the priority of the *causa* runs up against *cogitatio*, the only absolute term in the *Regulae*, and so subordinates itself to the onto-theo-logy of *ens qua cogitatum*, whose limits it, as their signal and industrious worker, precisely does not contest.

The *causa* can become principle and dictate of reason only by going beyond the very priority *cogitatio* concedes it (pp. 118–120) — to the point, in fact, of inverting the relation of comprehension between the two: no longer securing the priority of the *causa* through *cogitatio* and so under its aegis, but submitting *cogitatio* itself to the *causa*, possibly by means of *cogitatio* itself. Logically it will become possible and thinkable to submit God himself to the *causa sive ratio* only from the moment when *cogitatio* in all its dimensions, the *ego cogito* included, avows the *causa* as its *ultima ratio*. On the path of that reversal, the three letters to Mersenne of 1630 declaring the creation of the eternal truths mark a decisive stage, and one indication suffices. Asked in what genus of cause God disposed the eternal truths, Descartes answers that it is in the same genus of cause as that in which he created all things, that is, as efficient and total cause; for it is certain that he is as much author of the essence as of the existence of creatures, and this essence is nothing other than these eternal truths. That existences are created by God's efficient causality is banal. Nothing is less banal than the creation by efficient causality of the eternal essences of things — essences that include mathematical truths, logical principles, ethical values. Truths, thoughts, *cogitationes*, though imposing themselves on our understanding in necessary and unconditional evidence, remain created according to efficient causality. *Cogitatio*, in what it retains of most necessary, finds itself outflanked by the cause; and the cause, by an inevitable consequence, escapes *cogitatio*, which can henceforth apprehend it only as incomprehensible power. Independently of its overall significance for Cartesian thought, the flash of 1630 marks the first and definitive subversion of *cogitatio* by *causa*, and from it the sometimes subterranean work of the *Meditationes* becomes easier to trace. At a precise theoretical moment it is explicitly posited, as a principle without reason since it precisely gives reason universally for everything, that by the natural light it is manifest that there must be at least as much in the efficient and total cause as in the effect of that same cause. This is causality once more in its full splendour, total in the very measure in which it is efficient, exactly as in 1630 when it first subverted *cogitatio*. Why then does it intervene only in the middle of the third Meditation, if it is stated as suffering no exception — why does it not introduce its principle from the outset? The answer imposes itself: because at the origin stands *cogitatio*, whose proper office is to lead from doubt to the *ego cogito*. Causality, efficient in being total, must intervene only at the precise moment when the ego itself goes in quest of the foundation of its own cogitative existence; and to pass from the existence of a cogitative being to an absolutely founded existence, the ego must cease to define itself from the essence of *ens ut cogitatum* and appeal to a more essential word about the being in its being, *ens ut causatum*, whose formulation intervenes then and only then, as an unavoidable evidence. Posited as new principle and indeed as the second beginning of the *Meditationes*, the *causa* immediately and directly deploys its authority over what is to be surpassed, *cogitatio* itself. Whence the audacity Descartes is doubtless the first to risk: this is perspicuously true not only of those effects whose reality is actual or formal but also of ideas, in which only objective reality is considered. Every being is as *cogitatum*, the second Meditation had established; the third confirms it and completes it — every *cogitatum* depends on a *cogitatio*, which itself is only insofar as caused, as a *causatum*. More essential to the being than its status as *cogitatum* is its dignity as *causatum*, a dignity deeper than the *effectus indignitas* because directly produced by the *causa* as such. The redoubling leads first and exemplarily to the proof of God's existence said to proceed by the effects — more precisely, Marion suggests, it should be defined as a proof by the *causatum* status of the *idea Dei*, an idea I can in no way comprehend or perhaps even reach by thought.

The highest *cogitatio*, which delivers the idea of God, cannot deliver God's existence except by letting itself be taken up again by the efficient and total *causa*, *ut causatum* (pp. 120–121). If even this *cogitatio* can be interpreted as caused, so much the more can all the others, since they require only the ego, a finite substance, as their sufficient cause; no *cogitatio* survives that must not first be understood as caused and so included in the interpretation of the being as such *ut causatum*. Yet one last obstacle remains, and Marion states it fairly. That the *cogitationes* submit to causality is a considerable point but decides nothing about the cogito itself; could one not admit that so long as the *ego cogito* remains an autonomous and sufficient principle, its *cogitatio* escapes the empire of the *causa sive ratio* in its function if not in its products? Seductive as it looks, the fallback offers no security, because the ego itself is inscribed in a *causa* named God: God is the cause of me, I am his effect. The ego makes no exception to the universality of the divine creating cause, since the very idea of God as *summe potens* implies that both I myself and everything else is created by him; essences and the ego alike have God for cause, as much as existences do. And the argument can be pushed further: the *idea Dei* intervenes at least as an *ens causatum* with no possible cause other than God, and Descartes specifies that the resemblance of God contained in it is perceived by me through the same faculty by which I myself am perceived by me. The same faculty makes me perceive myself and perceive the *idea Dei*, and that faculty has a name, *cogitatio*. Marion proposes to read the ambivalence by reference to the duality of the words on the being of every being: *cogitatio* can either be understood according to *ens ut cogitatum* and deliver the ego, or — the same *cogitatio* — let itself be taken up according to *ens ut causatum* and issue in the existence of God. The divergence of the terminal points depends on nothing but the subversion of *cogitatio* by *causa*.

Heidegger announced, for the unpublished part of *Sein und Zeit*, a phenomenological destruction of the *cogito sum* (pp. 121–122), and Marion is willing to risk that Descartes himself accomplished that destruction. *Cogitatio*, as principal attribute of an *ego res cogitans*, can be led back from an onto-logy of *ens ut cogitatum*, of *cogitatio sui*, to an onto-logy of *ens ut causatum*; so that the critique of the absolute empire of cogitative representation, and of the transcendental egology it implies, in no way transgresses Cartesian thought but occupies a crucial moment within it. The destruction of the *ego cogito* was first Descartes' own task. But the nature of such a self-destruction can still be questioned, and Marion's verdict on it is severe. It has doubtless nothing phenomenological about it, since on the contrary it oversteps *cogitatio* in view of the *causa sive ratio*, hence in view of the principle of reason, in which the metaphysics of presence as subsistence is accomplished. The Cartesian destruction of the *cogito sum* therefore reinforces the metaphysics of presence rather than calling it into question, and against the phenomenological destruction projected but unaccomplished by *Sein und Zeit*, the third Meditation at its decisive point carries through only a *metaphysical* destruction of the *ego cogito* in favour of the *ego ut ens causatum*. The note attached here states Marion's own ambition for the book: that the whole of the present work might count as a contribution to such a duty of destroying, in the sense of rendering manifest a thought commensurate with the question of being which it misrecognizes at the very moment it answers it.

Having reduced every exception and resistance, the *causa* can now legitimately give itself as the most fundamental trait of the being (pp. 122–123), of the being taken in view as such, on the express formula of 1641: it seems known per se that everything which is, is either from a cause or from itself as from a cause. In that *omne id quod est* nothing can be read but the being universally taken as such, so that the sentence must be translated as saying that the being as such and universally is only by a cause, whether different from itself or identical with itself, no matter which. The *causa* becomes principle of an onto-logy strictly founded in reason: whatever there is will be founded in its being as caused. Why, then, if the founding in reason is universal, does Descartes add "or from itself as from a cause"? Before any factual and doctrinal explanation Marion refers the addition to the onto-theo-logical constitution of any thought that would constitute itself as metaphysics, in which the being of beings as such founds every being and, indissolubly, the being par excellence founds and supports the being of beings; the two maintain a relation of reciprocal foundation, though the modes of founding differ in each case. On that figure it becomes highly intelligible that Descartes should state twice the link uniting *omne id quod est* to *causa*, since the two formulas are not, despite the stylistic appearance, parallel. The first, which links *omne id quod est* to being from a cause, states an onto-logy that brings to light the manner of being of every being founded by it as of common right — in Cartesian terms, of every finite being and so *ab alio*. The second, which supports *omne id quod est* on being from itself as from a cause, concerns a single being, the only one that is *a se*, the being par excellence, and puts to work a returned rather than inverse foundation of all the other and finite beings by the excellence of a being of exception. The second formulation pronounces on the theo-logy that accomplishes the onto-theo-logy of the *causa*.

Proving it presents no insurmountable difficulty (pp. 123–126). By hypothesis, if the onto-theo-logy of the *causa* oversteps that of the *cogitatio*, the corresponding being par excellence must shift from the ego to God — more exactly, from the cogitative foundation of *ens ut cogitatum* to the causal foundation of *ens ut causatum*, and so from the *ego cogito* to a causing God who first causes himself as the ego first cogitates itself, *causa sui* after and on the model of *cogitatio sui*. Descartes not only undertakes this displacement explicitly in the third Meditation but accomplishes it in a text that first announces the hypothesis of a positive aseity — the text Caterus and then Arnauld will seize on to force him to publish the decision to name and define God as *causa sui*: if I were from myself I would neither doubt nor wish nor lack anything whatever, for I would have given myself all the perfections of which any idea is in me, and I would myself be God. The note is worth registering, since it settles the order of the debate: Caterus starts precisely from this text, Arnauld takes it up with express reference to Caterus' citation, and the discussion of the *causa sui* therefore opens with the hypothesis of the *ego's* positive aseity, at once refused by Descartes, and not with God's aseity. The *causa sui* is envisaged first for the ego's benefit — which suggests that, conceptually if not lexically, the formula *causa sui* is a tracing of *cogitatio sui* transposed into the order of the cause, so that *causa sui* imitates *cogitatio sui* and not the reverse; and one should not forget that in Descartes' sense *causa sui* counts as a neologism. Three features make the text astonishing. It seeks a being par excellence from the onto-logy of the *causa* whose hypothesis had been stated a few pages earlier and which implicitly contains the version "from itself as from a cause," since that formula appears in the First Replies precisely to comment on this text: the onto-logy of *ens ut causatum* here undertakes for the first time to be accomplished in a being par excellence. It is indeed a matter of the ego, since the thought of the *causa* intervenes only on the basis of what the thought of *cogitatio* has secured; just as, after accomplishing *cogitatio* in the ego, Descartes submits both to the more fundamental resumption by the *causa*, so he begins here by taking up the ultimate point at which the onto-theo-logy of *cogitatio* culminated — the ego, being par excellence as cogito. To that ego, held to be that being, he applies the onto-theo-logical hypothesis the second word on the being of beings imposes, testing whether the ego satisfies the *causa* par excellence; conceptually, the crucial question is whether the ego can conceive itself, and hence be, *a se* — understood positively, *tanquam a causa*. And the retreat of the onto-theo-logy of *cogitatio* before the onto-theo-logy of the *causa* is accomplished by the ego itself, which on examining itself finds it cannot satisfy causal excellence even while it can still illustrate cogitative excellence. The avowal of impotence — power in the strict sense, which by contrast will determine the true being par excellence as incomprehensible power — is spoken in the recognition that I doubt. Doubt can be understood in two ways with opposite consequences: as *cogitatio* it secures, despite the uncertainty of its modality, the certainty of the cogito and the ontic excellence of the ego; but considered as requiring a cause, the same *dubitatio* attests that the ego does not maintain a cause-to-effect relation with all its thoughts, and hence that in certain cases it does not fully exercise the efficient causality required even of ideas. In doubt the ego confirms its ontic primacy as *cogitatio* and invalidates any claim to ontic excellence as *causa*; it observes of itself that it is nothing other than a thinking thing, and the avowal of that reduction marks the ego's renunciation, at this second stage, of the accomplishment of the excellence of the being. Marion's note adds a chronological confirmation: it is in an indisputably late text that the existence of the ego is deduced from doubt, as though the cogito were repeated under the light of another principle, God reached from the cause; and these texts, set beside the *sum, ergo Deus est* of Rule XII, have allowed the shortcut *dubito, ergo Deus est* to be forged with some ground — which marks the decentring of the cogito as it passes under the jurisdiction of the second Cartesian word on *ens in quantum ens*. The onto-theo-logy of *ens ut causatum* will therefore be completed in God conceived as *causa sui*; and the audacity of this ultimate Cartesian name of God must not dissimulate a prior audacity. God must admit positive aseity, against Aquinas and his disciples, and must curve back on himself the imperative of a *causa sive ratio*, only insofar as in any case the being par excellence had to satisfy the *causa sui*, whatever that being might have turned out to be. Had the ego been able to hold the role of being par excellence, it too would have taken the name *causa sui* — for there is no other name, in heaven or on earth or in the underworld. God comes only to fill, after the ego's insufficiency, the function of *causa sui*, which imposes itself on him only insofar as it precedes him from the necessities proper to the onto-theo-logy issuing from the *causa*. The figure of the *causa sui*, model of every being par excellence founded on and founding *ens ut causatum*, is decided and drawn before the identity of the being that will subsequently render it effective is settled. Only in this sense can it be conceived that after 1641 the ego still tries to imitate tangentially what makes God a being par excellence: the imitation leads to reinterpreting morality and freedom as a way of performing independence within a limited field, by the perfect self-mastery free will secures the ego — for independence, distinctly conceived, contains infinity within itself. So Descartes' second word on the most fundamental trait of the being is accomplished: the being is as such insofar as caused, and this manner of being founds beings by deploying them as *causata* while indissolubly founding itself in a being par excellence marked as *causa sui*. The onto-theo-logical constitution deploys the being of beings according to cause, and so identifies the properly and definitively metaphysical dignity of Cartesian thought.

### § 10. A Redoubled Onto-theo-logy

The task is not finished, and the most considerable difficulty arrives here (p. 126) — one Marion has manufactured himself. Setting out to establish the strictly metaphysical character of Cartesian thought by recognizing in it a figure of the onto-theo-logical constitution of all metaphysics, he may have proved too much: not one figure of the constitution has been found but two, one according to *cogitatio* and one according to *causa*. What relation do they maintain, do they contradict one another, and should the proliferation not simply cast doubt on the operative rigour of the concept of onto-theo-logical constitution itself, and even on the imprudently univocal notion of *the* metaphysics?

The questions are not rhetorical and cannot receive definitive answers here, but they can at least be formulated more exactly (pp. 126–129), and Marion begins by conceding the historical point against himself. Heidegger introduces the thesis of an onto-theo-logical constitution only at the end of a Hegelian reflection, since a seminar on the *Science of Logic* in the winter of 1956–57 precedes the Todtnauberg lecture of February 1957, and the background should probably include the previous semester's Freiburg course on Leibniz and the principle of reason. Without prejudging a more precise textual examination — which only the *Gesamtausgabe* will make possible — Marion posits that the onto-theo-logical constitution imposes itself on Heidegger's thought from the two figures of metaphysics that put it most massively into evidence and into operation, Leibniz and Hegel. But just as one must admit an incubation period of the principle of reason, itself dependent on the incubation of Being, so one must admit an incubation period of the onto-theo-logical constitution — less a retroactive effect licensing a hermeneutic in which metaphysics reads as onto-theo-logy already with Plato and Aristotle, than a slow emergence, in often very complex figures, of what in the Leibnizian and Hegelian completions takes on the brilliance of a simple constitution because a definitively accomplished one. And Hegel himself was perfectly conscious of a decisive Cartesian filiation, since as early as 1807 he places the moment of absolute freedom under the aegis of the concept of Cartesian metaphysics, that in itself being and thought are the same. That equivalence, which goes so far as to require the Same between being and thought, characterizes not only Hegelian and Leibnizian onto-theo-logy but also, if thought is understood as *cogitatio*, the first figure of Cartesian onto-theo-logy. Far from the Hegelian inscription of onto-theo-logy forbidding a properly metaphysical approach to Cartesian thought, it rather leads back to it imperatively; and confirmation could as easily be found in Leibniz, whose great principle, alone permitting one to rise to metaphysics — that there is in nature a reason why something exists rather than nothing — refers literally if not expressly to what the Replies presented as the dictate of reason. Establishing a filiation between the two Cartesian figures and the later, more accomplished ones offers no insurmountable historical difficulty: the metaphysics of *ens ut cogitatum* points to Hegel, that of *ens ut causatum* to Leibniz, and the gaps, which remain massive, only indicate the road leading from Descartes to the later accomplishments of the destiny of metaphysics. The difficulty therefore transforms itself. It is no longer a question of determining *whether* the onto-theo-logical constitution operates pertinently for Cartesian thought and is not confined to Leibniz and Hegel, but of understanding what destiny affects the constitution of metaphysics with Descartes. Is the duality a contradiction, a competition, or an incoherence due to metaphysical unconsciousness? None of these answers will do, Marion says, since nothing in great thinkers sinks into non-thought — and the question should perhaps be turned round instead: can the complexity of the Cartesian figures be thematized in a single framework, and if so, what does Descartes teach us about the onto-theo-logical constitution itself? The reversal is the substantive claim of the section. The simple figure Heidegger thematizes apropos of Hegel and Leibniz might offer only one case of it, privileged certainly but neither normative nor unique; and the complication Descartes imposes on that simple figure might become the rule, or at least the most probable hypothesis, for examining the metaphysical character of philosophical thoughts in general. The Cartesian complexity would then make less of an exception to the primordial but elementary figure Heidegger released in Hegel than that elementary figure would offer the exceptional outline of a play elsewhere more complex and possibly infinitely varied, but no less onto-theo-logical. The Cartesian exception could in fact give the index of a rule: barring exception, the onto-theo-logical constitution takes on, in metaphysics, a complexity greater than that of the Hegelian completion. The hypothesis can be verified, and so falsified, only by work seeking the constitution in this or that thinker of metaphysics, or seeking the history of the concept of metaphysics itself; and Marion is content here to mark the inversion of the question, a laborious wait for the elements of an answer.

The framework in which the two Cartesian figures join can now be fixed (p. 129), and Marion's postulate is that they neither oppose nor contradict one another but *subordinate* themselves one to the other, in a manner of onto-theo-logy that is redoubled rather than split. He spatializes the schema into a figure, since as Descartes repeats the figure suits the imagination and the aid of imagination lets a question be perceived far more distinctly by the intellect. Along the upper line stand the three terms of the first constitution and the crown of the second: *ens ut cogitatum*, the *entia nota*; *ens ut cogitatio sui*, the ego cogito as first principle; and *ens ut causa sui*, the ultimate cause which will be God. An arrow rising from *cogitare : esse* reaches the first two; a wider arc, springing from *causare : esse* under the sign of the dictate of reason, passes beneath *ens ut causatum* and sweeps up to *ens ut causa sui*, enclosing within its curve the whole of the first constitution.

What the diagram shows is not a juxtaposition of two onto-theo-logical constitutions but the redoubling of a first constitution by a second (p. 130), each deploying separately but completely a thesis on *ens in quantum ens* — either *ens ut cogitatum* or *ens ut causatum* — and a thesis on the being par excellence — *cogitatio sui* and *causa sui* respectively. It is a redoubling and not an incoherent or conflictual splitting, because the whole of the first constitution finds itself, over and above its own articulation, taken up *inside* the second as a region of the being in general. The onto-theo-logical constitution imposed by the dictate of reason thinks as *ens causatum*, and so according to an ontology, the totality of the onto-theo-logical constitution deployed according to the first word on the being — which means both the *ens ut cogitatio sui*, the ego, its theology, and the *ens ut cogitatum*, its ontology. Descartes redoubles onto-theo-logy by reinterpreting the first acceptation of *esse*, *esse : cogitare*, through a second, *esse : causare*.

The redoubling strengthens rather than weakens the constitution that qualifies Cartesian thought as metaphysics (pp. 130–136), but if it is not to collapse into a banal contradiction it needs a series of clarifications, and Marion supplies them in order. The onto-theo-logy deployed from *cogitare*, in the conjoined play of *ens ut cogitatum* and a being par excellence *ut cogitatio sui*, is accomplished *before* the explicit enterprise of *prima philosophia* the *Meditationes* present, since the first figure is set in place already in the *Regulae*; and if the first two Meditations attach to it more closely, that is because they finally manage to formulate what the *Regulae* set working without thinking it explicitly, the reflection of *cogitatio* on itself which is finally attested in the ego. The sketches of the *ego cogito* in 1627 and 1637 should accordingly be neither overestimated, since the onto-theo-logy of *cogitatio* is not yet deployed there, nor underestimated: the structural homology between a *cogitatio* reflecting itself on an ego and a *mathesis universalis* implying a first *sapientia universalis* answers to a single requirement, that of founding the interpretation of *entia* as *cogitata* on a being, a being par excellence, itself conceived as a cogitant par excellence and so first of all of itself. Reciprocally, the second figure, deployed from *causare* in the conjoined play of *ens ut causatum* and the being par excellence *ut causa sui*, does not coincide with the area of the *Meditationes* either: it reaches its being par excellence expressly only with the First and Fourth Replies, and determines the being as *causatum* only from the third Meditation. A double lexical fact confirms the redoubling and finds its sense in it. *Substantia* never occurs in the order of reasons before the third Meditation, and precisely at AT VII, 40, 12; and by a chance that has all the marks of a barely dissimulated conceptual necessity, the same page carries the first theoretically significant occurrence of *causa* in the *Meditationes*, an occurrence lodged at the centre of nothing less than the first formulation of the dictate of reason, that by the natural light it is manifest that there must be at least as much in the efficient and total cause as in the effect of that same cause. The transition from one onto-theo-logy to the other is thus inscribed in the text of the *Meditationes* itself, and precisely at AT VII, 40, which appears as a decisive relaunching of the speculative impetus. More, this recently established double textual fact is superimposed on a conceptual difficulty long identified and discussed by the most authoritative critics — the possible break in continuity of the order of reasons, which alone would permit passing beyond the ego's solipsism to reach God's existence, a difficulty registered by Alquié, Gueroult and Halbfass. Like the textual fact, the conceptual difficulty is illuminated by the overlapping of the two onto-theo-logies at the centre of the third Meditation, and their redoubling finds there something like an experimental verification. Marion also uses that convergence to answer, in the note, the charge that his terms are less genuine Cartesian words than artefacts of the commentator. That the onto-theo-logical domain of *causare* does not coincide with the *Meditationes* lifts a further difficulty. One might object that any properly and purely ontological consideration abstracts the being by definition from every determination, and in particular from any determination of transcendence and creation, so that *ens creatum* excludes *ens in quantum ens* from the start — as Duns Scotus and Suarez among many others stressed. If *causare* appeared only within a first philosophy ordered to God and so became synonymous with *creare*, no ontology would remain possible. But *causare* precisely does not amount to *creare*, even while including it: the primacy of the *causa* is exercised between finite terms from the *Regulae* on, and supports the physics of the *Discours* and the *Essais*, with no reference to creation or to a transcendent causality. *Causa* determines the science of common being from the outset without thereby reducing it to an *ens creatum* — and the proof is the submission of the creator God himself to the requirement of causality, since *causa sui* attests, by the redoubling of the *causa*, that even the creator must submit to it. If God can be thought as *causa sui*, he could not, even for Descartes, be thought as *creator sui*; so *ens ut causatum* does not reduce to *ens creatum*. The most considerable difficulty comes from the status of the ego, which in this schema intervenes in two opposed acceptations: from the standpoint of *esse : cogitare* it fills the function of the being par excellence, while from the standpoint of *esse : causare* it falls under *ens in quantum ens*, *ut causatum*. That real duplicity resolves another textual difficulty. The *ego cogito* expressly receives the title of first principle several times — the truth that I think therefore I am, so firm and assured that the extravagant suppositions of the sceptics could not shake it, received without scruple as the first principle of the philosophy he was seeking; the first principle that our soul exists, since there is nothing whose existence is more notorious to us; the being or existence of this thought taken as the first principle from which the rest are very clearly deduced, namely that there is a God who is author of all that is in the world. How can this first principle, which subjects even God's existence to itself, elsewhere recognize itself as created, and so as an incomplete thing dependent on another, and admit that it does not deserve the title of *summum ens*? The distortion reveals no contradiction; it manifests only that the ego can be read from both Cartesian words on the being as such. From *cogitatio* the ego counts as a first principle precisely because it shows itself the most notorious, as a firm and assured truth, in short as the being or existence of a thought and so of a first *cogitatio* existing as *causa sui*. But from the *causa* introduced by the relaunching of the third Meditation, the same ego becomes an *ens causatum* of common right: the redoubling of onto-theo-logy imposes on it a second metaphysical site at the same time as it establishes God for the first time in the posture of being par excellence, of *summum ens*. The double status of the ego — a major difficulty for interpreters ancient and modern alike, and Marion cites Le Bossu on the tension between *Principia* I, §§ 7 and 24, and Merleau-Ponty's remark that the two senses of "nature" in Descartes sketch two ontologies — merely reflects, in exemplary fashion, the redoubling of the onto-theo-logical constitution of Cartesian metaphysics. The *causa sui*, finally, has sense only as the exact response to the requirement of a thought that seeks, following the dictate of reason, the cause of every being until one arrives at the ultimate cause, which will be God. That formula anticipates Leibniz more than Spinoza, who takes up the *causa sui* without questioning it and doubtless without thinking it radically; the principle of sufficient reason likewise seeks God under the figure of the ultimate cause, since the last reason of things must be in a necessary substance, and that is what we call God. Reason substitutes here for *causa* in designating the ultimate term; but besides the fact that Descartes had already posited the equivalence of *causa* and *ratio*, reason for Leibniz only accomplishes a sufficiency that cause still limits. The onto-theo-logy of the *causa* therefore signals toward Leibniz, by way of Spinoza and beyond him; and it is not imprudent to suggest that the onto-theo-logy of *cogitatio* opens the career of Malebranche, Locke and Berkeley, who think the being as cogito or *cogitatum* — *esse est percipi aut percipere* — and the supreme being as thought of self, whether they interpret God as Word or disqualify him as incomprehensible. The Cartesian redoubling might thus find a second quasi-experimental verification in the divergent prolongations it makes possible. Two last verifications are drawn from inside the Cartesian text. The *causa* can surpass the *cogitatio*: God could have created, and so instituted, truths entirely different from those established in the actual and definitive state of things, and in the discussion of the status of contradictories Descartes does envisage the possibility — impossible in fact, since not effectively realized — of another set of axioms. Conversely, the *cogitatio* can think what the *causa* cannot produce or let be produced: the ego can experience its freedom and in that sense think it, though it cannot comprehend its compatibility with God's omnipotence and omniscience; and far from reducing freedom to God's all-powerful causality, as Spinoza, Malebranche and Leibniz will strain to do and succeed in doing, Descartes maintains the two antagonists juxtaposed and irreducible. Why, in these two conflicts, does Descartes not attempt the reconciliation all his successors will obtain, willingly or not, while always criticizing him victoriously? Doubtless because in both cases the creation of the eternal truths is at stake; but above all because in that paradoxical thesis what is really at stake is the gap between the two onto-theo-logies — since the cogitable truths do here admit a cause, incomprehensible power.

Can the irreducible duality nevertheless be overcome, so that the rationality of *cogitatio* would merge with the empire of the *causa sive ratio* (p. 136)? Descartes' successors had fundamentally no other ambition than to establish it, and formally at least it looks possible to reduce one to the other. The formula *causa sive ratio* might itself be heard as the reconciliation of the two onto-theo-logies, since *cogitatio* has the function of rendering reason and so operates the *ratio*, while the efficient *causa sui* is recovered immediately in the *causa*. But the formal reconciliation does not efface the duality of the onto-theo-logies, which reappears in the gap, never abolished by Leibniz or Wolff, between the principle of sufficient reason and the principle of identity; and Kant, in consecrating the irreducibility of synthetic to analytic judgements, will confirm, perhaps without knowing it, the irreversible duality of the Cartesian onto-theo-logies. The metaphysical greatness of Descartes lies in having distinguished them and, above all, in having refused to confound them too easily afterwards.

With the exactly metaphysical dignity of Cartesian thought now secured, what remains is to confirm the play of the two onto-theo-logies by examining in turn the two privileged beings put into play in them, the ego and God (p. 136).

## III. Ego

### § 11. The "*cogito, sum*" as Protocol Statement

The institution of the ego as the cogito at the origin of all science does not confine it to a theory of knowledge however well assured (pp. 137–138): what functions for knowing is also the first principle of a metaphysics, and both the title of metaphysics and the concept of onto-theo-logy implied that dignity for the ego. But the dignity accrued to it there only from the architectonic requirements of an onto-theo-logical constitution applied to Cartesian theses, and such an architectonic organizes, not without constraint, statements that inevitably tend to overflow or complicate elementary schemas. Several questions must therefore be put. Does the ego play one role or several? Do all of them fall under an onto-theo-logy and so under a metaphysics? Can an acceptation be conceived that would except the ego from metaphysics? Marion's aside here matters for the book's last chapter: to overstep a metaphysics one must first accede to it. And the difficulty for the ego that exists insofar as it thinks is that it does not at first sight deserve the dignity of a metaphysical principle at all — as Descartes admits with great clarity. When Bourdin objects that *cogito, sum* issues in nothing but a well-worn commonplace familiar even to the vulgar, Descartes does not contest the banality of his own statement; he admits it with the less reserve because he justifies it by its consequences, which are less banal, since from this well-worn *cogito, sum* he demonstrated God's existence and much else besides. The banality of the premise does not disappear with the rarity of the conclusions; it is only justified by them. How then could Descartes have erected into a first principle and a metaphysical *fundamentum* what he himself acknowledges to be *tritum*? The elevation of *cogito, sum* from banality to the rank of principle is what attests the ego's possible metaphysical enthronement — and the texts confirm that Descartes saw the difficulty and worked long to overcome it.

The banality is established first by Augustine (pp. 138–142), who had formulated the point clearly and more than once, as Descartes was made aware from 1637. Among the texts proposed to him was the passage in which Augustine says he fears none of the Academics' arguments, since if he is deceived he is — *si fallor, sum* — for what is not cannot be deceived; and being certain that he is even if he is deceived, he cannot be deceived about his being. The similarities with the procedure of the second Meditation are beyond dispute, and agreement with an authority then immense could do Descartes no harm; he does in fact avail himself of it, glad to have met with Augustine if only to shut the mouths of the small minds who tried to quibble over the principle. Yet he never stops widening the gap between his argument and Augustine's reasoning. Is this mere authorial vanity, dissimulating sources and disowning predecessors? Marion argues it cannot be, on two grounds. Such an attitude would be pusillanimous, given how many real thinkers the Augustinian tradition had gathered around this argument without interruption down to the seventeenth century; and above all it would contradict an explicit declaration of intent, that — paradoxical as it may seem — there is nothing in the philosophy commonly called Peripatetic, insofar as it differs from others, that is not new, and nothing in his own that is not old, since among principles he admits only what has hitherto been common to all philosophies and is therefore of all things the most ancient, and shows what he deduces from them to have been already contained and implicit in them and so most ancient too, as implanted by nature in human minds. Aristotelian-inspired philosophy is the novelty, and a hazardous one, whereas Cartesian philosophy returns to natural evidence and so to principles of the highest antiquity — nothing is truer than the most ancient. Descartes therefore had good motives for invoking Augustine. Why did he not do so? His answer is that Augustine does not seem to put the point to the same use he does: what is at stake in the Cartesian *antiquissima* is establishing common notions, and *ego cogito sum* holds the rank of a principle. Descartes thinks rightly, Marion says, when he writes that he finds Augustine using it to prove the certainty of our being and then to show that there is in us some image of the Trinity. Augustine's whole procedure runs the other way. The reasoning aims to construct in man three terms recognizable *ad imaginem Dei* — to be, to know that one is, to love that one is; and the certainty of self accordingly leads the consciousness of the self back to the intimate consciousness of God as more essential to that consciousness than itself. The *si fallor, sum* does not aim at the ego and does not settle in a *res cogitans*, since the *interior intimo meo* deports it, as a derived image, toward the original exemplar. The moment of *si fallor, sum* remains the simple though first moment of an itinerary which, in two richer further moments, knowing one's being and loving it, dispossesses the mind of itself by the very gesture of reappropriating it to its original. It does not secure the mind its principle in itself, since it grants it neither being in itself nor being said through itself, as substance is; it forbids the mind to remain with itself, exiled from its truth, and refers it to the infinite original — the mind recovers itself only insofar as it exceeds itself. Augustine does not use the certainty of being that thought secures in order to erect the mind into a foundation, but to convince it that it bears the vestiges of a prior image which is the sole foundation and intimately other than itself: certainty refers the mind to a distant foundation, far from erecting it into a principle subsisting in itself. Descartes can therefore justly judge that Augustine's intention and his own are two very different things, since he aims, from the certainty of being that thought secures to what thereby becomes an ego, at making known that this *self* which thinks is an immaterial substance. What is at stake is not the connection, however certain, of thought with an existence — that the mind thinks and therefore is insofar as it thinks is an inference either banal or at least commonplace. What is proper to Descartes, as he lucidly indicates, is interpreting the certain and necessary connection of *cogitatio* with existence as the establishment of a substance, and of a substance that functions as first principle. As much as the connection of the two notions is so simple and natural in itself that anyone might have written it down, so much does its interpretation as the knowledge *omnium certissima et evidentissima* demand adventurous and decisive theoretical work. Pascal, often polemical against Descartes, is not mistaken about this, and Marion gives him the last word here.
