Pascal asks equitable persons whether the principle "I think, therefore I am" is really the same thing in Descartes' mind as in the mind of Augustine, who said the same thing twelve hundred years earlier; and he declares himself far from saying that Descartes is not its true author even if he learned it only from reading that great saint, since he knows the difference between writing a word at random without a longer and wider reflection on it and perceiving in that word an admirable sequence of consequences which proves the distinction of material and spiritual natures and makes of it a firm principle sustaining an entire physics. On Pascal's account Descartes' original contribution consists not in stating the proposition but in interpreting it as the discovery of a first principle — and Descartes will say, of a substance. The search for antecedents remains legitimate and fruitful but, for the essential stake, vain: what matters lies not in the statement but in the dignity of principle that metaphysical interpretation grants it, with Descartes, for the first time. Pascal marks the decisive distinction with an experimenter's methodological sureness — the protocol statement, which registers the intellectual effect *I think, therefore I am*, must not be confused with the theoretical interpretation that erects it into a first principle and a substance. The first belongs to experimentation, here of thought; the second to theory, and here to metaphysics.

The distinction governs all attempts to assign the cogito antecedents (pp. 142–147): approximations of the protocol statement are easy to find, but recovering the same interpretation is delicate and sometimes imprudent, and the protocol statement is occasionally seen to be *expressly refused* the metaphysical interpretation that would erect it into a first principle. The exemplary case is Aristotle. To demonstrate that a friend's life is as precious to us as our own, Aristotle mentions in passing, within a complex argument, the protocol statement that one who sees perceives that he sees, one who hears that he hears, one who walks that he walks, and in all other cases likewise there is something that perceives that we are acting, so that we perceive that we perceive and think that we think — and that this perceiving or thinking that we are is perceiving or thinking that we exist, since to be was to perceive or to think. To think that one thinks comes to thinking that one is, since to think one must be: Aristotle speaks here almost word for word as Descartes does. Why then does the Aristotelian protocol statement lead to no metaphysical interpretation of an ego? Marion first rules out the easy answer. There is no interdict on the self-reflection of thought, and its truth is expressly recognized, since the thinkable means that thought is relative to it while thought is not relative to that of which it is thought, just as sight is sight of something and not of that of which it is sight — though in a sense it is true to say so, *kaitoi alēthes touto eipein* — but is relative to colour. The vision of vision, which Descartes will record and interpret radically as *cogitatio* when he writes that when he sees, or rather, since he no longer distinguishes them, when he thinks that he sees, it is impossible that he himself thinking should not be something, is here neither illusory nor false. Aristotle neither ignores nor contests a thought of thought that leads reflexive and redoubled thought to think itself as being. The reason he does not proceed to the metaphysical interpretation of an ego is precisely metaphysical: thought, like sensation, falls under the category of relation, *pros ti*, without confirming one of that category's characteristic properties, correlation. Relative terms are in general relative reciprocally to one another; but if thought, like sensation, is relative to the things known and sensed, the converse does not hold — the known and the sensed precede knowledge and sensation, and better, they remain without them while they do not remain without the known and sensed. Knowledge in all its states can and must be set to work only relatively to prior things: most of the time we acquire the sciences of things that subsist beforehand; and that there should be neither sensibles nor sensations might perhaps be true, but that the *hypokeimena* which produce sensation should be without sensation is impossible. Thought cannot aim at itself any more than sensation can, because, not falling under the category of *ousia* but under that of non-reciprocal *pros ti*, it depends on other *pragmata* that are fully in act, in advance, as *hypokeimena*, and so in the mode of *ousia*. Thought can think only an *ousia* and therefore cannot think itself, since it remains a simple relation and *ousia* is defined, among other characters, by its absolute strangeness to relation: knowledge, sensation, opinion and reasoning always concern, as is manifestly evident, another term, and if they concern themselves it is by way of annexe, *en parergō*. The protocol statement from which the Cartesian interpretation will obtain a substance permits reaching, on the Aristotelian interpretation, only a *parergon* — an annexe, a by-the-way, an affair on the margin of the essential affair, a second-rank *ergon* beside the *energeia*. For the essential affair for Aristotle answers to the oldest and most often missed question, what the being is; and thought does not constitute an *ousia* but is constituted by the *ousia* it aims at. Consecrated to the inquiry into *ousia*, it has no business thinking itself, still less interpreting itself as an *ousia*. Descartes transgresses the Aristotelian interdict — not when he records a protocol statement Aristotle had already approached, but when he undertakes to interpret thought in it as an *ousia*. Aristotle and Descartes divide not over a protocol statement but solely over the dignity of *ousia* accorded to thought: does thought depend on an *ousia* other than itself, or does it accomplish itself of itself into an *ousia*? The decision, Marion insists, puts the general doctrine of all knowing at stake. Either knowing is governed according to the essence of the beings concerned in each case, so that each genus has its own science, precisely because in the relation between thought and the thing thought there is only one *ousia*, the thing's, so that thought can be only by borrowing from the thing; or knowing governs every being as its object, because thought keeps anteriority in being as in knowing, so that the thing can be only by borrowing from the *mens*, alone assured of its own entity. Before the same, or nearly the same, protocol statement, Descartes and Aristotle separate according to their properly metaphysical doctrines of the beingness of the being. The question of the *ego cogito* is not played out in identifying the fact of experience recorded in *cogito sum* but in the possibility of reading in it the emergence of an *ousia* — the same gap that separates Descartes from Augustine. And there is more, since the two antecedents converge against their common adversary. On the commonest Aristotelian definition of *ousia*, drawn from the *Categories*, it could not apply to the human mind without contradicting not only the Aristotelian interpretation of the protocol statement but also the Augustinian one. *Ousia* is defined by a double character, to remain in itself and in nothing else, and to be said only of itself and not of another term; it is determined by a perfect autarky in being and in predication, and to remain, which is its office and most proper trait, it need only stay in itself and by itself. Descartes, Aristotelian here by tradition, takes over the determination of *substantia* by the autarky of subsistence and defines it as a thing that so exists as to need no other thing in order to exist. From which it follows that interpreting *cogito, sum* as the emergence of a substantial ego requires thinking that ego as autarkic, and so excludes it from the structure of referral the Augustinian doctrine of the *imago Dei* demands. The only image a substantial ego could tolerate would come from the gaze it cogitatively directs on itself, so as then to lead *cogitatio sui* back to the originary and divine gaze reflected in it. Two occurrences of the *imago Dei* theme in the *Meditationes* are remarkable in this light. At the end of the third Meditation the theme is stated clearly and apparently in an Augustinian key, since from the sole fact that God created me it is very credible that I was made in some manner to his image and likeness. But by what and from what is the image of God constituted that so to speak absorbs the ego? The answer follows immediately: that likeness in which the idea of God is contained is perceived by me through the same faculty by which I myself am perceived by me. Which means that the idea of God, to which the *imago Dei* comes back, far from constituting the ego as its prior, incommensurable and unrepresentable horizon, is itself also constituted by the *cogitatio* which in *cogito sum* reflexively secures its autarkic existence. By a prodigious reversal the *imago Dei* follows *from* the ego, far from deporting it outside itself into God. At the end of the fourth Meditation the theme returns, since it is chiefly by reason of the will or freedom of choice that I understand myself to bear a certain image and likeness of God. The ego bears the image of God's likeness in a faculty, the will; does the will owe that privilege to a referral within it toward the corresponding perfection in God? On the contrary — it is because I already experience the will as so great in me that I apprehend the idea of no greater, and therefore because it reveals itself as already perfect in me, that I find in it the image of God. The will signifies the image of God in the ego not because it deports the ego into the infinite but because it already attests an infinite in act; it confirms the ego's autarky. In both cases the *imago Dei*, far from making the ego depend on God to the point of denying it the status of substance, serves to reinforce that status. Aristotle and Augustine, the one by refusing thought the status of *ousia*, the other by deporting it into the *imago Dei*, oppose Descartes in advance with a kind of common front. Yet they do not contest the protocol statement; they presuppose and interpret it. The opposition bears solely, and in advance, on its Cartesian interpretation as the emergence in the ego of a first principle.

Descartes establishes his originality, and more importantly his origin, only in interpreting *cogito, sum* as the first principle of the philosophy he was seeking (pp. 147–150). What is proper to principles is their multiform primacy, according to the being, according to time, and above all for Descartes according to knowledge, so that it is on them that the knowledge of other things depends, and they can be known without those things but not conversely. The ego can therefore be known, and then be, without the *ousia* of the things to be known and without God, that one substance whose image it is nonetheless supposed to bear; and the whole Cartesian interpretation of the protocol statement tends to this end. But how does one pass from *cogito, sum* to the ego as first principle and full substance? The difficulty of such a theoretical leap shows in the opposed interpretations of the same statement, and it shows equally in Descartes' own long hesitation. Having approached the protocol statement very early, he could not see at once the interpretation it could bear; he was late in recognizing the metaphysical dignity of *cogito, sum*, because that dignity depends not on an observation but on a hermeneutic. The *Discours* already wins the essentials of the ego's metaphysical position, if not the maximal extension of doubt, by conceding it the titles of first principle and substance — but it took him ten years to get there. The *Regulae*, from the winter of 1627, testify to a strange and instructive embarrassment: they never stop mobilizing existence, doubt and thought as so many simple and therefore primitive natures, without ever arriving at the decisive metaphysical statement. Marion reads four occurrences as three missed occasions. First, doubt does inaugurate a necessary connection, but far from issuing in the existence of the workman of that doubt it reaches only the certainty of the doubt itself: if Socrates says he doubts everything, it necessarily follows that he at least understands that he doubts; and the question concerned Socrates' ignorance, or rather his doubt, when Socrates first turned to it and began to inquire whether it was true that he doubted everything, and asserted this very thing. Thought enters a necessary connection with certainty without here reaching existence — a sterile ego of a literally inexistent cogito, in which Socrates folds thought back on the certainty of itself without transgressing the certainty of thought toward the certainty of existence. Second, a necessity can elsewhere issue in existence, but it draws its origin neither from thought nor from thought under the aspect of doubt, but from another existence already presupposed, thought functioning only as middle term between two existences: although from the fact that I am I certainly conclude that God is, it is not permitted to affirm from the fact that God is that I too exist. From the standpoint of the *Meditationes* that conclusion would be wholly erroneous, since if I think that God is I must also conclude that I am, because if I am not it is still I who think it; to think that I am not, and that God is, I must think, and so be as a thought thinking my own non-existence. But at the moment of the *Regulae* thought remains a mere route of access to existence without itself existing as thought — it lacks the status of substance. Third, existence and thought can nevertheless meet, and even jointly with *intuitus*, though the meeting never accomplishes itself in a *sum*, because the terms remain simply juxtaposed without logical structuring: anyone can intuit in his mind that he exists, that he thinks, that a triangle is bounded by three lines only. Marion finds the sequence remarkable on three counts. The elements of the future demonstration of the mind's irreducibility to extension appear there on exactly the same footing as extension, so that instead of distinguishing the substances the indifferent enumeration confounds them. The indistinctness of the enumerated terms requires no more than an unspecified gaze — *anyone* can intuit — which will still characterize the Augustinian formula in 1640, when the inference might have fallen from anyone's pen; the simple juxtaposition of a few simple natures, without disposition according to order, requires no particular speculative attention. And third, the juxtaposed terms might, it seems, have acceded to metaphysical formulation the more easily in that what will take the title of the ego already governs them explicitly: the *Regulae* do not merely register thought beside existence but *that he exists, that he thinks*, where the reflexive refers to the remarkable anteriority of intuiting in the mind. *Cogitatio* does not intervene here only as one simple nature among others; it already works as the essential reflexive operator in which the primacy of the ego over *cogitatio* as over existence is fixed. That anyone can intuit in his mind that he thinks confirms in advance the famous and wrongly contested formalization of *cogito, sum* as, at bottom, a *cogito me cogitare*: to the letter, *cogitatio* as thinking thought first thinks thought thought, in a reflex arc whose reflection designates exactly the ego. By right, however, the arc of reflection does not close, since it does not bind existence to *cogitatio* in the nodal singularity of an ego; the ego does not conclude the sketch of an inference because it has not of itself instituted itself at its origin — it lacks the status of principle. The two criteria that in Descartes' own eyes distinguish his enterprise from Augustine's and Aristotle's, that *cogito, sum* qualifies the ego as principle and as substance, thus also discriminate stages within Cartesian thought, or non-thought, of the ego. The debates over the evolution of doubt and its scope between 1637 and 1641, and over its various logical formalizations, important as they are, must not mask an incomparably more essential caesura: between the epistemic use of the simple natures of *cogitatio*, *intuitus* and *existentia* without fundamental coordination, because without fixation by and for the ego, in the *Regulae*, and on the other hand the conquest, by the conjunction of existence with the reflexive arc of *cogitatio*, of the ego as absolutely autarkic substance and radically first principle in 1637 and 1641. The caesura reveals a gulf all the deeper, and so all the more dissimulated from the critic, in that it separates not only Descartes from his predecessors but Descartes from Descartes.

The ego's accession to an authentic metaphysical status therefore does not depend on locating this or that protocol statement (pp. 150–151) but on an interpretation itself metaphysical, whose terms the debate among Aristotle, Augustine and Descartes allows to be sketched: does the ego, as thinking, accede to the rank of substance or to that of first principle, both determinations converging in a single autarky? The gap Pascal marks so clearly, between protocol statement and interpretation, between the *hoc tritum* and a metaphysical institution, can it be crossed, and if so how is the interpretation to be legitimated? Marion subdivides the question in two. What criterion can regulate the interpretation of the protocol statement? Augustine adopts a trinitarian and so theological criterion, which Descartes sufficiently marks that he refuses by invoking a substance for the ego. Does he thereby rejoin Aristotle, who interprets the protocol statement from *ousia*? He does not, since *ousia* remains for Aristotle forbidden to the mind, which relates to it without acceding to it. It remains that the ego decides its identity, for Descartes, according to *ousia* understood as substance — which would imply that its metaphysical status is played out on the possible or impossible equivalence between ego and substantiality, mediated by *cogitatio*. Can that equivalence, so far presumed, be performed; can we effectively produce, from its guiding thread, the event the protocol statement records, and if so by what right can we privilege that singular interpretation? The second question bears on the ego's completeness. The protocol statement uses two verbs and implies a single subject, and the metaphysical interpretation is to give that ego a purely metaphysical status. But does it accede to one, and if it does, does it accede *totally*? Which means not only whether the ego can become wholly metaphysical, but whether the one who thinks and exists under the face of the ego, a given man, is exhausted in such a metaphysical persona. Between the individual, who is nothing but human, and the ego, which aims to be nothing but metaphysical, is a perfect coincidence to be admitted? Both questions designate a single point: the transition from thought to the being, as manifestation of the proper beingness of that being which is only in thinking, and which therefore, in thinking, discovers a manner of being of the being.

### § 12. The Indeterminate Equivalence of Being and Thought

The metaphysical interpretation that shows the ego to have the rank of substance and first principle does not go without saying (pp. 152–153), and Marion lists the reasons for doubt before defending it. Descartes doubtless never produced that demonstration, either because it was too difficult — it presupposes a concept of metaphysics, of its essence and its limits, that a metaphysics cannot reach — or because it seemed too evident, since it goes without saying that any first certainty would have metaphysical validity. But the demonstration is missing above all because Descartes' metaphysical intention in general remains open to challenge: even if the titulature and the constitution attest a metaphysical enterprise, the status of the ego of *cogito sum* remains liable to be withdrawn from metaphysics in favour of an epistemic or a theological function. More, the protocol statement might never reach the rank of a principle and might record only an empirical assertion, which is what Kant reduces it to in speaking of Descartes' problematic idealism, which declares indubitable only a single empirical assertion, *I am*. If the ego secured only its own existence, empirically or even a priori, it would in no way attain the dignity of a principle, whose function is to determine other existences made possible by its own. And if it secured only its existence it would leave its essence indeterminate — the reproach Heidegger makes, that with the *cogito sum* Descartes claimed to establish a new and secure ground for philosophy but left undetermined in that radical beginning the manner of being of the *res cogitans*, more exactly the sense of being of the *sum*. If the ego does not attain an essence it cannot posit itself as substance and so cannot exhibit the ontology a metaphysics requires. For the ego to reach a metaphysical status it must satisfy requirements so strict that its ever doing so looks problematic; and if Kant and Heidegger have refused it, is there any reason still to plead its cause?

Marion's answer turns the objections into evidence (pp. 153–156). The ego could not *fail* in its metaphysical functions unless those functions characterized it as so many intrinsic requirements; it could not fall short of metaphysics if it did not by right already belong to it — which is why Kant and Heidegger stigmatize its insufficiencies still from the standpoint of metaphysics. As for stating positively the metaphysical status of the ego, that task falls to the thinkers of metaphysics, since they alone accede sufficiently to the essence of metaphysics to recognize authentic figures of it in their predecessors. And metaphysicians have not hesitated to recognize a metaphysical status in the ego by releasing the significance of its protocol statement: *cogito sum* records the experience of the equivalence between being and thought in a *subjectum*, the ego. Three philosophers otherwise separated on essentials agree here. Hegel, from the *Phenomenology* onward, fixes the terms of his constant interpretation while overcoming in the Enlightenment the conflict between materialism and idealism: the two parties have not arrived at the concept of Cartesian metaphysics, namely that being and thought are in themselves the same; that to the thought that being, pure being, is not a concrete actuality but a pure abstraction, and reciprocally that pure thought, self-equality or essence, is partly the negative of self-consciousness and thereby also being, and partly, as immediate simplicity, nothing other than being; thought is thinghood, or thinghood is thought. In the long exposition the *Lectures on the History of Philosophy* devote to him twenty years later, Hegel recovers the same interpretation to illustrate exemplarily the thesis that metaphysics is the tendency toward substance: the determination of being is in my I, and this conjunction is itself the first term; thought as being and being as thought, such is my certainty, I — this is the famous *cogito, ergo sum*. The protocol is to be interpreted as recording, on a particular fact, the experience of the universal principle of the unity of being and thought, a principle whose consideration is from now on that of the metaphysics of Descartes. Cartesian thought accedes to metaphysics when *cogito, sum* reveals the identity of being and thought. Schelling agrees at least on this point, holding that Descartes wanted to find a point where thought, representation — for he does not distinguish them — and being coincide immediately with one another, and believed he had found it in the *cogito ergo sum*, in which he thought he had recognized the immediate identity of thought and being. As for Hegel so for Schelling, the fundamental principle of all metaphysical modernity will find its Cartesian accomplishment only in the ontological argument, where both terms play with the index of the absolute; yet the fundamental principle is released already in the protocol statement of the ego, which thereby accedes to metaphysical rank. Feuerbach, a few years later and in a quite different context, takes up a very close interpretation. In 1833, putting the concept of spirit in the first rank, he posits that the essence of spirit is consciousness, that spirit is nothing but consciousness, the *cogito ergo sum*, that is, the immediate unity of my thought and my being, my essence as spirit, or my essence, and through thought, which is at the same time immediacy, my certainty of myself, the immediate affirmation of myself. The Erlangen lectures of 1835–36 deepen it: I think — for to distinguish is thinking, and thought is my essence since I cannot abstract from it without ceasing to be — I am spirit, and this being-spirit is my indubitably certain being; the indubitable reality of spirit, not as a statement or a dogma but as an effective act, that act of thought by which I differ from everything sensible and grasp myself in that difference and am certain of myself and conscious of myself, is *the principle of philosophy*. Metaphysics comes into possession of its principle only from the moment when thought is accomplished immediately as being; and the interpretation remains valid and shared with Schelling and Hegel even though it no longer rests on a system of identity or a science of absolute spirit. What is at stake, then, is the principle presupposed by *all* metaphysics in its modernity. Nietzsche gives the confirmation a contrario by deconstructing the interpretation, which in his eyes does not found the ego's existence but presupposes it: it is thought, therefore there is something thinking — from which Descartes' argument unrolls; but this amounts to beginning by positing as true a priori our belief in the concept of substance, and that when it is thought something must be given "which thinks" is simply a formulation of our grammatical habit, which posits an agent for every act. A logico-metaphysical postulate is established from the outset, and one does not merely observe; on Descartes' road one arrives not at something absolutely certain but only at the fact of a very strong belief. Marion finds the critique admirably pertinent and takes it as confirming his own interrogation on three counts. It shows that *cogito, sum* remains a simple protocol statement, affirming no thesis and stating no principle. It marks that the passage to the concept of substance demands an interpretation, here qualified — and this is no small thing for Nietzsche — as a grammatical habit. And it reveals that the Cartesian interpretation mobilizes nothing less than the concept of substance, which by a remarkable coincidence amounts to a metaphysical postulate. Nietzsche thus confirms the terms of the debate at the very moment he refuses its most widely received solution: the ego accedes to the metaphysical status of a principle in the exact measure in which the protocol statement is interpreted as an identification of thought with being, or as a deduction of substance by thought, or a leading-back of substance to thought. Marion is careful to say what "substance" does not mean here. It makes no allusion to the classical debate over Descartes' substantification of the subject, in which substance falls under a trivial interpretation of *ousia* as *hypokeimenon*, substratum, suppositum and ultimately matter; here substance is heard as *ousia* in the sense of *Wesen*, *Seiendheit*, the beingness of the being. The question is therefore how, from thought at work under the figure of the ego in *cogito sum*, a doctrine of substance can be formulated — how the ego can constitute an ontology.

The ego affirms its pretensions to be from the outset if one takes literally the formula that in the *Meditationes* replaces the usual protocol statement, *ego sum, ego existo* (pp. 156–160), and Marion finds it surprising for three reasons of increasing importance. Being is understood there immediately as existence. The ego is established in immediate relation with being so understood, with no apparent middle term. And, unlike the usual *cogito, sum*, the third instance of *cogitatio* is missing, in an exception proper to the *Meditationes* alone. From these three characteristics a single conclusion seems to follow, that the ego here constitutes an ontology immediately, without the help of *cogitatio*. Natural as it looks, the conclusion leads to a misreading, and to see why the three characteristics must be taken in reverse order. It seems that in *ego sum, ego existo* no mediation of *cogitatio* intervenes; in fact the reverse is true, and thought plays a more essential role in the statement that does not mention it than in the one that did. In the first case thought is registered *within* the statement and so represented as a thought thought, a *cogitatio cogitata*: I think the protocol statement *cogito sum*, which by including thought among the things it records transforms it into a thought object on the same footing as the others, *sum*, *ergo*, even *ego*. Paradoxically, mentioning thought in the protocol devalues it; but mentioning it is possible only within a protocol; so to avoid devaluing it, it must be excluded. The aporia converts at once into an opening: if thought is not to be mentioned as a thought thought, it must be *exercised* in act as a thinking thought, *cogitatio cogitans*. And the ego does nothing else, since its thought, in the act of uttering the performative statement, sets itself to work in thinking — and in the very act of thinking the statement that excludes it, thought accomplishes itself by accomplishing its own existence. Thought, when it thinks in fact and so redoubles its thoughts without including itself in them, attests to itself that it is: it cannot be that when I see, or since I no longer distinguish them, when I think that I see, I myself thinking should not be something. *Ego ipse cogitans* issues in *aliquid sum*: I myself am something insofar as thinking. And the *ego cogitans* must not be understood as a subject otherwise endowed with the property of thinking, a thing per se apt to think, but as a thing whose *esse* is accomplished and exhausted in the act of thinking; *ego cogitans* is to be heard verbally, in the present and hence active participle of the frequentative *cogitare* — the ego in the act of thinking, and only in that measure existing. The ego touches existence only through the mediation of *cogitatio*, which alone cannot be torn from it, but a *cogitatio* outside the representation of ego and existence, since it is what secures that representation. *Cogitatio* does not reach existence as an ego by thinking *itself*, but by thinking the very act that it thinks, and only then by thinking that this act implies a subject, an ego, which is. Second, it seems that in *ego sum, ego existo* the ego enters into immediate relation with being understood as existence, with *cogitatio* securing no mediation whatever; and again the reverse is true, since *cogitatio* mediates the relation because it secures it *under condition*. The ego's existence attests itself conditioned first according to time, since the ego is only as long as, and as often as, it actually thinks — I exist while I think — and negatively the same limitation suspends the ego's existence as soon as it ceases to think, since if I ceased from all thought I should straightway cease to be entirely. Time, ceaselessly to be reconquered as presence, renders the ego contingent through and through and obliges it to seize at each instant, by thought, an existence itself strictly instantaneous. The ego as thinking thought does secure an existence, but an existence that time has undermined, fragmented and reduced to the point of rendering it contingent and lapsing. Existing under the temporal condition of thought, the ego exists only *as* thinking thought: it accedes to existence not only *by* thought but *as* thought and nothing else, and its sole title to exist consists in thought. If thought alone cannot be torn from the ego, the ego is by thought and better, it is *as* thought — not that it is in thought, as an *ens rationis*, but that it is through thought to the point of identifying itself with its mode of being, the thinking nature in me, or rather which I myself am. Outside thought in act there is no being for the ego, so that *cogitatio* offers not merely a provisional mediation between the ego and existence but constitutes the definitive horizon of all existence accessible to the ego. Third, it might seem that being is straightaway equivalent to existence; and lexically it is so, here for the ego and elsewhere for God, who is or exists. But so clear an equivalence does not indicate that the sense of "being" is sufficiently determined — on the contrary, the indetermination of "being" reaches a peak here. First because the mere fact of equating, without justification or precaution, a notion as narrow as *existentia* with a concept as imprecise and enigmatic as being reveals a deep unconsciousness of the very question of the sense of being. Then because the indetermination is attested by the question the ego, now a being as existent, immediately raises: I am a true thing and truly existing, but what sort of thing? Taken literally, the question comes to inquiring after the *ti estin*, the *quid sit*, having answered the *ei estin*, the *an sit*. But by way of a banal questionnaire it asks what *res* can mean when it can be reached only within the limits of simple cogitative thought. The interrogation bears on the essence of the *res* whose existence has just resulted from *cogitatio*, but also and indissolubly on the sense that "being" or "existing" can still have now that they open only through *cogitatio*. One will object to so radical a reading that the answer comes without delay — what sort of thing? I have said, a thinking one. But the answer reinforces the question rather than annulling it, since determining the *res* as *cogitans* redoubles the enigma of the sense of its being and its existence. If it is only insofar as thinking, qualifying it as thinking adds no determination to its mode of existence and casts no light on its sense of being; more, qualifying the *res* as *cogitans* amounts to redoubling the indetermination of the cogitative *an sit* by the indetermination of the cogitative *quid sit*, which raises the original indetermination to a power. What does "being" signify when being opens only through, from and within the horizon of *cogitatio*? The watchword *esse est cogitari aut cogitare* resolves nothing but states the enigma — doubtless without even hearing it as a question.

The equation of being and thought that Hegel, Schelling and Feuerbach attribute to Descartes, and that Nietzsche's critique confirms, therefore cannot be verified so long as one stays with the interpretation of the protocol statement alone (p. 160). What is called the cogito finds its metaphysical interpretation, as equivalence of being and thought, only with the intervention of a third term that mediates it — a term that has never stopped playing its part, though in the background, and must now be brought to the front: the ego. The sense of being required by an authentically metaphysical interpretation of *cogito, sum* is determined only from the ego. The ego gives itself as a being — I am something, I am the one who — but as a being that interrogates itself about what it is, asking who is this I that I know. It interrogates only its own manner of being; but since that manner determines by definition every other being, it does not seem unthinkable that through it the sense of being in general should become accessible.

### § 13. The Egological Deduction of Substance

It falls to the ego to mediate the equivalence of thought and being metaphysically (pp. 161–164), in that it thinks on one side and is on the other, but above all because it introduces a third instance: itself, no longer as an indeterminate *res cogitans* but as a substance remaining in itself and said by itself. What was vaguely identified in the second Meditation as *ego aliquid sum* is stated exactly in the third as *ego autem substantia*. The late intervention of the concept of substance in the order of reasons marks a decisive stage a second time; the first time it relaunched the order of reasons as a kind of second beginning and helped effect the transit from one figure of onto-theo-logy to the other. But beside that ontic stake there is a second that may be called ontological. If the protocol statement must be interpreted from the equivalence of thought with being, and if that equivalence remains indeterminate so long as the mediating ego is not specified as a substance, then with *ego autem substantia* what is at stake is the equivalence in general of thought and being, and so the sense of being according to Descartes. The surging of *substantia* puts into play not merely the ontic status of the ego but the general sense of Cartesian onto-logy. Such an identification of the stake can be confirmed only if the Cartesian concept of substance can be shown to be equivalent, by privilege and by principle, to the ego; only on that condition can substance take over the mediation the ego secures between thought and being. And substance can belong intrinsically to the ego only by coming *from* it: the ontological pertinence of the concept implies that it be deduced from the ego and from it alone. Before that thesis is proved, Marion notes that two authorities confirm it. Heidegger posits that Leibniz, like Descartes before him, sees in the I, in the *ego cogito*, the dimension from which all metaphysical concepts must be drawn; and if Descartes remains, as regards the ontological elaboration of the problem of substance, far behind scholasticism, to the point of leaving unexposed the sense of being contained in the idea of substantiality, that is because more fundamentally he left the *sum* entirely unexposed. Descartes would thus have transferred onto substance as such the ontological indetermination of the sense of being of the *sum*, and so of the ego, thereby avowing that he thinks substance from the ego. Nietzsche, anticipating Heidegger on this point, states it more flatly: the concept of substance is a consequence of the concept of subject, not the reverse. Marion's use of the pair is exact. The over-classical debate on Descartes' substantialization of thought suffers from a radical indetermination, since the concept of substance might well borrow all its characteristics from the ego, which, before its own substantialization as *substantia cogitans*, governs from top to bottom the substantiality of *all* substances and imposes on them its own manner of being. Before the ego is substantified, substance must be deduced from the ego. The Cartesian text confirms both readings. The first occurrence of *substantia* does intervene in the third Meditation to construct a proof of God's existence, but it mentions first *finitae substantiae*; and *substantia* counts among the simple natures at the ego's disposal, like figure, number and duration. On that count substance falls among the notions the ego seems to have been able to borrow from the idea of itself: substance emerges as a loan the ego makes to itself, since because I am a substance I can, despite the difference of attributes — thought in my case, extension in the other's — and in the name of a *ratio* intelligible to me because deduced from me, *transfer* that substance onto other things. The transfer attests that finite substance is borrowed first from the ego as first substance, for the knowledge and recognition of the other finite substances. One should not object that God alone offers the substance par excellence, being infinite, and so ought to constitute its origin. For while the ontic argument concludes to the independent existence of the infinite substance by observing that a finite substance could not produce an infinite objective reality, it implies just as clearly that the ego remains *ontologically* the origin of all substantiality. The proof supposes, far from excluding, that the substantiality of every substance is measured and deduced from the ego: it is precisely the exception to that principle that constrains the ego to recognize, so to speak against the ontological tendency, the existence of a substance which, instead of being included in the *ratio substantiae*, is in fact understood from the infinite. The a posteriori proof does issue in the ontic primacy of an infinite substance, but that substance imposes itself only by contradicting the deduction of the notion of substance from the ego, and so as an exception to the ontological characteristic of substantiality, its borrowing from the ego. The ontic independence of infinite substance from finite substance presupposes, and therefore confirms, the ontological deduction of substance from the ego. Which is why substance, as soon as it becomes infinite, loses all common definition with all other substances, since it no longer falls univocally under the *ratio substantiae* deduced from the ego — whence the irremediable equivocity affecting every definition of substance in general, since the name of substance does not apply univocally to God and to finite things, no signification of that name being distinctly intelligible that is common to God and creatures. The substantiality of the world is deduced and transferred from the ego, whereas in God it is understood from the infinite, where it is at once abolished and accomplished. The world's substances therefore owe their qualification and their manner of being to the ego, itself a created substance. One point is formally acquired: the *ratio substantiae* is deduced from the ego, and the other substances, the infinite one excepted, borrow the notion itself from it. Either substance returns to the ego, or it comes from it.

The formal result does not suffice, since it shows the egological deduction without accomplishing it concretely (pp. 164–168); what substance borrows from the ego must be released determination by determination, over and above the Aristotelian determinations of *ousia*, and Marion takes three in turn — autonomy of existence, mediation of the attribute, reality of the distinction. Autonomy of existence characterizes substance par excellence, and here Descartes follows Aristotle to the letter: everything that can naturally be without a subject is a substance, *en hypokeimenō oudeni estin*. Substance has no substrate but itself, being the substrate of existence and of attribution for all other terms; it *can* be without substrate, hence without foundation or condition — *esse potest*. Being able to be unconditionally defines the substantiality of substance: this is the very notion of substance, that it can exist per se, that is, without the help of any other substance; a thing per se apt to exist; a thing that so exists as to need no other thing in order to exist. But the definition, literally Aristotelian, appears inadequate as soon as it is posited. Apart from God, infinite substance, every other substance, because finite and created, will fall under an equivocal substantiality placed under the double condition of its creation and its conservation by God; the power to exist inverts at once into an indigence to exist, save through God's ordinary concurrence, and the *ratio substantiae* becomes the common concept of things that need only God's concurrence in order to exist. In this new schema dependence for existing is redoubled, playing first between attribute or accident and substance, and then above all between created substance and creator God. A difficulty follows logically: why, instead of putting God's concurrence out of play as negligible because ordinary, so as to maintain the usual relation of attributes to substance, not dispense with a conditioned substance so close to an accident? The difficulty sharpens singularly in the case of bodies, where the very possibility of a created extended substance wavers twice. The first time is in the debate on Eucharistic transubstantiation, where Descartes recognizes that whatever, by however extraordinary a power of God, can be without a subject, is a substance — even where what is at issue is in fact accidents. The hypothesis of real accidents can therefore disappear, since accidents without a *subjectum* would already count as substances; whence, inversely, substances count only as accidents dispensed from a *subjectum*. But if substance changes while the accidents remain, and all depend equally on the divine concurrence, why still privilege the now accidental substance? An argument blocks the interrogation in principle: what holds in the physical explanation of Eucharistic transubstantiation cannot be extended to the whole doctrine of substance. Marion accepts it despite its weakness, since Descartes never stops stressing that his theory appeals to miracle less than any other and indeed dispenses with it. But a second time the difficulty does concern the whole doctrine of substance. When the sixth Meditation undertakes to demonstrate that corporeal things exist, it appeals to substantiality in general: just as cogitative faculties are in me, a thinking thing, as modes in a substance, so I can envisage that the faculties belonging to extension could neither be conceived nor remain without some substance in which they inhere. The hypothesis consecrates the possibility of a corporeal, finite and created substance situated between the accidents of extension and God's omnipotence, and Descartes opposes two competing objections to it. On the first, the ideas concerning extension would come directly from a *facultas ideas producendi* located in me, so that they would result, as thought modes, from a thinking substance, and not, as extended modes, from an extended substance now useless; but I sense the sensible involuntarily, which excludes my producing its ideas myself, and the first hypothesis disappears. There remains the second: God might exercise the *facultas ideas producendi* directly, in which case the infinite substance would dispense with the finite extended substance in affecting me with ideas of the sensible — and the counter-objection of my receptive passivity no longer holds, since God would act and I would remain passive. The motive that excludes this second hypothesis, that God would become *fallax* if he did not act according to the conviction he has placed in me, is very weak, because it presupposes what had to be demonstrated, the homogeneity or *similitudo* of effect to cause, of mode and accident to a substance. Descartes is nonetheless satisfied with it, and Marion explains why: the existence of a finite extended substance is not demonstrated here by itself but by virtue of its supposed parallelism with the finite thinking substance. As cogitative acts, which agree among themselves under the common *ratio* of *cogitatio*, directly imply a substantial *res cogitans*, so corporeal acts, which agree among themselves under one common *ratio* of extension, necessarily presuppose an extended substance; the sixth Meditation would demonstrate nothing without the parallelism, which carries over onto extension the finite substantiality that shows itself in thought and so dispenses with establishing extension's substantiality on its own. Whence the new question: how does Descartes establish the parallelism? The very text that states the *ratio substantiae* by deducing it from the idea of myself also establishes the parallelism at once — and at that very moment skews it, since of the two substances one prevails over the other, according to the order of reasons and also in itself: when I think a stone to be a substance or a thing apt to exist, and likewise myself to be a substance, though I conceive myself to be a thinking and non-extended thing and the stone an extended and non-thinking one, and so very different, they nonetheless seem to agree in the *ratio* of substance. Substance is not, despite Descartes, equally distributed between cogitating and extended substance, and the motive is evident: to the duo extension/substance there answers not a duo *cogitatio*/substance but a trio, *cogito* or *concipio* / *cogitatio* / substance. For the ego cogitates the extended substance, but also the thinking substance, whereas extension can never concern anything but extended substance; more, for extension to be able to relate to a substance at all, a *cogitatio* is still needed to think it as a simple nature and interpret it as a principal attribute. The apparent parallelism of the substances submits the duo of the one to the trio of the other from the outset. And there is more than a submission here: only *cogitatio* attains substantiality, thanks to the privilege of its double intervention. Where extension does not secure its own substance directly, *cogitatio* can; for it alone substantiality is verified, because if I think the attribute *cogitatio* I accomplish it at once absolutely, without remainder, without condition or external concurrence. *Cogitatio* becomes substance from the simple fact that I think it. Cogitation of cogitation issuing in substance — the schema merely repeats in terms of substance what the protocol statement formulated in terms of *res cogitans*. If substance is what needs no other thing in order to exist, then only the ego, insofar as it cogitates its own *cogitatio*, deserves the name, since it needs nothing but its thinking thought to make itself exist as thought thought. Which is why Husserl, at the very moment of separating the region of pure consciousness from the world, will apply the Cartesian definition of substance, in principle neutral, to consciousness alone: immanent being is indubitable in the sense of absolute being, so that in principle it needs no *res* in order to exist, while the world of transcendent *res* is referred throughout to consciousness — and not a logically conceived consciousness but a consciousness in act. He explicitly renews a coup de force already implicit in Descartes. Had *cogitatio* not ceaselessly secured itself as thinking thought reduced to that which is certain and unshakeable, the concept in general of a substance finite, created and dependent on God would have remained empty, contradictory and useless; and if, despite the aporias that make it nearly unthinkable, Descartes retained that concept, it is by virtue of the ego that thinks substantiality in cogitating itself, and despite extension, which by itself does not attain substantiality. The autonomy of existence, first character of substance, belongs only to the ego — because for Descartes, contrary to Aristotle, it comes from it.

The second character is the mediation of the attribute (pp. 168–173), and Marion begins from a contradiction in the texts. Substance as such does not affect us except by way of an attribute: substance cannot first be noticed from the sole fact that it is an existing thing, since this alone does not affect us, but we easily recognize it from any of its attributes; and consequently it does not manifest itself immediately, since Descartes never thought anything else required for manifesting a substance beyond its various attributes. Whence we know a substance only by the mediation of the attribute: we do not know substance itself immediately through itself but only through this, that it is the subject of certain acts; nor do we know substances immediately but only from perceiving certain forms or attributes. Substance forbids itself to immediacy, and the point of doctrine is stated as constantly and clearly as could be — whence Heidegger's comment seeing in it an anticipation of the Kantian thesis that being is not a real predicate. Yet another Cartesian thesis must invalidate it, at least partially. In the geometrically ordered appendix to the Second Replies substance is defined as every thing in which there is immediately, as in a subject, or through which there exists, something that we perceive, that is, some property or quality or attribute of which there is a real idea in us. The definition astonishes, since if the attribute is immediately in the substance it can make it known only immediately; and an alternative imposes itself. Either the attribute makes substance known mediately because it is immediately neither in it nor identical with it; or it is immediately in it, and even if it is not immediately identical with it, it can still make it known immediately — in which case the mediation is inscribed immediately in the substance and abolishes itself into an immediate mediation. Descartes gives no echo to the astonishment; on the contrary he repeats the immediate inherence of attributes on the usual parallel between substances: the substance in which thought is immediately is called Mind, and the substance which is the immediate subject of local extension and of the accidents presupposing extension is called Body. How does he not remark the patent difficulty — if *cogitatio* is immediately in the substance, why would it not manifest it immediately as such? And is the immediacy that would authorize such a manifestation even found in the second case of substance, extension? Marion answers that it is not, despite the obvious parallel of the definitions, which is more rhetorical than conceptual. Between extension and the substance called body the relation, though qualified as immediate, is concretized in a new term, *subjectum immediatum*; and while the general definition mentioned it, it is remarkable that the definition of thinking substance ignored it — as the note observes, the same disappearance of *subjectum* recurs in the Third Replies, where the general definition and the definition of extended substance speak of a subject of acts, of figure, of local motion, while thinking substance elides it. So here the immediacy of substance to its attributes implies the immediacy of a substrate, *subjectum*, *hypokeimenon*: extension is in the thing called extended as an accident in a *hypokeimenon*, that is, as *in alio*; extension remains immediately in the substance as in its other; substance alienates extension in immediately welcoming it, and its immediacy does not contradict the alienation of the attribute but accomplishes it. Immediacy comes back to mediation — at least in the case of extension, where the usual doctrine of the mediate knowledge of substance is confirmed despite the exceptional occurrences of immediacy. But what of *cogitatio*? In the definition of the *mens* the *subjectum* does not intervene to burden immediacy with mediation; is this a simple oversight, or a more originary comprehension of immediacy that dispenses with recourse to a *subjectum*? The answer lies in the sense of the adverb *immediate*, which for the *mens* must be understood from the two occurrences that introduce *cogitatio* and *idea* in the first two definitions. By the name of thought Descartes comprehends everything that is in us in such a way that we are immediately conscious of it: between the ego and *cogitatio* a relation of radical immediacy intervenes, since I am conscious of it and it is found without mediation *in nobis*. The *cogitationes* are in the ego as in their subject; but such a subject — and this is why the word *subjectum* does not appear — does not freeze into another substrate, since it *is* the ego, and the ego is exercised as the taking-consciousness in it of thoughts other than it which can only remain in it, being themselves only through it. It does not devalue itself into a hidden and receiving *hypokeimenon*; it accomplishes itself as an already intentional consciousness. What holds of thoughts holds of ideas: by the name of idea Descartes understands the form of any thought, through the immediate perception of which he is conscious of that same thought. The idea does not weaken cogitative immediacy but redoubles it — if the ego is conscious of any thought whose *cogitatio* is already characterized by immediacy, then the idea raises immediacy to a power, being the *forma* of each thing that renders it immediately available to consciousness. One will rightly object that it remains to be shown whether this cogitative and ideal immediacy can be applied to the relation between substance and attribute in general, and Marion answers in three remarks. It is no longer a question of explaining the relation in general between substance and attribute, but solely the relation of immediacy proper to the *mens*, which mobilizes no *subjectum immediatum*, unlike the relation proper to corporeal substance. The general definition of substance, while mentioning the possible use of a *subjectum*, also has recourse to a determination that dispenses with it and understands substance from perception, from the *idea* and so from *cogitatio*: we have of substance itself, precisely taken, no other idea than that it is a thing in which formally or eminently exists that something which we perceive. Of substance we therefore have an idea directly bound to what we perceive, namely that it is that in which what we perceive exists; and if one must not conclude that we perceive it immediately, one must at least admit that it is that in which exists what we perceive immediately. But does substance not then still constitute, without the word, a *subjectum*? It does not, for a fundamental reason. If for corporeal substance *cogitatio* knows extension immediately but the *subjectum immediatum* only mediately, that is because to the duo of clear and distinct knowledge, thought and object, a third term is added, designated as *subjectum*, an opaque and distant residue. In the case of thinking substance not three but exactly two terms intervene. The ego enters first into immediate relation with its *cogitationes* and *ideae*; then these relate to the thing in which they are, the substance; but since they are there only as *ideae* and *cogitationes*, that thing will still be the ego. The ego as *cogitans* relates a first time immediately to its *cogitationes* as objects; and when these must, as attributes, relate to a thing in which they subsist, they need no third term — it suffices that they relate a second time, as immediately as the first, to the ego, then interpreted as a substance, but an immediate substance, unlike the *subjectum* of extension. If two relations are found here as with extended substance, they mobilize only two terms: from the ego to the *cogitationes* the immediate epistemic relation deploys itself, and from the *cogitationes* as attributes back to the ego as substance the substantial relation deploys itself in return, always immediate. In the sole case of thinking substance, then, the reversal of the epistemic relation into a substantial relation secures the latter the immediacy every other substance forbids. Only the ego constitutes an immediately knowable substance. The contradiction noted earlier between the immediacy proclaimed in the appendix to the Second Replies and the mediation of the attribute affirmed everywhere else is therefore neither illusory nor insoluble. In most texts Descartes tries to define substance from the most widespread example, corporeal substance, and must then think it as an ultimate *hypokeimenon*, a *subjectum* possibly *immediatum* but in fact inaccessible to *cogitatio* except through the mediation of *cogitatio*'s only true immediate object, extension and the other material simple natures. In other and fewer cases he thinks his own concept of substance, reformulating it from the sole immediate relation of the ego to its *cogitationes*: instead of that relation leading the ego epistemically to its objects, the *cogitationes* turn back toward the ego as attributes refer to their substance, and the immediacy of the first relation then plays to the profit of the second. One must conclude, a second time, that the substantiality of substance is defined at bottom from the ego.

The third character confirms the deduction (pp. 173–175): the reality of the distinction between substances. Distinguishing substances really matters to their essential definition, since every substance must be able to exist by itself and so apart from the others. Descartes' ordinary doctrine is stated apropos the most exemplary case, the union of soul and body: on one hand I have the idea of myself insofar as I am only a thinking and non-extended thing, on the other the idea of body insofar as it is only an extended, non-thinking thing; and this intelligible distinction counts as real because I know that everything I clearly and distinctly understand can be made by God such as I understand it, so that from the separate intelligibility of the two ideas I can conclude to a real distinction between two *res*. At least three times Descartes' own commentaries extend the text to the distinction of all substances. The Third Replies conclude that once we have formed two distinct concepts of these two substances it is easy, from what was said in the sixth Meditation, to know whether they are one and the same or diverse — a real distinction drawn from distinct concepts. The Fourth Replies summarize that no one who perceives two substances through two different concepts has ever judged them not to be really distinct, referring to the conclusion about the real distinction of mind from body perfected in the sixth Meditation. And the *Conversation with Burman*, citing the same text, stresses that you would be acting against your own understanding, and quite absurdly, if you called one and the same substance two things which you clearly conceive as two substances, one of which not only does not involve the other but denies it. The reasoning is firmly clear: substances are really distinguished if and only if their concepts rationally exclude one another. The disjudicatory role accorded to concepts cannot surprise, since concepts take up the principal attributes, whose first character is clear and distinct intelligibility; the attributes reproduce the strict parallel of the substances so as to secure their real distinction. A question surges at once. Marion has just stressed, under the first two headings, not a parallel but a hierarchy of the two substances; must the primacy of the *res cogitans* in substantiality in general not then be called into question here? No, because it is evidently confirmed here again: if the distinction of substances is determined by that of their concepts, the distinction of concepts is in turn determined by recourse to the *ego cogito*, so that one of the two substances is fundamentally distinguished from the other only in defining, determining and governing it. The *ego cogito* attests itself not first or only as one of the two distinguished substances, as thinking substance thought, but as the thinking substance *thinking* the real distinction of substances by the difference of attributes. That the thinking substance thinks the distinction of substances before any substance, extended or cogitating, is thought, occurrences attest in which the ego thinks first: it *understands* the distinction, since we perceive these substances to be really distinct from one another from this alone, that we clearly and distinctly understand one without the other; it *has the idea* of each of the substances; it *apprehends* them and forms the concept — I form a clear and distinct concept of that thinking substance. The primacy of the ego is confirmed not because the cogitating substance enjoys some privilege over the extended one, but because both discover themselves thought by the anterior thinking ego. Such an anteriority of thinking thought even over the substantiality of substance in general could surprise, and it sometimes dissimulates itself in the banality of a common opinion: commonly we judge all things to stand in the same way in the order of truth as they stand in relation to our perception. But the supposedly banal opinion contradicts a fundamental thesis of the method and of grey ontology, that singular things are to be regarded otherwise in the order to our knowledge than if we spoke of them as they really exist. The distinction of substances is in fact ordered to our knowledge; their completeness is understood from their simplicity, and that simplicity is delimited insofar as it is understood. The ego at work in thinking *cogitatio* deduces from itself the real distinction of substances. Having examined the autonomy of its existence, the mediation of its principal attribute and the reality of its distinction, Marion takes it as established that substance is deduced, for Descartes, from the ego.

Deducing substance from the ego means, on the Kantian sense of deduction, justifying by right the application of the ego to substance (pp. 175–177); so a last task remains, to produce the egological model of substance that justifies by right the passage from one to the other. The passage was spotted and denounced by Nietzsche, for whom the concept of substance is a consequence of the concept of subject and not the reverse; and it was confirmed and approved by Leibniz, who has substance itself, endowed with active and passive power, for an indivisible or perfect monad, like the I or its like — and better, holds that it is by the knowledge of necessary truths and by their abstraction that we are raised to the reflexive acts which make us think of what is called I and consider that this or that is in us, so that in thinking of ourselves we think of Being and of substance. Under these two authorities, which agree in recognizing the same deduction only to oppose one another in interpreting it, a model can be sketched running from the ego through the reflection of thinking thought on thought thought, then to unity, then to the equivalence of the one and being, and so to substance. Marion explicates the transitions. The ego does not think the thinkable so much as thought thought: to see equals to seem to see, because it cannot be that when I see, or since I no longer distinguish them, when I think that I see, I myself thinking should not be something. The ego accedes to existence not in thinking but in thinking that it thinks, that is, by the reflection of thought as thinking on thought as thought. From reflection there is passage to the only authentic unity, which is lacking to every being that cannot say the I that says much; for true unity has an analogy with the soul, and there is a true unity answering to what is called I in us. The *animae analogon* alone secures real unity because it culminates in identity with self, which is perfectly accomplished only in the thinking thought of thought — and Nietzsche again confirms the passage by denouncing it, since we have borrowed the concept of unity from our concept of the I, our oldest article of faith. From unity to being the transition goes back at least to Aristotle, for whom being and the one are the same and a single nature, since one man and man are the same; and Leibniz will cite the ancient thesis to the letter, holding as an axiom the identical proposition, diversified only by the accent, that what is not truly one *being* is not truly one *being* either, since the one and being have always been believed reciprocal. Unity is attributed less to being than it is equivalent to being: for something to be, it must be itself and so one. As for the passage from being to substance, it presents no difficulty, first because Descartes often makes them synonymous, saying that the soul is a being or a substance, and then because Aristotle first led the question what the being is back to the question what *ousia* is. An egological model of substance is thereby drawn, completing its deduction from the ego. The essence of substance must be said from the ego, which far from undergoing the indetermination of substance imposes on it its own determinations. Not only am I a substance: substance has first the essence of an I, because it is deduced from an ego.

The thesis Heidegger sustains in *Sein und Zeit* must therefore be refused (pp. 177–180). It seems inexact that the mode of being of the *res cogitans*, more precisely the sense of being of the *sum*, remains undetermined, or that the *cogitationes* remain ontologically indeterminate; and it seems very doubtful that the *sum* is found totally unexposed, simply because it appears hard to maintain that Descartes leaves unexposed the sense of being enclosed in the idea of substantiality and the character of universality of that signification. Marion's counter-thesis is sharper than a simple denial. If the universality of substantiality never stops raising grave difficulties in the Cartesian enterprise, those difficulties result neither from an indetermination nor from an inexposition of substantiality but, on the contrary, from its determination and exposition too precisely deduced from the ego alone. Properly speaking, the characters of substance are verified only for and by the ego, and raise aporias as soon as Descartes tries to apply them either to extension or to God. Concerning extension, the aporias arise as soon as one claims to impose on it, along with substantiality, the autonomy of existence, the mediation of a principal attribute different from it, and its distinction from other substances. Concerning God, if autonomy poses no difficulty, that is because his eminent substantiality puts in question the self-sufficiency of all other creatures; if his real distinction from other substances raises no obstacle, since it amounts to the gap of creation, that is because it could as easily forbid the possibility of any substance other than God. As for the mediation of a principal attribute, it remains undecided: neither extension nor *cogitatio* is invoked, nor any other determination, unless one takes infinity, perfection and power for one or several principal attributes of a single substance — a difficulty the note refers forward to the chapter on God, and which Descartes' successors will try to escape by defining substance without privileged reference to the ego, with reference instead to God in Spinoza, to extension in Malebranche, or to any being whatever in Leibniz. None of these aporias would appear if the ego did not determine the substantiality of substance. A further step in criticizing Heidegger is therefore required. Not only does substance determine the being of the *ego sum* and of its *cogitationes*; substance extends, only afterwards and perhaps illegitimately, to other beings. Heidegger firmly stresses an inverse movement: Descartes would first have thought substance from extension, itself interpreted as the certain and permanent object of objectivating knowledge, and would then have folded the certain knowledge of the permanent and subsisting being back onto the *sum* of the ego, imposing on it the manner of being of subsistence — engendering what metaphysics from Kant on named consciousness. The being of the Dasein, to whose constitution being-in-the-world belongs, Descartes grasps in the same manner as he grasps the being of the *res extensa*. On that reading the attribution of substantiality to the ego would result first from an indetermination of the being of that *sum*, unthought ontologically because ontically too well known, and then from a back-lighting or back-interpretation of *cogitatio* from *extensio* — more exactly of the manner of being of the Dasein recorded as ego from the manner of being of the intra-worldly being, itself reduced to pure and simple subsistence, both misunderstandings accomplishing themselves in the horizon of a common and complete misrecognition of what "world" could signify. Without calling into question the uninterrogated privilege Descartes accords to subsisting presence, one must at least refuse that he thinks substance first from the *res extensa* in order then to extend it to the *res cogitans* left ontologically incomprehensible. Not only is the sense of being of the ego indeed thought, since it is thought as substance, but substance is indeed situated in and determined by the ego; the back-interpretation concerns only extension and God. For when the simple existence of the ego is acquired and its concept interrogated — examining attentively what I was — the answer is stated without the least ambiguity: I knew that I was a substance whose whole essence or nature is only to think. The formulation deserves the more attention in that here, unlike in the *Meditationes*, the term substance intervenes from the demonstration of the ego onward, before any mention of God; and better, it will not reappear to qualify either God or extension as such. I am a substance — the human mind is a pure substance, I am a substance, *ego autem substantia*. Not only does substantiality determine the being of the ego; more radically the ego fixes the place of substantiality by rendering it thinkable from its own thought. The ego gives place, and so gives its place, to substance — Marion writes *erörtet*, taking up the German Heidegger used to say the *sum* was left unplaced. Substance determines its own essence in that the characters of *ousia*, subsisting in itself and being said by itself, must henceforth be understood from *cogitatio*: *cogitatio* subsists at each instant with no other condition or substrate than itself, and it can say itself, since as thinking thought it says every other being, which it reduces to thought thought. The ego, determined by *cogitatio* at work, gives its place to every substance. As the metaphysicians he made possible understood, Descartes does establish the equivalence between being and thought, by constructing a substantiality that owes everything to the ego since it is deduced from it: *ego autem substantia*. Which may be heard as "substance, that's me," provided at least that the equivalence be turned into this other one — the ego alone, because it determines its manner of being according to substance, gives place to substance; in short, substance is ego, or is not. And if substance is ego or is not, the Heideggerian interpretation must be radically refused: the difficulty of the Cartesian doctrine of substance follows not from insufficient consideration of the specificity of the ego but, inversely, from its exclusive determination from the ego, at the risk of suiting none of the beings irreducible to it.

### § 14. The Subsistent Temporality of the Ego

One of the major conclusions of the Heideggerian interpretation must therefore be contested (pp. 180–186): it does not seem tenable to maintain the primacy of the intra-worldly being, understood as *res extensa*, in the Cartesian elaboration of substance. Quite the contrary — substantiality accrues to the *res extensa* from the ego as first substance, and the difficulties the doctrine of material substance meets follow directly from the more originary difficulty of extending a concept originally elaborated for and by the ego. But pointing out a weakness does not disqualify the whole pertinence of the interpretation, and Marion is careful here to separate what he rejects from what he accepts. The essential stake does not lie in the erroneous thesis of the intra-worldly origin of substance, since even that thesis in the end designates phenomenologically all substantiality in Descartes as subsistence and permanence. Whatever its origin, Cartesian substance manifests its substantiality as the permanence of a subsisting being — and on Heidegger's own procedure in *Sein und Zeit* that is the only decisive point. That subsistence finally concerns *every* substance, that Descartes verifies in this sense the ontological thesis Hegel and others attributed to him, and that Cartesian substance does pose the question of the ego, Heidegger himself sometimes recognizes explicitly, as when he stigmatizes an ontological groundlessness in the problematic of the self from Descartes' *res cogitans* to the Hegelian concept of spirit. Whether one reaches that interrogation indirectly from the substantiality of the *res extensa*, as most often in *Sein und Zeit*, or directly as here, the strength of the Heideggerian interpretation — by which it does not merely propose one more reading among others but imposes an unavoidable question on any reading of Descartes — concerns the mode of being of the ego that cogitates. More precisely, Heidegger asks whether the ego, insofar as it accomplishes the equation of being and thought, does not carry to its most perfect completion the interpretation of the being of beings as pure and simple persistence in subsistence, and so inaugurate the imperial errancy that, by way of Kant, issues in Husserl. Husserl's indistinction between the manners of being of two ontically distinguished regions, world and consciousness, goes back to an earlier unthought and, through Kant, to Descartes: consciousness of my Dasein means for Kant consciousness of my being-subsisting in Descartes' sense, and the term Dasein indicates the being-subsisting of consciousness as well as the being-subsisting of the thing. With Descartes, and from the ego interrogated in its manner of being, what is at stake is the subversion of the being of beings, as such and universally, by permanent subsistence. Taking Heidegger seriously, when he undertakes to think the Cartesian event through in the history of being, therefore comes to trying to answer a single question: does Descartes elaborate a thought of being as *Vorhandenheit*, as subsistence, does he obey the idea of being as permanent subsistence, to the point that the equation his metaphysical posterity explicitly recognized rests, more fundamentally, on another equation, implicit and visible only through a destruction of the history of ontology — Being equals permanent subsistence? Or again, does the indetermination initially reproached to the manner of being of the *sum* not in fact cover over its determination, all the more radical for being dissimulated even from Descartes' own eyes, as pure and simple subsistence? The formula *ego autem substantia* would then no longer signify only that I am substance because substance is ego or is not; it would signify also that every substance retains from its origin in the ego the property of subsisting, itself too, in the mode of the ego's subsistence. The ego would then determine nothing less than the manner of being of the being in general. By a significant paradox, Marion observes, Heidegger himself puts us on the road to refuting his secondary thesis in stating his principal one. The principal thesis releases maintenance in permanence, *ständiger Verbleib*, as the characteristic trait of the being.
