But Heidegger recognizes here only a character of the *res extensa*, of which he will therefore be led, wrongly, to make the first depositary of permanent subsistence — since he reaches it by taking up the famous analysis in which Descartes, in *Principia* II, § 4, abstracts the intra-worldly being from all sensible qualities so as to reduce it to extension alone, because extension alone remains: all such qualities as are sensed in corporeal matter can be removed from it, itself remaining intact. *Remanere* perfectly admits the equivalence of *ständiger Verbleib* by which Heidegger translates it. It would even have been more expedient, Marion notes, to conduct the same demonstration from the analysis of the piece of wax in the second Meditation, where for the first time the disappearance of all the sensible qualities of the being named wax leaves an abstract residue subsisting which bears that same name only in that it still subsists, though undone of its earlier species: does the same wax remain? one must confess it remains; no one denies it, no one thinks otherwise; the wax remains. The wax remains such, despite its total sensible disappearance, only insofar as — for *cogitatio* at least — it remains; remaining according to a maintained permanence constitutes its sole definition. The note here sharpens the translation: German *verbleiben* can correspond to persevering, and in that light *remanere* takes on its full significance, since the wax, despite the total abstraction of its sensible qualities, *perseveres* in its existence — it does not merely stay on but obstinately remains, manifesting that to be comes precisely to persisting in subsistence in the present. And this text confirms Heidegger's principal thesis better than the one he invokes, because the subsisting persistence appears in the *Meditationes before* any elaboration of substance, which intervenes only later, so that far from resulting from substance, persistence could on the contrary make it possible: the decision in favour of the being of beings as permanent subsistence prepared and decided the later sense of substantiality. But the confirmation of the principal thesis already raises a suspicion about the secondary one, since here the persistence of a maintenance in permanence results less from the intra-worldly being as such — which cannot yet be defined as *res extensa* founded in certainty, still less as substance — than from the *ego cogito* that analyses and reduces it, by the inspection of the mind alone. Whence Marion's question: could the *ego cogito* observe a persistence in permanence apropos the piece of wax if it did not itself first remain in the mode of subsisting permanence? Must it not, more than any *cogitatum*, persist in order to see persistence? For an intra-worldly being to be able to remain, the ego must first and radically remain; and if everything that remains remains through the ego, then the ego alone, and first, remains. Textual confirmation is not lacking, since *remanere* appears first not to designate the piece of wax or the intra-worldly being in general but to qualify the ego in person, in its first appearance and its new certainty: I shall now meditate anew on what I formerly believed myself to be, before I fell into these thoughts, from which I shall then subtract whatever could be in the least weakened by the reasons brought forward, so that at last there may remain precisely only that which is certain and unshakeable. It is the same process, exemplarily applied to the ego itself, before the analysis that abstracts the piece of wax from everything not reducible to remaining: the earlier ego, prior to thinking thought, is reduced by subtraction of the uncertain in it to the unshakeable, which alone remains — where *remanere* can and must be heard in the sense of a residue but also of a persistence in permanence. The determination of the ego by persisting permanence is neither provisional nor approximate, since it reappears in the last Meditation, to abstract the ego as pure understanding from its adjacent faculty of imagining: for although that were absent from me, without doubt I should nonetheless remain the same as I now am. And it is all the less appropriate to weaken such an attribution of permanence to the ego in that it coincides with the formula *certum et inconcussum*, which Heidegger privileges to the point of completing it with *fundamentum*, forging a formula — *fundamentum certum et inconcussum* — that literally never appears in Descartes' texts. It must therefore be held as acquired that maintenance in permanence concerns first and above all the ego, which, before any intra-worldly being and in order to attribute it to that being, must itself institute it; more exactly, the ego institutes itself according to maintenance in permanence. Its ontic primacy over other beings depends ontologically on the comprehension of the being of beings as subsisting, hence as persisting in maintaining itself, so that it can satisfy the unconditioned requirement of certainty — and certainty counts here as an ontological and not first an epistemic determination, one that imposes on beings to be in the mode of subsistence. The ego, precisely because it offers the first and most certain of beings, must more than any other satisfy subsistence and render visible in itself the maintenance in permanence of the being in general. Three questions follow. Is the ego indeed in the mode of subsistence — since subsistence is defined by persistence and maintenance in presence, is it only insofar as it remains in the present within presence? Can one determine, from the ego's own mode of being, a mode of being persisting in the present that concerns the being in general, so as to confirm in general the temporality of being according to subsistence? And can one, inversely, confirm the Cartesian temporality of the being of beings from the dimensions of time irreducible to the present — does the ego temporalize itself exclusively and primordially in the present, and what being could the ego's subsistence recognize in past and future if to be equals strictly to subsist according to persistent maintenance? Only the answers to these three questions can release the temporality of the being in the Cartesian sense, or what comes to the same, the being of the being in the Cartesian situation.

The first question receives a positive answer from the way the ego observes its own existence (pp. 186–188), in a series of convergent statements that declare that existence according to time and in view of presence in the present. The first characterization: the ego is each time it thinks — this pronouncement, *ego sum, ego existo*, is necessarily true whenever it is uttered by me or conceived in my mind. A similar *quoties* recurs to mark the actual thought of other thinkable thoughts, since whenever it pleases me to think of the first and supreme being I must attribute to him all perfections; but here alone the occasion of thinking releases and provokes the occasion of *being*, or rather being as an occasion arriving in the present instant. The instant of *cogitatio* becomes, in the case where it thinks thought itself, the instant of existing an instantaneous existence, strictly bound to the instantaneity of a performance of *cogitatio*. The second characterization: the ego is as long as it effectively cogitates its own thought and redoubles its thought thought, the doubts, by its thinking thought — let him deceive as much as he can, he will never bring it about that I am nothing so long as I shall think that I am something; and, I am, I exist, that is certain; but for how long? namely, as long as I think, for perhaps it could happen that if I ceased from all thought I should at once wholly cease to be. The *praecise* immediately following, that I am therefore precisely only a thinking thing, can be understood not only of a distinction of faculties — I am only insofar as understanding, mind and reason think in me — but still more of temporality: I am only very exactly as long as I think. The repetitions are many, and Marion draws from them a formulation worth holding onto: the evidence of thought thought before thinking thought anticipated the temporal characterization of every thought thinking in evidence, since evidence itself constrains thought only as long as thought effectively thinks. The time of evidence, that is, the time of the presence of thinking thought to the evident object, determines the time of existence, since existence itself is only in being thought — I exist while I think, and he who thinks cannot not exist while he thinks. Whence the third characterization: that the ego can exist only as long as, and each time that, it effectively thinks does not imply that its certain existence lasts only an instant or several juxtaposed and heterogeneous instants. The stake lies not in the instantaneity of existence but in the submission of its duration to that of *cogitatio*, in short in its finite and contingent temporalization measured by the temporality of *cogitatio*. Which is why Descartes here perfectly admits a moment — we perspicuously understand that it can happen that I exist this moment, in which I think one thing — that can last in a time, since it is repugnant to think that what thinks, at the very time in which it thinks, does not exist. The ego's existence deploys itself temporally, but on a temporality first and radically determined by *cogitatio*; and inversely, if the ego exists only as often and as long as the present moment of *cogitatio* lasts, that is because *cogitatio* itself privileges presence in the present in its own temporality. The triple characterization therefore demands a stricter determination of the temporality that originally governs it — the temporality of *cogitatio*, in which the privilege accorded to presence in the present can become intelligible.

Descartes reaches his definitive determination of that originary temporality already in the *Regulae* (pp. 188–197), and from the outset *cogitatio* temporalizes itself in the present, because the evidence it wants requires presence. First the presence of the object, lest a superfluous recollection steal some part of our mind from the present object — so that the memory of a past object must always be refused outside the experience of a certain thought. Then presence determines the faculties of the knowing mind, requiring the *praesens attentio* of an *intellectus in praesenti*; and only on condition of a perfect *intuitus* will evidence be released, the present evidence such as intuition requires. So *praesentes ideae* can appear which, by a perfect synchrony of presences — object, attention, evidence, ideas — *cogitatio* can regard as its *cogitatum*. Marion presses on the verb. To regard says here, on the same duality as *intueri*, first to take into view, but also to keep and to protect, *tueri*: to regard implies keeping under the eyes, keeping in view, having in one's eye. Such a regard that keeps cannot be accomplished if its object does not remain present to its sight; better, it can keep the object only if it loses it from view at no instant, only if it maintains it present by a permanent keeping that makes it persist in presence. *Cogitatio*, once it aims at present evidence, requires of its object that it be precisely in the mode of persisting presence. This fundamental decision governs the whole development of Cartesian thought, and the 1644 definition of the clear idea proves it: I call clear that idea which is present and open to an attending mind, just as we say those things are clearly seen by us which, present to the intuiting eye, move it strongly and openly enough. The regard, of mind as of eye, is moved only by an idea as present and so open — available and accessible to its attention — as the regard itself is made present by that same attention; evidence comes about when the two presences, presence of mind and present thing, coincide. On that coincidence they determine presence, unless inversely it is presence that makes possible the coincidence of the two present terms, since presence alone can put them in one another's presence. In the *Meditationes* as in the *Regulae* presence characterizes the object most easily known in evidence, the mathematical object of imagination — I intuit those three lines with the mind's eye as present — but it can equally be the object of sensation, since I could not sense any object even if I wished unless it were present to the sense organ, nor could I fail to sense it when it was present. More, the presence of the object to *cogitatio* can reach such acuity that it seems possible to infer existence directly from it: to one considering more attentively what imagination is, it appears to be nothing other than a certain application of the cognitive faculty to a body intimately present to it, and therefore existing. Without claiming to widen a formula that concerns only one's own body, it remains licit to meditate on the equivalence that appears there with perfect clarity, *praesens ac proinde existens*: existence, if it does not always effectively follow from presence, nonetheless always presupposes it, and nothing existing is known or knowable except in the mode of presence. *Cogitatio* regards only presence, and so keeps existence as a certain object only insofar as existence presents itself in the mode of presence in the present; it temporalizes its *cogitatum* exclusively in the present because it first temporalizes itself exclusively in presence. But there is more, since *cogitatio* limits neither its own temporalization nor its object's to a presence reduced to the instant as an atomic and imperceptible point. Present evidence must *last* if the science of objects disposed according to order is to be constituted; so the *Regulae*, to widen the field of present evidence even to the discursivity of the *series rerum*, admit beside the punctual evidence of *intuitus* a *motus cogitationis* whose function is to run through the deductions punctual intuition cannot treat. But it accomplishes this only in view of present evidence, and reaches it only by reproducing, in temporal succession, the presence of the regard of *intuitus*, and by a double particularity. First the movement of *cogitatio* remains continuous and never interrupts itself, so as to keep the constancy of presence within the chainings of consecution: very many things are known with certainty although they are not themselves evident, provided only they are deduced from true and known principles by a continuous and nowhere interrupted movement of thought intuiting each thing perspicuously. The *motus cogitationis* is continuous only in order to continue, by displacing without undoing it, the regard of *intuitus*, which, overstepping the present instant, does not renounce the epistemic primacy of presence but extends and accomplishes it. Whence the second particularity: the movement of thought permits not only seeing several objects successively but above all running through them so quickly that the regard can at the end know their plurality and apprehend it *simul* as strictly present — by a certain continuous movement of imagination intuiting each and passing to the others, running through everything with the swiftest movement of thought and intuiting as many as possible at once. Presence thereby deploys a field of application much wider than instantaneous presence: the *motus cogitationis* folds back onto the present instant, the elective temporality of evidence present to the regard, wide margins of past discursivity, so that the horizon of presence overflows the present, because *cogitatio*, redoubling itself with a *motus cogitationis*, leads back into it what seemed definitively to escape it. Presence, by the setting in motion of *cogitatio*, exercises over temporality an empire exceeding the present and prolonging it onto the marches of the past. What does it matter, one will object, that a temporality of the present dominates, if it is confined to the time of thought and does not determine that of things? But precisely, in Cartesian terms there is only ever one single concept of time, dominant throughout the horizon of the being; and Descartes explicitly repeats the time of the being in general from the time of the *mens* understood as a *cogitatio*. To prove it, the stages of the Cartesian determination of time from *cogitatio* must be retraced. Descartes first refuses to distinguish between the duration of mobile and so extended beings and the duration of beings that cannot move: despite the cardinal difference between thought and extension, duration remains common to them, counting already in the *Regulae* among the common simple natures, and keeping in the *Principia* the same universality while being opposed to time, which follows from duration without redoubling or limiting it — when we distinguish time from duration taken generally and say it is the number of motion, it is only a mode of thinking, for we do not understand in motion any other duration than in things not moved. What lasts, first, is not the extended thing in movement but *cogitatio* itself, and Burman's testimony holds nothing surprising in making thinking thought last par excellence: all my action by nature happens in time, and I can be said to continue and persevere in the same thought for some time. This is only an explication of the *quamdiu* and the *momentum* analysed above as the manner of being present to presence proper to the ego. And not only does the *mens* also last; it above all lasts, and lasts originally, all the more as it apportions all duration as an origin. The *mens* lasts because duration comes from it, so to speak mentally — and Descartes does not hesitate to state, in the very sequence that advances the egological deduction of substance, an egological deduction of duration: certain things I seem to have been able to borrow from the idea of myself, namely substance, duration, number, and any others of the sort. The two deductions in fact mingle in a single development. The reasoning runs: when I perceive that I now am, and recall that I also existed before for some time, and when I have various thoughts of which I understand the number, I acquire the ideas of duration and number, which I can then transfer to any other things whatever. Marion analyses the sequence in three stages. First the ego perceives, before any duration, its being at the strict present: *nunc esse* indicates ontically its present existence, but above all it marks that ontologically, being is said according to presence — the fundamental equation between *esse* and *nunc*, between being and presence, as such. The second stage is the ego's taking consciousness of its *cogitationes*, and of the fact that these are already past *cogitationes*, so that perceiving them comes to remembering them; Descartes thus anticipates Kant, with the synthesis of reproduction in imagination, as much as Husserl, with retention. In the third stage duration is released, engendered by the exercise of thinking in the past as well as in the present, or rather by the consciousness that, in order to think past thoughts in the present, one must be able to lead them back to that single present. The genesis of duration from the present being of the ego is accomplished by the consciousness of the succession of *cogitationes*; and that conscious succession also offers the origin of number, which measures and orders them. There remains to understand how two notions so strictly bound to the ego can be transferred, so easily that Descartes does not explain it, to other things — whether *transferre* indicates only an imprecise metaphorization of the duration of consciousness, or implies a rigorous process of transposition. The second hypothesis imposes itself once one notices the relation, here again uninterrogated, that binds number to duration, all the more since Descartes defines time as a number, the number of motion. Such a formula refers explicitly to Aristotle's definition, which it cites in part literally: this is time, the number of motion according to the before and after. Another Cartesian text completes the citation: I do not understand the successive duration of things that move, or even of motion itself, otherwise than that of things not moved; for the before and after of any duration whatever becomes known to me through the before and after of the successive duration which I apprehend in my thought, with which other things coexist. The successive duration of moving things, whatever they be, is understood by the before and after of the successive duration of *cogitatio*; time is indeed the number of a movement for Descartes as for Aristotle. Marion registers that Descartes recovers the Aristotelian definition as the medievals transmit it, *without criticizing it*, whereas he does not fail to criticize the comparable definitions of motion and of place — a remarkable truce in the habitual polemic against Aristotle, which the note reads not as a subjective variation but as the tacit recognition of an ineluctable determination by earlier metaphysics on this decisive point. The agreement raises a double remark. If one admits, following Heidegger, that a single vulgar concept of time traverses and governs the tradition of metaphysics from Aristotle to Hegel, it seems probable that Descartes is inscribed in it, and the earlier analyses had already released a fundamental trait of it: Descartes too thinks time from presence in the present, which dominates every other temporalization because it makes possible the interpretation of the being of beings as insisting persistence. The note supporting this is worth its space, since it observes that the vulgar concept of time is defined as a sequence of nows persisting in subsistence, at once supervening and disappearing — terms that suit the Cartesian determination of time — and that it is precisely on the occasion of this ultimate analysis that Heidegger introduces for the first time, in the texts published today, the interpretation of Descartes' *cogito me cogitare*, so that it is not at all out of place to hold Descartes present in the last pages of *Sein und Zeit*. But the literal agreement of the two definitions must not dissimulate a difference of great consequence. If Descartes, defining time as *numerus motus*, does recover Aristotle's *arithmos kinēseōs*, the identity of formulas does not yet decide whether the respective determinations of *kinēsis* and *motus* agree: what, in each case, is numbered? For Aristotle the numbered movement unrolls, as for Descartes, according to the before and the after, but these immediately imply place, *topos*, since the before and after is first in place, place offering the only frame in which a before-and-after can take place. Two corollaries follow. Movement deploys itself inside the categories of the being in its being, among which place figures at a derived rank, and there can be no movement apart from things themselves and so apart from the categories that govern them, since what changes always changes according to substance, quantity, quality or place. And movement can affect, as numbered number, only a numerable being: it plays between one *hypokeimenon* and another, or between states of these *hypokeimena*. The *psychē* does intervene to number, but without itself offering the numbered number, precisely because it exercises itself as a numbering number, or rather exercises the numbering, namely time; for Aristotle movement bears on the being that offers itself to the *psychē*, in no way on the *psychē* itself. Not so for Descartes, and for a decisive reason: time, or rather the duration that makes it possible, intervenes in the third Meditation *before* the existence or even the essence of any *res* outside thought is established. Duration is already completely determined, to the point of taking up the letter of the Aristotelian definition of time, before the appearance of beings — since the successive duration of any thing whatever becomes known to me only from a more originary duration, defined by the succession of my *cogitationes*. The initial movement that time, or the duration producing it, determines and measures does not unroll in the thing passing from one of its states to another by the entelechy of a potency, but in thought, more exactly among the *cogitationes*: the *motus cogitationis*. Duration is indeed defined as the number of movement, but of the first movement the ego can observe. Does this radical disagreement with Aristotle put in question Descartes' resumption of the vulgar concept of time, and with it the primacy of presence in the present? On the contrary, Marion answers, it reinforces it and carries it to a new accomplishment. For the *motus cogitationis* always tries to reproduce present evidence, even and above all where discursivity, and so the empire of the past, seems to forbid it; it serves presence beyond the present, to which it tries to lead back the past of evidence. Better still, the *motus*, deploying itself in *cogitatio*, can attain a present still more immediate — still more present, if one may say — than movement in things themselves; for those things, at the moment of their presence, evidently have no consciousness of it, having no consciousness, whence it follows that even when present they are neither present to themselves nor present to the presence consciousness dispenses. Consciousness, and so the *motus cogitationis* it governs, is alone in the absolute present, which it produces absolutely as presence to self. Cartesian time therefore privileges not only presence in the present, as Aristotelian time already did, but the *presence to self* of the present — time becomes presence to self of the ego. Which is why, when the ego endures in presence understood as insisting persistence, it endures in and by *cogitatio* as the ultimate figure of presence: I can be said to continue and persevere in the same thought for some time. To the first of the three questions the answer can now be given: the ego, both by its presence to self and by its *motus cogitationis*, reinterprets the Aristotelian definition of time so as to accentuate the vulgar concept of time, to the point of passing from presence in the present to presence to self of presence in the present. One must therefore speak of an egological deduction of time, which interprets it from top to bottom as subsisting presence.

The second question can now be taken up (pp. 197–202): can a mode of temporality and presence of the being in general be determined from the ego's effectively persisting mode of being in presence in the present? Marion has established that for Descartes the determinations of substance and duration go together, first because their origin is common, both being deduced from the ego before any other existence and in a strict parallel, and then because more generally existence and duration offer themselves to be meditated together. But these indices take their full weight only with a universal principle that binds the two intimately: since any substance whatever, if it ceases to last, ceases also to be, it is distinguished from its duration only by reason. In other words, a substance that does not last thereby ceases to be a substance, because a substance has as its own to be; whence two consequences, one explicitly formulated by Descartes, that substance is distinguished from duration only in reason, and the other left implicit but decisive, that *to be equals to last*. And to last has sense only in the present, or rather in a presence that ceaselessly leads the other periods of time back to the present, so that the equivalence between substance and duration directly implies the domination of substance by presence in the present. Several indices confirm it, and the first is lexical: substance *subsists*, being a thing subsisting per se, a true substance or thing subsisting per se. *Subsistere* does not absolutely equal *substare*: the latter refers simply to the vertical station, *stare*, to stand up, while the former refers to *sistere*, to hold oneself, taken in the reinforced sense of holding fast, holding a position, staying on one's positions, hence resisting in place, in short lasting and making last. Whence the attribution of permanence, *manere* and *permanere*, as a constitutive character to every thing that is, insofar as it truly is by itself. The permanence that lasts in presence qualifies not only the thinking thing, or extension as a thing stripped of its sensible qualities; it qualifies also extension as principal attribute, and place — there is no permanent place of any thing except insofar as it is determined by our thought — because place draws its permanence from an originally permanent *cogitatio*. And finally God himself accomplishes the most perfect of permanences, since as inventor of the concept he alone deserves the title of substance by his complete independence: to attribute to the creature the perfection of being independent, namely that it perseveres in being independently of another. Even God's existence is understood in terms of permanence, better of perseverance — and what other mode of being would God illustrate, if not the only one cogitative thought recognizes, *esse*? God, in order to be, since he has to be, must secure his insistence in presence and so persevere in the present. Of such a universality Marion concludes by another index, which in fact states a principle: each thing, insofar as it is simple and undivided, remains, so far as in it lies, always in the same state and is never changed except by external causes; each thing, insofar as it is simple and undivided, always perseveres in being in the same state so long as it is not destroyed by an external cause; each thing tends, so far as in it lies, to remain in the same state in which it is. Everything that is, is according to presence in the present, and in consequence of the requirement in it of being as presence, perseveres in being in the mode of insisting permanence, so as to issue in subsistence. The comprehension at once raises an apparently irrefutable objection: what has just been cited as the principle of a determination of the mode of being of beings are formulations of what has always been agreed to be the principle of inertia, so that a principle of physics, concerning objects at rest or in movement and in any case extension, has been illegitimately transposed into the domain of ontology, which oversteps extension and concerns the *res cogitans* and God. Marion answers with several counter-objections. First and above all, must one exclude that the principle of inertia can receive an ontological interpretation? More, as a principle of physics, does it not fall — in fact for Descartes since the theses of 1630, by right for its concept — under a domain anterior to physics? Does the history of its slow emergence not confirm that what was at issue was less experimental physics than an ontological meditation on the conditions of possibility of movement and of its mathematical expression, as even Koyré's treatment of the principle as an ontological one suggests? And there is more: it seems licit and even obligatory to recognize that for Descartes, on the 1644 formulation, the principle that every being, at rest as in movement, perseveres in being in the sense of an insistence in presence, falls under metaphysics — even and precisely because it determines physics. For it follows from God's immutability: we understand also that it is a perfection in God not only that he is immutable in himself but that he operates in a manner as constant and immutable as possible; from this same immutability of God certain rules or laws of nature can be known; and the other part is demonstrated from the immutability of God's operation, who now conserves the world by the same action by which he once created it. From 1643 the metaphysical status of what is called the principle of inertia is stated in express terms, in the letter that gives as the second principle that whatever is, or exists, always remains in the state it is in, if some external cause does not change it — which Descartes proves by metaphysics, since God, author of all things, being wholly perfect and immutable, it seems repugnant that any simple thing that exists, and of which consequently God is author, should have in itself the principle of its own destruction. The text confirms not only that God alone, by his originary immutability, makes possible the derived permanence of created beings, and not only that the proof falls under metaphysics, but above all that the principle so determined concerns everything that is or exists, and not the physical domain alone. One must therefore conclude that for Descartes at least the principle of inertia states only one of the regional ontic applications of a metaphysical principle — a principle declaring that every being is according to presence in the present, and that in consequence it can only persevere, insist and persist in presence, hence last in the present. These characters accrue to every being as substance first accrued to it, by deduction from the ego, and the principle must be understood as an ontological thesis on the manner of being of all beings, possible and real. Its strangest confirmation, and Marion notes that this owes nothing to chance, comes from the application Descartes dares to make of it to the mystery of transubstantiation. If, he argues with Arnauld, the Church has taught that the species of bread and wine remaining in the Sacrament of the Eucharist are certain real accidents which, the substance in which they inhered being removed, subsist miraculously, then these real accidents must be held to be authentic substances, because substance consists entirely in subsistence: since nothing real can be understood to remain unless it subsists, and although it be called by the word accident, it is nonetheless conceived as a substance. Inversely, no confirmation of the ontological statement could arise from the disputed question whether continued creation contradicts a discontinuous duration or not. For it is precisely because the finite being — first the ego, but also the world — is not a perfectly independent substance that on one hand it cannot last, persevere and subsist in presence, and on the other it must have recourse to continued divine creation in order to verify in itself the insisting permanence that defines the temporality of being. The second question therefore has at least a sketched answer: the insisting persistence that the ego's temporality sets to work deploys itself in everything that is or exists, according to an egological deduction of presence in the present that redoubles the egological deduction of the substantiality of every being. Not only is being understood from thought in the mode of the *ego cogito* — *ego autem substantia* — but, since I perceive that I now am, the temporality of every being is understood from the presence to self of the ego persisting in the present. For Descartes, being and time converge in a single subsistence.

One question remains wholly intact (p. 202): are there other dimensions of time analysed as such by Descartes, and if so, do past and future confirm the primacy of presence interpreting being from the present as the persistence of subsistence? But envisaging it requires entering entirely new regions of Cartesian thought.

### § 15. The Ego Outside Subsistence

Before determining whether the ego admits modes of being irreducible to subsistence in presence, Marion risks an a priori induction (p. 203). If persistence determines as subsistence nothing less than substance itself, then the time of the present governs not only the existence of substance but every possible existence. Existence counts here already as the category that on Kant's account dominates the whole of modality, actual existence, *Dasein*, and so stands opposed to the other two categories of modality, possibility and necessity. The question therefore becomes whether a relation can be established between past and future on one side and possibility and necessity on the other, corresponding to the one Descartes explicitly established between the present and effectivity in the single subsisting presence. That Cartesian time should play, in its other two dimensions too, according to and under the heading of modality would confirm that from presence in the present onward the manner of being of the being was being decided. In envisaging the temporality of past and future, Marion is therefore doing two things at once: measuring possible exceptions to the primacy of presence in the present, and so to persisting subsistence as the privileged manner of being of the being; and asking these other temporalizations to confirm, a contrario, that the question of time decided the manner of being of the being from the start.

The exception to the present is met first in the past and thanks to it (pp. 203–207), and Marion begins by stressing a paradox: it is not easy to reach the past in a situation so governed by the privilege of presence as the Cartesian one. Acceding to the past implies here undoing oneself from presence understood as the only correct epistemic disposition, present evidence, and the only accomplished ontological position, persisting subsistence — so it implies leaving presence in the present by entering into dissidence, toward an absolutely unthinkable horizon. For at the start and by right presence governs *cogitatio*, which cannot not last provided it remains exposed to present evidence: such is the nature of my mind that I could not fail to assent to these things, at least as long as I clearly perceive them; and when I consider the nature of a triangle it appears most evidently to me that its three angles equal two right angles, nor can I fail to believe it true as long as I attend to its demonstration. The effectivity of present evidence radicalizes into a necessity: as long as present evidence lasts, *cogitatio* cannot cease to agree with it, nor, in agreeing, cease to last itself. The effective presence of evidence does not merely posit it effectively; it *imposes* it necessarily on *cogitatio*, which, unable to withdraw from it, submits to it in lasting with it. Even the most declared of sceptics cannot dispense with assent to present evidence: Descartes never denied that the sceptics themselves, as long as they clearly perceive the truth, spontaneously assent to it. One should not object that this concerns only the temporality of knowledge, so as to restrict the necessity that effectively present evidence exercises on thought to that domain — for precisely, in Descartes, temporality in general is defined from the *motus cogitationis*. And it is easy to see that on the principle that every thing perseveres, so far as in it lies, in its state whatever that state may be, every effective presence perseveres in its effectivity so far as in it lies, and so imposes itself as a necessary existence necessarily on the *cogitatio* that represents it. Whence the paradox that must be admitted: if time is defined first according to *cogitatio*, and if present evidence necessarily determines *cogitatio*, then the greatest difficulty consists not in reaching present evidence but in acceding to the *past* — in tearing oneself from the necessity of thinking the ceaselessly persevering effective presence. How would an exception to the effectivity of presence be possible, if the thing perseveres by definition in itself and if *cogitatio* necessarily submits to present evidence? Presence in the present would thus render impossible both the past and contingency, and so the freedom of the *ego cogito*. The task is to subtract *cogitatio* from the necessity of evidence that a persisting presence exercises upon it, and the possibility of the past implies the possibility for *cogitatio* of withdrawing from the necessary thought of present evidence. Marion notes that "possibility of the past" states in fact a pleonasm: if presence in the present deploys its effectivity as a necessity, possibility will itself become possible and thinkable only by breaking with that necessary effectivity, that is, by passing to the past. Possibility thus comes back to the past, as it falls to the past to dissolve the persevering necessity of present effectivity. And if what is to be undone is present effectivity, it should not surprise that the past is born of what, from the standpoint of the present, can appear only as an impotence — since only an impotence can suspend the sempiternal power of presence. The text is decisive: for although I am of such a nature that as long as I perceive something very clearly and distinctly I cannot fail to believe it true, yet because I am also of such a nature that I cannot always fix the mind's gaze on the same thing so as to perceive it clearly, and the memory of a judgement made earlier often returns... My nature implies two contrary temporal postulations: my mind cannot fail to follow a present evidence, and so to undergo its necessity, as long as it remains attentive to it; and my mind cannot remain always attentive to the same present evidence. It cannot escape present evidence, but it does not remain present to evidence; the *quamdiu* of present evidence finds itself suspended by the *non semper* of inattention. The power of suspending presence characterizes the human mind constantly: the mind cannot always attend to those things; our mind cannot attend to anything without some difficulty and fatigue; the infirmity of human nature does not dwell always in the same thoughts; and, in French, we cannot be continually attentive to the same thing, however clear and evident the reasons that persuaded us of some truth beforehand. Inattention suspends the necessary hold of effectively present evidence on thought: evidence now reaches *cogitatio* only through the indirect mediation of a memory of a judgement made earlier, a memory most often lying, which blurs the last flashes of an evidence dissolved into the past. Marion's note sharpens the point by tracing memory's disqualification back to the *Regulae*, where *intuitus*, for which present evidence is necessary, is conquered precisely against the changing testimony of the senses and the deceptive judgement of a badly composing imagination — and what holds of imagination holds of memory, since the phantasy in question is the same as what is called memory, so that memory, as a temporal application of imagination, is opposed in principle to *intuitus*. The stress must fall exactly here: it is not the past that requires and makes possible memory, but memory that, as the dissolution of the present evidence inattention has just torn from its effectivity, *opens* the past — and so opens, in suspending the effectivity of necessary presence, the domain of the possible. Inattention to the present and the lying memory of effectivity, by suspending the necessity of effective presence, give rise to freedom in its highest figure, positive freedom of indifference. The 1644 exposition takes up every moment just identified: seeing very clearly that a thing is good for us, it is very hard and even, I believe, impossible, while one remains in that thought, to stop the course of our desire; but because the nature of the soul is to be attentive to the same thing scarcely a moment, as soon as our attention turns away from the reasons that make us know the thing is good for us, and we retain only in our memory that it appeared desirable, we can represent to our mind some other reason that makes us doubt it, and so suspend our judgement, and even perhaps form a contrary one. Read on the guiding thread of time, the text confirms three things: that the present evidence of a truth imposes its effectivity so necessarily on the mind that any autonomous decision becomes impossible, so that the effectivity of presence in the present destroys by its necessity all possibility; that suspending the necessity implies modifying the mode of temporalization that institutes it, since inattention dodges present evidence and cheats with the empire of effectivity, escaping its necessity without possibility each time by weakening the present with the screens of memory, forgetting and other reasons; and that suspending present effectivity does, in refusing the impossible, re-establish a real and positive power of self-determination. Temporality is thereby verified as the temporality of the being of beings, since just as the present put effectivity in play as the central category of modality, so the past puts possibility in play and reveals that the present, as presence persevering in subsistence, overflowed from effectivity into necessity. The result raises difficulties of its own, and Marion states them rather than smoothing them: how is it to be understood that the ego accedes to possibility, which welcomes its freedom, only by refusing the present of *cogitatio* through the past of memory? Does it not go without saying that freedom temporalizes itself according to the future, and that the future opens by the possible — and that the past concerns the elapsed and irreversible being and so accomplishes the necessity of the impossible?

It might be, though, that the temporalization of the being of beings according to the categories of modality does not obey the most banal succession (pp. 207–210), and that circumventing the effectivity of subsisting presence in the present demands subtler combinations of past and future, necessity and possibility. An analysis of the time of the future confirms it at once. The future intervenes explicitly in the *Passions of the Soul*, where, to mark the order and enumeration of the passions, after wonder and the love–hatred doublet, which all concern only the present, Descartes introduces the parameter of time so as to put them in order, distinguishing the times and considering that they carry us far more to look to the future than to the past or the present. Desire raises the question of the possibility of an object, since for a thing to be desired it must admit possibility and so make an exception to what would certainly be hated or certainly obtained; and the desires divide into classes, those that depend only on us reducing in fact to free will and implying the effectivity of nothing, so that they do not pose the question of possibility, while on the contrary the desires that depend only on other causes set to work things that may not happen. Are we then to infer that Descartes will for the first time think as such the modality of possibility, which the future opens par excellence? Marion answers that the inverse occurs. The possible implies an uncertainty and escapes representation as the future escapes the present; and all uncertainty implies of itself a harm and a danger, so the uncertainty of a possible to come and not representable must be reduced. To safeguard the rights of effective presence, Descartes undertakes to reduce the possibility the future opens by the necessity in which presence perseveres: we must often reflect on divine providence and represent to ourselves that it is impossible for anything to happen otherwise than as it has been determined from all eternity by that Providence, so that it is like a Fatality or an immutable Necessity; and we must think that with respect to us nothing happens that is not necessary and as it were fated, so that we cannot without error desire it to happen otherwise. In a few lines Descartes already traces explicitly the essential of Spinoza's ethical intention: the possible implies a contingency that seems to us acquired only through ignorance of the totality of causes concurring in every event, an event necessary because effective — this opinion is founded only on our not knowing all the causes that contribute to each effect. Marion's note develops the rapprochement and then, importantly, limits it. Spinoza's scholium that a thing is called contingent for no other reason than a defect in our knowledge is literally anticipated here, and contingency does not really determine the thing but attests that we have of it only an inadequate knowledge. But one must not yield too easily to the opposition between a Cartesian free will presupposing contingency in the world and a necessity of natured nature excluding free will: Spinoza in fact repeats Descartes' initial position, since from the epistemic standpoint and on *cogitatio* contingency has no occasion to appear, and the requirement of order for *cogitatio* and the dictate of reason for the *causa* banish in principle the least interruption of the *series rerum*. The difference intervenes only afterwards, in interpreting the observation of necessity — Spinoza transposes the necessity of natured nature into ethics without distinguishing orders or domains, while Descartes refuses to think physical events and the freedom of the human mind according to the same representation of necessity, and so risks thinking two literally contradictory statements without elimination as in Spinoza or conciliation as in Malebranche and Leibniz; here as often, Marion suggests, Descartes points directly toward Kant. Necessity may not be effectively known, but it is in principle postulated, so as to reduce the very possibility of a possible event to impossibility: the future merely ratifies effective necessity without doing justice to the advent of the least possible. A future without a to-come, a possible under necessity — desire renounces entirely the representation of its object as to come. Effective presence persists to the point of dominating, as necessity of itself, the future and the possible. To the earlier paradox that the past opens the modality of possibility, a second and symmetrical one is added: the future closes according to the modality of necessity. The categories of modality therefore temporalize themselves on a strict but strange schema — past to possibility, present to effectivity, future to necessity.

Can the paradox be confirmed and understood (pp. 210–214)? Marion asks whether the two times of the paradox proceed from a common character, and finds that they do. The past opens onto possibility insofar as it suspends, by inattention and memory, the empire of effective presence; inversely the future sinks into necessity insofar as it lets itself be represented from the effective presence that perseveres into it. In both temporalizations the two modalities enter into play only by a common opposition to the presence of effectivity. Confirmation comes from returning to desire: it is now established that so long as one holds to the representation of the event and to knowledge of the object's causes, not the least possibility appears and so not the least freedom. Does it follow that one must renounce the possibility of a freedom? Not at all, on condition of acting *as if* necessity might not intervene, even though we know certainly that it does — for perhaps the decree of Providence is such that if we go by the road held safest we shall not fail to be robbed, and on the contrary we could pass by another without any danger; and we must not for all that be indifferent in choosing one or the other, nor rely on the immutable fatality of that decree. The ambiguity of this double position redoubles the ambiguity of the third class of desires, those that depend on us and on others: since in fact we are ignorant of the causes that impose necessity on the event, everything happens as if it remained possible, and one must act as if it were undetermined — undetermined for representation. That what is at issue is transgressing the representation of present and effective evidence is confirmed by the argument Descartes always invokes to justify assuming a freedom and a possibility that are unrepresentable: freedom is not represented but *experienced*. And the experience imposes itself whatever the theoretical arguments contradicting it: from whomever we may be, however powerful and however deceiving he be, we nonetheless experience this freedom in us; I shall certainly rejoice in my freedom, since I experience it in myself and you attack it by no reason but only by bare denials, and I shall perhaps deserve greater credit with others because I affirm what I have experienced and what anyone can experience in himself, than you who deny the same for no other reason than that you perhaps have not experienced it. Freedom is not represented, Marion explains, because representation implies the presence of the object to *cogitatio*, and every presence tends to persist in its state and so to prolong its effectivity into necessity; freedom becomes possible — accedes to the possible as its proper domain — only in overstepping present representation. The past found the possible by suspending present evidence through inattentive memory; here freedom conquers its possibility only by opposing to the present evidence of *cogitatio* the irrecusable and therefore unrepresentable experience of arbitrary choice. Freedom exposes itself in the very measure in which it experiences itself as the possibility of the impossible. Whence an aporia Descartes constructs and assumes clearly. On one hand we understand that God's omnipotence entails his prescience and so necessity, and this we grasp sufficiently to perceive clearly and distinctly that it is in God. On the other we understand that this necessity contradicts the freedom of which we remain intimately conscious: we do not comprehend it sufficiently to see how it leaves human actions undetermined, whereas of the freedom and indifference that is in us we are so conscious that there is nothing we comprehend more evidently and more perfectly. How are these contradictory requirements to be conciliated and understood? Marion insists on the Cartesian decision here: Descartes decides without attempting a conciliation doubtless impossible for cogitative representation; he decides that he *can* decide, in favour of freedom, even if he cannot comprehend how he can; he decides to take a decision he cannot comprehend and whose possibility he cannot represent, because he experiences it beyond the *cogitatio* present to effectivity — for it would be absurd, because we do not comprehend one thing which we know must by its nature be incomprehensible to us, to doubt another which we comprehend intimately and experience within ourselves. Necessity yields not to a cogitative representation of possibility, since representation necessarily produces presence, but to the incomprehensible experience of freedom, which opens the possibility of an event to come only in quitting the face-to-face of presence with the present, and so in overstepping *cogitatio*. Just as inattentive memory gave rise to the past and not the reverse, so the decision in favour of freedom opens the possibility to come, and not the reverse. In both cases past and future work to the profit of the single possibility, even if in the second case the future began, in letting itself be understood and represented, by consecrating necessity. The ultimate distribution of the categories of modality across time must therefore be rectified: past to possibility without representation, by inattentive memory; present to effectivity extended into necessity by representation of the persisting present evidence in the present; future to the possibility of a to-come without representation, by the decision in favour of experienced freedom. In every case the ego accedes to modalities of being other than presence, or to temporalizations other than the present, by accomplishing a single and identical gesture — transgressing the *cogitatio* of present evidence, such as it presents to itself the persisting presence of the being. Because effective presence engenders itself as the necessity of its own perseverance, a single modality exceeds the necessary present: possibility. And possibility opens onto past or future only insofar as these themselves overstep the present of cogitative representation. Effective existence temporalizes itself so radically in the present, and the present accomplishes itself so intimately in *cogitatio*, that in Cartesian terms only the abandonment of *cogitatio* and of effectivity opens access to the possible, and in consequence to the possibility of past or future. The third and last question about the egological deduction of time is thereby answered: past and future confirm Descartes' temporal interpretation of the being of beings, and in it the primacy of presence in the present, since the ego can overstep persisting presence only by renouncing at the same stroke, in order to accede to the possibility of the past or the freedom of the future, the ordinary exercise of *cogitatio*, namely representation by and in present evidence. Temporalization according to presence thus discriminates effective beings from beings that no longer are or are not yet, but above all it forbids the ego to think outside presence to self. The equation of being and thought itself temporalizes itself according to the present persisting in the present; the ego could not have deduced subsisting substantiality from itself had it not first accomplished presence to self persisting in the present. Time — according to the present — prepared the egological deduction of substance from the outset.

The ego therefore accomplishes its metaphysical function fully (pp. 214–216): not only does it impose itself as the being par excellence, but it determines from its own mode of being the universal mode of being of all beings. From the ego, and because *ego autem substantia*, every being will, in order to be, be or not be in the mode of substance; likewise from the ego, and because I perceive that I now am, every being will, in order to be, last or not last in the mode of presence persevering in the present. The ego does not so much belong to Cartesian metaphysics as constitute it for a decisive and perhaps exclusive part. Yet the conquest of these positively metaphysical results reveals a fault line in the ego. It can accomplish itself as the operator of *cogitatio* only by holding to present evidence and remaining fixed in its own persisting substantiality, without possibility or freedom; and inversely it accedes to possibility only by renouncing present evidence, and can exercise its freedom only by renouncing comprehension of it, in order merely to experience it. Does the ego split, then, into a sovereign but fixed operator of *cogitatio* and a free possibility obscured to itself? Marion's answer names what he takes to be Descartes' distinctive virtue: here as often, his almost unique greatness lies in his capacity to confront an internal contradiction, because what is at issue is not an incoherence of reasoning but the duality of the things themselves. Otherwise put — if the ego perfectly acquires a metaphysical status, is it perfectly *exhausted* in it? The possibility of overflowing the persisting presence of *cogitatio*, and as it were of passing through the mirror, attests itself in the possibility of an incomprehensible freedom. When Descartes substitutes for all other primitive notions freedom and the union of soul with body, explicitly renouncing their conciliation with the preceding ones, does he not admit a non-cogitative and non-substantial ego? The note assembles the evidence and is worth following. On one side, we are as assured of our free will as of any first notion, for it truly is one — which means that free will depends on no theoretical presupposition but presupposes its own *factum* and inaugurates an absolutely new order of reasons, since being primitive, each such notion can be understood only through itself. On the other, the union of soul and body constitutes a primitive notion, since for soul and body together we have only the notion of their union, on which depends the notion of the force the soul has to move the body and the body to act on the soul; and here the union remains inaccessible to the understanding alone, even aided by imagination, so that one must have recourse to the senses and to ordinary conversations in order to comprehend it. The union thus shares with free will, besides the rank of primitive notion, the obligatory recourse — in order to take the least knowledge of it — not to *cogitatio* but to a more confused experience that delivers no object to the regard of a present evidence. And to this retreat of *cogitatio*, as mode and as object of knowledge alike, answers the disappearance of *substance* in defining the ego: in the *Passions of the Soul* the term is reduced to a hapax designating only the brain, and never the *mens* or the ego. Marion then puts the alternative in its sharpest form: am I, as ego, because and insofar as I exercise the *cogitatio* persisting in presence, or because and insofar as, as free possible, I am destined to die? Heidegger supplies the formulation — the certainty that I am that very thing in my having-to-die is the fundamental certainty of Dasein itself, and the authentic enunciation of Dasein, whereas the *cogito sum* is only its appearance; if over-sharpened formulas can mean anything, the appropriate and pertinent enunciation for the essence of Dasein would be *sum moribundus*, and not *moribundus* as gravely ill or wounded, but: insofar as I am, I am *moribundus* — it is only the *moribundus* that first gives Dasein its sense. The section closes on a question rather than a verdict. Is this only a meditation proper to the existential analytic of Dasein, in which the Cartesian ego would occasionally undergo an ambiguous interest — or is it an attempt to tear the ego of *cogito, sum* from the metaphysical status it has just attained under our eyes, and to attempt directly, too directly no doubt to succeed, an overcoming of metaphysics on the Cartesian ego? The note appends Derrida's formulation of the same movement: *I am* originarily means *I am mortal*, and the movement leading from the *I am* to the determination of my being as *res cogitans*, hence as immortality, is the movement by which the origin of presence and ideality conceals itself in the presence and ideality it makes possible.

## IV. God

### § 16. The Question of the Divine Names

A frontier separates the ego from itself (pp. 217–222), or rather separates a strictly metaphysical acceptation of the ego, which is then in the mode of permanent subsistence, from another and rarer acceptation, less easily thematizable, in which the ego exercises possibility and even impossibility. Before that second face of the ego can be identified, the reality and legitimacy of such a separation must be confirmed, since it claims to manifest nothing less than limits of metaphysics — and Marion puts the risk to himself squarely: are we not imprudently attributing to Descartes either a damaging incoherence or anachronistic interrogations? The distinction must therefore be repeated on another being, God, and four motives indicate the route. First, the ego accedes to its metaphysical status, and so eventually escapes it, only from the equivalence of thought with being that Hegel posited as the fundamental metaphysical thesis; and that equivalence, as Hegel often insists, concerns not only or first the ego but God, since the unity of thought and being given here in the form of God is no other representation than the one given in the *cogito, ergo sum* — being and thought inseparably bound. The theoretical decision that metaphysically institutes the ego also metaphysically enthrones God; and just as they share a similar metaphysical status, they might similarly arrange it and eventually transgress it, in a measure at least partial that remains to be determined for each. Second, there is reason to suspect that God enters metaphysics by an equivocal institution, namely the redoubling of onto-theo-logy released earlier, in which God receives not one but two metaphysical sites, first as the most cogitable being and then as the being *causa sui*. The distortion deserves clarification the more in that it deepens into a second disequilibrium: of these two sites only the second offers God the rank of being par excellence, of the *causa sui* that accomplishes in its effectivity the universal determination of every being *ut causatum*; in the first site, on the contrary, God does not accomplish the universal determination of the being *ut cogitatum*, since he too remains a *cogitatum* submitted, despite his divinity, to the being par excellence, the ego as the sole *cogitatio sui*. The inquiry can no longer evade this multiform aporia and must ask whether the two metaphysical names attributed to God can hold together without contradiction, and so whether they exhaust the Cartesian thought of God and confine it strictly to the metaphysical domain marked out by the redoubled onto-theo-logy. Third, Descartes consciously attempted to determine, by and beyond his existence, the nature of God, since he claimed to have thought his attributes: he proved very expressly that God was Creator of all things, and together all his other attributes. Admitting the idea of God necessarily implies admitting divine attributes, and whoever denies the implication contradicts himself — how could he assert that these attributes, infinity, incomprehensibility, and an infinity of others expressing God's greatness, belong to him, if he had no idea of him? So the attributes are as inseparable from the idea of God as the idea of God is from the attributes: to say that God is and that some attribute or perfection belongs to him would be to say nothing, since it would carry no signification to our mind, which would be the most impious and impertinent thing in the world. The idea of God can therefore be known — and we do know it, since we have the idea of God — only if its attributes are clarified with clear evidence. The *Meditationes* accordingly undertake an inquiry into the several attributes of God of which we recognize some vestige in us, on the presupposition that we understand actual existence to be necessarily and always conjoined with the other attributes of God; so that inversely God's existence cannot be separated from the other attributes and demands their study. When God's existence is established for the first time it gives rise to a contemplation immediately widened to the attributes — to dwell in the contemplation of God himself and weigh his attributes; and when his existence is about to be established demonstratively a second time, Descartes mentions the divine attributes in advance, since many things remain for him to investigate concerning God's attributes. The examination of God's attributes being an integral part of the project of demonstrating his existence, Descartes had therefore to decide, in studying them, more clearly than has yet been seen, about his essence — and so, in the terms of this inquiry, about the coherence of the various definitions applied to God and their belonging, or not, to the metaphysics designated by the redoubled onto-theo-logy of *cogitatio* and *causa*. The fourth argument is extrinsic but confirming. Descartes acknowledges once, in passing, to have said nothing touching the knowledge of God that all the theologians do not also say; and although in context this concerns the possibility of a natural knowledge of God in general, it is permitted to hear the protestation as the index of a discussion, or at least of a tacit confrontation, with contemporary theologians, particularly on the determination of the divine attributes. How could Descartes have been wholly ignorant of theological debates famous in his time? Marion suggests a hypothesis here rather than asserting a source: Lessius, a Jesuit and former pupil of Suarez, born at Antwerp in 1554 and dead at Louvain in 1623 after teaching there with distinction and no little controversy, had published a treatise on the divine perfections and characters in 1620 and then, posthumously and at the very moment of the drafting of the *Meditationes*, a treatise on the fifty names of God in 1640. Without claiming, since no argument suggests it, that Descartes underwent his direct influence or even read him seriously, Marion will not exclude the possibility of an at least indirect acquaintance — adding drily that experience shows daily that such a relation is not the weakest. The rapprochement would have a double utility: allowing a comparison, in certain delicate cases, between Cartesian decisions on the divine attributes and those of a recognized theologian; and above all reinscribing the Cartesian enterprise of giving metaphysically rigorous names to God, and to the God of Christian revelation, within the constant theological debate played out in the treatise of the divine names, which, thematically inaugurated by Denys the Areopagite, traverses the whole Middle Ages and finds one of its last notable representatives precisely in Lessius. What Marion finds remarkable in Lessius is his insistence on maintaining the Dionysian distinction between knowledge of God by affirmations or positive concepts and knowledge by negations or negative concepts. The pair permits naming God at once as a most sublime, most good, most great, eternal, most powerful, most wise, most benevolent, most holy, most just, most merciful, most beautiful spirit, present to all things, inwardly creating, forming, conserving, governing and ordering all things to his glory as their first principle and ultimate end; and inversely as an infinite, immense, eternal spirit raised infinitely beyond all perfection, excellence and greatness conceivable by a created mind — beyond all substance, all power, all wisdom, all understanding, all light, all beauty, all holiness, all justice, all goodness, all beatitude, all glory, so that he is properly none of these things, like none of them, and infinitely more sublime and more elevated than they. This double statement defines the horizon within which the Cartesian determination of God's attributes appears not only more intelligible, in its innovations as in its resumptions, but as a manner of treatise of the divine names. Marion states the proposal plainly: to read the Cartesian discussion of God's attributes as a metaphysical repetition of the theological treatise of the divine names. Only on that condition will it become possible to answer the crucial question of how and within what limits God enters into metaphysics with Descartes' redoubled onto-theo-logy. The rapprochement with Lessius constitutes more than an arbitrary recourse — a precious landmark, and one all the less illegitimate in that among other common points Lessius and Descartes closely bind the infinite to incomprehensibility: when Descartes retorts to Gassendi that for the idea of the infinite to be true it must in no way be comprehended, since incomprehensibility itself is contained in the formal *ratio* of the infinite, he seems to be citing Lessius, that God by reason of his infinity is incomprehensible. And the note extends the line: if Descartes' formula depended, even distantly, on Lessius, it would go back directly to Denys and through him to the whole tradition of the Fathers — a fascinating hypothesis Marion leaves open.

The *Meditationes* evoke the name or names of God from their first moment, the universal setting-in-doubt (pp. 222–223), yet no explicit definition of God is then fixed, for rigorous reasons Marion will specify later. Only with the third Meditation do developed formulations of God's essence and attributes intervene, to sustain the proof of his existence — and at that point Descartes enters, consciously or not, into the debate on the divine names. Two formulas are introduced. The first is the idea through which I understand a certain supreme God, eternal, infinite, omniscient, omnipotent, and creator of all things that are besides himself. The second is that by the name of God I understand a certain infinite substance, independent, supremely intelligent, supremely powerful, and by which both I myself and everything else, if anything else exists, whatever exists has been created. These two statements, which Marion will cite from here on as [1] and [2], frame the proof of God's existence said to be by the effects, which opens shortly before [1] and closes shortly after [2]. The very localized situation at once raises a multiform difficulty. Both [1] and [2] evidently intervene only within the frame of the second onto-theo-logy, since [1] appears on the same page where, in a prodigious relaunching of the order of reasons, *substantia* and the principle of universal causality also surge up. What link do these three theses maintain, if their textual proximity is not conceptually fortuitous? Do the divine attributes stated by [1] and [2] confine their validity to the onto-theo-logy of the being *ut causatum*, or do they overflow it in advance? And can [1] and [2], both introduced by the same *intelligo*, claim to give a real definition of God and his attributes, or must they propose themselves as simple working hypotheses? In that case, how is it to be understood that this idea of God can at once remain hypothetical and be effectively given to us — if the idea of God be given, as it is manifest that it is given; and, we have the idea of God? Such difficulties admit no rapid or easy resolution and demand a detailed examination of each attribute successively mentioned by [1] and [2]. Only on that condition will the objection of *inconsistency*, raised by Frankfurt, find better than a formal and superficial confirmation or refutation — a measured validation, historially and conceptually measured.

*Quaedam*: the first determination of God is indetermination itself (pp. 223–226), and the two formulas agree on it, since the *quaedam* of [2] answers the *aliquis* of [1]. Masked in the French translation by a simple indefinite article, this indetermination constantly determines all the earlier mentions of God in the *Meditationes* — or rather all the sketches of definition of what, before the first demonstration of the existence of the true God, could lay claim to the title. Two occurrences confirm it. At the end of the first Meditation, when Descartes constructs hyperbolic doubt by invoking, equally and indifferently, the two contrary hypotheses of an all-powerful God and an evil genius, he gathers them nonetheless in the imprecision of *some such powerful God*, a phrase the French does not render. And when the third Meditation recalls at its opening the final situation of the first, it again evokes *some God*. So regular a marking would not occur if indetermination had no essential function, and Marion finds it several. First, throughout all the moments of the reasoning that precede the first proof of the existence of a God, Descartes reasons with no yet precise concept of God, or rather hesitates among several hypotheses: a God who can do everything and so can also let me deceive myself; a *Deus fictitius* identifiable indifferently with fate, with chance, with the necessary order of nature — in short with any mode of deception whatever; or an evil genius itself indefinite. The indefinite evil genius can be imagined only insofar as the concept of God invoked until then remained itself fundamentally indeterminate. And the whole second Meditation works inside the *determined* hypothesis of a decidedly indeterminate God: the ego discovers itself certain of itself only in struggling against an uncertain God, whence the decidedly undecided denominations — there is some God, or by whatever name I call him, who by virtue of that very indefinition can also become *some deceiver I know not what*, and so properly *some deceiver*. In fact, before the proof by the effects, Descartes reasons not on the hypothesis of God, nor of the evil genius, nor of chance, necessity or fate, but on the determined hypothesis of an indeterminate — the only point common to all these hypotheses lying in their very indetermination. Before the proof by the effects the ego confronts only an adversary dissimulated by its own indetermination, one that names itself with, for sole proper name, a name so common that it is not even a name: *aliquis*. When the definition of the third Meditation begins, in both [1] and [2], with a quantifier of indetermination, it therefore says something rather than nothing: it takes up in one word the only proper character of the earlier hypotheses, their radical impropriety. The *aliquis* of [2] adds an indetermination to the later and precise qualifiers less than it *opposes* the indetermination acquired earlier, as sole determination, to the determinations to come. Of what he tries for the first time to define categorically as God — and no longer merely as some definable whatever, provided it be other than the ego — Descartes says first the only thing he knows of it by experience at that instant: that it is indeterminate. By God, I understand someone indeterminate, *aliquis*. Marion turns the theatrical figure with care: here it is not the ego that could say *larvatus prodeo*, but God, who like Voetius later does not come forward except masked. God accedes to the ego only under the mask of the role he has until then held on the stage of the earlier Meditations — that of an *aliquis*, and so under the most dissimulating of masks, that of the most total indetermination. Contrary to theology, which says negations of God only after exhausting the affirmations, the ego begins here by saying of God that he is named *nescio quis*, an I-know-not-who: a negative philosophy in which one must recognize the echo, doubtless barely conscious, of the negating moment of the divine names — all things are denied of him, Lessius confirmed, because he is above all reason and every species conceivable by a created mind. But Descartes is distinguished from the common opinion Lessius still formulates on two counts, and Marion presses both. He begins with indetermination instead of reaching it after affirmations themselves negated in turn; and he practises denegating indetermination only *by default*, not by excess — indetermination provisionally stands in for the affirmations, it does not correct them after the fact. The negative moment therefore loses its theological originality: far from leading to a surpassing of predicative and categorical discourse in general, it still aims at that discourse and aspires to it, the more so in that it plainly designates its lack. Whence one senses that affirmations will be able to establish themselves further on which no negation or indetermination will temper, opening metaphysical discourse on God's essence to the threat of idolatry.

Indetermination is justified by a second function as well (pp. 226–227). Hyperbolic doubt can exercise its radical epochē on true science — for it destroys Cartesian science itself — only by confounding two different characteristics. There must first be an instance offering enough omnipotence to disqualify all mathematical and logical rationality; and that instance, itself absolutely unsurpassable, must overstep the humanly unsurpassable conditions of science, and so identify itself in one way or another with God.
