But on reflection the two characteristics contradict one another: as soon as their extreme consequences are developed they can be stated in the untenable paradox of a deceiving omnipotence, directly with the evil genius or indirectly with an all-powerful God who creates the conditions of my self-deception; and as soon as the question of its logical possibility and non-contradiction is posed, the initial hypothesis of the *Meditationes* appears not only hyperbolic but incoherent. Descartes does not dissimulate this, and outside the strict and provisional order of reasons he will always answer objections of atheism by saying that an all-powerful God can neither deceive nor let one be deceived: it is inept to add that God is thought of as a deceiver, for although in the first Meditation he spoke of some supremely powerful deceiver, the true God was in no way conceived there, since as Voetius himself says it cannot happen that the true God be a deceiver — and if he be asked whence he knows this cannot happen, he must answer that he knows it because it implies a contradiction in the concept, that is, because it cannot be conceived. To Burman he avows that the author speaks contradictories, since malignity cannot subsist with supreme power; and again, the author speaks contradictories if he says most powerful and evil, because power and malignity cannot subsist together. But it is precisely this conceptual contradiction that the march of the *Meditationes* provisionally requires; and to render it tolerable as a simple hypothesis not yet refuted, it must be attenuated and even dissimulated — which is what the indetermination of an *aliquis* has the function of accomplishing. The instructive debate on God's omnipotence opposed, or not, to the merely very great power of the evil genius therefore has in the end only a very limited stake, as does the distinction in general between these two operators of hyperbolic doubt; for the single indetermination suffices, despite their incompatibility by right, to confound them in a role as provisional as it is unified.

There is more, since indetermination also lifts another difficulty, again provisionally (pp. 228–229). One could and even should raise an objection to the primacy of indetermination as first determination of what lays claim to the title of God. The evil genius, the *Deus fictitius*, chance, necessity and fate all intervene by virtue of a single common point, the function of omnipotence, by which they could, whatever they are, disqualify the evidence of order and measure; omnipotence would thus precede indetermination itself, and nothing would submit to indetermination except in order to accede to the empire of power. And in fact, as will be seen, omnipotence constitutes an essential qualification of the Cartesian God. But precisely, one must ask by what right it enters the order of reasons *before* any clear and distinct knowledge of God's essence or existence — whence comes it that before envisaging God in the strict sense and as such, it should already be possible and licit to mobilize the idea of an all-powerful God? Descartes' answer is constant: there is a certain old opinion fixed in my mind, that there is a God who can do all things, says the first Meditation, relayed by the parallel in the third, this preconceived opinion of God's supreme power. Omnipotence qualifies something like God, but solely as an *opinion*. One can prove elsewhere that such an equivalence has its origin in Ockham's nominalism; but what matters here is only the modality by which a positive doctrine, and so one foreign to the order of reasons, can nonetheless insinuate itself into it — on the mode of *opinio*, that is, of a confused thought, not rationally determined, without origin or reason, in short indeterminate. Omnipotence therefore precedes indetermination only in appearance, since in fact only its indetermination can allow it to enter the order of reasons, as an opinion without genealogy or status.

In opening the list of divine attributes and names with the reserve of an *aliquis* or *quaedam*, then, Descartes does not merely signal the inevitably provisional character of a definition still awaiting the answer to the *quid sit* as much as to the *an sit* (pp. 229–230). To see this it suffices to compare his procedure with Suarez's. Suarez too, observing the impossibility of defining God, had recourse at once, in order still to be able to reason, to a *praeconceptio*: in order that we may reason about God, it is necessary at least to preconceive and presuppose what is meant by this word. But the *praeconceptio* exercises for Suarez a function inverse to its role in Descartes, since it precedes and introduces a *correct* and universally receivable nominal definition of God, far from provisionally making possible an unreceivable contradiction: this name signifies a certain most noble being, which surpasses all others, and on which all others depend as on their first author, and which for that very reason must be served and venerated as the supreme divinity — for this is the vulgar and as it were first concept that we all form on hearing the name of God. The Suarezian precomprehension does indeed introduce, like the Cartesian preconceived opinion, a vague and indeterminate concept of God. But where in Descartes that indeterminate concept is contradictory, provisional and false, in Suarez it is already correct, and to that extent definitive and acquired. In the Cartesian situation indetermination forms an integral part of the concept, which without it would collapse; so that the true concept of God, if there is one, will have to be conquered from an indetermination already constitutive and forever irreducible. Imprecision belongs intrinsically to the concept of God, whether to dissimulate its impossibility throughout hyperbolic doubt or to permit its brutal irruption in the relaunching of the order of reasons. A new task therefore imposes itself: to interpret the constitutive indetermination of the concept of God no longer as the condition of possibility of a logical and theological impossibility, but as the way of access to a new conceptual deal. That the formulas [1] and [2] appear in the same sequence as the first occurrence of *substantia* takes on its full sense here — indetermination, as God's preliminary determination, opens onto substance.

### § 17. Substance and the Infinite

*Substantia*: the occurrence of the term characterizes not only formula [2], where by the name of God Descartes understands a certain infinite substance, but indirectly the definition [1] as well (p. 230), since [1] intervenes just after the first opposition between substance and accident, whose hierarchy permits the conclusion that a certain idea of God has more objective reality in it than the ideas through which finite substances are exhibited. The still indeterminate idea of God is really made precise, that is, by opposing itself not only to every accident but to every *finite* substance. And in fact Descartes shortly introduces the third term, *substantia infinita*, first in [2] and then in its sequence: it would not on that account be the idea of an infinite substance, since I am finite, unless it proceeded from some substance which really was infinite — otherwise put, I manifestly understand that there is more reality in an infinite substance than in a finite one. The Second Replies confirm it, that a substance has more reality than an accident or a mode, and an infinite substance more than a finite one; and the Third, that if there be an infinite and independent substance, it is more a thing than a finite and dependent one.

The introduction of the concept of substance confirms two acquisitions and raises one difficulty (p. 231). The first acquisition is the deep coherence of [1] and [2]: the one introduces substance into God so as to oppose him to accident and above all to *substantia finita*, the other positively determines that substance as *infinite*. The second is that Descartes oversteps the necessary imprecision of the God imposed by the contradictory hypotheses sustaining hyperbolic doubt — the inevitable indetermination of his first point of departure — only by constructing a second definition of something like God, this time solely from the new parameters imposed by the "certain other way still": causality, the non-representative status of the idea, and above all, here, substantiality. That [1] and [2] are built around and from *substantia* means that they belong throughout to the new beginning of the order of reasons; which is to say that without that beginning and without *substantia* no definition of God could overstep the initial indetermination. For Descartes, in short, God will be defined according to substantiality and causality or not be defined at all — or, what comes to the same, he will be defined by the indetermination of the *aliquis Deus*. But precisely: what does recourse to substantiality, as obligatory as it is deliberate, imply for defining God?

Consciously or not, and Marion says it hardly matters which, Descartes here meets an earlier theological debate that imposes on him a very precise conceptual difficulty (pp. 231–238): theologically, can God be named after substance? One might support a positive answer on the formula of a certain ocean of infinite substance, which invariably translated an expression of John Damascene. But the authority does not entirely lift a difficulty originating with at least three first-rank authors. Augustine holds that it is manifestly abusive to name God by the name of substance, and proposes to translate *ousia* by *essentia* rather than *substantia*. The motive lies in the very definition of substance, which implies the permanence of a substrate that thereby makes possible accidental qualifications from which it simultaneously distinguishes itself — so that it is mutable things devoid of simplicity that can properly be called substance. A body is indeed a substance, since qualities, accidents and attributes find their subsistence in it, on the Aristotelian pair of *ousia* and *sumbebēkos*. But in God the propria — greatness, goodness, omnipotence — do not come to be added as accidents in a different substance; everything in God is identically God, so that it is forbidden to say that God subsists and is the subject of his goodness, and that this same goodness is not substance or rather essence, and that God himself is not identically his own goodness but that it is in him only as in a subject. Anselm likewise refuses substantiality to God: substance is said principally of individuals, which consist to the highest degree in plurality, and those individuals to the highest degree stand under, that is, lie beneath their accidents, and on that idea most properly receive the name of substance. Substance implies accidents that it supports; the inevitable plurality of accidents implies in turn a plurality of substances; and that plurality finally contradicts divine unicity. Hence it is manifest that the supreme essence, which is substrate of no accident, cannot be called substance, unless substance be put for essence. Nor is such a refusal of *substantia* confined to the Augustinian tradition, since Aquinas confirms it as well — for one evident motive, that God has no ontic determination at all, being defined only as the pure act of being, where *esse* stands in for all *essentia* and a fortiori excludes *substantia*; but also because the traditional motive stays at work, presenting itself first as an objection, that God cannot be called an individual substance since matter is the principle of individuation and God is immaterial, and neither does he stand under accidents so as to be able to be called substance. The answer concedes the essential of the objection and claims the name of substance for God only in a now very restricted acceptation: substance suits God insofar as it signifies existing per se. Aquinas admits *substantia* to say God only by interpreting it first as a quasi-synonym of the divine *esse* held to be subsistent — God is *ipsum esse subsistens*. Marion draws the consequence exactly: subsistence in permanence returns to God without his having to bear the status of substance or the relation to accidentality and materiality that status implies; subsistence here dispenses with substantiality in order to reach the *actus essendi* directly, so that Aquinas rejoins by his own routes the Augustinian thesis. Descartes' decision to attribute to God the name of *substantia* cannot therefore pass for a banal repetition of earlier decisions. Nor would the echoes of the debate among his contemporaries allow the question to be supposed minor or settled. Gassendi confirms on one hand that God is usually excluded from the category of substance, and on the other contradicts that common opinion — God is wrongly excluded from the category of substance — treating divine substance, exactly as Descartes does, in strict parallel with divine causality: just as God is truly a cause and is therefore rightly counted among the causes, and as the first, so he deserves to be a substance and to be counted among them, and indeed as the first substance, since one would call God first substance on a far stronger reason than Socrates, Bucephalus or this stone. Gassendi is perfectly conscious of opposing a dominant thesis, and he is not alone. Goclenius in 1613 juxtaposes the two ways of justifying such an attribution, tangential univocity or restriction to being-by-self: substance is taken first generally, so as to suit God too, and second in a special sense so as to suit only creatures; substance is understood more properly and abusively — more properly in the most universal sense, so as to suit God as well, and thus the being really subsisting by itself. The perseity of subsistence alone legitimates calling God a substance. Eustache de Saint-Paul takes up the same solution, opposing to substance said *a substando*, as subject of accidents, a substance thinkable exclusively as existing by itself, or not existing in another, which is to subsist. Scipion Dupleix applies substantiality to God only in that restricted sense: God is a true and most perfect substance, not however predicamental or categorial, not being the subject or suppositum of any accident. Against this current Descartes appears as one of the partisans of a weak acceptation of substantiality that renders it acceptable for God — no longer the supposed subject of attributes but subsistence by self, not categorial substance but self-subsistence referred to itself: substances, that is, things subsisting per se; this is the very notion of substance, that it can exist per se, that is, without the help of any other substance; by substance we can understand nothing other than a thing that so exists as to need no other thing in order to exist. One can thus observe, in Descartes and his contemporaries, that the concept of substance itself undergoes a radical modification — it frees itself from all reference to attributes and comes down to subsistence by self. The principal objection that held back Augustine, Anselm and Aquinas doubtless disappears with it. But not every objection disappears, if perseity does not qualify God alone but also defines all the other substances. By itself the indisputable displacement of the sense of substantiality does not suffice to make intelligible why and how Descartes can determine God properly by the name of substance — since the first precise examples in the third Meditation of a substance apt to exist by itself lead not to God but to a stone or to the ego, *ego autem substantia*. If substance can define God, it can equally define correctly any finite extended or thinking thing. The new comprehension of substantiality opens a way of access to God only by stopping at once on a formidable aporia: the at least tangential univocity of a notion now indifferent. Descartes sees the danger, since he denounces it vigorously — the name of substance does not suit God and those things univocally, as they say in the Schools, that is, no signification of that name can be distinctly understood that is common to God and to creatures. And in the Schools substance does tend to become a determination taken in the same sense of God and of other things, if one believes Suarez: created substance agrees with uncreated substance not only in the *ratio* of being but also in the *ratio* of substance. Substantiality is therefore intrinsically attributed to the creature on the same footing as to God, in a finally indeterminate relation — even if God agrees in some manner in the *ratio* of substance with some created beings, it is not univocally but analogically. Keeping in mind the univocist drift analogy undergoes with Suarez and others, one senses that this stylistic reserve covers a univocity in fact, and it is that univocity Descartes stigmatizes. But seeing a danger does not suffice to avoid it. Though refusing all univocity of substance in its applications to God and to other beings, Descartes never manages to conceptualize the two different acceptations of a single term; he maintains the same concept and merely juxtaposes two contradictory uses of it. On the first, substance is said of a thing that needs no other in order to exist; in the strict and only true sense this definition admits a single consequence — a substance that needs no thing at all can be understood as only one, namely God, while all others we perceive to be able to exist only by the help of God's concurrence. In other words, if the old definition excluded applying substance to God, the new one excludes applying it to anything other than God alone; God alone is absolutely by himself and so is the only effective substance; God is not merely the only substance but *the* substance — which is already Spinoza's solution. Yet Descartes does not hold to that position, though he never explicitly refuses it. Without the least theoretical justification he juxtaposes to the first definition of the sole substance, God, a second definition of what he persists, without reason, in still naming substance: in the *Meditationes* it is the *ratio substantiae* common to extension and to the ego and echoing Suarez; in the *Principia* I, § 52 it is a *communis conceptus* which, moreover, in contradiction with the preceding definition, names as substances the things that need only God's concurrence in order to exist. Here the divine concurrence finds itself so to speak put out of play, so that it comes to the same — to the same denomination of substance — not to need God's concurrence and to need it totally. This is still the lexicon, if not the intention, of the very imperfect Suarezian solution; and the note records Marion's correction of his own earlier claim in *Sur la théologie blanche*: Descartes does not refuse the Suarezian doctrine of substance by confining it to the created, but *deduces* it, in the case of God, from the infinite. Descartes thus fails to elaborate a sufficiently articulated definition of substantiality to avoid an untenable alternative: either repeat Suarez or anticipate Spinoza. The three terms he hierarchizes in the third Meditation do not yet dispose, with the simple notion of substance, of a sufficient criterion, since substance without the help of another thing suffices to distinguish between accident and substance but not between created and uncreated substance.

This is why Descartes introduces a new criterion (pp. 238–240): the infinite, which determines, over against finite substance, the plenary substance. Marion stresses that the infinite appears in definition [2] in the very impulse in which its third term is explicitly fixed, "a certain infinite substance" — for only the appearance of the criterion of infinity makes possible the application of substantiality to God. God is not defined *first* as a substance and *then* qualified as infinite; on the contrary, God could never be called substance if Descartes did not understand substance, as regards God, as through and through infinite. The terms *substantia infinita* form a unique and indissociable syntagm, which is added with no sub-set common to the two preceding terms; or, on the hierarchy of perfection, accident is less than substance (always finite) which is less than infinite substance. One would even have to risk saying that substance, applied to God, becomes there a simple qualifier added to the infinite, which alone is substantival — less a matter of an infinite substance than of a substantial infinite. Marion concedes at once that the texts do not explicitly confirm this interpretation, and that God sometimes seems to let himself be named substance by simple arrangement: the substance which we understand to be supremely perfect, and in which we conceive nothing at all involving any defect or limitation of perfection, is called God. But that text, drawn from a set disposed *more geometrico* whose so strangely non-Cartesian order often alters the most Cartesian of conceptions, remains isolated; and it is above all counterbalanced by Descartes' own commentary on the syntagm *substantia infinita*: by infinite substance I understand a substance having true and real perfections actually infinite and immense; which is not an accident superadded to the notion of substance, but the very essence of substance taken absolutely and terminated by no defects — which defects, in respect of substance, are accidents, whereas infinity and infinitude are not. We are thereby clearly warned of three things: that the perfections defining infinite substance are themselves totally affected by infinity, so that their reality becomes in act synonymous with the infinite itself, which incidentally eliminates the new definition of substantiality; that this substance admits no accident, which eliminates the categorial definition the older theologians discussed; and that the infinite and infinitude, not constituting accidents added to substance, are identified with it. The definition of substance — the substance of substance — is therefore stated: the infinite. The infinite is not added, as regards God, to substance; it is substance that results from the originary infinite in God and only thus suits him. *Infinita substantia* rather than *substantia infinita*. The infinite comes therefore, in the sequence of attributes of [2], to be added lexically to substance because conceptually substance itself suits God only under the primordial condition of infinity. Just as the imprecision of the *aliquis* called for the initiative of substance, so substance calls in turn for the infinite.

*Infinita/infinitus [Deus]*: God is at last properly qualified as infinite (pp. 240–242), and this name in fact dominated Descartes' rational theology from 1630, against the vulgar who almost always imagine God as a finite thing and against most men who do not consider God as an infinite being. In 1637 the infinite already counts among all the perfections he could remark to be in God; the preface to the *Meditationes* recommended from the outset remembering that our minds are to be considered as finite and God as infinite; and even in the conversation with Burman God is defined by *infinita perfectio*. Marion calls this an absolute Cartesian invariant, at work in all Descartes' writings, and its privilege rests on constraining theoretical motives already established: the infinite alone permits attributing substantiality to God, and only that substantiality permits overstepping the inevitable indetermination and so entering the "certain other way still." With the infinite what is at issue is the highest Cartesian determination of God, which overhangs and permits all the terms of [1] and [2] and all other conceivable definitions. The infinite does not express merely one of God's propria among others, nor even directly the divine essence — that God's nature is infinite — since it defines God as such: I judge God to be actually infinite; there is in me a perception of the infinite, that is, of God; God is understood to be infinite. This excellence attests itself expressly by the anteriority of the infinite over the finite, of which it constitutes not the posterior negation but the a priori horizon: there is in a certain way in me a perception of the infinite prior to that of the finite, that is, of God prior to myself; and, in the thing itself God's infinite perfection is prior to our imperfection. Far from our reconstituting the infinite by negation from the finite, it is the finite which, despite the appearance of its positivity, results from a negation of the infinite, alone positive by right and in fact: nor is it true that the infinite is understood through the negation of end or limitation, since on the contrary every limitation contains the negation of the infinite. Marion then insists that the priority of the infinite must not be heard in a restricted sense. It is not merely a logical priority as negation of negation, nor an epistemological priority as the idea most clear and distinct, but the priority of an a priori: the infinite precedes the finite in that it makes possible experience and the objects of that experience. Doubt, and indeed all the finite modalities of *cogitatio*, find their condition of possibility in the idea of God as idea of the infinite; and Descartes insists on the paradoxical fact that it is by the same faculty and even at once that the ego perceives itself and apprehends the infinite, and so God. The whole force of the argument lies in that coincidence: the infinite precedes the finite not merely as a transcendent being but as the transcendental condition of the possibility of the finite. The idea of the infinite can paradoxically claim to be prior, first after a long route, only insofar as it reveals itself an a priori of the finite. The infinite therefore imposes itself as the first proper name of God on at least two motives: because it alone can render substantiality attributable to God without univocity, and because it makes God conceivable as the a priori of finite experience and of the finite objects of experience — the second motive reinforcing and perhaps sustaining the first, of which it constitutes the intimate truth.

The transcendental primacy of the infinite develops immediately into several further determinations (pp. 242–246), all of which take up the apparently negative but in fact supereminent and more-than-affirmative prefix that identifies the infinite: *in-*. Whence immensity, incomprehensibility, independence. *Immensitas* qualifies God directly, or through his essence, or through his power, or even through any of his faculties. In every case immensity, and more exactly *absoluta immensitas*, is to be heard in its most banal and strictest sense: what cannot be measured. Now since the *Regulae*, measure determines, from the order that commands and institutes it, the whole field of the method — there is method only in the domains of objectivity in which some order or measure is examined, which is how the *mathesis universalis* is exercised. Qualifying God by immensity, which affects all his properties in return, therefore comes to subtracting him from the domain of application of the method and excepting him from the sciences the method elaborates. Immensity does not take rank as one indication among others among the various divine properties; it marks, directly deduced from the infinite, God's methodical extra-territoriality. God, unmeasurable in consequence of infinity, has no place, at least in the method, which in this instance must admit a *non-place*. Once measure — and so the *mathesis universalis* — can no longer comprehend the divine determinations within itself, one must conclude that nothing in the definition of God, whether in [1] and [2] or in the other developments, will fall under methodical science, and so nothing can be treated in the mode of objectivity. Between methodical science, which uses all the simple natures indistinctly, and the knowledge of God, immensity — immediately deduced from the infinite — digs an impassable frontier. Nothing of God can be constituted as the object of a methodical science; nothing of methodical objectivity can be attributed to God. Whence *incomprehensibilitas*, the second determination, which Descartes deduces directly from divine infinity as well: God as incomprehensible and infinite; God's nature is immense, incomprehensible, infinite. The deduction appears in the same terms as in 1641 already in 1630, in one of the texts introducing the doctrine of the creation of the eternal truths — God as an infinite and incomprehensible being — and Marion insists the encounter is not fortuitous: if the eternal truths, and so also the simple natures the method sets to work, are created by a God, that God evidently oversteps the method and therefore appears to it as incomprehensible. If God's infinity oversteps in principle, through the creation of the eternal truths, the strictly objectivating method, then incomprehensibility will not betray an imperfection, a failure or an irrationality in the definition of God but on the contrary will attest its perfection, as the sign of another rationality. The infinite God is known not despite his incomprehensibility but *by* it; for human reason, striking against divine incomprehensibility, truly surpasses itself and transcends the finite that it is toward the infinite that is. Whence a doctrine as constant as it is paradoxical: one can know that God is infinite and all-powerful, even though our soul being finite cannot comprehend or conceive him; my understanding, which is finite, does not grasp the infinite; the infinite, insofar as infinite, is in no way comprehended but is nonetheless understood; for the idea of the infinite to be true it must in no way be comprehended, since incomprehensibility itself is contained in the formal *ratio* of the infinite; it belongs to the *ratio* of the infinite that it not be comprehended by us who are finite. With divine incomprehensibility it is not a matter of renouncing rational knowledge of God but of permitting rationality to know the infinite, and so to know, beyond the objectivity it masters methodically, the infinite as such, as incomprehensible to the finite. Incomprehensibility will even become the surest index that it is effectively God whom *cogitatio* knows, on the rule that nothing divine can be thought except as incomprehensible, and nothing truly incomprehensible can offer itself to *cogitatio* that does not in the end concern God. All the subsequent elements of definitions [1] and [2] will verify the rule and carry, with incomprehensibility, the mark of the infinite they illustrate. *Independens*: the attribute of independence in [2] is deduced immediately from the infinite on a fundamental equivalence — independence, being distinctly conceived, comprehends infinity in itself. The idea of God implies independence as necessarily as non-creation and substantiality par excellence: a clear and distinct idea of uncreated and independent thinking substance, that is, of God. Substance accedes in God to independence only by virtue of the infinite, over against the finite which will therefore be dependent: if there be an infinite and independent substance, it is more a thing than a finite and dependent one. The same opposition can be deduced directly from doubt, where divine independence appears as the transcendental a priori of the experience thus had of the ego's incompleteness: when I attend to my doubting, or to being an incomplete and dependent thing, so clear and distinct an idea of an independent and complete being, that is, of God, occurs to me. Independence is directly equivalent to aseity, as the French translation suggests when it glosses "if I were from myself" as "if I were independent of every other and were myself the author of my being." And if independence defines divine aseity as a priori, it also determines in advance everything that is not God as dependent. The concept of substance thus dislocates between the (substantial) infinite on one side and (substantial) dependence on the other; one might even risk saying that "substance" plays in both cases the role of a simple attribute of the infinite or of dependence. Since dependence always characterizes the created, nothing is more coherent than to qualify the created eternal truths as dependent: the mathematical truths you call eternal were established by God and depend entirely on him, like the rest of the creatures, and it is to speak of God as of a Jupiter or a Saturn, and to subject him to Styx and the fates, to say that these truths are independent of him; and again, the truths are not known by God as if they were true independently of him — a thesis of 1630 unchanged in 1641, where Descartes does not think the essences of things and the mathematical truths knowable of them independent of God, but thinks them nonetheless immutable and eternal because God so willed and so disposed. Even the problem of human free will will not modify the caesura between divine independence and the dependence of the finite, since man, though free, remains dependent. One must therefore conclude first that independence is deduced directly from God's infinity, and then that by that property the infinite determines a contrario what is not itself — so that definition [2], precisely because it is anchored in the infinite as such, now inclines, by the transition of independence, toward the finite, or rather lets infinity be declined from the point of view of the finite. And how, Marion asks, could the infinite give itself to be conceived except from the point of view of the finite that the ego is? The following attributes will therefore only explicate the infinite, managing the relation between infinite and finite either directly, as creator to creatures, or indirectly, by the passage to infinity of finite perfections.

### § 18. Power and Perfections

The denominations so far attributed to God share a common point (pp. 246–247): they are stated and justified negatively, since God refuses the finite, measure, dependence. These negations are doubtless corrected, first by the initial indetermination and then by the claim of the positive primacy of the infinite. Still, one can recognize in them the echo of the theological negations, all the more since those lead directly to the way of eminence, which transcends negation as much as affirmation by passage to excess. And a further confirmation of this theological identification of the first divine attributes and names offers itself: Descartes, beginning with infinity and attaching to it incomprehensibility, immensity and God's possible substance, recovers the very order Lessius followed in expounding God's names and perfections. Marion's note traces both of Lessius' sequences and the parallel is close — in the treatise on the fifty names, after two chapters on God and divinity, come infinity, then immensity, indivisibility, eternity, altitude, immutability, immortality, invisibility and, as for Descartes, incomprehensibility accompanied by ineffability, a series of theological negations; only then does omnipotence appear, making the transition to the affirmative attributes, wisdom, beauty, goodness, holiness, mercy, justice. In the treatise on the divine perfections the order is more significant still: God's infinity intervenes at once and engenders incomprehensibility, then immensity and immutability, and here again it is omnipotence that permits the passage to the affirmative perfections. Should one not then seek to determine a theological status for the qualifications about to follow? They too offer a common point: they open with the index of a superlative — *summe intelligens*, *summe potens*, *summe perfectum* — and the mention of the creator makes no real exception, since it results directly from the ego's consciousness of its own imperfection, from the inversion of the *summe*. The superlative still indicates eminence, but this time from affirmation and, in the end, on the mode of affirmation. Descartes therefore recovers here the theological affirmations — not *before* the theological negations, as Denys the Areopagite and Aquinas wanted, but after them. And the double consequence is the one that will govern the rest of the chapter. Eminence no longer constitutes a third moment, autonomous if not equivalent to the first two, but merely crowns the two ways without truly surmounting them; and the negations, positive though they are, and the affirmations, superlative though they are, since they issue in no third term, now risk being juxtaposed and even contradicting one another, with no arbitration or recourse — giving rise to an irremediable tension in the Cartesian doctrine of the divine attributes and names.

*Summe intelligens*: to this qualification of [2] answer the omniscient God of [1], the all-knowing perfect being of the *Discours*, and the *ens summe intelligens* of the *Principia* (pp. 247–248). In itself the attribute calls for no particular commentary, so evident does it seem that God should be attributed supreme intelligence. Yet not only in [1] and [2] but across the whole Cartesian corpus the attribute of supreme intelligence receives no remarkable elaboration. The discretion is the more noticeable in that the superlative *summe* imposes the parallel with the immediately subsequent attribute, *summe potens*, whose privilege has already been seen in the initial indetermination and whose prolongations will be traced. Whence an unavoidable interrogation: why such a disequilibrium between two attributes usually on the same footing? Why does supreme intelligence not hold a role comparable to omnipotence — why does it not develop into a supreme *cogitatio* answering to omnipotence, without for all that regressing into a distinction of faculties, understanding and will, in God? Marion's answer is offered as at least plausible though provisional: God is not essentially determined by intelligence, which Descartes therefore mentions only summarily.

*Summe potens*: this title, formulated by [2], confirms the *omnipotens* of [1] (pp. 248–251). Its occurrences in the third Meditation are not so numerous as to seem to demand particular commentary — but omnipotence does not surge for the first time in [1] and [2]. It appears already before the attempt at a real definition of God's true essence, precisely in the confused, non-critical but operative notion of a "certain" God who provokes hyperbolic doubt, whose notion Descartes identifies very clearly: this preconceived opinion of God's supreme power; there is a certain old opinion fixed in my mind, that there is a God who can do all things. The first and third Meditations answer one another very precisely: God gives himself to be thought as all-powerful from before the setting to work of the order of reasons, on an *old* and *pre*-conceived conception transmitted historically, *fixed* as an *opinion*. Omnipotence thus anticipates the order of reasons and precedes even the correct definitions of God; and its status as opinion takes nothing from this privilege in fact — first because that status constituted the only possible mode of appearance at that moment of the order of reasons, and above all because this determination of God according to a preconceived opinion finds itself subsequently confirmed in reason by [1] and [2]. Omnipotence of opinion becomes concept; the modality varies, the sense remains. Omnipotence therefore truly qualifies God in [1] and [2], but also, by anticipation, from the first Meditation, whose old opinion was definitively *true* though not yet certain. From before the lifting of doubt, divine omnipotence, which provokes that doubt, oversteps it and withdraws from it, being hyperbolically certain — for if omnipotence deceives me, it is certain that it is. God's omnipotence frees itself from the order of reasons, since it always plays its multiple roles there efficaciously though in different statuses. How is this exorbitant privilege to be understood, which no other term of [1] and [2] shares, of a theoretical validity indifferent to the order of reasons? In fact omnipotence does not exercise its privilege only in 1641. To what does the opinion long fixed in my mind refer? Before seeking probable historical origins, one must go back to the origin of Descartes' properly metaphysical career: from 1630, following a definitive decision, he thinks God as and by incomprehensible power. That acquisition, maintained in 1637 by the *Discours*, traverses the texts as a very ancient constant and, beyond the first and third Meditations, flowers in the Replies, which take up the 1630 syntagm to the letter — attending to the immense and incomprehensible power contained in his idea. The resumption permits a superabundant orchestration: infinite power, immensity of power, immense power, exceeding power, exceedingness of power. In defining God as *summe potens*, [2] does not merely anticipate the First Replies in justifying after the fact the hypothesis of the first Meditation; it designates a concept privileged by Descartes from 1630 to the end for defining God's essence — to the end in the chronological sense, but also because everything in God can be deduced from omnipotence, since God alone can give himself the whole of omnipotence at once, or the other divine perfections taken collectively. As an invariant, omnipotence determines the divine essence, whether epistemologically in 1630, hypothetically in the first Meditation, as only one of the divine attributes in the third Meditation and the *Discours* and *Principia*, or as the very essence of God — the immensity of power, or of essence — as in the Replies. Permanent, invariant, indifferent to the order of reasons, omnipotence refuses all parallel with other attributes such as supreme intelligence. Gilson saw in it nothing less than a new idea of God; Marion discerns in it the oldest and most constant Cartesian idea of God. But is he not thereby massively contradicting himself? He has posited omnipotence here as the primordial definition of God, having earlier established the infinite as God's proper name; and the contradiction cannot be dissimulated. Two things remain to be asked: whether it might be attenuated by interpreting the infinite itself as the infinite of an omnipotence; and whether this contradiction, provisional or definitive, does not betray a tension constitutive of Descartes' discourse on God — that is, whether definitions [1] and [2], indisputably built on the primacy of the infinite as God's proper name, do not themselves harbour the possibility, already under tension, of their displacement and even subversion by omnipotence, pregnant with another definition. Does the contradiction, finally, stigmatize the interpreter, fallible of course, or an incoherence characteristic of the rational theology of Cartesian metaphysics? Since omnipotence, instead of inscribing itself harmoniously in the order of reasons as the infinite does, deploys itself in complete indifference to it and authorizes itself by an anteriority without justification, it might well, precisely by the contradiction it exercises, put us on the track of an incoherence that is nonetheless architectonic.

*Et a qua tum ego ipse, tum aliud omne... est creatum*: this formulation of [2] confirms the creator of all things that are besides himself of [1] (pp. 251–253). Although Descartes claims to have proved very expressly that God was Creator of all things, he does not confer on the title *Deus creator omnium* in the metaphysical texts a frequency proportioned to its dogmatic importance. It exercises there two functions. Either that of a God who creates the ego such that the greatest evidences can in fact deceive it — there is a God who can do all things, by whom I was created such as I am; that he created me such that I am always deceived; there is no doubt that God could have created me such that I never erred. Or that of a God creator, among all things, of the theoretical truths, the eternal truths: you ask me in what genus of cause God disposed the eternal truths; I tell you it is in the same genus of cause as that in which he created all things, as efficient and total cause; for it is certain that he is as much Author of the essence as of the existence of creatures, and this essence is nothing other than these eternal truths. In 1644 the *Principia* still formulate the doctrine, always mobilizing the concept of creation: God omnipotent, source of all truth and goodness, of all things; and since God alone is the true cause of all things that are or can be, it is perspicuous that we shall follow the best way of philosophizing if we try to deduce from the knowledge of God himself the explanation of the things created by him, so as to acquire the most perfect science, which is that of effects through causes. Of these two functions of creation only the first appears in 1641 — but Marion argues that the first in fact holds in the *Meditationes* the role the second holds in the other texts. For God, or his provisional stand-in, could not overdetermine a perfect evidence in the eyes of my consciousness if he did not exceed that evidence itself, and so the truths and essences in general; and he can exceed them only insofar as he creates them and so distinguishes himself from them. The two metaphysical functions of the creating attribute therefore make only one. And by the same stroke it becomes clear that in every case creation is understood from efficient causality, mentioned in 1630 and 1644, itself directly deduced from divine omnipotence, mentioned in 1630, 1641 and 1644. Descartes adds no new qualification to his definition of God in mentioning that he is creator, since for him creation becomes intelligible only from omnipotence exercising itself as *ultima causa*. Marion states the reversal exactly: contrary to the nominalists, who justified God's metaphysical omnipotence by reference to theological creation, Descartes renders creation intelligible by reduction to omnipotence, itself rationalized as efficient cause. Which is also why definitions [1] and [2] do not *begin* with the creating function conceived as an *explicans* drawn from theology, but *end* with it — as an *explicandum* now rationally reduced to the preceding determinations and first of all to omnipotence. The attempt at the two definitions of God in the third Meditation ought to close here; and yet it does not, since a last name, strangely absent here, must still appear.

*Ens summe perfectum*: one must come to this statement because [1] and [2] suffer, besides the incoherence they introduce between the infinite and omnipotence, from a very visible incompleteness (pp. 253–257). They omit a number of the qualifiers that theologians, but also philosophers and among them Descartes himself, mention elsewhere: unity, eternity, veracity, goodness, beauty, justice. That the order of reasons cannot at first recense them all is admissible, but it cannot for all that eliminate them tacitly. Descartes, doubtless aware of the difficulty, therefore introduces within the third Meditation a kind of complementary definition which will serve above all in the fifth Meditation's effort to restate the a priori proof — call it [3]: the unity, simplicity or inseparability of all those things that are in God is one of the chief perfections I understand to be in him. It must be completed by the formulations surrounding it — all the perfections I attribute to God; all the other perfections of God; nothing is more perfect than he; the idea of all the perfections I attribute to God; all those I conceive to be in God; and finally, God, I say, that same one whose idea is in me, that is, having all those perfections which I can in any way whatever reach by thought. God becomes accessible to thought on a new mode: his idea, and so his existence, do not impose themselves at once as the idea of the infinite imposed itself earlier, but let themselves be *constructed* by the human understanding, which recenses perfections, reconciles them together, and then attributes them to God. Three operations follow. First recension: after unity, simplicity and inseparability, Descartes mentions goodness, truth — since God being the supreme being cannot fail to be also the supreme good and true — beauty, and one would even have to count among the perfections *summum ens* and above all *existentia*. In fact the list of perfections to be recensed for the new notion of God can never close, since all the perfections I could remark to be in God comprehend nothing less than absolutely all perfections whatever — God says such a thing as comprehends in itself absolutely all perfections. Quantitatively, God accumulates perfections indefinitely, and their recension will therefore always remain in principle incomplete. But God can legitimately receive these perfections by marking them with the infinite that characterizes him in the first place; so to the quantitative operation of recension the human mind must add a qualitative one, amplification. Descartes describes its functioning by recourse to a faculty of amplifying all created perfections, that is, of conceiving something greater or ampler than they; or a power of amplifying human perfections so far that they are known to be more than human. For it is clear that the perfections as the ego apprehends them in the recension still belong to the finite and to the created human; the idea of the infinite that inhabits the ego must therefore "stretch" each of them to infinity, in an operation that will render them incomprehensible but only thereby appropriate to the infinite. Only then can the perfections, quantitatively and qualitatively carried to the infinite, be attributed to God, conceived as the summit and accomplishment of perfections. God does not merely accomplish the summation of perfections but above all their elevation to the infinite power: from perfections infinite in number to the infinite of perfections. So God's highest perfection consists in the statement of perfection itself carried to the superlative — the idea of the most perfect being, that is, of God, as the third Meditation already states among other names; and thereafter the syntagm reappears constantly. The superlative of perfection, that is, the perfection of perfection, plays the role of dominant qualification of God, by its frequency in 1641 as by its permanence in 1637 and 1644. Definition [3] of God by perfection at the superlative proves its primordial role in sustaining the a priori proof of God's existence; but it attests it above all by its boundless capacity to integrate into a determination of God any perfection whatever, at the sole price of its passage to the superlative or the adjunction of the adverb *summe*. Marion draws the consequence carefully: [3] furnishes much less a definition of the divine essence than the *matrix* of an infinity of possible definitions of the divine essence, from any perfection whatever carried to infinity — the infinite accomplishing itself concretely in the summation of perfections, as Descartes indicates at least once when he says that the name *infinite* could more rightly be called *ens amplissimum*. The sum of perfections designates God as *ens perfectissimum*, in the manner of a divine name and not merely in the manner of a concept of the being in general — and Marion's note refuses on this ground Heidegger's thesis that God is simply the title for that being in which the idea of being in general is realized in the genuine sense, so that "God" is here a purely ontological concept and is therefore also called *ens perfectissimum*. God as *ens summe perfectum* ought therefore to appear in the first rank; and yet it does not, since this divine name, directly implied by the last attributes stated in [1] and [2], is explicitly thematized only with [3]. The tension between two divine names within each of the first two formulations is thus complicated by a new tension between those two formulas and a third, since [3] alone plainly states the third divine name presupposed but not explicated by the first two. Recensing the attributes that define God in the third Meditation, it now becomes possible to refer each to a precise divine name. To the divine name *infinitum* belong the attributes of substance, infinity, independence, immensity and incomprehensibility, all under the regime of the *via negativa*. To the divine name *potentia* belong the attributes of creation and supreme power, under the regime of eminence, since power remains at once incomprehensible, which is negation, and productive, which is affirmation. To the divine name *ens summe perfectum* belong the attribute of supreme intelligence, and further all the thinkable perfections cited in [3], under the regime of the *via affirmativa*. Descartes therefore recognizes three principal divine names, to which he attaches, following the three moments of the theology of the divine names as it had developed since Denys the Areopagite, three series of attributes of God. And if the classification is correct, a question imposes itself at once. Descartes simply juxtaposes, without even letting it be suspected that an organization was possible, three names, three modes of enunciation and three series of attributes — where the theologians, the ancient ones at least, disposed these terms in the coherent itinerary of a mystical theology. The rhapsody Descartes substitutes for that coherence does not eliminate it entirely; it only masks it, since it has just been reconstituted. But does it not expose itself to a disorder amounting to incoherence? Can three divine names juxtaposed without theologically justified organization deploy themselves without contradicting one another; does the want of any hierarchy not condemn them to unveil their incompatibility in the single field of metaphysical enunciation? In short, does the metaphysical resumption of the divine names without their theological situation not lead to tensions among them so strong that they appear, in the end, contradictory? The hesitations and imprecisions stigmatized in [1], [2] and [3] would then offer only the indices of a more essential conceptual distortion.

### § 19. The System of Contradictions

The task is now to test, so as to confirm or refute it, the irreducible plurality of divine names implied by the attributes enumerated in [1], [2] and [3] (pp. 257–258). The three names, approximately designated as *infinitum*, *potentia* and *ens summe perfectum*, are already opposed for one motive: each takes up one of the ways of mystical theology, but instead of organizing them along a single anabasis they merely juxtapose them without conceptual link — everything happens as though Descartes deployed successively but without conceptual connection a *via negativa* with the infinite God, a *via affirmativa* with God as *ens summe perfectum*, and finally a passage by eminence with *potentia*. Marion proposes to test the arrangement by two procedures, internal and external. Internally, one asks what conceptual ensembles drawn from the Cartesian texts underline the irreducibility of each of the three terms, and he advances a hypothesis with real explanatory reach: each of the three divine names is exposed in one of the three principal proofs of God's existence Descartes constructed — *infinitum* names God in the third Meditation, *ens summe perfectum* in the fifth, *potentia* in the First and Fourth Replies by way of the proof through the *causa sui*. The compatibility or not of the three divine names would thus be decided to the measure of the compossibility or not of the three principal Cartesian proofs; the organization of the proofs would reveal the disposition of the divine names they set to work and presuppose. Marion's note registers the methodological cost: this opposes him, at least methodologically, to Alquié, for whom the distinction of the various proofs Descartes employs could not be essential and their nature is better discovered by considering their unity. Externally, the divine names Descartes privileges have, like the corresponding proofs, a history; in identifying their respective genealogies, if only summarily, it might become possible to locate conceptual decisions sharper than their Cartesian consequences — the tensions among divine names appearing all the better for being envisaged diachronically and no longer only synchronically. Marion will therefore try to identify for each of the three names a historically probable and conceptually significant origin, so as to widen the debate on the Cartesian divine names into the debate on the history of the divine names in general. On that double condition it may become possible to situate the Cartesian decisions on God's essence within the framework of the theological treatise of the divine names — that is, really to broach the fundamental question: does God, for and through Descartes, enter into metaphysics, and if so under what names?

The first determination of God's essence, noted from here on as [A], apprehends him as infinite (pp. 258–264). In [1] and [2] the infinite commands substantiality, independence, immensity and incomprehensibility, and it also makes possible the proof said to be by the effects in the third Meditation. Can a historical origin be fixed for it? Caterus at least was convinced there could, and discussing the derived and pedagogical form of the proof by the idea of the infinite, which expounds it as a search for the cause producing that idea in me, he concludes that this is the very way Aquinas enters upon, which he calls the way from the causality of the efficient cause, taken from the Philosopher — except that those authors are not concerned with the causes of ideas. One can indeed bring together the proof by the effects, which issues in God as an *ultima causa* which will be God, and the second of Aquinas' five ways, that it is necessary to posit a certain first efficient cause, which all name God. But the limits of the rapprochement appear at once, and Caterus himself sees them clearly: causality is exercised not on beings, on a banal Aristotelian necessity of stopping, but on the idea of the infinite, and of an infinite understood positively, so that one must never stop anywhere. This is why Descartes discreetly but firmly declines the improper support of a Thomist authority — let him permit me, I beg, to keep silent about others and to render an account only of what I myself have written. Recourse to Aquinas, in fact inexact, seems also perfectly useless, since Descartes could have had direct access to two denominations of God by the infinite: Lessius, who makes it the first of the divine names, and Suarez, who establishes that the first and most essential division of being is into finite and infinite, so that God's manner of being consists in the infinite itself. Through this double intermediary Descartes finds himself in fact in relation with Duns Scotus, who had most plainly, if not perhaps first, thought God as infinite. The Scotist texts are worth following, since Marion leans on them heavily. The most perfect and at the same time simplest concept possible for us is the concept of infinite being; for it is simpler than the concept of good being, of true being or the like, because "infinite" is not like an attribute or a passion of being, or of that of which it is said, but says an intrinsic mode of that entity — so that when I say "infinite being" I do not have a concept as it were by accident, formed from a subject and a passion, but a concept per se of the subject in a certain degree of perfection, namely of infinity. Otherwise put, that most perfect concept we form about God is the concept of infinite being; for it is the simplest, most proper and most perfect of the concepts we conceive about God. It is the simplest because every other concept already extends to beings other than God, such as the true or the good; the most proper because it does not make number with God's other attributes but on the contrary overdetermines them, since the infinite is formally included in any attribute whatever as a degree of any perfection; and the most perfect because it contains virtually all the other concepts said of God. Like Descartes after him, Duns Scotus privileges the infinite *as a concept*, from the point of view of conception and so of knowledge in its particular requirements: God is said by the infinite as simpler, more proper and more perfect to conceive. By that presupposition both break with Aquinas, who, while recognizing infinity in God, subordinates it to *esse* at the expense of the epistemic privilege of conceptualization — since the divine *esse* is not an *esse* received in something other than itself but is itself its own subsistent *esse*, it is manifest that God himself is infinite and perfect. Here the infinite is deduced from subsistent *esse* and so remains a second attribute from the privileged point of view of subsisting beingness. Descartes therefore agrees with Duns Scotus on a second point, from which the first directly derives — the primacy of the concept over beingness. It is as conceivable that God must first be said as the infinite; and Descartes, concluding that he judges God to be actually infinite, cites so to speak Duns Scotus, that therefore some infinite being exists in act. The infinite is of course understood not negatively but positively; and besides Duns Scotus' authority Descartes could invoke Suarez's, that although the term infinite is negative, by that negation we circumscribe a certain supreme entity or excellence. Before the same paradox Descartes will say that there is in a certain way in me a perception of the infinite prior to that of the finite — that is, he will invert the literal negation by a semantic affirmation: nor is it true that the infinite is understood through the negation of end or limitation, since on the contrary every limitation contains the negation of the infinite. Marion adds that an objection of Hegelian style — that the infinite, remaining without determination or delimitation, offers no rationally thinkable content — would fall wide here, since the infinite is determined as first, simpler, more proper, more perfect, more intelligible, condition of possibility of the finite. In choosing the infinite as first divine name Descartes does not make a solitary start but inscribes himself in a constant tradition reaching him from Duns Scotus by way of Suarez in philosophy and Lessius in theology; and that he is not the first so to distinguish the infinite confirms all the more that it can count as first among the divine names. Having identified the status and origin of [A], it remains to determine how the *via negativa*, under which it indisputably falls, does not weaken its theoretical rigour — or again, how a denomination negatively obtained, even if it claims to be semantically positive, and itself the origin of a series of negations, can exercise the function of the chief argument for proving God's existence. It does not hold this privilege from its anteriority in the order of reasons, which depends on it, but from its intrinsic excellence; and that excellence follows from its characteristic property, incomprehensibility. Incomprehensibility results directly from the infinite, since for the idea of the infinite to be true it must in no way be comprehended, incomprehensibility itself being contained in the formal *ratio* of the infinite. And Marion's note makes two clarifications that matter more than the debate they set aside — whether the idea of the infinite has an ontological or a purely representative character, disputed between Alquié and Gueroult. Non-comprehension does not signify non-knowledge but a knowledge that does not construct its object according to the parameters of the *mathesis universalis*; so one can and must know God as infinite, at once without the clarity of the methodical object and with a greater evidence than it. And this deduction of incomprehensibility from the infinite does not prevent Descartes, on the same page, from speaking of a representation of the infinite in the manner in which a human idea must do so. The incomprehensibility of the infinite does not amount to the impossibility of knowing it; it fixes the particular mode of its knowability. The infinite is known without being comprehended, that is, without letting itself be constructed on the rules and parameters of the method as a measurable and ordered object, set in evidence and available for the mind. Marion recalls the massive textual fact: definition [A], and so the corresponding proof, intervene in a sequence in which, between the end of the second Meditation and the ultimate conclusion of the third, the evidence of the objects of the method finds itself suspended and disqualified. If God is nevertheless to be known, he must be known on another mode than the evidence of the objects of the method — whence an evidence which, by virtue of hyperbolic doubt, overflows and transgresses hyperbolically the evidence, then out of play, of the simple natures and of the created eternal truths. But then, one will ask, what do we really know of the infinite if it merges with the incomprehensible; what reality will keep its sense if we cannot at all apprehend it as an object? Descartes had already formulated the answer in 1630: we cannot comprehend God's greatness even though we know it, and the very fact that we judge it incomprehensible makes us esteem it the more, since his power is incomprehensible. The infinite transgresses by its incomprehensibility the truths commensurable with our mind, under the figure of power itself incomprehensible. The situation of 1641, in [1] and [2], stands thus in the direct line of the creation of the eternal truths of 1630 — whence a decisive consequence concerning [A]: it is *incompatible* with any determination of God that would maintain his essence accessible to the same evidence as the mathematical truths and the essences of finite objects; and if such a determination comes to light in the Cartesian texts, it will be inconsistent with [A]. There is a second consequence. [A] applies to God the determination of cause, as *causa ultima* in 1641 as already in 1630 with a cause whose power surpasses the bounds of the human understanding, an efficient and total cause. Yet that causality is here confined to defining God's action exterior to himself, in regard to creatures and to them alone; for cause belongs, since the *Regulae*, to the simple natures, and so presents a perfectly comprehensible and exemplary evidence — better, it produces, more and before any other simple nature, methodical evidence. It renders intelligible, among other things, the relation of God to the world. But precisely because it falls under comprehensible evidence and always produces it, causality cannot apply intrinsically to God: comprehensible causality is an extrinsic determination of the infinite and incomprehensible God, *ad extra*. Whence a second incompatibility: the determination of God as incomprehensible infinite will be inconsistent with any determination of God's essence from an *intrinsic* causality. This double incompatibility of [A] — first with the methodical objectivity and evidence of the created truths, then with causality as one of the simple natures — characterizes God as infinite, but above all decides in advance his relations with two other essential determinations of God.

The determination [B] announced itself already in the third Meditation, in formula [3], and also in [1] and [2] whenever they mobilized the superlative (pp. 264–270). But it develops fully only in the fifth Meditation, where it permits first defining God's essence and then proving his existence, previously understood as one of the perfections — whence the accomplished determination of God as *ens summe perfectum*. Under certain conditions it seems possible to refer this determination to the fourth of Aquinas' ways, which also claims to reach God from the superlative: there is something most true, best and most noble, and consequently a greatest being. But holding more exactly to perfection, one observes that though Aquinas posits in principle that whatever there is of perfection must be attributed to God, he does not think perfection for itself. As he had earlier deduced the divine infinite from *esse subsistens*, far from clarifying the one by the other as Descartes does, so here he derives divine perfection from God's *esse*. Several texts mark the reduction: God is pure act and simply and universally perfect, and in him no imperfection has place; the first being, which is God, must be the most perfect and consequently the best; the first being must be the most perfect, and it has been shown that God is the first being, therefore he is the most perfect. Perfection results here — immediately, certainly, but for that very reason as a *consequence* — from the more fundamental determination of God as *ipsum esse*, whose essence consists in *esse* itself. Aquinas thinks divine perfection from the *esse* of the divine being, whereas Descartes thinks divine perfection first and only afterwards eventually qualifies God by the name of being. Aquinas does not rest until he has led perfection, like all the other attributes, back to the more fundamental determination of the *actus purus essendi*, while Descartes, who in no way privileges the divine *esse*, accedes to God directly through the thought of supreme perfection. For situating [B] historically, then, Aquinas is doubtless not the best reference. Another offers itself at once to warrant Descartes' choice, Suarez: God is the first being, therefore he is also essentially the greatest and most perfect; it is therefore of his essence that he include in some manner all possible perfection in the total breadth of being. Through Suarez, Descartes rejoins an earlier tradition — and it remains to specify which. At first sight it seems evident, even to the best-informed critics, that one must have recourse to Anselm, both because of his presumed and real Augustinianism and as a corollary of the famous argument for proving God's existence taken up precisely by the fifth Meditation. But while the continuity on that point admits no contestation, Marion holds that the hypothesis of a borrowing by Descartes from Anselm for [B] should be admitted only with prudence. The texts of the *Monologion* mention perfection only rarely, and always subordinate it to beingness — he alone will appear to be simply, perfectly and absolutely, all the rest almost not being or scarcely being; each of the trinitarian persons is perfectly the supreme essence. The work of the *Monologion* bears less on perfection in particular than on the passage of every property to the superlative, with the intention of rendering it attributable to God; less on perfection, even supreme, than on the passage to the limit of each quality, in the direction of a common superlative that juxtaposes them all in God. Whence enumerations of qualities exercising the affirmative way: it is therefore the supreme essence, supreme life, supreme reason, supreme salvation, supreme justice, supreme wisdom, supreme truth, supreme goodness, supreme greatness, supreme beauty, supreme immortality, supreme incorruptibility, supreme immutability, supreme beatitude, supreme eternity, supreme power, supreme unity, which is nothing other than supremely being, supremely living, and other things similarly. Some Cartesian determinations of [1] and [2] are recognizable there — *summa potestas* is equivalent to *summe potens*, *summa veritas* announces *summe intelligens*, *summa aeternitas* recurs in *aeternum*, *summa essentia* and *summe ens* answer in advance to *substantia*, and likewise for certain qualifications of [3], since *unitas* corresponds to Cartesian unity and *incorruptibilitas* bears on *inseparabilitas*. Yet these qualities, even carried to their maximum of intensity, remain in the *Monologion* merely juxtaposed, without any deduction or organization concentrating them in a last, unique and infinite perfection. Where Descartes states an essential determination — God is the most perfect being — Anselm uses the superlative, and not in fact perfection as such, only to carry each quality to its maximum, but within its genus alone. For him perfection states not so much a name of God as it operates on each quality the passage to the limit. In that precise sense Descartes could find in Anselmian conceptualization no antecedent or help for his own denomination of God as *ens summe perfectum*; and indeed he always declined the rapprochement his correspondents kept repeating to him, declaring in December 1640 that he would see Anselm at the first opportunity, without ever afterwards giving the slightest indication of having done so. What author, then, could have preceded and possibly influenced him? Without a decisive argument, but taking account of other probable rapprochements, Marion risks the hypothesis of an affinity with William of Ockham, who explicitly defines God by perfection taken at the superlative and considered as unique: God is the most perfect being, wherefore, since he is in a certain manner knowable to us, he will be the perfect intelligible. Here particular perfection does not precede perfection understood as such but is rather deduced from it, from the perfection simply speaking which is God. That perfection taken simply as such can be understood in two very distinct though convergent acceptations: in the proper sense perfection suits only God; in the broad and so improper sense, common perfections are said first of creatures so as, by passage to the limit, to apply afterwards to God — in which case these common concepts remain purely nominal and do not reach the divine essence as such. The identification of such a nominalist antecedent for [B], if it secures it, at once raises a difficulty. For when Ockham privileges the *ens perfectissimum* as first divine name, he knows clearly that he is opposing himself — on this point as on so many others — to Duns Scotus. Where Scotus concludes that that most perfect concept we conceive about God is the concept of the infinite, reducing perfection to infinity, Ockham objects that the concept of infinite being is not formally in itself a more perfect concept than every possible concept about God, because no negative concept is formally more perfect than a positive one, and the concept of infinite being includes, beyond the concept of being, something negative. The clean-cut opposition indirectly confirms that the Cartesian determination [A] of God by infinity belongs, on its own proponent's account, to the *via negativa*, while the Cartesian determination [B] of God by perfection belongs, on its proponent Ockham's account, to the *via affirmativa* — and so fully justifies the initial effort to situate Descartes' proofs within a problematic of the divine names. But it raises an unavoidable difficulty. Descartes sustains at once and successively two determinations of God, [A] and [B], which historically were posited as adverse and irreconcilable; can the antagonism between Duns Scotus and Ockham disappear simply because the same author claims, in the same text, to use both theses? Must one not rather admit that in all likelihood the antagonism between Scotus, holding for the primacy of the infinite, and Ockham, holding for the priority of perfection, must be found again, barely veiled, inside the third and fifth Meditations? The hypothesis seems the less avoidable in that the majority of critics have noted, as an unexpected encounter in Hamelin's phrase, that on the threshold of the fifth Meditation and even at the end of the third the perfect replaces the infinite, as Beyssade puts it. And it suffices to recall the famous polemic this difficulty provoked: either the proof issuing from [A] depends on the *idea infiniti* secured by [A] in the third Meditation, with Alquié, Gouhier and Kenny, or the proof issuing from [B] remains perfectly independent, with Gueroult and Beck. In the one case, subordination implies without explaining it that a first divine name, *ens summe perfectum*, becomes a simple derived name; in the other, independence suggests a rupture of the order of reasons and so an irreducibility between [A] and [B], which could deepen into antagonism and contradiction. In every case the question is posed, and will have to be taken up: does the divine name of infinity not remain incompatible with the divine name of perfection, for motives not only historical but intrinsically and conceptually Cartesian?

There remains a third and last fundamental determination of God's essence and so of his existence, noted [C] (pp. 270–275). It appears in almost the same terms in the First and Fourth Replies. In the discussion with Caterus Descartes defines God as by himself, *a se*, positively, that is, as exercising a cause toward himself, and no longer negatively as withdrawn from the empire of causality — God is in a certain manner toward himself what an efficient cause is toward its effect. What stands in for a cause internal to God is nothing less than God's own essence, interpreted as an immense and incomprehensible power such that he perseveres in being; and this determination of the divine essence as power defines him at once as *summe potens* and recovers the very terms of [1] and [2], *omnipotens* and *summe potens*. Interpretation [C] would be neither useful nor necessary, Marion notes, if Descartes did not himself impose it in the name of a principle stated as a dictate of the natural light — that no thing exists of which it is not permitted to ask why it exists, or to inquire into its efficient cause, or, if it has none, to demand why it needs none. The discussion with Arnauld poses the same problem in the same terms, since the divine essence exercises a formal causality which has a great analogy with the efficient cause and can therefore be called a quasi-efficient cause; and here again the immensity of power or of essence assimilates the divine essence to a power that then stands in for a positive cause, since God's inexhaustible power is the cause or reason why he needs no cause. If God becomes quasi the efficient cause of himself, letting his essence be interpreted as power, it is because he *must*; and he must, in Descartes' eyes, because the consideration of the efficient cause is the first and chief means, not to say the only one, we have for proving God's existence. The two developments order themselves in two parallel sequences: the principle of universal causality, and already of reason, imposes seeking even for God a cause; that cause is sought in the essence that can stand in for it, since it is interpreted as an inexhaustible, immense and incomprehensible power; and so God will be said to be *a se* positively, that is, *causa sui*. Before envisaging the relation of [C] to [A] and [B], its historical site must also be fixed briefly. And it is precisely because Gilson was right to declare Descartes here *without known predecessor* that one must seek historically the motive of that almost total absence of genealogy.
