Going back once more to the five Thomist ways, one observes at once, in the course of the second, the explicit refusal of the hypothesis, clearly envisaged, of a *causa efficiens sui*: it is not possible that someone be the efficient cause of himself, for he would thus be prior to himself, which is impossible. Such a censure presupposes, it is true, that efficient causality be exclusively transitive and not immanent, so that a being can exercise it only on a being other than itself, or that in exercising it on itself it would have to become other than itself. Two others of the ways, which could have issued in the *causa sui*, corroborate the condemnation: the first leads to some first mover, itself immobile, and the third to something necessary by itself, borrowing no cause of its necessity from anywhere but being the cause of necessity for the others. God is determined against things in movement, and so is not himself in movement; against contingents, and so is the cause of their necessity as something external; and thus, against what needs a cause in order to be, God is defined as the cause of beings, itself free of all cause. Absolute, God appears free of movement, of necessity and of the efficience that he nonetheless — better, for that very reason — provokes and secures. If God exercises efficience and manifests himself by it, he does not fall under it: causality touches God because it comes from him, but not because it would comprehend him; it remains contiguous to him but neither coextensive nor connected. And when Aquinas admits an acceptation of *causa sui* — that is free which is *causa sui* — it is in no way a matter of causality, still less of efficient causality, but solely of a translation of the classical Aristotelian formula about what exists for its own sake and not for another's. Nor does Aquinas single himself out on this point, since even his adversaries reason as he does. Anselm: the supreme nature could not have come about either through itself or through anything other than itself. Duns Scotus: the universality of things caused per essence is itself caused, and therefore caused by some cause which is not part of that universality — otherwise it would be *causa sui*. Only Suarez sketches some ambiguity. He does maintain that God is without principle and without cause, and that *a se*, though it seems to be positive, adds only a negation to being itself, since a being cannot be *a se* by a positive origin and emanation — and that it is in this sense that certain saints should be explained when they say that God is cause for himself of his being, his substance or his wisdom. Yet Suarez also envisages the possibility of a *causa sui*, evoking it only to restrict its range at once: God has no *causa sui* by which it would be demonstrated a priori that he is, and if he had one we do not know him exactly and perfectly enough to be able to reach him from his own principles. Why not, then, a *causa sui*? Why does Suarez state the question at all — and Marion's answer is that a more radical interpretation of the *ratio* is at work, one Descartes will push to its term: everything, in whatever manner it is, must give reason for itself, and even God, having no cause, must render reason for his existence in a still more essential manner. Although we grant that God has no causes properly and strictly taken, Suarez writes, he yet has a certain reason for his properties; and reasons of this sort or kind can be required even in God, for from God's infinite perfection we render the reason why he is one only, and so of the rest. Suarez anticipates Descartes' audacious formula, and precisely because, like him, he leads God back to what will become the principle of reason and posits the divine essence as *ratio* — this can be asked even of God himself, not because he needs any cause in order to exist, but because the very immensity of his nature is the cause or reason why he needs no cause to exist. Suarez submits the divine essence only tangentially to a *ratio* of its infinite perfection; it falls to Descartes to take the step by interpreting the divine essence directly as power, and so as an efficient cause of itself. Gilson's judgement must therefore be attenuated: Descartes doubtless finds in Suarez a partial predecessor. But precisely on that account the question rebounds. If even Suarez hesitates to think God as *causa sui*, because he hesitates to submit his essence to the *causa sive ratio* and so returns, in his fundamental position, to the traditional thesis, how will Descartes conciliate the categorical affirmation of the *causa sui* in [C] with two other divine names, [A] and [B], supposed to take up traditional theses? Can Descartes at once submit God to the *causa sive ratio*, defining him as a *causa sui* exercising on himself the common requirement of a *causa*, and recognize in him supreme names absolutely unconditioned, such as infinite and *ens summe perfectum*? Does the conditioned name of *causa sui*, in which *potentia* is accomplished, not contradict *in fine* the first two names, without prejudging the compatibility of those two with each other? Should one not envisage, on the contrary, that the difficulty raised by the *causa sui* — which made even Suarez recoil, virtuoso though he was in audacious conciliations — gives rise, once admitted by Descartes, to a new contradiction in the corpus of divine names, and redoubles the incompatibility between [A] and [B] by another, between [A] and [B] on one side and [C] on the other? On this latent conflict the critics do not fail to diverge: Gouhier perceives here the real unity of the new theodicy, Alquié recognizes that Descartes subordinates God himself to causality, and Gueroult salutes in Descartes the conciliation, by surpassing, of Anselm and Duns Scotus and of the God efficient cause with the God archetype.

Can the contradictions be reduced, or do they form a system (p. 276)? Having their elements to hand, one must now try to gather them, in order to conciliate or oppose them, in the unity of a system.

### § 20. The Name Out of Play

Descartes therefore uses successively, but within a single order of reasons, three determinations of God's essence that stand in for three divine names (p. 276). What remains is to measure their respective relations, and so in fact their compatibilities and their contradictions two by two — only then will it become possible to try to hierarchize the divine names as Descartes elaborates them.

The first task concerns the relations between [A], God as infinite, and [B], God as most perfect being (pp. 276–281), and the criterion distinguishing them seems to lie in the possibility, or not, for the idea of God to be exactly comprehended. It is established that the idea of the infinite remains in 1641 as incomprehensible as the creator of the eternal truths was in 1630; that incomprehensibility transposed the ontic transcendence between infinite being and finite being into an epistemic caesura between the objects comprehensible to the human mind and the divine non-object, incomprehensible though known all the more certainly. Now in the fifth Meditation such a caesura is reabsorbed, so as to reduce, in the end, the idea of a supremely perfect God to the rank of ideas of finite objects — and three arguments establish it. First, exactly as all the innate ideas are drawn from thought, the idea of God is drawn from the reserve where, among others, it was as it were stocked: whenever it pleases me to think of the first and supreme being, and to draw its idea as it were from the treasury of my mind. In [1] the idea of the infinite gave itself before all experience; yet, surging with the act of the cogito and in opening the horizon of possibility, it did not remain permanently and subsistently "in" thought as in a receptacle of extension. Here on the contrary the idea of a supreme being is not accomplished but remains stocked in the war-chest of ideas the cogito keeps available. The idea of God, from then on, is precisely only one innate idea among others, though the first: the difference passes not between it and the innate ideas but between the innate ideas, God's among them, and the forged ideas — there is a great difference between such false positions and the true ideas innate in me, of which the first and chief is the idea of God. Second, and consequently, a second reduction of incomprehensibility follows: the idea of God, as an innate idea, finds itself inevitably compared to other innate ideas — namely all those that offer their true and immutable natures and so attest a certain determinate nature or essence or form of it, immutable and eternal. Which refers, evidently if not explicitly, to two earlier themes, and Marion takes the first, the simple natures. The innate ideas of the fifth Meditation define as true natures and forms quantity, extension, figure, magnitudes, position in space, movement and duration — that is, the same as the mathematical truths the first Meditation enumerated before revoking them in doubt; and in both cases what is at issue are the *naturae simplicissimae* whose theory the *Regulae* elaborated in 1628. It is indeed a matter here of pure and abstract mathematics, echoing the *mathesis universalis*, and whence the setting of the idea of God on a footing of equality with the idea of a mathematical simple nature: certainly I find in myself the idea of God, namely of a supremely perfect being, no less than the idea of any figure or number. Marion calls the declaration as clear as it is stupefying: the idea of God is found in me no less, that is, at most as much, as the idea of a triangle, and so as the simple natures of extension, figure, number. God comes to the idea in the same manner as the simple natures — which, referred to the order of reasons of the *Meditationes*, takes on a very clear sense. The simple natures can validly intervene only before or after hyperbolic doubt, and in fact only the opening of the first Meditation and the fifth Meditation invoke them, never during that doubt. The idea of God constructed in [B] could therefore be homogeneous with the simple natures only by falling, itself too, under the domain that hyperbolic doubt does not or no longer strikes: the science of corporeal things, of the material simple natures, and so of the method. The consequence must not be shirked — the fundamental determination of God's essence in [B] remains comprised, and so comprehensible, within the field and on the mode of the method. It cannot therefore, by definition, coincide with the fundamental definition of God's essence given in [A], which, belonging like the whole third Meditation to the moment of hyperbolic doubt, transgresses the field of the method. [A] and [B] do not merely differ in the sense in which the idea of the infinite differs from the idea of a supremely perfect being; they *contradict* one another as a thought interior to hyperbolic doubt contradicts a thought exterior to that same doubt. More: insofar as [B] shares strictly, neither more nor less, the status of the simple natures revoked by hyperbolic doubt, one must say that it too would be revoked by the determination of God as infinite in [A], if by chance — as would have been possible from a strictly theoretical point of view — it had been mentioned before hyperbolic doubt. And has it not in fact been revoked from the first Meditation by that doubt? That doubt mobilizes an all-powerful God who renders caducous the perfection of goodness and remains indifferent to the thinkable degrees of perfection, to the point of letting himself be named chance, fate or the necessity of things, and even replaced by a fiction of an evil genius; the God who disqualifies evidence anticipates [A], while the equivalent names situate themselves within the horizon of perfection and so anticipate [B]. The third argument follows: what appears there first as simple nature is defined also as a nature true, immutable and eternal — in which Marion believes he can recognize the mathematical truths, immutable, true and eternal, whose created character Descartes proclaims in 1630. One will object at once that the rapprochement is senseless, since the idea of God is caused in me by the infinite and cannot therefore be a created truth. But the objection holds only insofar as the idea of God constructed in [B] would be identified with the infinite, that is, with [A] — which is evidently not the case, since on the contrary the following paradox must be sustained: if one juxtaposes the topic of 1630, where God creates by his incomprehensible power comprehensible mathematical truths, with the topic of the fifth Meditation, the determination [B] of God as supremely perfect does not merge with divine omnipotence but *takes place among the created mathematical truths*. This can be proved. In 1630 the practitioners of the simple natures comprehend mathematical truths perfectly and not that of God's existence, precisely because that existence is demonstrated in a way more evident than the demonstrations of geometry and makes one know more certainly that God is than one knows the truth of any proposition of geometry. So the idea of God — in fact, as has been seen, the determination [A] — then oversteps the mathematical truths, themselves created, in evidence and in certainty. In the fifth Meditation the situation is radically inverted, since God's existence is concluded from his perfect essence with *non minus* and *non magis*, with neither more nor less certainty and evidence than the sum of its angles is deduced from a triangle's essence; God's existence is neither more nor less evident than the mathematical truths, at the least in the same degree — which contradicts the greater evidence it offered in 1630. Is God's existence more evident, or only as evident as the mathematical truths; is it a created truth or not? Before the question there are only two answers. Either Descartes contradicts himself grossly; or the fifth Meditation is dealing with another idea of God than in 1630 — which, referred to the present problematic, comes to saying that [B], the divine essence as supremely perfect being, implying a rational construction of comprehensible perfections, remains within the domain of the simple natures, of mathematical truths and of the method, and so of the creations of God's incomprehensible and infinite power; while on the contrary [A], God as infinite, implying his incomprehensibility as his immensity, makes him surpass, by way of creation, measure and methodical order, and so even the supreme perfection [B] applies to God. From the three arguments Marion concludes to the incompatibility of the first two fundamental determinations of God's essence, and also of the two corresponding proofs of his existence. The one thinks him as infinite, creator of truths and surpassing the method, hence incomprehensible; the other thinks him as supreme perfection constructed from created truths and simple natures, hence comprehensible. In the one case God's evidence transgresses the common regime of methodical evidences; in the other it inscribes itself there by full right. To keep the incompatibility from degrading into pure contradiction it remains open to see in it only a subordination — one will say that as the incomprehensible power creates the eternal truths, so [A] surpasses [B] hyperbolically. But that hierarchy between two determinations of the divine essence marks their irreducible inconsistency more than it masks it, and it must definitively be admitted that [A] and [B] contradict one another on the parameter of incomprehensibility.

A second task remains (pp. 281–284): to [A] and [B] is added a last determination [C], stated by a formula [4] in the Replies, from divine omnipotence — I plainly admit that there can be something in which there is such and so inexhaustible a power that it never needed anyone's help in order to exist, nor even needs it now in order to be conserved, and so is in a certain manner the cause of itself; and I understand God to be such. What relations does it maintain with the two preceding determinations? To decide, one must note that the parameter of incomprehensibility does not permit distinguishing between [A] and [C] as it discerned [A] from [B]. For the determination of God by cause, pushed to the figure of the *causa sui*, interprets the incomprehensible divine essence as a cause, possibly efficient, of itself, by way of its tangential status as power — itself in turn incomprehensible. If God can be assimilated to a *causa sui*, it is because his essence exercises a power still incomprehensible, an *incomprehensibilis potentia* answering to the "incomprehensible power" of 1630. The transition from infinite essence to the power of essence, and then to the *causa sui*, deploys itself within God's own incomprehensibility; and consequently, on the criterion that opposes [A] and [B], [A] on the contrary always agrees with [C]. The analysis must therefore continue on a new criterion of differentiation, and to release it, it suffices to consider the *causa sui* itself. Among the many objections it has raised — is the *causa sui* contradictory in itself, logically or temporally; must it be understood *quodammodo* and analogically, or directly as efficient cause of itself? — the gravest receives the least attention. Descartes establishes in principle that nothing escapes the requirement of a reason for existing, since we can feign nothing to be so from itself that no reason can be given why it should exist rather than not; or reciprocally, that of any thing whatever an efficient cause can be sought. This requirement without exception results from a *dictate* of the natural light, which suffers no exception. Under that common right God falls on the same footing as any other being: this can be asked of God himself; the licence of inquiring into the efficient causes of all things, even of God himself — for by what right would we except God before it has been proved that he exists? Doubtless, once the demonstration is made, God will benefit from arrangements: efficience remains for him analogical, the cause is always equivalent to the power of his essence, the *ratio* of his existence does not merge totally with a *causa*. But these arrangements, which Descartes concedes reluctantly under his objectors' pressure, would have no occasion to be if precisely the *dictate* did not remain, stating that every existence must justify an efficient cause — or some *ratio* standing in for one. Marion's formulation of the point is exact and important: the more facilities God obtains for satisfying the dictate, and Descartes grants them liberally, the more he recognizes that same dictate as of right, and so submits to it. The proper character of [C] can now be released, and it opposes [A] on a new criterion, causality. On [A] God *exercises* causality, whereas on [C] God *is subjected to* causality. For on [A], God is a cause whose power surpasses the bounds of the human understanding, because as efficient and total cause it creates, transcends and produces all at once the totality of its effectively finite effects; the effects alone are effective, effectivity characterizing finite effects; in short efficience belongs only to creation, as act and as region. So one can go back from the effects, finite, created and subjected to effectivity, to God as their efficient cause, and therefore itself exempt from causality and efficience — until one arrives at the ultimate cause, which will be God. Here God escapes efficient causality because he *exercises* it: Descartes, in his determination [A] of God's essence, respects the dominant medieval interpretation of divine causality, namely that it remains strictly external to him. On [C] it is quite otherwise. God is subjected to causality because it falls to the human mind to exercise upon him the search for a cause justifying his existence; God satisfies a causality he first *undergoes* — if we have first inquired into the cause why he is, or why he perseveres in being. We, that is, the light of reason stating its dictate, conduct an inquiry into the reason that authorizes God to exist and to persevere in that existence; and it is always the same dictate that finds itself satisfied afterwards, when the power of the divine essence is *understood* as the reason of his existence, standing in for a cause, possibly efficient — the word cause can in no way be understood of the efficient, but only in that God's inexhaustible power is the cause or reason why he needs no cause. If God does not submit to efficience, it is precisely because he submits already, fully and even without it, to the requirement of a *causa sive ratio*; and he submits to it the more radically in that he *is* himself, directly and by his essence, such a cause and reason, since the very immensity of his nature is the cause or reason why he needs no cause in order to exist. Essentially, God submits to the demand of an inquiry in search of a *causa sive ratio* justifying existence. Determination [C] anticipates the Leibnizian formulation of the principle of reason and submits divine existence to it in advance — causality, as reason of existence, becomes internal to God and even identical with his essence. So on the criterion of causality a second contradiction appears among the determinations of God's essence. On [A], as in the earlier medieval metaphysics, God transcends all causality because he exercises it, and so remains free before what cannot then present itself as the principle of reason; on [C], which in fact decides the development of all subsequent metaphysics, the principle of reason transcends God, since it rises to proving and legitimating his very existence, and can therefore already sketch its definitive formulation. The contradiction between [A] and [C] does not merely mark a new tension in the Cartesian resumption of the thematic of the divine names; it decides above all the coming orientation of the history of metaphysics, which might be played out not between Descartes and some other adversary but, more originarily, between Descartes and himself.

Having reached this result, the three Cartesian determinations of God's essence can now be compared with one another (pp. 284–287), and the multiple comparison organizes itself clearly around the two consequences of [A] — God is incomprehensible, and God exercises causality without undergoing it. [A] is opposed to [B] on the criterion of incomprehensibility, since in [B] the divine essence regresses to the rank of objects of the method; but [A] is also opposed to [B] on the second criterion, since the idea of the supremely perfect being admits a cause that constructs it or produces it in us. [A] is opposed to [C] over cause and the principle of reason; but on the criterion of incomprehensibility [A] agrees with [C], which plainly determines the divine essence as incomprehensible power. And [B] is opposed to [C] on the criterion of incomprehensibility, since an essence methodically constructed from perfections contradicts the incomprehensible power maintained from 1630 to 1641 — while [B] agrees with [C] on the criterion of causality, since the *causa sive ratio* that exercises its dictate on the God *causa sui* exercises it also on the eternal truths it creates as their efficient and total cause, and so finally on the sum of comprehensible perfections culminating in the *ens summe perfectum*. Marion recapitulates the term-by-term comparisons in a table. Under identification: the divine name of [A] is the *idea infiniti*, of [B] the *ens summe perfectum*, of [C] the *causa sui*; the formula of [A] is the third Meditation's [1] and [2], of [B] the fifth Meditation's [3], of [C] the First and Fourth Replies' [4]; the antecedent of [A] is John Duns Scotus, of [B] William of Ockham, of [C] Suarez, with a question mark. Under (in)coherence: incomprehensibility is satisfied by [A] and [C] but not [B]; non-causability by [A] alone; non-methodicity by [A] and [C] but not [B]. Several lessons follow directly. Concerning identification, it now seems to Marion evident that one cannot avoid the simple question of the plurality of divine names in Cartesian metaphysics. The surprising use of the term "divine names" in metaphysics is justified for the same reasons that make their plurality admissible: Descartes uses several determinations that can no longer be confounded once their origins are identified, even summarily, and those historical origins all refer back to theological debates in which the determinations gained an irreducible singularity. Cartesian discourse depends, as a general rule, very closely on its predecessors, and the more so where discourse on God is at issue; without the consideration of these genealogies interpretation becomes nearly impossible. So the theological origins of this discourse do impose speaking of a debate on the divine names — but the proper characters of the Cartesian new beginning also require measuring the displacement and transmutation of those divine names into metaphysics. Descartes translates, doubtless for the first time, certain of the divine names elaborated by medieval Christian theology into the field of the principially systematic metaphysics of modernity; and the meditation of that historial translation constitutes today the stake of the history of philosophy applied to Descartes. Concerning coherence, or rather incoherence, Marion reads the table twice. Horizontally: the three determinations never agree in satisfying unanimously any one of the three criteria — incomprehensibility, satisfied by [A] and [C], suffers the contradiction of [B]; transcendence with respect to cause is denied by [B] and [C] but satisfied by [A]; and transgression of the methodical horizon, which does not coincide with submission to causality, is confirmed in [A] and [C] but denied in [B]. The tension, going as far as a complex network of contradictions, spares no determination and no criterion, but traverses in depth the ultimate strata of Cartesian thought about God — such is the risk incurred by the audacity of a new beginning that nonetheless claims to take up several distinct theological inheritances. Vertically: each of the three determinations does not contradict the three criteria of differentiation equally. Without minimizing the doubtless limited but still probable arbitrariness of their choice, and without underestimating that the criteria are borrowed from particular moments of the order of reasons, a hierarchy plainly imposes itself. [B] contradicts all three criteria retained; conformably to its already noted character as a divine name belonging to the *via affirmativa*, the *ens summe perfectum* can only refuse the three at least grammatically negative criteria here retained. The *causa sui*, [C], satisfies two of the three, because the *potentia incomprehensibilis* on which it leans safeguards precisely incomprehensibility and excludes itself from the horizon of the method, in an equilibrium that might, at least formally, render some echo of eminence. There remains the idea of the infinite, which in [A] alone answers to the three criteria distinguishing the idea of God from every other knowable object; those three criteria define it negatively — neither comprehensible, nor subject to the exercise of causality in it, nor held within the methodical horizon — which confirms its at least formal belonging to the *via negativa*. Yet it does not strictly confine itself there, since Descartes never stops stressing that the infinite, under all its angles and in all its implications, states negatively positive properties. The affirmative and negative ways are here fused in a tension in which, better than in the *causa sui*, authentic eminence should doubtless be recognized. The *causa sui*, in fact, accomplishes affirmations under negative appearances: incomprehensibility does not prevent a *ratio* from being given for divine existence, formally univocal with the *ratio* of all other existences; its transcendence toward the method does not forbid it from obeying, like the objects of the *mathesis universalis*, the privilege of the *causa*, itself one of the first simple natures. Consequently, if the *causa sui* and the *ens summe perfectum* tend to privilege in their concept, if not in its statement, the *via affirmativa*, the idea of the infinite alone would occupy not only the function of the *via negativa* but that of eminence. From the internal examination of the network of crossed contradictions among the three Cartesian determinations of the divine essence, Marion posits the primacy — as more operative, more complete and alone unconditioned — of the idea of the infinite.

There is nonetheless a more constraining motive for admitting that primacy (pp. 287–292), and it returns the whole chapter to the architecture of Chapter II. Returning to the earlier results, which concluded to a redoubling of the onto-theo-logy of *cogitatio* by an onto-theo-logy of the *causa*, one can risk a rapprochement itself redoubled. To the onto-theo-logy of *cogitatio* would correspond the determination [B] of God as *ens summe perfectum*: the summation of perfections offers to *cogitatio* an infinite sum of perfect objects to think, and God manifests his excellence the more in that all the thinkable perfections find themselves effectively accomplished in him, so that God's supreme perfection carries to its accomplishment the search for the most perfect of *cogitata*. To the onto-theo-logy of the *causa* would correspond the determination [C] of God as *causa sui*: the third proof issues directly in the divine name answering to the second onto-theo-logy, since God accomplishes exemplarily, as his own essence, the causality he exercises on the other beings, as reason and above all as manner of being. The double encounter would confirm, with a remarkable coherence, two distinct analyses — the redoubling of onto-theo-logy in Chapter II manifested a tension the less contestable in that it is detected a second time in the plurality of the determinations of God's essence, and so of the divine names, in Chapter IV. But an objection surges immediately: the redoubling of onto-theo-logy can take up only *two* of the three divine names, and so leaves the idea of the infinite indeterminate — does such a distortion not disqualify the reconstitutions and modellings just attempted in both chapters? Marion's answer is that on the contrary it might be that we attain here a fundamental result. What does the Cartesian redoubling of onto-theo-logy signify? The conceptually most rigorous and most operative determination, in the present state of thought, of the fact and the limits of Descartes' belonging to metaphysics. Therefore, if the idea of the infinite, as one of the divine names according to Descartes and in fact the first, finds asylum in none of the Cartesian figures of onto-theo-logy, it must inevitably be concluded that it does not fall under the Cartesian constitution of metaphysics. Surprising as the conclusion may appear, it perhaps states only an evidence. When God bears the names of *ens summe perfectum* and of *causa sui*, he directly sets to work the manner of being of all other beings — and in fact Descartes thereby elaborates regional ontologies, under the appearances of a physics, a theory of the soul, a cosmology, which manage simple natures and so intelligible perfections by recourse to efficient causality. When he bears the name of the infinite, by contrast, God sets to work no science of beings or of their manners of being: the infinite–finite couple does not permit thinking the being of finite beings or elaborating their particular sciences. It authorizes only the irremediable and unconfusable distinction between God and creatures — which, for Descartes, already constitutes a decisive acquisition. And that distinction serves nothing in the elaboration of the sciences, since it issues in the incomprehensibility of the infinite by the finite and in the strict delimitation of the sciences exercised by the finite understanding; nor does it serve anything in the elaboration of a general ontology, since even those who privilege it, Duns Scotus and Suarez, suspend it at once when it comes to establishing a univocal concept of being. The metaphysical extra-territoriality of the divine name drawn from the idea of the infinite could moreover find a confirmation external to Cartesian thought and texts, since the posterity of the themes Descartes puts in play reproduces diachronically the topic released synchronically within the strictly Cartesian domain. Malebranche and Leibniz supply the illumination. Of the three determinations, which do they privilege? Malebranche certainly knows [A], but uses it only by integrating it into [B], on a clean transition: by divinity we all understand the infinite, Being without restriction, the infinitely perfect Being — and in fact the most frequent and most functional name is "the infinitely perfect Being," where the infinite is reabsorbed into a simple index of perfection and comes to saying *summe*, losing all proper signification. One may presume that this evolution toward the massive privilege of the divine name drawn from the perfections maintains a close relation, though one still to be clarified, with the interpretation of God as the very place of the knowledge of truth and of the exercise of the sciences — against the doctrine of the creation of the eternal truths. Marion's hypothesis is accordingly that Malebranche develops only one of the two figures of the onto-theo-logy Descartes redoubled, that of the *ens* as *cogitatum*, and consequently privileges the one of the three Cartesian determinations of God's essence that best corresponds to it: the *ens summe perfectum* become, by recuperation of the infinite, the infinitely perfect Being. Leibniz evidently knows [A] and [B] and sometimes does them justice, but he indisputably privileges [C] when he evokes the last reason of things: the sufficient Reason, which needs no further Reason, must be outside this series of contingent things and be found in a substance which is its cause, and which is a necessary Being carrying the Reason of its existence with it; otherwise one would not yet have a sufficient reason at which one could stop — and this last reason of things is called God. And again: the last reason of things must be in a necessary substance in which the detail of changes is only eminently, as in its source; and this is what we call God. God, *ratio* of the world and of himself, thus accomplishes, echoing the *causa sui* and without doubt more radically than it, the function of manifesting the being of each being on the measure of the cause, *ut causatum*. The Leibnizian privilege granted, by way of the divine name of last reason of things, to the Cartesian determination [C] answers to the parallel choice to take up the onto-theo-logy of the *ens ut causatum*. So Malebranche and Leibniz might each deploy one of the two figures of the onto-theo-logy Descartes redoubled, and in each of these efforts of metaphysical simplification privilege the one of the three determinations of the divine essence that corresponds to the metaphysical figure previously retained. Whence a consequence at least probable and deserving to be tested in detail: the divine name drawn from the idea of the infinite takes place in no figure of post-Cartesian onto-theo-logy, and must therefore be heard as a *non-metaphysical statement* of Cartesian thought about God, falling more under the earlier theology of the divine names than under the later onto-theo-logies — where the conceptual idol excludes God from the horizon of metaphysics in believing it sequesters him in onto-theo-logical functions. Descartes here as elsewhere overhangs his successors, as the incomprehensible complex of contradictions imposed by the secret of the thing itself surpasses and also makes possible the simplicity of multiple rival intelligibilities, widowed above all of the incomprehensible itself. In releasing and maintaining the primacy of the idea of the infinite, Descartes not only accomplishes one last time the itinerary of a treatise of the divine names, a fragment fallen from a silent disappearance; he marks above all, at the very moment when he fixes the onto-theo-logical figures that will govern all modern metaphysics, their *limits*. The ego, which nonetheless dominates the first onto-theo-logy, can itself transgress metaphysics when it temporalizes itself by possibility; but the ego would not manage it if God, who nonetheless dominates the second onto-theo-logy, did not first himself transgress metaphysics when he is said under the most divine name of infinite. Instituting the possible figures of modern metaphysics, Descartes redoubled his own institution with an unsurpassable authority, by himself tracing, first and in advance, its bounds. Marion ends on Descartes' own avowal — that he never treated of the Infinite except to submit himself to it — and on a deliberate misquotation. If the Cartesian names of God organize themselves into a confused complex of contradictions, it is not that Descartes lacked conceptual power or rigour; it is on the contrary because he dared to confront the contradiction that access to the infinite advancing upon it necessarily imposes on the finite — and to which perhaps only a certain conceptual panic can testify without indignity. Before God, reverently, and one of the few among metaphysicians, Descartes holds himself hidden: he neither dissimulates nor steals away, but hides his face before the face of the infinite — *larvatus pro Deo*. The note concedes at once that Descartes of course wrote *larvatus prodeo*, and that Marion transforms "I come forward masked" into "masked before the face of God" only because the modification gives something to think, and because others have risked it before him in quite another sense.

## V. Overcoming

### § 21. Pascal According to Cartesian Metaphysics

Reconstituting the strict metaphysics of Descartes has issued in a redoubled onto-theo-logy; and if that confirms all the better Cartesian thought's belonging to metaphysics in general, it makes all the more strangely apparent a *double remainder* (pp. 293–296). Two elements of doctrine remain irreducible to the constitution of Cartesian metaphysics so released. The thought of the ego defines it, on the first onto-theo-logy, as a *cogitatio sui*, and on the second as an *ens causatum*, that is, a *substantia creata*; but its ultimate determination, by the freedom that in morals re-establishes possibility as the first modality of the being, finds place in neither of the two onto-theo-logical figures. And yet the ego acting freely still mobilizes their respective distinctive concepts, since *cogitatio* and cause permit it to exercise a causality by representation, on the Kantian formula; so the third determination of the ego still interferes with metaphysics though it does not belong to the redoubled onto-theo-logy. What status can be recognized in it? The interrogation appears the less avoidable in that the isolation of a second element of doctrine confirms it: among the competing determinations of the divine essence, if the *ens perfectissimum* agrees exactly with the onto-theo-logy of the *ens cogitatum* and the *causa sui* corresponds literally to the onto-theo-logy of the *ens ut causatum*, the first and most fundamental, *infinitum*, finds justification in no metaphysical figure — and it might even expressly contradict the figures of the redoubled onto-theo-logy. What status, then, can be recognized in the determination of God as infinite? Marion refuses to treat these two exceptions as negligible and insignificant residues, since their excellence forbids it: it belongs as radically to the ego to act freely as to God to deploy himself infinitely. That the two properties know no ample development among Descartes' heirs, until Kant takes them up, does not weaken but confirms their interest as much as their difficulty. The dilemma is easily stated. Either the determination of Cartesian metaphysics by a redoubled onto-theo-logy remains insufficient — because a third figure had to be mobilized, or because the very notion of onto-theo-logy is not pertinent, or for both motives together — and butts against essential theses it leaves uninterpreted; or these theses resist the redoubled onto-theo-logy only because, more radically, they simply *do not belong to metaphysics*. The strangeness of that brutal affirmation cannot be attenuated by quick accommodations, but Marion notes that in fact post-Cartesian metaphysics privileges neither the determination of God as infinite nor the determination of the ego as freely acting, developing instead the orientations made accessible by the *ens ut cogitatum* and the *ens ut causatum*; and that in fact the two irreducible theses can be gathered in a single formula, *ego, ut ad imaginem Dei* — freedom in the ego follows from the infinity of its will, which itself depends on its resemblance to God. The note is careful about what it claims: the formula only synthesizes classical Cartesian sequences about being made in some manner to God's image and likeness, and about the will bearing the image or some resemblance of God; and it asks, rather than asserts, whether *cogitatio* does not change status the moment it is taken up by the *imago Dei*, the biblical formulation of the determination of the finite by the infinite. The difficulty of interpretation lies therefore less in recognizing the particular status of these theses in relation to the redoubled onto-theo-logy than in *identifying* it. Supposing they transgress metaphysics, can one require of Descartes that he think clearly such a transgression of the transgression metaphysics already accomplishes, when he thinks his first concept of metaphysics so little explicitly? And would the interpreter's eventual arbitrariness not become untenable if it went so far as to introduce, after the redoubling of onto-theo-logy, a new duplication inside Cartesian thought — since like entities and hypotheses, distinctions must not be multiplied uselessly? If a metaphysical extra-territoriality of the ego as free and of God as infinite can be sustained, it will have to impose and confirm itself by other arguments; and those arguments will be the more powerful for coming from a more exterior instance. Marion therefore seeks a fulcrum from which it becomes possible to consider Cartesian thought as metaphysical, that is, also possibly and partially as non-metaphysical — a fulcrum that could not itself be defined metaphysically but would have to identify itself on another instance. The more that instance tries to think and to think itself outside metaphysics, the more it will permit testing a contrario the metaphysical character, partial or total, of Cartesian thought; and it must of course affirm itself while taking position against Descartes, failing which it would offer no reference and measure no distance. What is sought, in a word, is an explicitly non-philosophical and so possibly non-metaphysical thought, which knows Cartesian thought, recognizes it as metaphysical, and criticizes it in terms that may permit conceiving an overcoming of metaphysics at the Cartesian epoch of its destiny.

Such a thought can be met only in Pascal (pp. 296–297), who takes up and generalizes the Cartesian statement of the *ego ad imaginem Dei* — nature has perfections to show that it is the image of God, and defects to show that it is only the image — while applying himself to understand it *against* philosophy, in particular the philosophy of "Descartes useless and uncertain," and outside all metaphysics. Marion's claim is that Pascal measures Descartes' belonging to metaphysics only from his own theological exile outside philosophy; and the note refuses in passing both the attribution to Pascal of a philosophy and the evocation of a "metaphysics of Pascal," since the precept that to mock philosophy is truly to philosophize must be heard in all its force, however banalized. A knowledge and a discussion of Descartes by Pascal matter essentially as much to Descartes, so that we can measure how far and in what Descartes falls under metaphysics, as to Pascal, who had to refuse philosophy — and so Descartes' philosophy — in order to accede to his theological position. Pascal and Descartes thus measure one another reciprocally; or rather they measure, in confronting one another, the limits between metaphysics and theology. Not that Descartes holds the part of metaphysics alone, since the contrary has been established and makes the whole stake of this debate, nor that Pascal plays the exclusive role of a theologian, as will be seen. Rather Descartes and Pascal reveal or trace the limits between metaphysics and theology insofar as they sustain them but also overstep and transgress them. That at least is the theoretical result aimed at here — otherwise put, from what instance do the freedom of the ego and the infinity of God derive?

Examining that question presupposes first establishing Pascal's theoretical relation to Descartes (pp. 297–301). Pascal can revoke Descartes, and so possibly overcome metaphysics in him, only insofar as he accedes to an authentic intelligence of him, to the point of expressly recognizing in Cartesian thought a figure of metaphysics. The debate sought will take place only if it can be shown that Pascal, before and precisely in order to criticize him, *read* Descartes — in a word, only if Pascal confirms himself Cartesian. The relations between the two present no difficulty, even if they were sometimes difficult: a public meeting in September 1647, the discussion over the experiment establishing the physical reality of the vacuum and over its interpretation, the interested parties themselves related them. But the essential lies elsewhere: Pascal read and approved Descartes. Doubtless when he cites him explicitly it is often first to criticize him, and doubtless the famous reproach of uselessness and uncertainty will dominate the whole interpretation, to the point that Malebranche will shortly have to stress that though it is true that most sciences are uncertain and very useless, it remains constant that there are purely human sciences very certain and useful enough, and in particular that it is very necessary to know some truths of Metaphysics. Still, Pascal could pass for a Cartesian in the eyes even of enlightened acquaintances such as Méré — "Descartes, whom you esteem so highly." Marion then documents the reading with a precision he insists on. In *De l'esprit géométrique* of 1657–58 Pascal takes up, in plainly Cartesian terms, the search for the true method of conducting reasoning in all things — undoubtedly the true method for arriving at the knowledge of all the things his mind would be capable of, which the *Discours* had established. That true method comprises, Pascal says, two principal things: never to employ any term whose sense has not previously been clearly explained, and never to advance any proposition not demonstrated by truths already known. Together the two precepts define the true order, which consists in defining everything and proving everything — in which one recognizes the Cartesian definition of method by order, since the whole method consists in the order and disposition of the things to which the mind's regard must be turned; just as the "true method" repeats the Cartesian reduction of truth to method, since method is necessary for investigating the truth of things. Of the two precepts, the second corresponds to deduction and induction, the first to *intuitus*. As for the requirement of definition, it too rejoins Cartesian theses, since for Pascal definition suffers two limitations. First by excess of evidence: pushing research further and further one necessarily arrives at primitive words that can no longer be defined and at principles so clear that no clearer can be found to serve them as proof — an echo of the very simple natures which, apprehended first, can no longer be divided into simpler ones, save paradoxically by appearing more complicated. Then by default, when the explanation is more confused than the notion to be explained; and Pascal gives two examples, both taken from Descartes. What necessity is there to explain what is meant by the word *man*; and what advantage did Plato think to procure us in saying it was a two-legged animal without feathers, as if the idea I naturally have of it, and cannot express, were not clearer and surer than the one he gives me by his useless and even ridiculous explanation? — in which one easily recognizes a Cartesian denegation: what then did I formerly think myself to be? A man, of course. But what is a man? Shall I say a rational animal? No, because afterwards it would have to be asked what an animal is and what rational, and thus from one question I should slip into more and more difficult ones. And second: how many are there likewise who believe they have defined motion when they have said that motion is neither simply act nor mere potency, but the act of a being in potency? — in which one finds again Rule XII, that those who say motion, a thing most known to everyone, is the act of a being in potency insofar as it is in potency, seem to utter magic words having an occult force above the grasp of the human mind, since who understands these words, and who is ignorant of what motion is? The Cartesian intention is moreover recognized in *De l'art de persuader* in very clear terms — to prove the distinction of material and spiritual natures and make of it a firm and sustained principle of an entire physics, as Descartes claimed to do — echoing evidently the second Meditation's Archimedes, who asked for nothing but a point firm and immobile in order to move the whole earth from its place. Without collecting other correspondences, which doubtless belong as much to the air of the time as to precise readings, one can already conclude to a notable resumption of the Cartesian doctrine of method by Pascal, with nothing indicating that he later refused it in its own domain. The correspondences appear no less precise between certain of the *Pensées* and Cartesian texts. The 1647 Preface to the French *Principes* governs the fragments on order, as the abridged title "Prin." indicates — and not "Pyrr.," a faulty reading. Pascal, declaring that he will write his thoughts without order and not perhaps in an aimless confusion, since that is the true order and will always mark his object by disorder itself, reverses the intention Descartes avowed, that there have been at all times great men who tried to find a fifth degree for arriving at wisdom, namely to seek the first causes and the true Principles from which can be deduced the reasons of everything one is capable of knowing. Consequently, where Descartes discusses dogmatically the principles of Plato and Aristotle and measures what gave them much authority, Pascal ironically turns Descartes' at least apparent respect back on him: one imagines Plato and Aristotle only in the great robes of pedants; they were decent men and like others, laughing with their friends. The continuity of the allusions confirms their precision. The same Preface also inspires the fragment whose sequence — a seed thrown in good earth produces; a principle thrown in a good mind produces; the root, the branches, the fruits, the principles, the consequences — takes up not only the banal theme of the seeds of truth that bear fruit in us, but answers above all to the parallel the Preface established between the results of the sciences and the fertility of a tree: not only that all Philosophy is like a tree whose roots are Metaphysics, whose trunk is Physics, and whose branches are all the other sciences reducible to Medicine, Mechanics and Morals, but also that as it is not from the roots or the trunk of trees that fruits are gathered but only from the extremities of their branches, so the principal utility of Philosophy depends on those of its parts that can only be learned last. Confirming that this fragment is only a reading-note on the *Principes*, the next fragment, describing relative movement and immobility on the example of a ship, again reproduces *Principia* II, § 13; and the note collects further precise examples, including a fragment that must be read as a refutation of Descartes' Eucharistic doctrine, since its closing — the same river that flows there is the same in number as the one flowing at the same time in China — answers to Descartes' remark that the Loire is the same river that was there ten years ago although it is no longer the same water.

Pascal's Cartesianism is not confined, however, to reading-notes and methodological precepts; it concerns first philosophy (pp. 301–306). Not only is it to be noted that in the *Entretien avec M. de Saci* it is Pascal to whom the role of the Philosopher falls; it must be stressed that among these philosophers the most present is precisely never named — Descartes — though he never stops intervening, since the first Meditation nourishes the *Entretien* directly at least twice. First there appears the hypothesis of a divine deception: since we know only by faith that an all-good Being gave us these principles as true, in creating us to know the truth, who will know without this light whether, being formed by chance, they are not uncertain, or whether, being formed by a false and wicked being, he has not given them to us false in order to seduce us. The hypothesis reappears in the *Pensées* — there is no certainty outside faith, whether man is created by a good god, by a wicked demon, or at random — that is, Pascal sums up in the same sequence not only the *genius aliquis malignus* of the first Meditation but its equivalent hypotheses of a God who would let us deceive ourselves: let us grant this whole business of God to be fictitious; whether they suppose I have arrived at what I am by fate, or by chance, or by a continuous series of things, or in any other way whatever. In perfect cohesion with this first implicit citation, Pascal mentions also the effect of such a deception of the principles, error about the most evident truths: if the soul knows when it errs, seeing that the essence of a mistake consists in not knowing it — if in these obscurings it does not believe as firmly that two and three make six as it knows afterwards that they make five. One cannot fail to recognize here one of the examples of undetectable error provoked by the Cartesian hypothesis of a God who can do all things: just as I judge that others sometimes err about what they think they know most perfectly, so I might be deceived whenever I add two and three, or count the sides of a square, or do anything simpler if anything simpler can be imagined. Marion stresses that the insistent presence of the first Meditation is the more remarkable in that Pascal's text is presented as issuing from Montaigne alone; the censure of Descartes' name renders the rapprochement a contrario all the more determining, since Descartes is not merely a reserve of concepts but the very frame of the reasonings lent to Montaigne. The same holds, more directly and radically, with the second Meditation. Descartes had written that he could feign himself to have no body, though he could not feign himself not to think, since to think is the only attribute inseparable from him; Pascal takes this up exactly and never puts it in question: I can well conceive a man without hands, feet, head — for it is only experience that teaches us the head is more necessary than the feet; but I cannot conceive man without thought: he would be a stone or a brute. And again: thought therefore makes man's being; all our dignity consists in thought; let us labour, then, to think well: that is the principle of morals. Consequently, on a perfectly Cartesian position, Pascal takes over the two corollaries as well. First that the knowledge of extension and of the sensible reduces to the inspection of the mind alone, since in asking what it is that feels pleasure in us — is it the hand, the arm, the flesh, the blood? we shall see that it must be something immaterial — Pascal repeats the Cartesian reduction of the piece of wax first to the *extensum quid* and then and above all to what makes that possible, that is, thinkable, namely thought itself; for it is well enough known that it is the soul that feels and not the body. In a few lines Pascal thus maintains the whole Cartesian doctrine of perception as representation by *cogitatio*. The second corollary depends on it too, since the famous and difficult fragment that seems to conclude to an unfindable self — where then is this Self, if it is neither in the body nor in the soul? — leans at its opening on a famous experience: a man who puts himself at the window to see the passers-by; if I pass by, can I say he put himself there to see me? No, for he is not thinking of me in particular. And that experience appears first in the second Meditation: unless I happened to look out of the window at men crossing the square, whom I say I see no less customarily than the wax; yet what do I see but hats and coats, under which automata could be hidden? But I judge them to be men. Marion registers a complete reversal in the Pascalian re-use: it is no longer a matter of seeing other egos beneath the clothed automata strolling below the window, but of *being seen* as a self loved or lovable by a loving spectator-ego. It nonetheless remains that the problem Pascal poses, and his sense of the phenomenological situation, are induced by the Cartesian doctrine both of the *res cogitans* and of the *inspectio mentis*; so Pascal remains strictly faithful to the second Meditation, and so to Cartesian egology. Could one not, however, hold to a restricted position — Pascal taking up Cartesian theses in the profane domains, methodology, definition of the sciences and of philosophy, theory of knowledge, essence of the soul, without following Descartes where theological questions and first of all the very definition of God are at issue? The counter-test would be furnished by examining the implicit citations of the third Meditation: on such a restricted Cartesianism, the third Meditation would either be passed over in silence or be contradicted. What does the third Meditation state of God? It thinks him quite particularly as infinite — a certain infinite substance, some supreme God, eternal, infinite, God to be actually infinite. And it appears evident that Pascal takes up and privileges that definition of the divine essence, first in the *Entretien*, with that sovereign Being who is infinite by his own definition, and then repeatedly in the *Pensées*: there is in nature a being necessary, eternal and infinite; an object infinite and immutable, that is, God himself; to pray to this being infinite and without parts; that God be infinite and without parts, God is a thing infinite and indivisible; an infinite good. And, like Descartes, Pascal deduces from God's infinity his incomprehensibility at once — if there is a God, he is infinitely incomprehensible. The infinite, on Descartes' account, characterizes God only by manifesting thereby to man that he is finite and so bears in himself the mark of the infinite, in the idea of the infinite, free will, will; whence Descartes also recognizes in man a participating in the perfect being as well as a participating in nothingness. Pascal, in reply, posits that man infinitely surpasses man, because in the state of creation or in that of grace he is raised above all nature, rendered as it were like God and participating in the divinity, and inversely, in the state of corruption and sin, is fallen from that state and rendered like the beasts. Despite the difference of registers, the double postulation is in both cases articulated on the ambivalence of the infinite in man. Even where Pascal aims — implicitly but clearly — at Descartes, for having shown by the mere title *Principles of Philosophy* a presumption as infinite as his object, he is only returning to its author a warning first advanced by Descartes himself: he has run through Morin's booklet, whose principal defect is that it treats everywhere of the Infinite as if his mind were above it and could comprehend its properties, a fault common to nearly all — which Descartes has tried carefully to avoid, since he never treated of the infinite except to submit himself to it, and not to determine what it is or is not. Pascal's reproach reproduces Descartes', who, if he perhaps did not always succeed in carefully avoiding the pretension to comprehend the infinite, at least always denounced it; so this criterion does not suffice to oppose Pascal to Descartes. Marion concludes without hesitation: the third Meditation determines the Pascalian definition of God as radically as the first and second decided Pascal's conceptions of doubt and of thought. Pascal thinks from Descartes and maintains metaphysical theses strictly conformable to Cartesian orthodoxy, at least on the essential. What draws Pascal near to Descartes conceptually establishes itself more easily — up to this point — than what opposes them.

### § 22. Descartes Useless and Uncertain

In maintaining the determination of God as infinite Pascal seems to assume the Cartesian heritage, and in fact he does so to the highest point (pp. 307–310); yet his very treatment of the infinite is what marks the inversion of his relation to Descartes, so that what brings him nearest also marks the first rupture. For Descartes, not only does God admit the qualification of infinite, but he alone deserves it; every other being, offering some limit to our finite knowledge, deserves only the title of indefinite. Here I distinguish between the indefinite and the infinite, and I call properly infinite only that in which no limits are found in any respect, in which sense God alone is infinite; whereas those things in which under some one respect alone I recognize no end, such as the extension of imaginary space, the multitude of numbers, the divisibility of the parts of quantity and the like, I call indefinite indeed, but not infinite, since they do not lack an end on every side. The infinite requires the absence of limit on an infinity of parameters and not merely on one of them; in the latter case non-finitude results from the conditions of exercise of our finite mind, which privileges this or that parameter so as to produce this or that finitude and, more exactly, this or that indefinite. The infinite appears on the contrary when the absence of limits results from the positive self-affirmation of the infinite, transgressing every limit but also every parameter of measure — and so the name of infinite is reserved to God alone. Marion draws out what the distinction bought Descartes: it permitted him to dispose of three different determinations for composing the system of the three privileged beings of special metaphysics without confusion or separation — God infinite, ego finite, world indefinite — since in that system the mediation between the infinite and the indefinite, God and the world, can be secured by the ego, which on one hand dominates and produces the indefinite by representation according to the *mathesis universalis*, and on the other knows, without comprehending it and by reversal, the infinite whose idea it bears within it. Far from the question of the infinite and the indefinite leading the finite ego to its confusion, it manifests on the contrary its assurance. And here is where Pascal essentially separates himself from Descartes: he maintains the determination of God as infinite only by annulling the nonetheless indispensable distinction between infinite and indefinite. He never stops qualifying as infinite the very things Descartes would never have wished to name otherwise than indefinite — infinite immensity of spaces, infinite spaces, infinite sphere, infinite numbers, infinite speed, infinite movement. The infinite thus returns to extension and to mathematical idealities on exactly the same title as to God, and the upheaval entails several consequences. The same title of infinite, belonging to two terms as distinct as the world and God, no longer counts as a name proper to God nor as the first of the divine names; the most decisive acquisition of Descartes finds itself lowered to the role of an index of incommensurability, whatever it be, with regard to the finite ego. God therefore admits no proper name in the discourse of philosophy; to name him infinite says residually nothing of him, or rather what it says of him it says equally of other notions — the crisis of the divine names opens once more. Second, for the three privileged beings of special metaphysics Pascal now disposes of only two determinations and not three; it becomes impossible to articulate them into a system, since each of the terms could claim the infinite — God by his own definition, the world and the mathematical idealities by the almost already infinitesimal calculus, and the ego, though properly finite alone, because man infinitely surpasses man. The infinite multiplied forbids the system of the three privileged beings by uniformizing all possible mediations, and substitutes for it only a monstrous chaos and confusion. Marion anticipates the sequel: nothing will be able to remedy the annulment of the three Cartesian mediations among the three privileged beings of special metaphysics except the recognition of three *orders*, distinct to the point of heterogeneity — flesh, mind, charity — which will henceforth mediate nothing less than beings, on nothing less than special metaphysics. The Pascalian banalization of the Cartesian infinite will thus provoke, in return, the distinction of several orders where the Cartesian method admitted only one. Third, the inflation of occurrences of "infinite" in the *Pensées* attests a weakening and not a deepening of the concept: it now suffices, strictly inversely to Cartesian usage, that the end remain unassignable in some one respect for one legitimately to speak of the infinite. The mathematical treatment of the infinite marks, at the same time as a decisive mathematical conquest, a characterized regression in the thought of the positive and absolute infinite as Descartes understood it — or, to avoid all misunderstanding, what can be apprehended mathematically by and for a calculus does not by definition deserve the title of infinite. Pascal passes from the strong to the weak sense of the infinite; must one conclude that he thinks the essence of the infinite weakly? Marion's suggestion is subtler: perhaps Pascal has precisely perceived the infinite as *deprived of essence*. The infinite, or rather the infinity of infinites, does not give itself as a concept but as the deformed, reformulated index of the absence of mediation between the ego and every other possible object; the infinite becomes a category of relation — between the ego and the being — indeed of modality, of the ego in effective situation of an impossibility of measuring the possible. It can therefore apply to an infinity of beings provided they enter into unmediated relation with the ego. The infinite multiplies itself infinitely, not as an equivocal or weak concept but as the metaphor of the measureless situation of the ego, exiled in the incommensurable. Whereby the opposition to Descartes is confirmed. The Cartesian ego disposes, with the *mathesis universalis*, of a means of putting in order that secures the measure of beings reduced to the rank of objects; only the ego itself and God are excepted. The Pascalian self discovers itself universally and perpetually in a situation of incommensurability, surrounded by an infinity of infinites: the method of calculation may extend its hold over new domains, the mathematical infinite itself included, but it secures no mediation whatever with these infinites. The infinite, sliding toward its weak acceptation, escapes the concept — even and above all the concept in which Descartes wanted to establish it, the being the *mathesis universalis* does not comprehend. God is said by the infinite for Pascal as for Descartes; but for Pascal *infinite* no longer says anything, while for Descartes it states a privileged concept. Henceforth, as the infinite dissolves, God, receding correspondingly, fades. His nameless silence deserts the discourse, become garrulous, easy and vain, of the sciences and first of all of metaphysics.

The rupture marks itself clearly in a refusal of principle (pp. 310–316): Pascal's refusal of the proofs of God's existence, whatever they be. What is at issue is less a refusal of natural theology than the disqualification of metaphysical discourse applied to God — and that is how close witnesses understood it. Gilberte Périer: he claimed to show that the Christian religion had as many marks of certitude as the things received in the world as the most indubitable, and for that he made no use of metaphysical proofs. Filleau de la Chaise: after expounding what he thought of the proofs ordinarily used and showing how ill-suited men's heads are to metaphysical reasonings, he showed clearly that only the moral and historical proofs are within their reach. Marion adds a more remarkable point still: in all the *Pensées* the term "metaphysical" remains a hapax — but a hapax that qualifies precisely the metaphysical proofs of God's existence, and moreover in a "Preface," and so with the authority of a declaration of principle. The metaphysical proofs of God are so remote from men's reasoning and so involved that they strike little; and when they served some, it would serve only during the instant they see that demonstration, since an hour later they fear they have deceived themselves. The allusion to Descartes seems to Marion transparent: it is a question of the memory of evidences and, possibly, of the objection of the circle raised, as Pascal knew, by Arnauld. Pascal thus radically calls in question the Cartesian ambition not only of demonstrating God's existence but of *adoring* him by following the metaphysical ways of demonstration; having ruined the pretension of the infinite to fix a first divine name in metaphysics, he contests in general the legitimacy of any metaphysical attempt at access to God — in short, metaphysics cannot know God because it has no business knowing him. Marion sets aside the critique of the physical proofs, which perhaps aim at the Thomists, and examines the critique of the metaphysical proofs, with the intention of verifying whether and how far it finally reaches Descartes' special metaphysics as reconstituted. Two questions: do the reproaches addressed to metaphysical proofs correspond to the reproaches addressed in particular to Descartes; and is there a Cartesian proof literally cited and contested as such? On the first, a first reproach is stated, impotence — the metaphysical proofs of God are so remote from men's reasoning and so involved that they strike little; all those who claimed to know God and prove him without Jesus Christ had only impotent proofs. But to theoretical impotence a second grievance is often added, uselessness: it is not only impossible but useless to know God without Jesus Christ; the knowledge of God without that of one's misery makes for pride; and above all, I shall not undertake here to prove by natural reasons either God's existence, or the Trinity, or the immortality of the soul, or any of the things of that nature — not only because I should not feel strong enough to find in nature what would convince hardened atheists, but because this knowledge, without Jesus Christ, is useless and sterile. The metaphysical proofs of God by natural reasons thus expose themselves to two objections: their difficulty and imperfection render them little convincing, that is, impotent and so uncertain; and they do not bring one into the mystery of Jesus Christ, God but God who saves us from sin, that is, they are useless. Marion then asks whether they are in fact all uncertain — and answers that Pascal admits the contrary at least once, since he seems indeed to deploy one: I feel that I might not have been, for the self consists in my thought; therefore I who think would not have been if my mother had been killed before I was quickened; therefore I am not a necessary being. I am not eternal or infinite either, but I see clearly that there is in nature a being necessary, eternal and infinite. One recognizes without difficulty the popular version of the a posteriori proof of the third Meditation, as it repeats the argument drawn from the inherence in the ego of the idea of the infinite insofar as the ego recognizes itself produced by parents — and some even recognize in it, which does not contradict the first source, an Augustinian argumentation. On these two motives, should one not admit it as demonstrative? But precisely, supposing its evidence lasts more than an hour, it would still not make Jesus Christ known, and would deploy a *useless certainty*. Pascal illustrates his own double requirement perfectly in the "Infini rien" fragment, the so-called argument of the wager, which comprises three moments. First the metaphysical argument, which, barely sketched, concludes in the Cartesian manner from the finite to the infinite — we know that there is an infinite and are ignorant of its nature; so one can well know that there is a God without knowing what he is. Second, so that the perhaps certain proof does not sink into uselessness, it repeats itself on a more striking mode and issues in a success: that is demonstrative, and if men are capable of any truth this is it. Yet the demonstrative success does not dispense with a third moment, alone fundamental: to pray this infinite being without parts, and to submit yours also for your own good and for his glory. Pascal therefore admits at least one certain metaphysical demonstration, and the banal reproach of irrationalism or fideism does not hold; but he adds to it a second effort, the more indispensable in that the rational effort has succeeded, so as to redouble the constraint of evidence by the impetus of faith, or more precisely by God's help in believing in him. Uselessness would break out all the more if, at the term of a constraining and rigorous metaphysical proof, the interlocutor refused to engage his faith in God — even convinced, if he does not abandon himself, I should not find him much advanced for his salvation. The metaphysical proofs offer the more danger, and so uselessness, the more they prove certain. The scope of the suspicion of uselessness can now be made precise: the aim and stake of a discourse on God are *not summed up in the knowledge of God*; knowing God even exposes one to a redoubtable peril, pride. It is not first or only a question of knowing God but of loving him; and loving him implies recognizing his glory and admitting that men's sin obscures it, and so having recourse to Christ, who at a stroke reveals the God he is and frees from the sin we are. The pride of knowing God, deism, brings one no nearer God than ignorance of him, atheism, for God is worthily known only in loving him — since we know at the same time our misery, because that God is nothing other than the repairer of our misery, so that we cannot know God well except by knowing our iniquities; what curiosity has known, pride has lost; and it is equally dangerous for man to know God without knowing his misery and to know his misery without knowing the Redeemer who can heal him of it, since one alone of these knowledges makes either the pride of the philosophers, who have known God and not their misery, or the despair of the atheists, who know their misery without a Redeemer. To the metaphysical stake of the proofs of God's existence — does God exist or not, what name defines his essence? — Pascal substitutes a different stake, which metaphysics cannot but miss, since it depends on a parameter foreign to the question of being: do I love God to the point of renouncing, in order to approach him, my pride and my sin, which merge? The ultimate question no longer interrogates in view of the relation of God, being par excellence, to existence in general, but in view of the relation between man and his own pride or sin, and so between man and charity or Jesus Christ. Marion draws the consequence in the terms of his own project: the irruption of the parameter instituted by charity or pride disqualifies the pretension of metaphysical discourse to pose, concerning God, the *highest question*. As for God, it is not a matter of knowing whether he exists or not, as if being and existing enjoyed, before God, an unconditioned and unsuspectable precedence and excellence. On the contrary, before God, being and existing disclose *an idol among others*, though doubtless the most radical, since it permits the metaphysician to steal away by dissimulation from the reversal of the interrogation — which, far from deploying itself from him toward God, summoned to establish his rights to existence, would henceforth deploy itself from God to man, stripped of the ontological idol and summoned to decide whether he loves God or not. God no longer has so much to prove his existence before the metaphysician as the latter has, unveiled in his humanity, to decide whether he can say "Lord, I give you all." A metaphysical proof can therefore well be called useless even if, extraordinarily, it offers every certainty; it suffices that it provoke pride and forbid salvation. In this context the rejection of "Descartes useless and uncertain" becomes intelligible. Though illustrated at greater length apropos his physical theory — one must say roughly, this is done by figure and motion, for that is true; but to say which, and to compose the machine, that is ridiculous, for it is ridiculous and uncertain and painful — uselessness refers here, as in most of its other occurrences, to the love of God, the one thing necessary. Descartes is not reproached with ignoring God but with knowing him only in order to use him to regulate the machine of the world, far from submitting himself to it: I cannot forgive Descartes; in all his philosophy he would gladly have done without God, but he could not help making him give a flick to set the world in motion; after that he has no further use for God. What renders Descartes useless — for salvation — is that he uses God in order to know, instead of knowing God in order to serve him; whereby he offers the paradigm of the useless curiosity of the other sciences. Descartes misses the question of God not only because his metaphysical proofs remain uncertain but above all because they remain useless for salvation — in short because they simply do not see that with God what is at issue is less his existence than our decision toward him. Marion takes it as established that Pascal's objections against the metaphysical proofs of God aim exemplarily at Descartes' metaphysics.

The demonstration remains insufficient, however, until the second question is answered (pp. 316–320): is there a Cartesian proof literally cited and criticized by Pascal? Without such a verification the grievance of uselessness might bear on Cartesian thought in general without touching the special metaphysics of the third Meditation. And such a citation does occur, preceded and followed by a decisive critique, in a fragment of the first importance.
