Pascal shows there that the Christian religion consists in two points and not one — that there is a God of whom men are capable, and that there is a corruption that renders them unworthy of him. Consequently the knowledge alone of the existence of a God does not make one Christian but on the contrary deist, since men imagine that the Christian religion consists simply in the adoration of a God considered as great and powerful and eternal, which is properly deism. And among men it is the philosophers who commit that error par excellence, since one alone of these knowledges makes either the pride of the philosophers who have known God and not their misery, or the despair of the atheists. So Pascal explicitly cites the programme of special metaphysics in order to refuse it — and the programme he cites is the one announced by the title of Descartes' *Meditationes*: I shall not undertake here to prove by natural reasons either God's existence, or the Trinity, or the immortality of the soul, or any of the things of that nature — a sequence that opposes itself, to a term near, word for word to the Cartesian project of *Meditationes de prima Philosophia in qua Dei existentia et animae immortalitas demonstrantur*. And the project Descartes makes precise in two equally unacceptable points: that he treats not of God and the soul in particular but in general of all the first things one can know in philosophizing by order, which anticipates the "things of that nature"; and above all the opening of the address to the Sorbonne, that he always thought the two questions of God and of the soul chief among those to be demonstrated by philosophy rather than by theology. That is what Pascal does not hesitate to qualify a *blasphemy* — to claim to treat in strict philosophical rigour what falls first under charity and so under theology. And to disqualify the metaphysical enterprise concerning God radically, Pascal then cites Descartes directly, and — the remarkable point — cites his most original thesis, the least well received by his successors and without doubt the most powerful: the doctrine of the creation of the eternal truths, namely the three letters to Mersenne of April and May 1630. Marion follows the central development. Having stigmatized the useless knowledge, Pascal continues: if a man were persuaded that the proportions of numbers are immaterial truths, eternal and dependent on a first truth in which they subsist, and which one calls God, I should not find him much advanced for his salvation. He is citing Descartes literally, since the mathematical truths, which you call eternal, were established by God and depend entirely on him, as much as the rest of the creatures; and again, God's existence is the first and most eternal of all the truths that can be, and the only one from which the others proceed — granting the equivalence between the proportions of numbers and the mathematical truths. Pascal continues: the God of the Christians does not consist in a God simply author of geometrical truths and of the order of the elements; that is the portion of the pagans and of the Epicureans. Descartes had written that it is certain God is as much the author of the essence as of the existence of creatures, and that this essence is nothing other than these eternal truths. And more: by the very definition of God in which Pascal sees a blasphemy and the portion of the pagans and Epicureans, Descartes intends himself to avoid a blasphemy characteristic of the pagan philosophers — for it is to speak of God as of a Jupiter or Saturn, and to subject him to Styx and the fates, to say that the truths are independent of him. Marion's formulation of the irony is exact: the effort by which Descartes tries to tear himself from a pagan definition of God appears to Pascal as the pagan blasphemy par excellence. There is more; Pascal finally mentions, as the portion of the Jews, a second conception: God does not consist only in a God who exercises his providence over the life and goods of men, to give a happy series of years to those who adore him. It is perhaps here again an allusion to Descartes, who in the last effort of his thought recognized another determination of God, no longer theoretical but practical, and which he names precisely providence — we must often reflect on divine Providence and represent to ourselves that it is impossible for anything to happen otherwise than as it has been determined from all eternity by that Providence, so that it is like a Fatality or an immutable Necessity; and again, there is a God on whom all things depend, whose perfections are infinite, whose power is immense, whose decrees are infallible, which teaches us to take in good part everything that happens to us as being expressly sent to us by God. That conception of God remains indeed governed by the preceding, whose founding omnipotence it maintains. So Pascal does cite Descartes, in a manner the more significant for choosing his most original, least known and strongest argumentation — and the long note assembles the evidence that Pascal could have known the 1630 letters, indirectly through Mersenne, whom Descartes urged to publish everywhere that it is God who established these laws in nature as a King establishes laws in his kingdom, and directly through Clerselier's editions of the correspondence in 1657 and 1659, as well as through the manuscript collection published by Griselle, which records the opinion of a certain intellectual circle in Pascal's time and contains explicit discussion of the 1630 thesis, including the remark that Pascal called Descartes "the Doctor of reason." And so the evidently Cartesian enterprise of constructing metaphysical proofs of God loses all legitimacy before the only God to be known: but the God of Abraham, the God of Isaac, the God of Jacob, the God of the Christians, is a God of love and of consolation, a God who fills the soul and heart of those he possesses. And not in metaphysics but in Jesus Christ everything blazes with proofs. At the very moment when metaphysics, in reaching the God creator of the eternal truths, accomplishes its greatest effort to abolish the conceptual idols, it loses and strays itself — before the presence of a God who hides himself.

The examination of the texts has established that the antagonism between Descartes and Pascal concerns the legitimacy of a metaphysical proof, and so of a metaphysical name, of God (pp. 320–321); and it has identified the theoretical motive of that refusal. On Pascal's account the God of the Christians can be known only in Jesus Christ, indissolubly God and repairer, precisely because man remains indissociably capable of God by nature and incapable of God by sin — so that to claim to know God without Jesus Christ comes, for man, to denying his sin. And there is precisely no greater sin. To construct a metaphysical proof of God therefore comes to blaspheming.

The double result does not yet permit understanding why Pascal chooses precisely to cite and refute the Cartesian doctrine of the God creator of the eternal truths (pp. 321–324). The choice surprises because these are texts less accessible and known than those of the *Meditationes*; and it surprises above all because it seems possible — and Marion notes that this was his own earlier position — that the evident demonstration given in 1630 sacrificed less to metaphysics than the two other proofs and divine names, which operate exactly within an onto-theo-logy, God as *ens perfectissimum* in that of the *ens ut cogitatum* and God as *causa sui* in that of the *ens ut causatum*. It could seem, and can still seem, that just like the *idea infiniti* of 1641 which it announces, the "incomprehensible power" of 1630 takes a remarkable distance from every metaphysical interpretation of God — and in both cases Descartes stresses at will that he opposes himself to the blasphemy of the pagans and knows in order to admire and adore. Far from Pascal's recognizing in that already somewhat heroic effort an at least sketched overcoming of the metaphysical interpretation of God, he brings the iron of his critique to bear on it precisely, without concession or reserve. Why, then, does he refuse the 1630 doctrine any capacity, as he concedes to Plato, to dispose toward Christianity? Before so considerable a difficulty any anecdotal answer — ignorance, misunderstanding — would be inadmissible, and Marion risks two arguments while awaiting a more fundamental reflection. The Cartesian thesis of an all-powerful God who oversteps every creature and every rationality can seem the less Christian to Pascal in that he perhaps read it under Montaigne's pen, attributing it moreover to *pagans*. For the *Apologie de Raimond Sebond* anticipates Descartes: of all the human and ancient opinions touching religion, the one that seems to have had most verisimilitude and most excuse is the one that recognized God as an incomprehensible power, origin and conserver of all things, all goodness, all perfection. On Montaigne's account Paul on the Areopagus would have saluted this conception in evoking the unknown god, and Pythagoras came closest to the truth in judging that the knowledge of this first cause and being of beings must be indefinite, without prescription, without declaration. And Montaigne concludes, in Descartes' manner, to the impossibility of confining God within the limits of our understanding: has God put into our hands the keys and the ultimate springs of his power; has he bound himself not to overstep the bounds of our science? I do not find it good thus to enclose the divine power under the laws of our speech. How rashly the Stoics attached God to destiny — and, willingly, that some who bear the surname of Christians should not still do so — and Thales, Plato and Pythagoras enslaved him to necessity. If Montaigne had thus sustained, sometimes to the benefit of pagans, the very doctrine Descartes takes up to the benefit of Christians, Pascal could reasonably doubt that it traces a clean frontier between the idols and the God of the Christians; whence his refusal to read in it a privileged metaphysical proof, even though Descartes had by it effectively surmounted proofs and divine names more essentially metaphysical. But this argument of external criticism does not suffice, since Pascal could have read Descartes without thinking — this time at least — of Montaigne. A second argument can be released, internal to the Pascalian reasonings at issue. The very text that uses the hapax "metaphysical" stigmatizes the *curiositas* that the knowledge of God drawn without Jesus Christ produces, so as to imagine one knows God too well while dispensing with admitting also the misery due to sin. The text that criticizes the Cartesian doctrine takes up and makes precise the same reproach: knowing God requires in truth knowing not one but *two* truths, that there is a God of whom men are capable and that there is a corruption in nature that renders them unworthy of him. Consequently acceding to God comes to progressing toward a God who does not manifest himself to men with all the evidence he could, by virtue precisely of sin; it is a matter of knowing not according to evidence but according to the half-light of a presence of a God who hides himself — in short, one must see and not see. Now the ambition of all metaphysics concerning God, and more than any other of Cartesian metaphysics, consists on the contrary in knowing with full evidence, without remainder, without confusion, without appeal. In each of his proofs Descartes aims at and claims an evident knowledge of God. In 1630 it is a matter of demonstrating the metaphysical truths in a way more evident than the demonstrations of Geometry. In the proof by the *idea infiniti*, that idea and the knowledge it permits give themselves as *maxime clara et distincta*. In the a priori proof the *ens summe perfectum* appears the most clearly of all beings, since nothing would be recognized as prior or easier, for what is more open? In the proof by the *causa sui*, Descartes leans on an anticipation of the principle of reason, which the natural light dictates as an indisputable common notion manifest to all. The knowledge of God, for Descartes, is accomplished in evidence because it first accomplishes evidence itself: to know God one must first, always and only *know*; and if it will afterwards be fitting to adore him and even to love him, that will be possible and justified only on the basis of a certain knowledge, because clear and distinct. This is the blasphemy and the idolatry in Pascal's eyes, for whom the love of God precedes and makes possible the knowledge of him, since whoever reasons about God thinks, whether he knows it or not, in a theoretical situation affected by the misery of sin: truth is so obscured that unless one loves the truth one cannot know it. The very fact that knowledge orients itself from the objects of the world toward God imposes on it a kind of epistemological conversion — renouncing the horizon of evidence for that of love. Not that love dispenses with knowing or requires any sacrifice of intelligibility; but love becomes, in place of *intuitus*, the guardian of evidence, the royal road of knowledge: whereas in speaking of human things one says they must be known before being loved, which has passed into a proverb, the saints on the contrary say in speaking of divine things that they must be loved in order to be known, and that one enters into truth only through charity. Metaphysics remains widowed of the thought of God — less through the insufficiency of its procedures and the inadaptation of its concepts, which it never stops refining and amending, as Descartes shows well enough, than through its epistemological and methodological bankruptcy, since it still claims to lean on evidence at the very moment when, with the question of God, method and more radically the regard of evidence lose all efficacy. Better: the God of the Christians would not be so accessible to charity alone if he were not exhausted in it; to claim to know him without loving him comes to missing, from the outset and in principle, both the way and the term. Metaphysics closes itself to God precisely because it wants to accede to him in full certainty and total evidence. Between evidence and charity one must choose, and what separates Pascal and Descartes is nothing but that choice — a choice that separates them infinitely, for how far it is from the knowledge of God to loving him. Marion adds one reservation in the note, and it is a real one: Pascal passes over in silence the distinction, nonetheless essential for Descartes, between evidence obtained by the method, comprehending, and evidence seen without method, understanding.

### § 23. The Distance Between the Orders

Before metaphysics as Descartes represents it to him, Pascal accomplishes a step back — the very step that separates the knowledge of God from the love of God (pp. 325–329). That step back releases, for the first time, a "distance." But it does not suffice to name such a distance in order to conceive it, and still less to justify it: before Pascal had crossed, in reverse, the gap that henceforth separates him from Cartesian metaphysics, we had no notion of it and not the least presentiment; the evidence of evidence still appeared unsurpassable and unreformable, and the metaphysical proofs of God still furnished the ultimate goal. To understand the Pascalian critique, then, one must not only give a name to his procedure but — short of taking up the received foolishness about his exaltation, his fideism and his irrationalism — *repeat it step by step*. Otherwise put, it is a question of releasing the fundamental operation that permits Pascal to destitute metaphysics: either to derange its concepts or to refuse them. To derange the concepts — thus the infinite became, from a divine name and privileged determination of God, the endlessly multiplied index of the incommensurability of the shattered elements of what can no longer be named a cosmos; from a concept it finds itself lowered to the rank of a metaphor used to undo other concepts. To refuse the concepts — evidence, metaphysical proofs, author of geometrical truths, providence, principles, philosophy, all find themselves disqualified at a stroke, without any itself conceptual refutation ever justifying it; everything happens as though Pascal substituted for rational discussion a refusal to hear that annuls the thesis without according it the least attention. In no case did the debate take place: metaphysics was disqualified, not really refuted but destroyed. How then not to conclude to an ideological or fanatical violence? Precisely, Marion answers, by undertaking to accomplish the step back out of metaphysics, which alone permitted Pascal to have no further debate with it, because he henceforth saw it from a place definitively foreign to it. So long as that place is not identified and, at least in outline, reached, so long as we have not really traversed the withdrawal at whose term Pascal precedes us, we can see in it only a senseless violence. Step back — one should even say a *retreat*, since Pascal's withdrawal out of metaphysics under its Cartesian face borrows its status at once from a religious retreat, distracting oneself from the world so as no longer to be distracted from God, and from a military retreat, refusing to fight too frightening an adversary. The step back out of metaphysics does not here go back to the non-metaphysical origin of metaphysics; it tries to reach a ground other than metaphysics itself. Whence the difficulty of the Pascalian enterprise, a continual reversal from one instance to another, but also the difficulty for its interpreter, who must ceaselessly put back into relation — in order to understand their distance — two terms that Pascal's whole effort wanted to separate; the ambivalence of "distance," indissolubly a gap and a common station, becomes here the stake but also the condition of the debate. The question would therefore be stated in these terms: does Pascal know that he destitutes metaphysics of its status as primordial science, and if he knows it, does he risk, if only once, an open confrontation with it? Marion holds that this is the case in the fragment on the three orders. Before any examination, a massive though dissimulated fact must be stressed: *the three orders take up the three objects of special metaphysics*. The first order gathers the bodies, that is, the world, whether one understands it as the cosmos of powers, goods and things or interprets it already as the extension of physics. The second order bears on the soul as possibly rational intelligence — Archimedes, great geniuses, the production of science, the curious and the learned who have the mind for object. The third order concerns God, whether in the person of Jesus Christ, humble, patient, holy, holy, holy to God, terrible to demons, without any sin, or directly, since the saints are seen by God and the angels and not by bodies or curious minds, God being enough for them. The encounter cannot be underestimated, since it attests not only that Pascal in fact gathered the three objects of special metaphysics as Descartes had treated them in the *Meditationes*, but establishes above all that he recenses them only in order to *disjoin* them. Far from constituting a system in which the existence of some refers to that of others by links of efficient causality or logical implication, here, in the Pascalian resumption, a distance separates them definitively. The whole text orchestrates that irremediable shattering, and opens on the statement of the catastrophe: the infinite distance of bodies from minds figures the infinitely more infinite distance of minds from charity, for it is supernatural. The elements of special metaphysics are indeed taken up and recensed, but it falls to the "infinite distance" to mediate them, where infinite counts for incommensurability; the infinite, then infinitely infinite distance abolishes at a stroke every commensurable relation and even every ordinance among the terms — neither *ordo* nor *mensura* secures, in a systematized series, the linking of the objects of special metaphysics. The three terms, world, soul and God, juxtapose themselves so as to manifest that they do not make a system, and that no common parameter gathers them into a univocal intelligibility. Metaphysics, on the contrary, submits the three privileged beings to the common measure of a parameter by right if not always in title univocal — the concept of being; and Descartes makes no exception, trying to think the objects of special metaphysics now on the *ens ut cogitatum*, now on the *ens ut causatum*, in both cases with the same parameter concerning God and soul and world alike, since God is known as *cogitatum* or as *causa sui* on concepts that make a system with all the other concepts the corresponding onto-theo-logies permit. The heterogeneity implied by the infinite and then infinitely infinite distance excludes the least univocal parameter or concept that would render the three objects accessible on a single logic. More, it seems licit to infer from the heterogeneity the distances provoke the inadmissibility of *every onto-theo-logy in general*, if onto-theo-logy at least implies a concept common, indeed univocal, to all the terms it covers. The argumentation so far lacking marks itself first, then, in the architectonic that juxtaposes at a distance and in perfect heterogeneity the very terms of which special metaphysics erects the system and of which every onto-theo-logy tries to render the knowledge and the foundation univocal. A second remark follows. Between each of the three terms Pascal institutes the incommensurable caesura of an infinitely infinite distance, which can be crossed only by a transgression. Now metaphysics is defined precisely by transgression, and that transgression plays between the things of nature and immaterial things, so that the Pascalian transgression too plays between the sensible material — flesh — and the insensible immaterial — minds. Would the two transgressions not coincide, Pascal repeating between the first two orders the transgression that already properly characterizes metaphysics? Doubtless. But then does Pascal not recognize the legitimacy of metaphysics far from disqualifying it? On the contrary — and Marion's answer is the section's decisive move. It is precisely here that he destitutes it most decidedly: for supposing that the infinite distance between bodies and minds repeats the metaphysical transgression, one must immediately add that this first transgression itself undergoes a second transgression, and moreover a transgression that raises the first incommensurability to a power, an infinitely infinite distance. If metaphysics were by chance to find a legitimate place in the second order, it would at once be convicted of illegitimacy — of injustice, Pascal will say — by the second transgression that opens onto the third order. Here is the decisive paradox: if one re-establishes metaphysics at the centre of the hierarchy of the three orders, which literally can be conceived, one must at once destitute it of all authority before the irruption of a transgression of the transgression, in short of a *metabasis* out of metaphysics. It is not a question of a "metaphysics of metaphysics" in Kant's phrase, but of a crossing beyond metaphysics. The step back out of metaphysics can be located in the very transgression of the infinite distance, which still mimes the metaphysical transgression, by the infinitely infinite distance. A second time, then, Pascal's still dissimulated argumentation is located in the architectonic of the three orders and their distances: even if metaphysics takes place there, it undergoes there an infinite transgression. There are now indices numerous enough of a formal correspondence between the question of the status of Cartesian metaphysics and the Pascalian doctrine of the three orders to study the latter with a view to determining the former — in short, to read the fragment as a structure of overcoming of Cartesian metaphysics.

"They are three orders differing in kind": three things must be determined — the sense of "order," which three terms are retained, and why they differ in kind (pp. 329–334). On the first, the two acceptations of "order" appear in the bundle "Proofs of Jesus Christ." Order is heard first of an ordered disposition of demonstrations and of discourse: against the objection that Scripture has no order, one must release that the heart has its order, the mind has its own which is by principle and demonstration, and the heart has another. In evoking two manners of disposing in order Pascal is evidently thinking of Descartes — to demonstrate by order as in geometry — but he frees himself from him just as much, since he claims to institute another order than that of evidence: I shall write my thoughts here without order and not perhaps in an aimless confusion; that is the true order, and it will always mark my object by disorder itself. The order of evidence does not always suit, above all when what must be treated is what excludes evidence: I should do my subject too much honour if I treated it with order, since I want to show that it is incapable of it. Thus is broken the equivalence that sums up the whole Cartesian method, between order as the disposition of a series and the production of evidence — the identification of bringing-to-evidence with putting-in-order. Order, as the ordinance of matters or reasons, becomes free again, for Pascal, for an end other than evidence; and the *esprit de finesse* has first the function of giving a name to a use of order and a justness of reasoning that owe nothing to method taken as disposition according to order in view of evidence. Order can be exercised beyond its Cartesian definition and can convince without demonstrating — it transgresses evidence. The second acceptation of order, understood as a *class*, operates a comparable distinction, and precisely because several orders or classes must be distinguished among beings: various sorts of right sense, some in a certain order of things and not in the other orders where they go astray. In particular the distinction between thought and extension implies nothing less than a distinction between two orders — our intelligence holds in the order of intelligible things the same rank as our body in the extension of nature; just as the earlier distinction between two modes of disposing thoughts, aiming at evidence or not, leans on the distinction of two classes: Jesus Christ and St Paul have the order of charity, not of the mind, for they wanted to abase and not to instruct. That the mind, the workman of a rationality ordered exclusively to evidence, must admit another order — and Pascal batters the universal privilege Descartes recognized in good sense and in the *bona mens*. Doubtless Descartes admitted the irreducible domain of faith and revelation, but he attributed it only to the will, without damage to the understanding, since faith, whatever concerns obscure things, is not an act of the mind but of the will. Pascal on the contrary defines a new order which, while itself also privileging the will, exercises in return an empire and a critique over the evidence it deranges, over the truth whose idolatry it can stigmatize, over thought itself, which it lowers to a vanity. Like the order of Melchizedek, the order that redoubles evidence owes nothing to the world or to the mind — without genealogy or condition, it could therefore call all the other orders into question. So the two acceptations of "order," though semantically distinct, share the same subversion of the Cartesian concept of order; they circumvent the uniform and homogeneous disposition of the series for the production of universal evidence. Next, the three terms that mobilize the three orders must be identified. It can be taken as established that Pascal first took up a biblical enumeration of the three concupiscences, everything in the world being the concupiscence of the flesh, the concupiscence of the eyes and the pride of life; and Augustine had given a very clear interpretation of it, since concupiscence of the flesh signifies those who love the lowest pleasure, concupiscence of the eyes the curious, and the ambition of the age the proud. That topic of three temptations inspires directly an apparent parallel: concupiscence of the flesh, concupiscence of the eyes, pride; there are three orders of things — the flesh, the mind, the will; the carnal are the rich, the kings, they have the body for object; the curious and the learned, they have the mind for object; the wise, they have justice for object; God must reign over all and all be referred to him; in things of the flesh concupiscence properly reigns, in the spiritual curiosity properly, in wisdom pride properly. The orders appear there already, certainly, but strictly *deduced from the concupiscences* — which is why the third order cannot yet receive the dignity of charity, remaining defined by pride, which the will exercises. Consequently God intervenes only outside the three orders, since all three still reduce directly to the three Johannine concupiscences and must equally be combated. Marion locates Pascal's characteristic and brilliant innovation exactly here: transforming the triple danger into a triple *world*. The concupiscence of the flesh no longer covers only the temptation of pleasures but designates first every thing insofar as corporeal, that is, everything eyes of flesh permit one to see — a first world is thus discovered. The concupiscence of the eyes is no longer confined to the *libido sciendi* understood as a mere distracted curiosity, or divertissement, but covers every thing insofar as intelligible, everything that the mind's regard alone manages to see — the mind's eyes, which see the demonstrations, in Spinoza's very Pascalian phrase — and this is the second world discovered, that of the sciences and of learning, to which only the *respublica litteraria* and the scientific community accede. The concupiscence of pride disappears to give place to its inverse, the order of charity, which becomes visible only on a third mode of vision, the eyes of the heart which see wisdom, seeing with the eyes of faith. Charity no longer intervenes as the pious and superfluous auxiliary of the passion of love; it opens a distinct world by opening other eyes in man: for my part, I confess that as soon as the Christian religion discovers this principle, that men's nature is corrupted and fallen from God, that opens everywhere the character of this truth. Charity, from a secondary and ambiguous passion, accedes to the rank of a hermeneutic principle: once its point of view is admitted, that is, as soon as the mind manages to accede to it, another world, or other dimensions of the same world, is discovered at once. Charity interprets because it *reveals*, as a developer makes appear, on the darkness of photographic paper, the unforeseeable fire of the colours; so charity provokes the world, first seen in its two natural orders, to impregnate itself, to take on colour and to redraw itself in the unthinkable and unforeseeably visible tints of its glory or its abandon; under the too powerful and iridescent light of charity the world appears in all its dimensions, on all its parameters, with all its contrasts — in short in truth. Pascal's decisive innovation therefore lies not in introducing the syntagm "order of charity," nor even in instituting three orders rather than three concupiscences, but in *establishing a third order*. His audacity becomes sharper as soon as one notices what contradictory traits define that order of charity. On one hand it dominates the two other orders, whose principle it brings to light and in which it recognizes itself only as in a figure — a hermeneutic function. On the other hand it transcends them by a distance infinitely more infinite than the distance of bodies from minds: the second gap does not merely repeat the first as in an analogy but raises its incommensurability to the second power, and the fragment is framed entirely between the two mentions of that incommensurability and of its motive — it is supernatural, closing the first paragraph, and of another supernatural order, defining the last. The same order of charity gives itself as at once necessary and supernatural, and the contradiction appears insurmountable: either the third order is necessary to the intelligence of the first two and remains natural, or, revealed by God's gracious gift, it no longer belongs to the natural field and does not necessarily define its logic.

The contradiction appears only so long as the third trait of the orders has not been correctly considered (pp. 334–336): they are three orders differing *in kind*. Heterogeneity does not forbid their respective relations but defines them precisely — so that the third order can without contradiction be a theoretical requirement necessary to the first two and remain supernatural. The carnal greatnesses have no relation with the great geniuses, and the saints in turn have no relation with the inferior greatnesses; in this sense too the concupiscences manifest, under the light of charity, that they are incommensurable with it: I see my abyss of pride, curiosity, concupiscence; there is no relation of me to God or to Jesus Christ the just. Does such an incommensurability, which forbids measuring the least relation among the orders, not also provoke their absolute heterogeneity, to the point that they would themselves raise a difficulty of interpretation far from securing a hermeneutic function? Such is not the case, Marion answers, because a relation — incommensurable, certainly — links the orders by fixing them in a hierarchy: the *regard*. The orders differ in kind because the inferior order cannot see the greatnesses of the superior order, because the greatnesses of each order become visible only to a regard appropriate to that order, and because the superior order always judges the greatnesses of the inferior. Contrary to sensible or worldly experience, which establishes that the higher one rises the more visible one becomes and the less one sees what one moves away from, in Pascal's topic the further one progresses in the hierarchy of orders the *less* one remains visible: the greatness of men of mind is invisible to kings, to the rich, to captains, to all these great ones of the flesh; the greatness of wisdom, which is nothing if not God, is invisible to the carnal and to men of mind. The more an order sets to work greatnesses of its own, the more the regard that claims to see it must itself belong to it. All are carnal, so the first order offers itself visibly to all. Not all think in mind, so the second order is invisible to the first: the legionary kills Archimedes, certainly because he does not know him, but above all because he does not recognize, in the traits Archimedes draws in the sand, lines, circles and polygons — to spare Archimedes one would first have had to be a geometer. Finally few accede to charity, so the third order remains invisible to the first two: Jesus Christ is put to death by legionaries, of the first order, but also by men of mind, scribes and Pharisees and judges, of the second, because none recognized in him the Word of God; not to kill Christ one would have had to see him as he was seen by God and the angels and not by bodies or curious minds. But the superior order still sees the inferior orders: the least of minds knows all that — bodies, the firmament, the stars, the earth and its kingdoms — and itself, and bodies know nothing. The second order judges the first in terms of value; and likewise the third judges the second in terms of value, since all bodies together and all minds together are not worth the least movement of charity — thought is therefore an admirable and incomparable thing by its nature, and it must have had strange defects to be contemptible, but it has such that nothing is more ridiculous. Consequently the invisible holiness norms and knows what ignores it and does not see it, exactly as, beside the sensible effects everyone sees, the causes are visible only to the mind. The heterogeneity of the orders verifies itself on the *ascent* of their hierarchy, where the superior order remains invisible to the inferior, and there the dislocation of the system of special metaphysics is accomplished. But on a *descent* along the hierarchy a continuity re-establishes itself, since the superior order evaluates and judges the inferior — whence opens the possibility of a judgement of everything by charity. Each order suffices to itself, appearing to its own eyes, governs the inferior, and dispenses with the superior; it binds itself necessarily to the one and facultatively, supernaturally, to the other. Only the third order sees the two others, so that paradoxically it must remain invisible to them. That we do not accede easily — naturally — to the order of charity therefore casts no doubt on its reality or efficacy but confirms on the contrary that charity gives to see only to the eyes of the heart and not at all to curious minds. To see the order of charity one must less know a new object than know on a new condition, to love: here one enters into truth only through charity. The second order, which does not have to love in order to produce its evidence, therefore does not attain the third and does not even see that it does not attain it — it sees neither the invisible nor that it does not see it.

The three principal characters of the doctrine of the orders have been released; it remains to establish that this is a structure of overcoming of Cartesian metaphysics, that is, that Descartes' metaphysics finds an exactly appropriate site in the structure of the orders, so that its overcoming is operated there (pp. 336–338). The ambition, however excessive it looks, does not require an endless development, and two arguments suffice. The first is that Descartes belongs to the second order, and the indices converge. The second order concerns the *researches of the mind*, since two of Descartes' works carry that expression in their title; Descartes recognizes himself under Archimedes, whom he takes for a model in the second Meditation, since Archimedes asked for nothing but a point; and Descartes, like Archimedes, gave no battle for the eyes but furnished all minds with his inventions — for it is truly to give battle to try to vanquish all the difficulties and errors that prevent us from attaining knowledge of the truth. Above all, the second order institutes itself by transgression of all bodies together, thanks to the thought the mind takes of them: for it knows all that, and itself, and bodies know nothing. What is at issue is evidently the distinction between the *res extensa* and the *res cogitans*, and self-consciousness, *cogitatio sui ipsius*, as condition of possibility of the consciousness of anything else; and the parallels are not lacking — it is not from space that I must seek my dignity but from the regulation of my thought; by space the universe comprehends me and swallows me like a point, by thought I comprehend it; man is only a reed, the weakest in nature, but a thinking reed, and even if the universe crushed him he would still be nobler than what kills him, since he knows that he dies and the advantage the universe has over him, while the universe knows nothing of it; all our dignity consists therefore in thought. The second order holds to thought, but thought also takes the figure of the ego, since the self consists in thought; and a supplementary Cartesian trait confirms the preceding one, that thought accomplishes itself in representing extension and thereby affirming the anteriority of the ego. Not only does the second order recover the determinations of *cogitatio* set to work by an ego; it manages to establish its transcendence over the first order only by the Cartesian privilege of *cogitatio* over every possible *cogitatum* — not to result from the world but to precede it as its a priori. Descartes thus enters into the structure of the three orders, at the hinge of the first two; and henceforth nothing will affect the second order that does not equally affect the metaphysical figure of Descartes.

Whence the second argument (pp. 338–342): just as the transition from the first to the second order is accomplished thanks to the metaphysical operation of the *ego cogito*, so the transition from the second to the third will be accomplished *at the expense* of the Cartesian figure of the *ego cogito*, confirming that the whole structure of the three orders takes it for privileged stake. From the second to the third order the discontinuity goes without saying: all minds together and all their productions are not worth the least movement of charity, which is of an infinitely higher order; from all bodies and minds one could not draw one movement of true charity, which is impossible and of another supernatural order. But the *explanation* of that discontinuity does not go without saying. Between the first two orders the discontinuity held its intelligibility first from the common experience of thought and corporeity, then from the doctrine, itself Cartesian, of perception: the sensible perceived subjectively can be figured in intelligible models, invisible in sensation and visible to the mind, which Nature is supposed to have effectively instituted. But between the last two orders the heterogeneity, and indeed the transition, offers a redoubtable difficulty. We cannot naturally claim to know the order of charity, for it is supernatural; more, it is no longer a question of knowing at all, since knowledge results from thought and so falls under the second order — and Pascal indeed eliminates every "thought" concerning charity and substitutes for it the "movement of charity": charity does not represent itself nor represent, it *acts*, as love is made more than it is known. How then accede to the order of charity? Strictly, Pascal admits only one direct way, holiness; and Marion concedes that it will discourage and repel the immense majority of the instructed tribe, and recognizes too that it will not permit conceiving the transition between the last two orders, nor how the truth of the *ego cogito* could fail to be worth a movement of charity. Paradoxically, the solution of the aporia can come to us in part from a recourse to Descartes. The transition between the first order, extension, and the second, thought, is located in Cartesian doctrine in the theory of the code: sensations are only the sensible effect of intelligible figures, mathematical models, effectively instituted by nature; between effects and causes a coding plays, since the sensible effects decode, and disfigure, intelligible and effective figures. Now Descartes put that two-term coding in doubt in the first Meditation by envisaging the hypothesis of an *overcoding*: just as the blue of the sky in fact results from figures and strictly rational causes, models, which in no way resemble it, might one not envisage that these initial rational models themselves obey a system of axioms totally different from those we know, of which they would be only the effects — effects of unknown principles? Just as I judge that others sometimes err about what they think they know most perfectly, so might I be deceived whenever I add two and three, or count the sides of a square, or do anything simpler if anything simpler can be imagined. Descartes finally set aside the hypothesis of overcoding, because it would have supposed a lie in God, and re-established the simple coding between extension and thought. Marion's suggestion is that the Pascalian structure of the three orders could be constituted on an analogy with the Cartesian hypotheses of coding and overcoding — the major objection against overcoding disappearing here, since Pascal has recourse to it in order to thematize not a divine deception but, on the contrary, God's very charity. The three stages resumed: the first order, extension and the sensible, abolishes itself in the second, thought, models and figures, by the operators of heterogeneity, representation, coding, figuration, disfiguration; the parameter of the second order remains Cartesian evidence, and Pascal follows Descartes exactly up to this point. They diverge at the limit of the second order. For Descartes it has precisely no limit but that of the human understanding, and exposes itself to no overcoding, since evidence remains unsurpassable; Pascal on the contrary takes up the Cartesian hypothesis of an overcoding and manages to confirm it — because he isolates a *new parameter*. Where Descartes envisaged only a second evidence, or an evidence redoubled against its first being and so against itself, and issued in an untenable contradiction, Pascal undertakes to overcode thought, itself already the code of extension, only by submitting it to a parameter other than its own — exposing it no longer to the natural light of evidence but to the invisible clarity of charity. The third order will thus reveal itself indirectly, by the distortion its lighting, the glow of charity, will bring to bear on the elements of the second order. The first and principal term of the second order, the first being it defines, has the name ego; and in the light of evidence Pascal admits and salutes the primacy of the ego on the order of thought — man is visibly made to think, and the order of thought is to begin with oneself, and with one's author and one's end — just as for Descartes the immortality of the soul, of the second order, precedes God's existence, of the third. What becomes of that primacy according to evidence in the glow of charity? Otherwise put, can the anteriority of the ego according to evidence — *primus enim sum* — maintain itself identically in the third order? Between the second and the third order the infinitely infinite distance lies in the gap between the parameters, evidence on one side and charity on the other; and one must adore only in one's own order, and not claim to displace the parameter from one order to another — which defines *tyranny*. When the ego, on the pretext that primacy according to evidence necessarily defines the second order, demands the same primacy in the third and so claims that other men should love it first because it, as ego, knows first, it confounds the orders and exercises a tyranny, since the recognized centre of the second order contradicts the third. Marion's note is careful about the charge: Descartes would be true in his own order and tyrannical only in claiming to overstep it — but does Descartes ever apply the *ego cogito* beyond the theoretical field? Doubtless he does, in maintaining it as the paradigm of morals, since the sovereign good is defined by contentment with oneself, and free will renders us in some fashion like God by making us masters of ourselves and seems to exempt us from being subject to him. And more, because the wise have justice for object, this tyranny reveals that the ego is *unjust*: I hate the self because it is unjust that it make itself the centre of everything; the nature of self-love and of this human self is to love only itself and consider only itself, but it cannot prevent the object it loves from being full of defect and misery, and it wants to be the object of men's love and esteem while seeing that its defects deserve only their aversion and contempt — an embarrassment that produces in it the most unjust and criminal of passions, a mortal hatred against the truth that reproves it. And who does not see that nothing is so opposed to justice and truth as making oneself God, since it is false that we deserve it and unjust and impossible to attain it. It is false that we are worthy of others' loving us; it is unjust that we should want it; for everything tends to itself, which is against all order — we are born therefore unjust and depraved. The Cartesian ego lacks justice, referred to charity as a centre, as much as it lacked correctness, referred to evidence as a first principle; the same ego becomes, from one order to the other, a strange monster, not by intrinsic variation but by relative situation. One must know oneself, *cogitatio sui*, and not love oneself, *amour-propre*; for knowledge of self permits the ego to know other beings, while love of self prevents the self from loving other beings. Self-knowledge attests the anteriority of the ego, which can in fact dispense with every other certainty, whereas self-love betrays its tyranny in having, in order to accomplish itself, to destroy every other object. The ego attests itself henceforth unjust with regard to the third order, and so also *useless* to charity — in the sense in which it would have been useless for Our Lord Jesus Christ to come as a king, but he came indeed with the brilliance of his own order.

Passing from the second order to the third implies subverting the ego (p. 342), or more exactly disqualifying in the order of charity the legitimate primacy Descartes recognized in it in the order of the mind: the ego must know itself first, and therefore cannot love itself exclusively. Overcoming the Cartesian figure of metaphysics consists in not loving oneself as one thinks oneself. One must hate the ego on charity as one had to see its dignity on thought, so that only God remains to be loved: one must love only God and hate only oneself. In the first order the universe moves according to body, extension. In the second, the ego thinks itself according to thought — Descartes. In the third, God gives himself to be loved according to charity. Cartesian metaphysics occupies the centre of the structure of the orders, and that is precisely why it cannot constitute the "centre" the third order aims at; intrinsically bound to the *ego cogito*, it shares its subversion by charity.

### § 24. The Ego Undone and the Decentring of the Self

Overcoming metaphysics in its Cartesian figure — if that is indeed the goal the doctrine of the three orders sets itself, in the central fragment which is like the summary of all the *Pensées*, as Balthasar calls it — requires at once overcoming its first principle: the originary and autonomous existence of the *ego cogito* (pp. 343–344). Pascal had the more to attempt it in that the ego marks and identifies the metaphysical enterprise not only in Descartes but already in Montaigne, moreover often read from Descartes or inversely. For Montaigne, who employs the term "metaphysics" only sparingly, identifies the ego — the experience we have of ourselves, which is more familiar to us and certainly sufficient to instruct us in what we need — precisely with metaphysics: I study myself more than any other subject; that is my metaphysics. Marion's note extends the point beyond the second order, since Montaigne never stops claiming to found morals on the self: the man of understanding has lost nothing if he has himself; the greatest thing in the world is to know how to belong to oneself; it is not my gestures that I write, it is myself, it is my essence; finding myself entirely destitute and void of every other matter, I presented myself to myself for argument and subject. In Pascalian terms, that void of every other matter proves that man's heart is hollow and full of filth — and Marion stresses that the Cartesian morals of contentment with oneself accomplishes Montaigne's intention exactly. Two motives, and not the least, since these are the two privileged interlocutors of the *Pensées*, trace something like a duty of overcoming, along with metaphysics, its privileged operator, the ego as it studies or cogitates itself. To the ego, then, Pascal will object that its autonomy in the second order becomes illusory in the third, and forbids it every claim to the status of an origin or a centre.

The defeat of the ego is accomplished above all in the fragment that asks exactly, what is the self? (pp. 344–347). A first interrogation: why does Pascal substitute *moi* for *je* and the ego? It is not a stylistic concern, as the very numerous occurrences of *je* prove; it is a conceptual decision. *Je* commands the verb, in the nominative, while *moi* completes it directly, in the accusative: *I* think, see, say; the *self* is thought, said, seen. And moreover the fragment takes up, inverting it on this point, a famous analysis of Descartes. In the second Meditation I lean out of the window to see appearances of men who are perhaps not men — I look out of the window at men whom I say I see. Here on the contrary the speaker no longer dominates the passing appearances from the window; he walks among them and sees the regard of an *I* who occupies the posture of a *self*: a man who puts himself at the window to see the passers-by; if I pass by, can I say he put himself there to see me? Not only does *I* become a *self* in no longer exercising the regard but undergoing it, from below upward, but from that regard the self no longer expects a bringing-to-evidence or a clear and distinct knowledge. Where the Cartesian analysis issues in confirming that everything I believe I see with my sensible eyes I see with the eyes of the mind — I comprehend it by the sole faculty of judging that is in my mind — the Pascalian counter-test discloses the eyes of the heart: no, for he is not thinking of me in particular; but does one who loves someone for her beauty love *her*? Under the inverted regard, *I*, become a self, asks neither to know nor even to be known, but to be *recognized*, that is, loved. The ego tries to exhaust the *mens humana* first in knowing by *intuitus*, then in turning the *intuitus* back on itself, in short in exercising the *intuitus mentis* on the double dimension of the genitive. That operation keeps all its legitimacy in the second order, where evidence is at issue; but in the glow of the third it dislocates: *je* splits into this *je* that I no longer am and a *moi* that waits to be seen in order to be. The regard deepens from evidence to love: to become myself, I must be neither seen, nor thought, nor cogitated, but nothing less than *loved*. To the Cartesian question about the conditions of exercise of my *cogitatio* is substituted the interrogation about the possibility that one love *me*, as me: and if one loves me for my judgement, for my memory, does one love *me*? No, for I can lose these qualities without losing myself. For the self to *be*, it must be loved, and not known. More: *je* is what it necessarily knows of itself, *mens*, *res cogitans*, *substantia*; but the self cannot be confined to its eventual substance — for would one love the substance of a person's soul abstractly, and whatever qualities were in it? The gap between substance and its principal attribute, which Descartes discerned without ever filling, can no longer be reduced. Descartes evaded the difficulty first because the ego, turning *cogitatio* back on itself, experienced itself directly in its thought, and then and above all because, remaining in the second order, he did not have to take account of persons — to push the regard as far as the unsubstitutable and irreducible individuality of the *haecceitas*. Pascal on the contrary, who transcends the order of evidence by that of love, imposes on the ego become self requirements infinitely more infinite: the self can be loved only as such, as the soul of a person; and neither the mind and its qualities nor a fortiori the body and its modalities cover the person. Whence it follows that, if it is a matter of loving, the ego become the self remains insufficient, summary, untenable. Consequence: one never loves anyone. Which is to be heard thus — the ego, to be loved as a self, not only cannot remain autonomous, since it needs another regard to love it, but does not suffice to define the only possible addressee of a love, the unsubstitutable person. In this context a sequence becomes intelligible: I do not know who put me into the world, nor what the world is, nor what I myself am; I am in a terrible ignorance of all these things; I do not know what my body is, what my senses are, what my soul is, and that part of me which thinks what I say, which reflects on everything and on itself, and knows itself no more than the rest. At a first reading the avowal of ignorance could surprise, since in the second order, Cartesian and admitted as such, I know that I think, I comprehend extension by thought, and I know myself — is it not acquired that the self consists in my thought? Must these interrogations be attributed to an atheist interlocutor dumbfounded before the infinite spaces, or to a rhetorical facility of Pascal's own? There remains another explanation: in this interrogation it is not a matter of thinking oneself, on the serenity of the second order, but of making oneself *recognized* as such, as a person, by the eyes of the heart — and by the heart of another than myself. For the sequence runs, in filigree, the gap from the initial *je* to the final *moi*. Henceforth the ego no longer holds the centre, no longer does the office of a centre, discovers itself excentred from itself. Anticipating Nietzsche's critique of the autonomy of the *ego cogito*, Pascal stigmatizes it as the effect, unconscious of its own origin, of a more essential cause; better, he deepens the critique, since far from confining himself to putting representation in contradiction with itself, Pascal calls it in question *from a superior order*: the sufficiency of the ego to fix the centre remains admissible only in the second order, where in fact it can think itself perfectly; but in the third, where the ego cannot of itself love itself perfectly, it can no longer occupy or define a centre. Central in thought, the ego reveals itself peripheral in charity. I can no longer be anything but a self, excentred from *je* to the point that already *je* is an other — not another self, nor an other than the self, but an other than *je*. I am alienated, or literally, *je* is other than *je*, namely a self. I am not a self; rather, the one that I am *is* a self.

Such a transformation of *je* into a self raises an objection at once (pp. 347–351): Pascal reproaches the self, on the contrary, with claiming to be an absolute centre and loving itself and itself alone. The texts leave no ambiguity — man wanted to make himself the centre of himself and independent of God's help, by the desire to find his felicity in himself; believing himself a whole and seeing no body on which he depends, he believes he depends only on himself and wants to make himself centre and body himself; it is unjust that he make himself the centre of everything. But Marion answers that this is only an observation and not an approval, since it is accompanied by the very clear diagnosis of the confusion of orders, tyranny, that sustains the self's claim to the situation of centre. In the second order it goes without saying that the order of thought is to begin with oneself; but in the order of charity, I am the end of no one and have not the wherewithal to satisfy others. Such a claim to hold the centre proceeds, before any moral fault, from a *logical* fault: misrecognizing the heterogeneity and the hierarchy of the orders. When the Cartesian ego claims to be independent in morals because it is so in thought, it sins against rigour before even sinning against God. For it does not suffice to claim the centre, including in the third order; one must still be able to *attain* it — and the ego does not permit the self to get there. The ego verifies its primacy as first principle by *subtraction*, by hyperbolic doubt and a quasi-phenomenological reduction, and so conquers its independence by elimination of all the *cogitata*, whose very absence releases the absolute anteriority of the *ego cogito*; the ego affirms itself precisely because it appears *solus in mundo*. In the third order the stake demands love — that the self, and it is precisely this that requires the inversion of the subject ego into the object self, receive as addressee the love of others, then defined as subjects. Consequently the self must operate inversely to the ego, no longer by subtraction but by summation, not by abstraction but by accumulation; it no longer aims at solitude but at the gathering of everyone around it, to the exclusion of every other centre or beneficiary — each self is the enemy and would be the tyrant of all the others. On the pretext that each is a whole to himself, since when he dies the whole is dead for him, and hence that each believes he is all to all, the ego, prisoner of its illusion to the point of erecting itself into a centre of idolatry, infers that the self not only must but simply *can* by its own means establish itself as the centre of everyone's love. Even if the self could without injustice make itself loved as a centre toward which to tend, without injustice or inconvenience, it would quite simply not have the means to succeed. Its independence would depend on the amorous dependence of others, its centrality on the periphery, and its self-love on unappropriated loves. The ego neither must nor can transpose its dignity as principle from the second order to the third; the ego remains first principle in thought, but for love the self could not on that account become the first loved. If then the self cannot impose itself as lovable, it must be concluded that it remains hateable — whence the thesis that has the function of consecrating the decentring of the self and the defeat of the ego: the self is hateable. So that the true and only virtue is to hate oneself, since one is hateable by one's concupiscence, and to seek a truly lovable being in order to love it. What does hating the self mean? Doubtless not, despite certain locutions, hating oneself by a very modern self-hatred — first because hatred of self belongs, to an inversion near, to the idolatrous love of self; and then because what is at issue is renouncing positively to love the ego when it claims tyrannically to impose itself in the last order, in short refusing that the Cartesian ego preside over love as it presides over thought. One must hate not the self but the figure my soul borrows from the ego, and which dissimulates from it the autonomous logic of charity. In charity the centre is not identified from the model that the first principle furnishes in thought. Hatred of the self would therefore have no sense, save a perverse one, if there were not revealed in it by contrast the love of the true centre, alone neither unjust nor inconvenient and alone effective, the love for God: they want to love only God, they want to hate only themselves; one must love only God and hate only oneself. Marion sets this beneath the fundamental Augustinian doctrine of the two loves that made two cities — love of self to the contempt of God making the earthly city, love of God to the contempt of self the heavenly, the one glorying in itself and the other in the Lord, the one seeking its glory from men while for the other God as witness of conscience is its greatest glory. But Pascal decidedly orients that topic into the field of the individual soul, conformably moreover to other Augustinian tendencies, and passes from universal history to individual history. The reinterpretation leaves no doubt: God created man with two loves, one for God and the other for himself, but with this law that the love for God should be infinite, that is, with no other end than God himself, and that the love for oneself should be finite and referred to God. That prelapsarian situation engenders, after the sin of origin, the disappearance of the infinite love for God, so that self-love, remaining alone in that great soul capable of an infinite love, spread and overflowed into the void the love of God had left; and thus he loved himself alone, and all things for himself, that is, infinitely. The duality of the orders exposes itself the more to confusion, and so to the tyranny of the ego become self, in that it deploys itself on the ground of a duality already inscribed *in the third order itself*: on the parameter of charity, and originally, two loves must be exercised on two just proportions — one finite for the finite self, the other infinite for the infinite self. When metaphysics deploys, in its own order, the primacy of the ego, it exposes itself the more to the injustice of an infinite love of self by the self in that it meets a conflict already confused between two postulations of the third order. The metaphysical ego does not so much provoke self-love as lend it new arms and the appearance of a reason. The order of charity alone could arouse its own perversion, since an inferior order cannot see or act on a superior one; charity alone can lead charity astray, because it alone practises and makes it. The ego of metaphysics becomes idolatrous only when taken up by charity become self-love, to the exclusion of the love of God. The authentic status reserved for the metaphysical ego in the order of charity can therefore ultimately be determined only on the requirements proper to that order and that charity — which requires identifying the self that justly merits the function of the centre before which the finite self, and with it the ego of the second order, must decentre itself; and fixing the just status of the finite self, and with it of the metaphysical ego, in the order of charity.

Identifying the just self according to charity presupposes that it is here again a matter of loving a *self* (pp. 351–353). Now the antagonist of the unjust self that self-love privileges does not take up the title of self: to hate the self in oneself one must consent to the good will of the universal soul, tend to the general, to the universal being. But universality does not indicate here the abstraction of the being, which would forbid the irreducibility of a person; it designates the index contrary to that of self-love, particular to the point of tyranny, without excluding that a person correspond to it. Universality fixes the requirement of every self that would not be unjust: perfectly lovable because transcending all particularity. Whence the naming of that universal, still as a centre, as Jesus Christ — Jesus Christ is the object of everything and the centre toward which everything tends. Doubtless the demonstration that Christ fixes in himself the only just centre proceeds first from the interpretation of Scripture, since Jesus Christ is what both Testaments regard as their centre, and the two most ancient books in the world both regard Jesus Christ as their common centre and object. But on one hand Scripture plays here the role of an absolute memory of history and anthropology; and on the other the first formula comes precisely from the fragment that had refused the God of the metaphysical proofs, and particularly of the creation of the eternal truths — so that the identification of the centre in Christ has recourse to Scripture only insofar as philosophical discourse no longer manages to release the object and centre toward which all things tend. Marion's note draws the methodological consequence: it is from the superior point of view that the inferior lets its bankruptcy appear, far from the critique of the inferior permitting one to accede afterwards to the superior; so one should not be astonished that the ego yields to a new centre only by revelation, discovering it not by reasoning but by the Scriptures — and that the doctrine of Christ as centre comes from the Scriptures in no way weakens its pertinence with regard to the ego of thought; the contrary is true. If Christ alone merits the function of centre, it is because he alone respects the requirements of the third order: he can make himself universally loved because he is in his order of holiness, gave no inventions, did not reign, but was humble, patient, holy, holy, holy to God, terrible to demons, without any sin — or rather he came in great pomp and prodigious magnificence to the eyes of the heart which see wisdom. The centre is identified in and as Jesus Christ, whose holiness accomplishes in all justice the order of charity: he is universally lovable because he himself never loves any but God, and not himself. He can say, to me be the glory and not to thee, worm and earth, claiming for his self the love of mine, precisely because he can say in truth, the Father loves everything I do. That the centre remains in Jesus Christ a *self* is confirmed further by his function as object, which evidently indicates the objective of a desire and a tension — and how could the Augustinian theme of the interior more intimate to me than myself, higher than my highest, apply to a self of which it designates separately the objective, if it did not indicate the effectivity of a self principially at work in the finite self? If Jesus Christ offers himself as the universal object of everyone's love, he discovers the universal self for all men, in consequence of his character as a self holy, holy, holy to God and to the eyes of the heart. In Christ charity can recognize a self — no longer unjust, inconvenient and tyrannical, but holy. Therefore it must love him.

Fixing the just status of the self now becomes possible (pp. 353–355), first because Christ re-establishes the legitimacy of that concept in exercising it without yielding to self-love, and then and above all because Christ assumes also the function of the *je*. For it is remarkable that *Le Mystère de Jésus* — which must be treated as a theoretical text essential to the Pascalian enterprise, precisely because it belongs to the soberest spirituality — in its second part gives the word directly, that is, the function of uttering the *je*, to Christ. And contrary to the "Word" that Malebranche blasphemes in lending it too human doctrines, his own, Christ says his own: I was thinking of you; I shall do it in you; what I do; I have led; I am present to you; I do not deliver you; I am; I love you. And the meditant can dare in turn to pronounce *je* only in response to the word and the regard that, fixing him, permit it to him: I shall lose him then, Lord; Lord, I give you all; I see my abyss of pride, of curiosity, of concupiscence. Because Christ makes himself centre and therefore self, man can invoke him as a self; but he makes himself centre only insofar as he can first utter the *je*. In that *je*, which merges neither with the *je*/ego of the second order nor with the unjust self of the third, one must evidently recognize the *egō eimi* that Christ takes up, in John, from the Name revealed in Exodus 3:14. But such a divine *egō eimi* cannot fail to evoke the metaphysical *ego sum, ego existo* Descartes conquered. The rapprochement should not surprise, Marion says; what should rather surprise is the silence that usually surrounds so exceptional an encounter — the same name, *egō eimi*, *ego sum*, applying to God or to the human ego according as the second or the third order opens. To avoid the confusion of orders, which would here redouble the tyranny with a blasphemy, the following difficulty must be clarified: on what condition can man conserve in the third order the title that belongs to him in the second — do the *je*/ego and the finite self keep a legitimacy in the charity where the *je* of Christ blazes in his reign of holiness? To try to decide, an eventual ego must be reinterpreted no longer from the *cogitatio* it masters but from the *centre* it loves; and the question becomes sharper: can the centre and the loved object, in return, define the one who loves them and aims at them? Can the one who loves be known by that other whom he loves, on the logic of the third order where it is only a matter of loving, rather than by himself who thinks, on the logic of the second where it is a matter of thinking? Pascal decides plainly: not only do we know God only by Jesus Christ, but we know ourselves only by Jesus Christ; outside Jesus Christ we do not know what we ourselves are. Does Jesus Christ know us better than we know ourselves because, in a Malebranchist sense, he would dispose over our soul of a clear and distinct idea refused to us — because he would think and represent us better than we manage to? That weak interpretation absolutely confounds the orders, since in the third God's excellence holds not to thought but to charity. There remains only to interpret the knowledge Christ has of us, better than ours, in terms of charity; and Pascal in fact risks it, defining men's duty as consent to the conduct of the whole soul to which they belong, which loves them better than they love themselves. That statement finds precise echoes in *Le Mystère de Jésus*, where Christ performs them in the person of his *je*: your conversion is my affair; I am more your friend than this or that; I love you more ardently than you have loved your defilements. If finite and sinful man can here, in the third order, still legitimately say *me*, he owes it to Christ, who recognizes him as a self; and that self does not expose itself to the injustice of an abusive self, since it no longer affirms itself of itself as the ego of the second order does, but *receives itself* from an instance more originary to it than itself. For Christ goes back "more" to the origin of that self, in loving it "more" than any self-love would idolize itself, "more" than any cogito thinks itself — I was thinking of you in my agony — because he institutes it in charity and so legitimates it in the last order. By a strange substitution, which manifests the *admirabile commercium*, the *je*/ego, which loses itself in passing from the second to the third order, receives itself back from Christ as a self infinitely loved, on the unique condition that it infinitely abandon its thought *je*/ego so as to recognize, and therefore to *love*, only the *je* of Christ as sole centre. In this exchange man loses a *je*/ego, finite in the second order and unjust in the third, in order to gain a self infinitely loved in the third; he abandons a finite sufficiency or else a tyranny in order to receive a self just and infinite by grace. It is for this economy of grace that it should have been said that our proposition is of infinite force when there is the finite to hazard in a game where there are equal chances of gain and loss, and the infinite to gain — with this considerable correction, that here no chance intervenes, since charity has given everything in advance. The ego, in which metaphysics secured itself, must lose itself — or better, give itself — in order to receive itself in the third order. Metaphysics accedes to charity only by decentring itself absolutely, or else it freezes into a centre of idolatry. Facing the *Meditationes* in their order, only *Le Mystère de Jésus* can hold in the order of charity.

### § 25. The Destitution of Metaphysics

Pascal conducts a double polemic against Descartes — against his determination of God and against the institution of the ego as first principle through *cogitatio* — and what remains is to fix its scope (pp. 356–360). Three hypotheses offer themselves. One may hold to the theses explicitly criticized and infer that in the rest of the conceptual field Pascal remains, consciously or not, a strict Cartesian. Or, radicalizing that influence, one may stress that a discussion refutes only by first confirming its dependence on the adversary, so that Pascal, precisely because he criticizes Cartesian metaphysics, admits it as the unsurpassable horizon of his thought. The two hypotheses agree in restricting the scope of Pascal's calling Descartes into question: what is at issue would be only punctual disagreements about the requirements of the heart against rationalism, in no case a step back out of metaphysics, and the metaphysical horizon would remain intact and Cartesian. Marion's note records how widely that restriction is shared — Brunschvicg and Laberthonnière agree in it paradoxically, as do Heidegger and Sebba; and it finds its clearest and most disputable expression in Beaufret's presentation of Kierkegaard as a religious writer, a poet of the religious, who only describes man's religious situation, since more originary than Christianity itself is the onto-theo-logical structure of metaphysics — Beaufret assimilating Kierkegaard and Pascal under the single title of religious writer. Marion's rejoinder is exact: it goes without saying that the primacy of onto-theo-logy over Christian theology would have appeared to Pascal as the tyranny and idolatry par excellence of the second order over the third. There remains a third hypothesis — that Pascal, through the contestation of certain Cartesian theses, calls in question not only the whole metaphysics of Descartes but any thought, Cartesian or not, that would constitute itself as metaphysics; in a word, that Pascal anticipates what we have learned to designate as the end of metaphysics. Of the three the last appears the most doubtful because the most audacious; but on reflection the most audacious could prove the most just. For Pascal does not merely criticize certain Cartesian theses as though he meant to rectify them or replace them with others — that is the work of the post-Cartesian metaphysicians. He does not even refuse the decisions Descartes takes about the two most privileged beings of a special metaphysics, the soul and God, for that too falls to other metaphysicians, non-Cartesian but workmen still of onto-theo-logy. What is proper to Pascal consists in disqualifying, apropos the two essential theses of Descartes' special metaphysics — God as creator of the eternal truths, the ego as first principle insofar as *ego cogito* — the legitimacy itself of all metaphysics as such. Or rather, Marion is careful to say, Pascal contests neither the justice nor the rigour of a metaphysics but only its unconditioned character: metaphysics is valid only in its own order — the mind, evidence, knowledge; beyond, a superior order is discovered, already infinitely distant from the one in which metaphysics can constitute itself, from which metaphysics becomes second and over which it exercises no empire. Metaphysics undergoes neither refutation nor denial nor contempt from the fact of a third order: charity abandons the evidence of the mind to its own logic. Simply — but this is the point decisive in advance, if one considers the rise in power of metaphysics from Descartes to Hegel — metaphysics must henceforth recognize the irreducibility of an order it does not see but which sees it, comprehends it and judges it, the order of charity. To the letter, moreover, Pascal proclaims neither the end of metaphysics nor the death of philosophy; in observing that great Pan is dead he registers only the already ancient exhaustion of paganism. After Pascal, philosophy will be able to pursue its metaphysical road in appearance as if nothing had changed, and to accomplish the destiny of onto-theo-logy from the Cartesian institution, determining as far as Husserl. But after Pascal one point will be definitively acquired: the order of mind and of evidence in which metaphysics deploys itself covers absolutely nothing of the order of charity.
