Otherwise put, the personal revelation of the God of Abraham, God of Isaac, God of Jacob, not of the philosophers and the learned, orchestrates itself, on a certain logic and with rigorous concepts, into a *non-metaphysical* doctrine of man, of God and also of the world. Henceforth metaphysics will reduce to its own assumptions, onto-theo-logy in all its variations, without ever being able to claim to comprehend the God who reveals himself in Jesus Christ or any of the words that follow; and in fact, above all when metaphysics wants, in a last supposedly religious effort, to confound the orders — Spinoza, Leibniz and above all Malebranche — to the point of reasoning by evidence about the economy of grace, the extent of the disaster will mark well enough that henceforth charity neither manifests nor says itself except in its own order. With Pascal metaphysics enters a long and doubtless definitive widowhood of the order of charity: its road will pass by the concept, absolute mind and absolute knowledge, until it endures their twilight, but without ever touching charity even from afar. The Pascalian precept that governs his whole hermeneutic of Scripture — everything that does not go to charity is figure; charity is not a figurative precept — could apply just as pertinently to metaphysics: it does not go to charity, so it is figure; but charity is not figurative, so metaphysics is once again a figure. Figure, but of what? The Old Testament bears the mark of Christ because it announces him without knowing it or having seen him; perhaps metaphysics bears the mark of Christ because it keeps in hollow the sites he occupied there and the scars of his words. Metaphysics undergoes from Pascal no refutation in its own order, any more than Descartes endures a critique on the order of his own reasons; it undergoes much more and not less. The Heideggerian fashion of evading Kierkegaard, and so also Pascal, as mere religious writers who do not broach the onto-theological seriousness of metaphysics, sets aside a simple question: if they do not practise onto-theo-logy, which will readily be granted, do they think *short of* it or rather *beyond* it? Marion suggests that Pascal at least does not ignore metaphysics but deserts it and *destitutes* it. By destitution is to be understood the disqualification that does not criticize metaphysics in its own order but prevents its unjust overflowings into the order of charity by reducing it from the point of view of that same charity. Just as phenomenology operates reductions, but to consciousness; just as Descartes operates reductions to evidence and then to the ego; just as Heidegger operates a reduction of beings to the being of beings; so Pascal accomplishes a reduction of everything that happens to charity. The unconditioned reduction to charity is named destitution. To destitute metaphysics signifies: to show that a superior order remains infinitely distant from it, and that from that order's point of view the evidences of the mind are not worth the least movement of charity and can therefore be judged in terms of charity. For if the inferior orders cannot without injustice govern the superior order, the order of charity sees every thing, the inferior orders included, according as they keep or do not keep the glow of charity. And if Pascal both informs himself from Descartes and separates himself from him, it is not by some unavowed dependence on the metaphysics then making an epoch; it is that destitution too exercises a continual reversal from pro to con, since it takes up the metaphysical theses in order to repeat them — and so to dislocate and judge them — from the point of view of charity. Reason of effects: metaphysics could develop in the second order the effects of a reason — in fact, charity — that it ignores and does not see, since that reason inhabits the inaccessible light of the third order. Would metaphysics for Pascal manage inadequate ideas, on the acceptation Spinoza defines at the same period, a knowledge of effects whose causes we ignore? Doubtless, on condition of bringing a correction: whereas for Spinoza the causes of effects remain, like them, ideas, in Pascalian terms metaphysics manages in the second order the insensate effects of a reason which has nothing ideal or representable about it, since it plays at an infinite distance, in the order of charity. Effect of an invisible reason, figure of a lost charity, metaphysics appears as such — destituted of charity.

The destitution of metaphysics by charity cannot in principle be *proved* (pp. 360–363): like all the other reductions it must be accomplished step by step, each for himself, in an experimentation the more rigorous in that charity leaves less place for approximation and solipsism. But it can be confirmed by following certain of its enactments, and Marion collects three that Pascal himself sketched, chosen because they concern the common fund of all metaphysics and so Descartes' too — the destitutions of truth, of being, and of philosophy itself. Truth is never defined by the *adaequatio* or by method as in the second order, but, seen from the third, obeys a new parameter: loving it or not. Certainly in human things one says they must be known before being loved, and Pascal in his scientific work illustrated that precept; but in divine things they must be loved in order to be known. Now on the course of the world we are much more often among divine things than before human things, since self-love invades every domain including those of the flesh and the mind, so that most of the time truth is so obscured in this age and lying so established that unless one loves the truth one cannot know it. For the self claims to make itself loved by all while ceaselessly observing that it does not deserve the love it demands; whence follows the most unjust and criminal passion imaginable, a mortal hatred against that truth. Truth, empirical as much as rational, does not remain neutral but always depends on the reinterpretations or preconceptions our interests impose on it — a point Nietzsche and the Frankfurt School, among others, have since developed — so that even a knowledge claiming perfect disinterest in fact obeys the iron rules of self-love and belongs ultimately to the third order. The caesura among men cuts less between those who know the truth and those who ignore it than between those who are *against* the truth by hatred and those who in fact love it, following the greatest of the Christian virtues, which is the love of truth. Truth, understood at least in its full amplitude, falls first under love before being produced by method as a certainty; and the proof of the paradox lies in the always possible and often effective refusal of even scientific evidence when it harms self-love. Consequently truth can sometimes become synonymous with charity: those who do not love the truth take the pretext of contestation and of the multitude of those who deny it, and so their error comes only from their not loving the truth or charity; everything that shocks truth or charity is bad. Charity does not intervene here as a value-judgement that would overdetermine after the fact an already certain evidence; it plays a priori as a quasi-epistemological condition of manifestation of the evidence, which without it can remain misrecognized. Yet Pascal seems to venture dangerously further when he claims that one makes an idol of truth itself, since truth outside charity is not God but his image and an idol that must not be loved or adored. How not to sense here the shadowless violence of ideology — on one side truth, established by its own proofs, and on the other charity, judging it on an exterior criterion, God in this instance, at the risk of refusing truth for the needs of an uncontested cause? Such an interpretation of the fragment would nonetheless be a misreading, Marion insists, and would reflect how far the ideological mode of thought infects us, since it leads us easily to condemn it in its contrary and to tolerate it better everywhere else. For the text continues: and still less must one love or adore its contrary, which is lying; and it makes precise that, just as of truth, it happens that I make myself an idol of obscurity separated from God's order. Error exposes one to idolatry no less than truth, and the Nietzschean reversal of the one into the other does not dispense with charity. Pascal therefore does not confound truth and charity here by anticipation on the ideology of accomplished nihilism; he dislocates the sufficiency of the two truth-values so as to substitute for them a *triple* topic: falsity, inadmissible in every case; truth without charity, refused by self-love or accepted with self-love, and so truth falsified in the eyes of the heart which see wisdom; and truth loved, and so accepted as such. Such a topic demands a redoubled truth, in which to falsified truth answers a truth redoubled because loved — and a draft of Pascal's will go so far as to risk a "true truth." Metaphysics thus discovers itself destituted of truth, not because a higher instance would disqualify truth, since Pascal expressly refuses obscurity, but because truth requires, more than method, the exercise of charity in reply to the self-love that tyrannizes it with a silent and violent empire. Metaphysics claims to accede to truth by method alone, as to an object to be constructed; it deceives itself and deceives, since one must love the truth in order to accede to it. Destituting metaphysics of truth permits precisely opening oneself to it not despite but thanks to the want of a right method. Truth passes from metaphysics, which misses its most determining instance, to charity, which sees it because it loves it enough not to take offence at it.

The destitution of being offers a particular difficulty, paradoxically because Pascal seems to accomplish it at once and too easily (pp. 363–366). Two famous declarations answer one another: who even knows what it is *to be*, which it is impossible to define, since there is nothing more general and since to explain it one would have to begin by using that very word, saying *it is*, to be; and one cannot undertake to define being without falling into this absurdity, for one cannot define a word without beginning with *it is*, whether one expresses it or understands it — so to define being one would have to say *it is*, and thus employ the defined word in the definition. The aporia lies paradoxically in the ease with which Pascal infers, from the impossibility of defining the use of the verb *to be* in the function of copula, the uselessness of a radical interrogation about the being of beings; it seems thus to go without saying that Pascal repeats the metaphysicians' decision — being is, on the contradiction of a definition, *notissimum* or *per se notum*, which is metaphysics' abandonment of being. Must one not conclude that Pascal belongs here exactly to metaphysics and so to the second order? Another interpretation nonetheless remains open. First because in the incriminated texts Pascal observes only the indemonstrable and unfinishable evidence of being in its copula function, without advancing any other decision — in particular without reducing it to the rank of *conceptus objectivus entis*: if the question of being remains in suspense, at least no metaphysical decision closes it. And then and above all because Pascal introduces a new acceptation of *being*: our being unintelligible to ourselves. How is that to be understood? From the Cartesian point of view our being and manner of being attain on the contrary a perfect intelligibility — I am insofar as I think, or I am insofar as cause. Yet Pascal maintains an unintelligibility, which refers here to the third order; and in fact what is at issue is the man who infinitely surpasses man, because man is not what he is but is what he is no longer or not yet, like God and participating in the divinity. Being, which signifies here indeed the manner of being and not the being, must be referred not to the order of metaphysics, the second, but to that of charity, the third; and by that displacement being will have to submit to other requirements than those of the question of being. As earlier truth did, being passes under the jurisdiction of charity. For the soul, and not the mind, it will be a matter of choosing ceaselessly between two dispositions: either the hatred of our being that the consecration of self-love necessarily engenders, or the conversion to the universal being that permits love of it — a truly lovable being, the heart loving the universal being naturally. The Aristotelian theme of the being always sought is henceforth declined on the measure of charity: to seek a truly lovable being in order to love it. Marion refuses to evade the force of that transposition by alleging a confusion — an entirely metaphysical one — between being and beings. The concept of universal being does not reduce, in already Malebranchist terms, to a divine name of abstract being erected into a divine name; what is at issue is indeed a manner of being of the being that I am, since I can love either on the being reduced to the sole being proper to me, or on the being that infinitely oversteps me. It is not a matter of loving a more or less universal object but of *loving universally*, which implies an acceptation of being freed from the tyrannical reduction that a being — the self of self-love — never stops accomplishing on it. Beyond the ontological difference another difference makes its way with difficulty: on what ontic, but also ontological, conditions could a being, moreover privileged by metaphysics, the *ego cogito* as *cogitatio sui*, manage to *love*? Answer: on condition that the being revealed by and to that being infinitely overstep it, in an opening that disappropriates that being of itself. The non-ontological difference is exercised on being by the infinitely infinite distance from the second to the third order. Metaphysics tries to have done with being by knowing the privileged beings certainly; non-metaphysical thought tries to think being as such, without beings; the order of charity undertakes to convert the privileged being by submitting being itself to the test of love — know that this discourse is made by a man who went down on his knees before and after, to pray this infinite being without parts, to whom he submits all his own, to submit yours also for your good and for his glory. And Marion asks whether what is at issue there might not be the anticipated antagonist of the god before whom man can neither pray nor fall to his knees. At the least, he holds himself entitled to conclude that for Pascal the question of being is not exhausted in the horizon of metaphysics alone, because beyond the second order the third claims being as much as it claims all beings — according as it offers itself to be loved or holds itself back for hatred. Love and hatred do not belong to metaphysics, so metaphysics undergoes a second destitution, that of being. And it is not to be excluded that non-metaphysical thought of being as such is also exposed to that requisite, the more insistently in that it never stops claiming to escape it.

A last destitution remains, the most radical since it is exercised on philosophy itself (pp. 366–369): Pascal destitutes metaphysics of philosophy. The operation is accomplished by the redoubling of the *thaumazein* proper to philosophy — the philosophers, they astonish the common run of men; the Christians, they astonish the philosophers. A distortion already appears: what is at issue is no longer the astonishment philosophers feel before the fact that the world *is*, but the astonishment their astonishment arouses among the common run of men — instead of seeing, the philosophers are *seen*. Seen from outside by the common, they are seen by those who do not understand them, and so insofar as invisible; the inferior order, the common, the flesh, the first order, sees without seeing it the superior order, here the mind. The astonishment before the invisible superior order can redouble: the philosophers then see the Christians without comprehending them, and so see them as invisible, on the third order. The *thaumazein* no longer characterizes exclusively the philosophical regard but defines phenomenologically the impossibility of crossing the distance from an inferior order to a superior; it can apply as well to the incomprehension of the philosophers as to that of the common. Henceforth the philosophers are going to be seen by the Christians on the third order. Under that obscure glow, how do they appear? They offer the spectacle of a double inanity, that of their object and that of their regard. The inanity of their object holds to its vanity — vanity of the sciences, because they know only exterior things; vanity of untenable doctrines, since what the Stoics propose is so difficult and so vain; vanity of the essays in wisdom, since one must show the vanity of common lives and then the vanity of philosophical, Pyrrhonian and Stoic lives. Whence comes such vanity? From the vanity of the world, so visible and so little known; and the world holds its vanity from not apprehending itself in the glow of the third order and from not seeing what economy of charity embraces it. The philosophers, exercising their knowledge and their wisdom only in the second order, do not see the vanity of the world and so share it: whoever does not see the vanity of the world is very vain himself. Whence the folly of philosophy and its uselessness. The inanity of the philosophers' object results, more originally, from the inanity of their regard, which ignores the last order to the point of ignoring that it ignores it. That bankruptcy attests as well that they are governed, without recognizing it, by the refusal of charity; the rational doctrines can indeed be read as so many effects, in the second order, of a reason — self-love — that belongs to the third; a philosophy can also, and even first, be interpreted as the rational symptom of a decision of non-charity: the three concupiscences made three sects, and the philosophers did nothing but follow one of the three. Which can be explicated thus — pride, concupiscence of the third order, the *libido dominandi*, designates the Stoics and generally the pride of the philosophers who have known God and not their misery; pleasure, concupiscence of the first order, the *libido sentiendi*, aims at the Epicureans; and knowledge, concupiscence of the second order, the *libido sciendi*, could concern the sceptics or Pyrrhonians, since in doubting they confirm the ideal of knowledge. In fact, in these three sects philosophy tends not first to love wisdom but to make the men who philosophize loved by those who do not: they believe that God alone is worthy of being loved and admired — the avowed design on the second order — and they desired to be loved and admired by men, and they do not know their corruption — the latent intention the order of charity discerns in it. The distortion between design and intention is accomplished in the unjust ideal of the sage, and that perfection is horrible. The philosophers make no exception, despite themselves, to the uniform and universal vanity: vanity is so anchored in man's heart that a soldier, a camp-follower, a cook, a porter boasts and wants to have his admirers, and even the philosophers want them. Pascal does not merely take up the polemic against the theoretical incoherence of the philosophers, though he cites the Ciceronian adage that nothing so absurd can be said that it is not said by some philosopher, and comments "divine!" He disqualifies philosophy's claim to norm itself exclusively on the requirements of the second order, without recognizing itself always also, and even first, *in via*, among the prestiges of self-love and the obscure glow of charity. A man, in order to be according to philosophy or even to be a philosopher, is nonetheless first a sinner — or a saint. And if philosophy mocks charity, charity judges it, and it blazes out that to mock philosophy is truly to philosophize. Philosophy must therefore destitute itself, or rather let itself be instituted in charity and no longer only in the second order; if philosophy truly loves wisdom, it must endure without fear the regard on it of the eyes of the heart which see wisdom — in short, it must love *the* Wisdom; and at once philosophy oversteps the domain and the range of the second order, and the order of charity is already at issue.

These three destitutions do not exclude others (p. 369). They suffice already to confirm the situation of metaphysics — precisely because metaphysics occupies a place on *its* order, it can be overcome, circumvented, and even confirmed. The doctrine of the three orders accomplishes, before and doubtless more soberly than the modern efforts, a calling into question of the sufficiency and pertinence of metaphysics, because it only puts metaphysics on notice. Pascal puts metaphysics on notice to recognize its own dwelling: it remains legitimate only in the second order, it cannot claim to govern the third, and it cannot withdraw from the third order's judgement upon it. He puts metaphysics on notice to admit its decentring before the only centre and object, the unique universal being, the sole eyes that see wisdom — charity, incommensurable and without analogy to any greatness of the inferior orders. In his confrontation with Descartes, Pascal conducted no polemic from metaphysician to metaphysician; nor did he merely refute Descartes as apologists and religious writers would have done, and in fact did; he constrained Cartesian metaphysics and, through it, every coming figure of onto-theo-logy, to admit that it dwells, ultimately and obscurely, *in charity* — just as the second order exposes itself to the regard of the third, which nonetheless remains invisible to it. So metaphysics undergoes its last destitution: far from seeing without being seen, it is seen without seeing, and without seeing that it is seen. Pascal's greatness holds to such a destitution of metaphysics by charity. But among Descartes' greatnesses, not the least was to have offered his redoubled onto-theo-logy to the test of such destitutions.

## The Question of an Opening

Before and in order to pose correctly the question of an opening, the closure of a question sketched at the outset as a programme must be completed (p. 371), and both operations can be accomplished in the recension of four principal conclusions. These echo the ones that already gathered the results of *Sur l'ontologie grise de Descartes* and *Sur la théologie blanche de Descartes*; in taking them up and confirming them in the essential, Marion will also sometimes have to displace and rectify them — which proves only that they were real questions and tested results. And precisely because such results are reached, new problems surge each time, awaiting new research in territories still little known.

Descartes takes up the title of *Metaphysica* but modifies its conceptual articulations radically (pp. 371–373). He substitutes in particular, for the *metaphysica generalis* and soon for *ontologia*, the until then unheard-of primacy of all the things that can be known first, and so of the ego — so that the *prima philosophia* of the *Meditationes* no longer concerns first, and despite the title, God and the soul, but redoubles that classical part of *Metaphysica* by another primacy, without refusing it. Do two primacies in search of a universality still constitute a metaphysics? Holding to the study of the nomenclature of the sciences in conflict alone, one would have to answer without doubt negatively. But if one refers, as to a working hypothesis, to the onto-theo-logical constitution of metaphysics as Heidegger released it, a positive though more complex answer appears plausible. For the new primacy the ego exercises interprets universally every being as *cogitatum*; whereby the grey ontology of the *Regulae* is not only confirmed into a clean ontology of the *ens ut cogitatum* but at last attains its foundation in the ego as *cogitatio sui*. The hesitation of the *Regulae* between the epistemological and the ontic interpretations of the cogitated object is definitively decided by the conquest, in the *Meditationes*, of a foundation, the ego, which folds *cogitatio* back on itself to the point of reaching itself there again as *ens* — *primus enim sum*. The new primacy therefore institutes not only an epistemological order of reasons, nor even merely a new being par excellence, but universally an ontology of the *ens* in general — and one that does not confine itself to making possible the simple position of Berkeley or Malebranche. For the first onto-theo-logy of the *ens ut cogitatum* is redoubled, in the middle of the *Meditationes*, by a second, in which the interpretation of the *ens* in general as *causatum* founds itself on the being par excellence thought as *causa sui*. Whence *conclusion I*: far from the difficulty of assigning a metaphysical status to Cartesian thought proceeding from a disappearance of the doctrine of the *ens* in general, it holds to the ambivalence of a redoubled onto-theo-logy. The conclusion raises at once a corollary and a demand. The corollary: Descartes belongs by full right to the history of metaphysics, whose destiny he shares and radically orients; to the interrogation that has led Marion for a decade — has Descartes, who refuses all the arguments of Plato and Aristotle, confronted the question of the being of beings? — the answer can be affirmative. The demand: since a continuity is henceforth recognized between Suarez and Kant *by way of* Descartes, and no longer only by way of the *Schulmetaphysik* despite Descartes, how is it to be explained that this same Descartes totally ignored the elaboration by his contemporaries, Calov and Goclenius, and even by his most faithful disciples, Clauberg, of the concept of *ontologia*? A history of the birth of *ontologia* remains to be written, which should clarify the paradox that the science of the being as such finds its name and its status in Descartes' epoch and, on Clauberg its inventor's own account, thanks to Descartes, without Descartes ever concerning himself with it. Otherwise put, why does the double ontology, clearly delivered by the redoubled onto-theo-logy of Descartes, remain anonymous at the very moment when, for the first time in the history of metaphysics and under Descartes' influence, it conquers its emblematic name?

If the metaphysical pertinence of Cartesian thought holds to the deployment in it of a figure of onto-theo-logical constitution, and if therefore that constitution explains Descartes, the relation must also be reversed (pp. 373–374): Descartes complicates the onto-theo-logical constitution in redoubling it. That complexification imposes deciding between two hypotheses about the essence of the constitution. Either it admits only the dual unity of a theo-logy and an ontology as Heidegger expounds it in the famous 1957 lecture — on which hypothesis the redoubling would attest either an error of the interpreter or an aberration of Descartes himself, though it would then be necessary to explain either the fecundity of an error of interpretation or the so perfect rigour of an aberration. Or there remains a second hypothesis: the Cartesian initiative of redoubling it reveals and deepens the essence of the onto-theo-logical constitution of metaphysics, of which Heidegger would have formulated only an elementary outline, and whose hermeneutic fecundity would grow in direct proportion to its eventual complexifications. From this hypothesis would follow *conclusion II*: Descartes lets himself be interpreted by the onto-theo-logical constitution only insofar as he confirms it in redoubling it, so as to relaunch it as a hermeneutic hypothesis. Corollary: never has the onto-theo-logical constitution been so visible as in its Cartesian redoubling, precisely because it splits in two there; but never either has it so manifested the *fragility* of metaphysics, precisely because the play plays there in broad daylight — play between two articulated dualities, play also of the daylight that filters between their ill-adjusted assemblages, play between two beings par excellence privileged in turn, where one should be less astonished at the ego's accession to the rank of first being than one should interrogate the attribution of the *causa sui* to God as a divine name, when it is only a function of one onto-theo-logy among others possible. Descartes, by the very fact that he illustrates to perfection the onto-theo-logical essence of metaphysics, very evidently discloses its fundamental inconstancy; and so he could enlighten us about the essence of onto-theo-logy as such more still than that constitution enlightens us about the metaphysical status of Cartesian thought. The paradoxical result provokes a new question. Descartes' successors, whether they give themselves out as his disciples or claim to be his adversaries, depend on him, particularly for their metaphysical status, which they have all, Leibniz excepted, left indeterminate or at best implicit. Could one not try to reconstitute their respective statuses from one or the other of the Cartesian postulations — who attaches himself to the onto-theo-logy of the *ens ut cogitatum*, and on what arrangements; who depends on the onto-theo-logy of the *ens ut causatum*, and following what variations; who links himself to neither and so does not accede to metaphysical dignity? Through the prism of the onto-theo-logy Descartes redoubled, the true metaphysical posterity of Cartesianism could thus be revealed, coinciding doubtless only very partially with the influences, critiques and doxographies the history of ideas legitimately records for its part. In this optic a metaphysical history of the *causa sui* would become possible — with at least the merit of not attributing its paternity to Spinoza, and the more arduous task of measuring its relation to the Leibnizian formulation of the *principium reddendae rationis*.

Authenticated as a metaphysician by the onto-theo-logical constitution whose essence he discovers better in complicating it, Descartes nonetheless escapes it in part (pp. 375–376). By a paradox that surprised Marion along the way, Descartes sets onto-theo-logy playing only in order to transgress it, by dint of manifesting its play. The ego dispenses with its role as privileged substance after conquering it in hard combat, so as to pass from effectivity to the complicit conflict of necessity with possibility — and to win freedom there. And among the three determinations of the divine essence, only two integrate themselves into the redoubled onto-theo-logy, *ens perfectissimum* and *causa sui*, leaving the third — in fact the first — without metaphysical site: the infinite remains out of metaphysics' play. Marion recovers here the ultimate conclusion of the earlier study, that the theiology of all metaphysics leaves behind it, like a fortress bypassed but not conquered, the question of a mediated relation of the finite to the infinite. And with the determination of God as infinite, what is also at issue is the incomprehensibility of the God creator of the eternal truths, such as from 1630 to 1649 he governs Descartes' thought. Whence *conclusion III*: Descartes belongs the more justly to metaphysics in that he establishes onto-theo-logically its exact limits and manages sometimes, under these conditions, to transgress them. Only a thesis that can be refuted deserves to be proved: the redoubled onto-theo-logical constitution of Cartesian metaphysics can often be confirmed, since it admits exceptions; Cartesian metaphysics has limits, since Descartes sometimes crosses them. Whence a question — has the enterprise sense and rigour that would discern within a single corpus between metaphysical and non-metaphysical statements; do the latter not always belong, whether we understand it or not, to metaphysics? The extreme difficulty of the question reinforces the objection, all the more since Marion's own work offers an argument against such a distinction. The creation of the eternal truths integrates itself in fact into none of the figures of the redoubled onto-theo-logy, on the same title as the determination of God as infinite, and Marion concluded that it does not belong to the metaphysics closed within the constitution; yet Pascal, criticizing Descartes as a metaphysician, stigmatizes precisely as a *metaphysical* doctrine that same creation of the eternal truths Marion claimed to except from it. Must one conclude to the impossibility or inconsistency of a delimitation of metaphysics? Marion suggests on the contrary that at each occurrence of metaphysics or of a metaphysical statement one would have to determine exactly which onto-theo-logy sustains and justifies it. In the present case a double answer lifts the difficulty: for Descartes, the metaphysical truths being created, the creating instance remains incomprehensible and trans-metaphysical, and in that sense does not fall under the redoubled onto-theo-logy; for Pascal, that creating instance deploys itself first as a "first truth" and as a cause, an "Author," and in that sense the creation of the eternal truths concentrates in itself the two words of the redoubled onto-theo-logy. The conflict here between Pascal and Descartes suggests that the establishment of the limits of a metaphysics forms an integral part of its constitution. It remains for interpreters to elaborate an onto-theo-logical hermeneutic fine enough to pick up the indices of a limit, and to make themselves its patient frontiersmen.

As for Pascal, he does not intervene marginally in a research on the Cartesian constitution of metaphysics but holds an absolutely essential role in it (pp. 376–378), because he refuses Descartes — as Marion has tried to establish — on a single point: that Descartes, as a metaphysician, claims to reintegrate the question of God into onto-theo-logy. So Pascal powerfully confirms that Descartes assumes throughout the function of a metaphysician, since it is only that function he reproaches him with; in refuting certain metaphysical doctrines of Descartes, Pascal guarantees his metaphysical authenticity in general. That first role evidently implies a second, to pose apropos Cartesian metaphysics the question of the limits of all metaphysics. But posing limits is possible, at least in principle, only if they are transgressed — whence Pascal's third role, to cross the limits of onto-theo-logy where Descartes barely senses them. Whence *conclusion IV*: Descartes redoubles onto-theo-logy so as to offer Pascal a perfect metaphysics to overcome. "To overcome metaphysics," short of sinking to the rank of a sinister watchword, can signify only inverting Platonism with Nietzsche, destroying the history of ontology with Heidegger, deconstructing sense with Derrida. However diverse these procedures remain, they keep two characteristics in common. They overcome metaphysics only *diachronically*, at the term of its deployment, so that none of them could have been exercised or appeared before the historical or historial closure of metaphysics; and consequently they all render impossible a new surging of metaphysics, whose terrain they at once forbid and occupy — so that they are exercised with an invisible violence, since they overcome metaphysics only in completing it and substituting themselves for it. Pascal on the contrary introduces a new mode of overcoming, destitution, which can be exercised *synchronically*, contemporaneously with each of the metaphysical epochs. It manages this without destruction, without interdiction, without claim, because it alone abandons metaphysics to itself: destitution passes *through* metaphysics and surpasses its limits — which it thereby discerns better than metaphysics itself — because it passes to another instance than metaphysics, which it therefore has no need to combat. Another instance: in Pascalian terms, another order. Pascal does not refute Descartes' redoubled onto-theo-logy; he only *sees* it — but he sees it from the point of view of a more powerful order, charity, which, in simply considering metaphysics as an inferior order, judges it and destitutes it. Metaphysics undergoes neither refutation nor recuperation nor even delimitation: it appears as such — vain in the regard of charity. Destituted metaphysics remains, but in its own order and place, which henceforth lose primacy. Between Pascal and Descartes Marion has been able only to sketch, on a particular case, the traits of such a destitution; to make them precise and confirm them in the analysis of comparable conflicts along the history of metaphysics, it would be necessary on one hand to reconquer the fundamental elements of a doctrine of charity taken in its own rigour, and on the other to locate the guiding axes of a *history of charity*, so as first to test its irreducibility to onto-theo-logy in its diverse figures and then to release the different occurrences of the destitution of metaphysics by charity. That double task suffices to open the last question — the most delicate, certainly, since it requires better than mind and resolution; but it is just that a God so pure should discover himself only to those whose heart is purified.

So Descartes remains one of our nearest contemporaries (p. 378). To the motives for that pertinence already established by Marion's predecessors, two are added: Descartes teaches us the play of the onto-theo-logical constitution of all metaphysics, and Descartes recognizes limits to the onto-theo-logical constitution to the point of exposing it to its eventual destitution.
